--- Page 1 ---
<meta>This is the title page of the 1640 edition of the Hortus Eystettensis. Continuity established with the 1613 first edition; this "second care" or second edition maintains the monumental scale of Besler's original work.</meta>

# THE GARDEN OF EICHSTÄTT
### OR THE
<insert>Belser</insert> <note>A handwritten name, likely a previous owner.</note>
DILIGENT AND ACCURATE DELINEATION AND REPRESENTATION FROM LIFE OF ALL THE PLANTS, FLOWERS, AND SHRUBS COLLECTED WITH SINGULAR ZEAL FROM VARIOUS PARTS OF THE WORLD, WHICH WERE FORMERLY TO BE SEEN IN THE MOST FAMOUS GARDENS SURROUNDING THE EPISCOPAL CASTLE IN THAT VERY PLACE.

### SECOND EDITION <note>original: "curis secundis," literally "with second cares," indicating a revised or subsequent printing.</note>
### IN THE YEAR
### 1640.
<insert>Missouri Botanical Garden 1890</insert> <note>Library acquisition stamp.</note>

---

->SOLOMON<- <note>The biblical King Solomon, traditionally associated with profound wisdom and knowledge of all plants "from the cedar of Lebanon to the hyssop that grows out of the wall."</note>

->CYRUS<- <note>Cyrus the Younger, a Persian prince celebrated by ancient writers for his personal dedication to gardening and for laying out the "paradise" gardens of Sardis.</note>

---

Wolfgang Kilian engraved this. <note>Kilian was a member of a famous family of Augsburg engravers.</note>

<summary>This is the ornate title page for the 1640 edition of the Hortus Eystettensis, the most celebrated botanical work of the 17th century, documenting the gardens of the Prince-Bishop of Eichstätt. The imagery links the garden to the wisdom of King Solomon and the horticultural skill of the Persian King Cyrus.</summary>
<keywords>Hortus Eystettensis, botanical illustration, King Solomon, King Cyrus, Wolfgang Kilian, Eichstätt, 17th-century botany, Prince-Bishop Johann Caspar von Westerstetten</keywords>

<meta>This page serves as the formal dedication for the "Hortus Eystettensis" (The Garden of Eichstätt). It establishes the patronage of the Bishop of Eichstätt and provides a poetic timeline of the book's production across different eras.</meta>

# TO THE MOST REVEREND

->AND<-

# MOST ILLUSTRIOUS

## PRINCE

->AND LORD, LORD<-

# MARQUARD <note>Marquard II Schenk von Castell, Prince-Bishop of Eichstätt from 1637 to 1685.</note>

## BISHOP

## OF EICHSTÄTT

->THIS<-

->GARDEN<-

## OF EICHSTÄTT

->CONSTRUCTED IN THE GOLDEN AGES<-

->DESCRIBED UPON COPPER PLATES<-

->REPRINTED IN THE IRON YEARS <note>A reference to the hardships of the Thirty Years' War, contrasting the "Golden Age" of the garden's founding with the "Iron" era of this later edition.</note><-

->MOST HUMBLY<-

->DEDICATES AND CONSECRATES<-

->OF HIS HIGHNESS<-

->*THE MOST DEVOTED SERVANT*<-

->CHARLES BERTIUS<-

<summary>This is the formal dedication page of the botanical masterpiece "Hortus Eystettensis," addressed to Prince-Bishop Marquard of Eichstätt by Charles Bertius. It poetically describes the work's history, from its creation in a "golden age" to its reprinting during the difficult "iron" years of the 17th century.</summary>

<keywords>Marquard II Schenk von Castell, Prince-Bishop of Eichstätt, Hortus Eystettensis, Charles Bertius, botanical patronage, 17th-century dedication, copperplate engraving</keywords>

--- Page 3 ---
<meta>This page serves as the frontispiece for the 1640 edition of the "Hortus Eystettensis." It follows the previous page's dedication, reinforcing the role of Prince-Bishop Marquard II in preserving the botanical work during the ravages of the Thirty Years' War.</meta>

->THE MOST REVEREND AND ILLUSTRIOUS PRINCE AND LORD, LORD MARQUARD II, BY THE GRACE OF GOD, BISHOP OF EICHSTÄTT, PRINCE OF THE HOLY ROMAN EMPIRE. IN THE YEAR OF CHRIST 1640. AT THE AGE OF 35.<- <note>original: "REVERENDISSIMVS ATQVE ILLVSTRISSIMVS PRINCEPS AC DOMINVS DNS MARQVARDVS II. DG. EPISCOPVS EISTETTENSIS S.R.I. PRINCEPS AO XPI CIƆ . IƆCXL. AET. XXXV."</note>

<margin>
**FAITH, HOPE, AND CHARITY** <note>original: "FIDES SPES CHARI." These are the three theological virtues represented by the figure on the left.</note>
</margin>

<margin>
**TEMPERANCE** <note>Represented by the figure on the right pouring liquid, symbolizing moderation.</note>
</margin>

# BY A SECOND EFFORT <note>original: "CVRIS SECVNDIS." A Latin idiom referring to a second edition or a work that has been revised and improved with further care.</note>

->To have guarded the Hesperian gardens by driving away the dragons,<- <note>In Greek mythology, Hercules' eleventh labor was to steal golden apples from the Garden of the Hesperides, which was guarded by a dragon.</note>
->Was believed to be a labor equal to that of Hercules.<-
->These gardens too, ripened under German suns<-
->(Which this new book dedicates with skillfully engraved copper)<-
->The care of Marquard rescued from Gothic fury:<- <note>original: "Gothico furori." This refers to the "Goths" as a metaphor for the barbaric destruction caused by the Thirty Years' War (1618–1648).</note>
->How much more worthy than the Herculean labor was this task.<-

<column-break/>

->**IN MY GOD**<- <note>original: "IN DEO MEO." Located beneath the Tetragrammaton (the Hebrew name of God, יהוה) on the left medallion.</note>

->**IN MY PRINCE**<- <note>original: "IN PRINCIPE MEO." Located on the right medallion, signifying the Bishop’s dual loyalty to God and the Holy Roman Emperor.</note>

<summary>This frontispiece features a portrait of Prince-Bishop Marquard II and a poem comparing his preservation of the "Hortus Eystettensis" to the labors of Hercules. It highlights the Bishop's role in saving the botanical work from the "Gothic fury" of the Thirty Years' War.</summary>

<keywords>Marquard II Schenk von Castell, Prince-Bishop of Eichstätt, Hercules, Hesperides, Thirty Years' War, copperplate engraving, frontispiece, theological virtues, Tetragrammaton</keywords>

--- Page 4 ---
<meta>This page serves as the original frontispiece honoring Prince-Bishop Johannes Conradus von Gemmingen (r. 1595–1612), the founder and original patron of the "Hortus Eystettensis." While the previous page represented the 1640 edition and the garden's survival, this page reflects the garden's initial creation in 1606, before the devastation of the Thirty Years' War.</meta>

->THE MOST REVEREND AND ILLUSTRIOUS PRINCE AND LORD, LORD JOHANNES CONRADUS, BISHOP OF EICHSTÄTT. IN THE YEAR 1606. AT THE AGE OF 45.<- <note>original: "REVERENDISSIMVS ATQVE ILLVSTRISSIMVS PRINCEPS AC DOMINVS D. IOANNES CONRADVS EPISCOPVS EYSTETTENSIS. ANNO 1606. ÆTATIS 45."</note>

Wolfgang Kilian, Engraver <note>original: "Wolfgang Kilian F." The "F" stands for *fecit*, Latin for "he made it." Kilian was a renowned Augsburg engraver.</note>

# UNDER HIS INITIAL AUSPICES <note>original: "PRIMIS AVSPICIIS." This phrase indicates that the following verses describe the garden's first period of growth under its founding patron.</note>

->Behold the happy labors under Prince Conrad,<-
->When Sweden did not yet know the riches of the Altmühl.<- <note>original: "ALMONIAS SVECIA." The Altmühl is the river flowing through Eichstätt. This line suggests a time of peace before the Swedish army's invasion during the Thirty Years' War.</note>
->From here shine rich necklaces with tawny gems,<-
->From there the field, rivaling them, takes pride in its various flowers:<-
->Truly, so that nothing beautiful would be lacking from the garden he began,<-
->He mixed these golden apples with the roses of his homeland.<- <note>The "golden apples" likely refer to exotic citrus fruits or metaphors for the rare plants Conrad collected.</note>

<margin>
**TO BIND FORTUNE** <note>original: "VINCIENDAE FORTVNAE." Located on the left emblem, suggesting that through wisdom and preparation, one can control the whims of fate.</note>
</margin>

<margin>
**TO CROWN VIRTUE** <note>original: "CORONANDAE VIRTVTI." Located on the right emblem, indicating that moral excellence and hard work deserve a reward.</note>
</margin>

<summary>This frontispiece commemorates Johannes Conradus von Gemmingen, the Prince-Bishop of Eichstätt who commissioned the original botanical garden and the book. The accompanying poem celebrates the peace and botanical wealth of the region before it was touched by the 17th-century wars.</summary>

<keywords>Johannes Conradus von Gemmingen, Bishop of Eichstätt, Wolfgang Kilian, botanical patronage, Altmühl River, 17th-century engraving, Garden of Eichstätt</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
# TO THE SPECTATOR.

<image-desc>A large ornamental woodcut initial letter 'E' featuring intricate floral and scrollwork patterns within a square frame.</image-desc>

WE present to you through these many pages the GARDEN OF EICHSTÄTT <note>original: "HORTVM EYSTETTENSEM"</note>, constructed some years ago at the very roots of WILLIBALD’S CASTLE <note>The Willibaldsburg, the bishop's residence in Eichstätt</note> during a GOLDEN AGE, by royal daring, at the greatest expense, and with incomparable success, under the auspices of the Most Reverend and Illustrious PRINCE JOHANN KONRAD <note>Johann Konrad von Gemmingen, Prince-Bishop of Eichstätt (1593–1612), who commissioned the original garden and book</note>. But, such are human affairs: a STORM recently arriving from the NORTH <note>A reference to the Swedish intervention in the Thirty Years' War</note> has shaken off the flowers and overturned the disturbed beds along with their little plants; so that now, in the place where once the Gentlest Species grew, nothing else remains but the fierce and grim Face of BELLONA <note>The Roman goddess of war</note>. In one place, ramparts stand over threatening rocks; in another, cliffs have been cast down to defend the mounds, with lightning-bolts <note>A metaphor for cannons or artillery</note> peering through the slanting crags, and trunks and protruding stakes, and baskets trembling with rough gravel; and whatever else the Ingenuity of the modern Age has devised for the destruction of warring men.

However, lest all Memory of this Immortal Undertaking should fade for Posterity, the Most Reverend and Illustrious PRINCE AND LORD, LORD MARQUARD <note>Marquard II Schenk von Castell, Prince-Bishop of Eichstätt (1637–1685)</note>, Bishop of Eichstätt—whom a petitioned GOD has granted again for the Mitigating of the Calamities of these Times—has acted. By His Gift and Expense, you have these things which we have elaborated in these Second Labors <note>Referring to this new edition or printing</note> according to the vow and opinion of those who seemed to desire this work; of whom there were many in FRANCE, BELGIUM, and BRITAIN.

We have followed the order which the first examples led, except that we believe it unnecessary to divide this harvest into so many small bundles; nevertheless, they come forth in a Three-part Series, so that the First Class displays the Spring Species, the Second the Summer ones, and the Last those which can scarcely be ripened among Us as Autumn rushes headlong.

The descriptions—which some deemed insufficiently accurate, and others insufficiently clever or unable to satisfy the impatience of this Century—we have omitted; especially so that we might not sin by the example of ancient artists, who found it necessary to aid the simplicity of their art by adding Indexes in good faith, so that the People might be reminded, for instance, that it is an Ox being looked at, and not a Man.

As for the rest that could be added here: look, judge, and run your eyes over it, and Give Thanks to the MOST ILLUSTRIOUS PRINCE MARQUARD, whose Honors deserve to be Crowned with even more FLOWERS than those you shall Read of here.

-> ) ❊ ( <-
-> ❦ <-

<summary>This preface introduces a new edition of the famous Garden of Eichstätt botanical work, lamenting the destruction of the physical gardens during the Thirty Years' War and praising Prince-Bishop Marquard for preserving the work's memory.</summary>
<keywords>Hortus Eystettensis, Willibaldsburg, Thirty Years' War, Johann Konrad von Gemmingen, Marquard II Schenk von Castell, botany, garden history, Eichstätt</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This is the title page for the Spring section of the work, establishing the botanical classification system used throughout the volume.</meta>

# OF PLANTS
## OF THE GARDEN AT EICHSTÄTT.
### *The Spring Class.*

<note>original: "Plantarum Horti Eystettensis, Classis Verna." This title page introduces the first seasonal division of the famous florilegium commissioned by Prince-Bishop Johann Konrad von Gemmingen. The "Spring Class" categorizes plants by their blooming season rather than modern biological families.</note>

<summary>The title page for the Spring section of the Garden of Eichstätt, marking the beginning of the botanical descriptions for that season.</summary>
<keywords>Hortus Eystettensis, Spring Class, botanical garden, Eichstätt, plant classification</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page serves as a botanical index for a collection of specimens, specifically categorized under "Spring Class." It lists plants by folio (page) number, providing both the Latin scientific names and the early modern German common names.</meta>

# SPRING CLASS.

### Folio 1.

1. Boxwood. <note>Latin: *Buxus*</note> **Box tree.**
2. Syringa with blue flowers. <note>Latin: *Syringa flore cæruleo*</note> **Spanish lilac.**
3. Syringa with ash-colored flowers. <note>Latin: *Syringa flore cinerei coloris*</note> **Ash-colored Spanish lilac.**

### Folio 2.

1. Butcher's broom. <note>Latin: *Ruscus*</note> **Butcher's broom.**
2. Italian Syringa with full white flowers. <note>Latin: *Syringa Italica flore albo pleno*</note> **White lilac with double blossoms.**
3. Syringa with simple white flowers. <note>Latin: *Syringa flore albo simplici*</note> **White lilac with single blossoms.**

### Folio 3.

1. Tree of Judas, Wild Pod. <note>Latin: *Arbor Judæ, Siliqua Siluestris*</note> **Judas tree / Wild carob.**
2. One-leaf, Wild Cotyledon. <note>Latin: *Vvifolium Cotyledon Siluestre*</note> **One-leaf.**
3. Lesser Moonwort, or Grape-fern. <note>Latin: *Lunaria minor, seu Botrytis*</note> **Moon-herb / Moon-rue.**
4. Marsh Liverwort. <note>Latin: *Hepatica palustris*</note> **Water-liverwort.**

### Folio 4.

1. Cherry with full flowers. <note>Latin: *Cerasus flore pleno*</note> **Double cherry blossom.**
2. Wild cluster cherry. <note>Latin: *Cerasus racemosa Siluestris*</note> **Alder buckthorn / wild bird cherry / or bird cherry.**
3. Tips of the Spruce. <note>Latin: *Summitates Piceæ*</note> **Spruce / red fir blossom.**

### Folio 5.

1. Apple with multiple flowers, or Full-flowered. <note>Latin: *Poma flore multiplici, seu Pleniflora*</note> **Double apple blossom.**
2. Wild viscous Lychnis, with flesh-colored flowers. <note>Latin: *Lychnis viscosa Siluestris, flore incarnato*</note> **Wild flesh-colored pinks.**
3. Lesser wild Rampion. <note>Latin: *Rapunculus Siluestris minor*</note> **Wild rampion / with purple-white flowers.**

### Folio 6.

1. Pistachio tree. <note>Latin: *Pistacia arbor*</note> **Foreign pistachio-nut.**
2. Laurel with flower. <note>Latin: *Laurus cum flore*</note> **Laurel tree with its blossom.**
3. Almond with flower. <note>Latin: *Amygdala cum flore*</note> **Almond tree with the blossom.**

### Folio 7.

1. Narrow-leaved Anagyris. <note>Latin: *Anagyris angustifolia*</note> **Lentil tree / Tree beans.**
2. Broad-leaved Anagyris. <note>Latin: *Anagyris latifolia*</note> **Broad-leaved tree beans.**

### Folio 8.

1. Bladder-nut tree, Bladder nut. <note>Latin: *Staphyllodendron, Nux Vesicaria*</note> **Bladder-nut.**
2. Laurel-like Clematis, with white flowers. <note>Latin: *Clematis Daphnoides, flore albo*</note> **Periwinkle / with white flowers.**
3. Laurel-like Clematis with blue flowers. <note>Latin: *Clematis Daphnoides flore cæruleo*</note> **Blue periwinkle.**
4. Laurel-like Clematis with purple flowers. <note>Latin: *Clematis Daphnoides flore purpureo*</note> **Purple-colored periwinkle.**
5. Laurel-like Clematis with full purple flowers. <note>Latin: *Clematis Daphnoides flore purpureo pleno*</note> **Full purple-colored periwinkle.**

### Folio 9.

1. Another Xylosteum. <note>Latin: *Xylosteum alterum*</note> **Little bone-wood.**
2. Xylosteum with white flowers. <note>Latin: *Xylosteum floribus albis*</note> **Common bone-wood with white flowers.**
3. Sharp-thorn. <note>Latin: *Oxyacantha*</note> **Barberry / Wine-vine / Sour-thorn / Barberry.**

### Folio 10.

1. Tree-like Elder. <note>Latin: *Sambucus arborea*</note> **Double Guelder-rose / or Snowballs.**
2. Cytisus. <note>Latin: *Cytisus*</note> **Tree-clover.**
3. Arborescent Trefoil. <note>Latin: *Trifoliū arborescens*</note> **Round-leaved tree-clover.**

### Folio 11.

1. Columella's Cytisus. <note>Latin: *Cytisus Columellæ*</note> **Rough tree-clover.**
2. Clusius's Cytisus. <note>Latin: *Cytisus Clusij*</note> **Clusius's tree-clover.**
3. Sweeping Broom. <note>Latin: *Genista Scoparia*</note> **Yellow broom / or broom-shrub.**
4. Montpellier Polemonium. <note>Latin: *Polemonium Monspeliense*</note> **Woody meadow-clover.**

### Folio 12.

1. Bladder Senna. <note>Latin: *Colutea Vesicaria*</note> **Foreign lentil tree / Sheep-lentils / Wild senna tree.**

1. Guaiacana. <note>Latin: *Guaiacana*</note> **Supposed Nettle-tree.**

### Folio 29.

1. Asian Buttercup with gummy root and full blood-red proliferating flower. <note>Latin: *Ranunculus Asiaticus gumosâ radice flore pleno sanguineo prolifero*</note> **Asian buttercup / elongated root / with full and blood-red flowers / one growing through the other.**
2. Asian Buttercup with tuberous root and very red full flower. <note>Latin: *Ranunculus Asiaticus grumosâ radice flore pleno ruberrimo*</note> **Asian buttercup / with elongated knotty roots / and very red flowers.**
3. Cretan Buttercup with tuberous root and snow-white flower. <note>Latin: *Ranunculus Creticus grumosâ radice flore niueo*</note> **Asian buttercup / with elongated knotty roots and snow-white flowers.**
4. Asian Buttercup with tuberous root and very red crimson flower. <note>Latin: *Ranunculus Asiaticus grumosâ radice flore phœniceo ruberrimo*</note> **Asian buttercup with elongated knotty roots and red simple flowers.**
5. Asian Buttercup with tuberous root and yellow flower distinguished by red veins. <note>Latin: *Ranunculus Asiaticus grumosâ radice flore flauo rubris venis diistincto*</note> **Asian buttercup / with elongated knotty roots.**

<summary>This page provides a detailed botanical index for the "Spring Class" of plants, organized by folio number. It lists various species including lilacs, fruit blossoms, periwinkles, and an extensive variety of buttercups and cranesbills, reflecting early modern botanical classification.</summary>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical index from the previous section, listing plants by their folio (page) numbers. It focuses heavily on spring-blooming bulbs and perennials including Cranesbills, Anemones, Hyacinths, and Narcissi.</meta>

Roots and pale yellow flowers, which are distinguished here and there with red veins.

### Fol. 30.
1. *Geranium Sanguinarium.* <note>original: "Bluetrößlein / Bluetwurz / Schnabelkraut"</note> Little blood-rose, Bloodroot, or Cranesbill.
2. *Geranium Macrorhizon.* Mountain Cranesbill with elongated roots and purple-red flowers.

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical survey of ornamental and medicinal plants, following the previous page's focus on lilacs and boxwood. It introduces the Butcher's Broom (Ruscus) alongside further white varieties of the lilac (Syringa).</meta>

<gloss>II</gloss>
# Italian Lilac with double white flowers. <note>original: "Syringa Italica flore albo pleno." The term "pleno" refers to "double" flowers, which have more than the standard number of petals, creating a lush, ruffled appearance.</note>

<gloss>I</gloss>
# Butcher's Broom. <note>original: "Ruscus." A hardy evergreen shrub where the flowers and berries appear to grow directly out of the center of the leaf-like structures, which are actually flattened stems called cladodes.</note>

<gloss>III</gloss>
# Lilac with single white flowers. <note>original: "Syringa flore albo simplici." This identifies the common white lilac with its typical four-petaled "simple" flowers, contrasting with the "double" variety shown in Figure II.</note>

<summary>This botanical plate features detailed engravings of two lilac varieties and the butcher's broom, illustrating the differences between "double" and "single" flower structures. It reflects the 17th-century interest in documenting specific cultivars and unique plant anatomies.</summary>

<keywords>Lilac, Syringa, Butcher's Broom, Ruscus, Italian Lilac, botanical illustration, double flowers, 17th-century botany</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical catalogue, moving from the garden shrubs (Lilacs and Butcher's Broom) of the previous page to a diverse set of flowering specimens, including the ornamental Judas Tree and several smaller woodland and marsh plants.</meta>

<gloss>II</gloss>
->**II. May Lily**<-
<note>original: "Vnifolium," literally "One-leaf." This is likely *Maianthemum bifolium*, a delicate woodland perennial. Despite its Latin name suggesting a single leaf, it often bears two heart-shaped leaves below a spike of tiny, fragrant white flowers.</note>

<gloss>I</gloss>
->**I. Judas Tree**<-
<note>original: "Arbor Iudæ." A striking tree (*Cercis siliquastrum*) that produces clusters of pea-like flowers directly on its trunk and branches. Its name stems from the ancient tradition that Judas Iscariot hanged himself from this species.</note>

<gloss>IIII</gloss>
->**IV. Grass of Parnassus**<-
<note>original: "Hepatica Palustris," literally "Marsh Liverwort." Historically, several plants with rounded leaves were called "Hepatica," but this specimen is the Grass of Parnassus (*Parnassia palustris*), recognized by its bright white flowers with distinctive delicate veins.</note>

<gloss>III</gloss>
->**III. Moonwort**<-
<note>original: "Lunaria Botrytis." This is a type of primitive fern, likely the common moonwort (*Botrychium lunaria*). In folklore, it was often associated with alchemy and the moon, and was even rumored to have the power to loosen the shoes from horses' hooves.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates four distinct plant species: the flowering Judas Tree, the delicate May Lily, the marsh-dwelling Grass of Parnassus, and the mystical Moonwort fern. It showcases the 17th-century practice of grouping trees and smaller herbaceous plants together based on their visual and medicinal interest.</summary>

<keywords>Judas Tree, Arbor Iudæ, May Lily, Vnifolium, Grass of Parnassus, Hepatica Palustris, Moonwort, Lunaria Botrytis, botanical engraving, 17th-century botany</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical survey, shifting from the herbaceous woodland plants and flowering shrubs of the previous page to woody species. Specifically, it contrasts the highly bred ornamental "double" cherry with its wild, cluster-flowering relative, alongside the evergreen growth of a spruce tree.</meta>

->I.<-
->**Double-flowered Cherry**<-
<note>original: "Cerasus flore pleno." A "double" flower refers to a variety with extra petals, often appearing like a small rose. Because these extra petals usually replace the reproductive parts of the flower, these ornamental trees were grown for their beauty rather than for fruit.</note>

->III.<-
->**Spruce Tips**<-
<note>original: "Summitates Piceæ." This illustration shows the new growth at the ends of spruce branches, including young cones. In historical medicine, these resinous tips were often used in "spruce beer" or distilled to treat various ailments, particularly respiratory issues and scurvy.</note>

->II.<-
->**Wild Bird Cherry**<-
<note>original: "Cerasus racemoſa ſylveſtris." Literally the "wild clustered cherry." This is likely the *Prunus padus*, known for its distinctive long, drooping spikes (racemes) of small, fragrant white flowers, which differ from the typical cherry blossom clusters.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three woody specimens: the ornamental double-flowered cherry, the wild bird cherry with its long flower clusters, and the resinous tips of a spruce tree. It highlights the 17th-century interest in both decorative garden varieties and the practical, medicinal growth of evergreens.</summary>

<keywords>Double-flowered Cherry, Cerasus flore pleno, Spruce, Picea, Wild Bird Cherry, Cerasus racemosa sylvestris, botanical engraving, medicinal plants, ornamental trees</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical catalog, transitioning from the fruit trees and evergreens of the previous page back to a mix of ornamental blossoms and wild flowering herbs. It maintains the collection's interest in "double-flowered" varieties—plants bred for extra petals—alongside common wild species found in the European countryside.</meta>

->II.<-
->**Wild Sticky Catchfly with Flesh-Colored Flowers**<-
<note>original: "Lychnis viscosa sylvestris flore incarnato." Now known as *Silene viscaria*, this plant is easily identified by the sticky, dark patches on its stems that prevent crawling insects from reaching the nectar. The Latin *incarnato* describes the distinct "flesh-pink" color of the blossoms.</note>

->I.<-
->**Double-flowered Apple**<-
<note>original: "Poma flore multiplici." Like the cherry blossoms featured on the preceding page, this apple variety has been cultivated for its "double" flowers. In the 17th century, these were popular ornamental features in "pleasure gardens" because the dense layers of petals resembled small roses.</note>

->III.<-
->**Greater Wild Rampion**<-
<note>original: "Rapunculus Sylvestris maior." This is a tall species of bellflower. The name "Rampion" is derived from the Latin *rapum* (turnip), as its thick, white root was once a popular vegetable in salads, said to have a nutty flavor similar to a radish or walnut.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three distinct flowering plants: the ornamental double-flowered apple blossom, the insect-trapping sticky catchfly, and the greater wild rampion. It showcases the era's dual interest in decorative garden mutations and the practical uses of wild meadow plants.</summary>

<keywords>Double-flowered Apple, Poma flore multiplici, Sticky Catchfly, Lychnis viscosa, Wild Rampion, Rapunculus, botanical illustration, 17th-century gardening, edible roots</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the wild herbs and double-flowered blossoms of the previous page to three significant woody plants: the laurel, the pistachio, and the almond. The focus here is on their botanical structure and flowering habits, continuing the 17th-century interest in both Mediterranean imports and plants with classical significance. The monogram 'DK' likely refers to the engraver, Danckert Danckerts.</meta>

II.
*Laurel with flower.*
<note>original: "Laurus cum flore." Known today as the Bay Laurel (*Laurus nobilis*), this evergreen tree was sacred to the god Apollo in Greek and Roman antiquity. Its glossy, fragrant leaves were woven into crowns for victors and poets, a tradition that survives in the term "poet laureate."</note>

<column-break/>

->I.<-
->**Pistachio.**<-
<note>original: "Pistacia." The pistachio tree (*Pistacia vera*) is native to Central Asia and the Middle East. In the 17th century, it was a prized curiosity in European botanical gardens. The engraving highlights its distinct compound leaves, which consist of several leaflets paired along a central stem.</note>

<column-break/>

III.
*Almond with flower.*
<note>original: "Amigdala cum flore." Now scientifically classified as *Prunus dulcis*, the almond is famous for being among the first trees to blossom in late winter or early spring. Because it blooms so early, it was often used in art and literature as a symbol of wakefulness or the coming of new life.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three Mediterranean woody plants: the Bay Laurel, the Pistachio, and the Almond. The engraving focuses on their leaf structures and delicate blossoms, reflecting the era's fascination with plants that carried both ancient symbolic weight and culinary value.</summary>

<keywords>Laurel, Laurus nobilis, Pistachio, Pistacia vera, Almond, Amygdalus communis, botanical engraving, 17th-century botany, Mediterranean flora</keywords>

<meta>Following the previous page’s study of Mediterranean trees like the laurel and almond, this page focuses on two varieties of the genus Anagyris. Known commonly as the Bean Trefoil or Stinking Bean Trefoil, these specimens continue the volume's documentation of woody shrubs found in Southern Europe and the Near East. The detail in the seed pods (legumes) suggests a particular interest in the plant's reproductive cycle.</meta>

II.
### Broad-leaved Anagyris.
<note>original: "Anagyris latifolijs." This specimen, likely *Anagyris foetida*, is a deciduous shrub native to the Mediterranean. It is easily identified by its "trifoliate" leaves (leaves divided into three leaflets) which are notably wide in this variety. The name *Anagyris* is derived from Greek words meaning "backwards" and "circle," referring to the curved shape of its seed pods.</note>

<column-break/>

I.
### Narrow-leaved Anagyris.
<note>original: "Anagyris angustifoliis." This variety is characterized by its slender, lance-shaped leaflets. The engraving emphasizes the plant’s "racemes"—the long, pendulous clusters of pea-like flowers. While beautiful, the plant is famous in historical botany for the unpleasant, cabbage-like odor its leaves emit when bruised, hence the common name "Stinking Bean Trefoil."</note>

<summary>This page features two varieties of the Anagyris shrub, illustrating the botanical differences between broad-leaved and narrow-leaved specimens. The detailed engravings highlight the trifoliate leaf structure and the distinctive hanging seed pods and flower clusters characteristic of the legume family.</summary>

<keywords>Anagyris, Bean Trefoil, Anagyris foetida, botanical illustration, trifoliate leaves, legumes, Mediterranean flora, 17th-century botany</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page's focus on garden shrubs and cultivars like the Periwinkle and Bladdernut, this plate shifts toward woody plants common in European woodlands and hedgerows. It highlights the Fly Honeysuckle and the Barberry, illustrating the early modern interest in classifying wild flora according to the systems of influential botanists like Dodoens.</meta>

->II.<-
->Fly Honeysuckle with white flowers.<- <note>original: "Xÿlostium floribus albis." The name *Xylosteum* is derived from the Greek for "bone-wood," referring to the remarkably hard, white wood found inside these shrubs. This specimen likely represents the Upright Fly Honeysuckle (*Lonicera xylosteum*).</note>

---

->I.<-
->Another variety of Fly Honeysuckle, according to Dodoens.<- <note>original: "Xÿlosteon alterum Dodonei." Rembert Dodoens (1517–1585) was a celebrated Flemish physician and botanist. His work, the *Stirpium historiae pemptades sex*, was a primary reference for 17th-century naturalists, who often cited his specific categorizations of "alternate" or "second" varieties of species.</note>

---

->III.<-
->Barberry.<- <note>original: "Oxyacantha." While the Greek name literally translates to "sharp thorn" and was sometimes used to describe Hawthorn, the botanical details here—specifically the serrated leaves and the pendulous racemes of berries—point to the Common Barberry (*Berberis vulgaris*). In this era, Barberry was highly regarded for its acidic fruit used in preserves and its medicinal bark.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three woody shrub specimens: two varieties of Fly Honeysuckle (genus Lonicera) and the Common Barberry (Berberis vulgaris). The plate emphasizes the taxonomic influence of the botanist Rembert Dodoens in identifying specific plant variations.</summary>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>Continuing the sequence of ornamental and woody shrubs from the previous page, this plate moves from woodland species like Barberry to garden favorites and Mediterranean leguminous shrubs. It highlights the taxonomic influence of Charles de l'Écluse (Clusius), a pivotal figure in Renaissance botany who helped introduce many exotic species to European gardens.</meta>

->I.<-
->**Guelder Rose.**<- <note>original: "Sambucus arbor rosea." While the Latin name literally translates to "Rose Elder Tree," this plant is actually the sterile cultivar of the Water Elder, commonly known today as the Snowball Bush (*Viburnum opulus* 'Roseum'). It was highly prized in 17th-century gardens for its large, globular clusters of white flowers that resemble snowballs rather than the flat-topped clusters of the wild Elderberry.</note>

---

->II.<-
->**Tree Medick.**<- <note>original: "Trifolium arborescens." The name literally means "tree-like trefoil." This shrubby plant, likely the Moon Trefoil (*Medicago arborea*), possesses the three-lobed leaves characteristic of clover (*Trifolium*) but grows with a sturdy, woody stem, making it a botanical curiosity for early modern naturalists who were fascinated by "giant" versions of common herbs.</note>

---

->III.<-
->**Clusius’s Fifth Broom.**<- <note>original: "Cytisus V. Clusij." Charles de l'Écluse, or Carolus Clusius (1526–1609), was a Flemish doctor and pioneering botanist. The "V" denotes the fifth species of *Cytisus*—a genus of shrubs in the pea family—cataloged in his influential works. These plants are characterized by their slender branches, narrow leaves, and distinctive "papilionaceous" (butterfly-like) flowers and seed pods.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three distinct woody shrubs: the ornamental Snowball Bush (Viburnum opulus), the clover-like Tree Medick (Medicago arborea), and a species of Broom (Cytisus) as classified by the botanist Carolus Clusius.</summary>

<keywords>Guelder Rose, Viburnum opulus, Snowball Bush, Tree Medick, Medicago arborea, Cytisus, Carolus Clusius, botanical engraving, 17th-century garden plants, Renaissance botany</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page's focus on ornamental shrubs and the classifications of Carolus Clusius, this page further explores the diversity of the broom and pea families (Fabaceae). It connects Renaissance botanical discovery with classical agricultural knowledge, referencing the Roman writer Columella alongside local varieties from Montpellier.</meta>

->IV.<-
->**Montpellier Valerian.**<- <note>original: "Polemonium Monspelliensium." Although modern readers associate "Polemonium" with the genus containing Jacob's Ladder, Renaissance botanists often used the name for different species. This "Montpellier" variety likely refers to the Red Valerian (*Centranthus ruber*), a common Mediterranean plant with clusters of small, five-petaled flowers.</note>

---

->II.<-
->**Clusius’s Fifth Broom.**<- <note>original: "Cytisus V. Clusij." This identifies the fifth species of shrubby pea-flowers described by the influential botanist Carolus Clusius. These plants are notable for their trifoliate leaves (sets of three) and long, hairy seed pods, which are characteristic of the broom family.</note>

<column-break/>

->III.<-
->**Common Broom.**<- <note>original: "Genista Scoparia." The specific name "Scoparia" is derived from the Latin *scopae*, meaning a broom or besom. Historically, the long, flexible green branches of this plant were bundled together to create floor-sweeping tools.</note>

---

->I.<-
->**Columella’s Cytisus.**<- <note>original: "Cytisus Columellæ." This plant is named in reference to Lucius Junius Moderatus Columella, a Roman author of the 1st century AD who wrote extensively on agriculture. He highly recommended this "Cytisus" (likely the Tree Medick, *Medicago arborea*) as a nutritious and drought-resistant fodder for goats and sheep.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates four woody shrubs, focusing on various species of Brooms and Valerians. The text highlights the transition from classical Roman agricultural knowledge to the systematic botanical classifications of the 16th century.</summary>

<keywords>Montpellier Valerian, Common Broom, Genista scoparia, Cytisus, Carolus Clusius, Columella, botanical illustration, Fabaceae</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>Continuing the exploration of the broom and pea families (Fabaceae) from the previous page, this plate moves from the general classifications of Clusius to specific varieties identified by the Renaissance botanist Bartolomeo Maranta and the German herbalist Tabernaemontanus. It places a particular emphasis on the distinctive shapes of the plants' seed pods as a primary means of identification.</meta>

### II.
## **Galen’s Horned Broom of Maranta.** <note>original: "Cytisus Galeni Marantæ cornutus." This plant was identified by the Italian physician Bartolomeo Maranta (1500–1571) as the "Cytisus" described in the ancient medical texts of Galen. The descriptive name "cornutus" (horned) refers to the plant’s curved, crescent-shaped seed pods, visible in the engraving on the left.</note>

### I.
## **Bladder Senna.** <note>original: "Colutea Vesicaria." A hardy shrub native to the Mediterranean. It is famous for its large, translucent, and papery seed pods that swell with air like a bladder (the Latin *vesica*). These pods are known to "pop" when squeezed, leading to its common name.</note>

### III.
## **Tabernaemontanus’s First Spiked Broom.** <note>original: "Cytisus primus Tabernæm: spicatus." This refers to the first variety of broom categorized by Jacobus Theodorus (1522–1590), who used the Latinized pen name Tabernaemontanus. The term "spicatus" (spiked) describes the way its small flowers are arranged in a dense, elongated cluster resembling a spike of grain.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three distinct shrubs of the pea family, highlighting the "horned" pods of Galen’s Broom and the uniquely inflated pods of the Bladder Senna. It demonstrates how Renaissance botanists attempted to categorize nature by combining ancient medical authority with physical characteristics like flower and seed shape.</summary>
<keywords>Broom (Cytisus), Bladder Senna (Colutea), Bartolomeo Maranta, Galen, Tabernaemontanus, seed pods, botanical engraving, Fabaceae</keywords>

->II.<-
->**Privet.** <note>original: "Ligustrum." This refers to the common privet (*Ligustrum vulgare*). Since the time of the Roman author Pliny the Elder, this hardy shrub has been prized for its ability to form dense, manageable hedges. The illustration captures its characteristic terminal panicles (flower clusters) and the dark, bitter berries that follow.</note><-

->I.<-
->**Date-plum.** <note>original: "Guaiacana." Today scientifically classified as *Diospyros lotus*, the date-plum is a species of persimmon native to southeast Europe and southwest Asia. The fruit, shown here with its prominent four-lobed base (the calyx), turns yellow or blue-black when ripe and has a sweet, date-like flavor, which earned it the name "lotus" among ancient Greek writers.</note><-

<summary>This page illustrates two varieties of fruiting shrubs: the common privet, frequently used in landscaping, and the date-plum, an ancient species of persimmon known for its sweet fruit. The engraving emphasizes the distinct leaf structures and fruiting bodies of each plant to aid in identification.</summary>
<keywords>Privet (Ligustrum), Date-plum (Guaiacana), Diospyros lotus, berries, botanical engraving, horticulture, Renaissance botany</keywords>

->V.<-
## **White-fruited currant.**
<note>original: "Ribes fructu albo." This refers to the white currant, a cultivar of the red currant (*Ribes rubrum*). These berries are often sweeter and less acidic than their red counterparts, making them a favorite for fresh eating in historic gardens.</note>

->IIII.<-
## **Large red-fruited currant.**
<note>original: "Ribes maior fructu rubro." This variety was likely selected by gardeners for its superior fruit size compared to the wild types, showing early efforts in horticultural improvement.</note>

---

->III.<-
## **Black-fruited currant.**
<note>original: "Ribes fructu nigro." Now known scientifically as *Ribes nigrum*, the black currant was valued not just for its fruit, but also for its medicinal properties and the distinct, pungent aroma of its leaves.</note>

->I.<-
## **Small red-fruited currant.**
<note>original: "Ribes minor fructu rubro." This specimen illustrates a variety with smaller berries, perhaps reflecting a wild or less developed cultivar of the red currant.</note>

->II.<-
## **Common red-fruited currant.**
<note>original: "Ribes vulgaris fructu rubro." This is the standard Red Currant (*Ribes rubrum*), a staple of European temperate gardens used for centuries in preserves, syrups, and traditional medicines.</note>

<summary>This page presents an illustrated survey of the genus Ribes, documenting five distinct varieties of currants categorized by their color (white, red, and black) and size. The engraving highlights the morphological similarities in their lobed leaves and hanging fruit clusters while distinguishing between the specific cultivars.</summary>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>While the previous page concluded a detailed study of the Ribes genus (currants and gooseberries), this page transitions to a collection of diverse early-spring woodland plants. These species, though from different botanical families, are grouped here perhaps due to their shared flowering time or their distinctive roots and leaves, which were of great interest to 17th-century herbalists.</meta>

->III.<-
### **Dog’s tooth violet with a red flower.**
<note>original: "Dens canis flore rubro." This is the *Erythronium dens-canis*. The name "dog's tooth" refers to the specific shape and white color of its bulbous root, which resembles a canine tooth. Despite the name, it is a member of the lily family rather than a true violet.</note>

->II.<-
### **Dog’s tooth violet with a white flower.**
<note>original: "Dens canis flore albo." A white-flowering variety of the dog's tooth violet. Early modern botanists often gave distinct entries to different color variants of the same species.</note>

->I.<-
### **Greater toothwort or scaly-root.**
<note>original: "Squammata seu dentaria maior." This describes *Lathraea squamaria*, a fascinating parasitic plant that lacks green leaves because it draws nutrients from the roots of trees like hazel or beech. The "scales" mentioned in the name are actually modified leaves that cover its underground stem.</note>

->V.<-
### **Wild primrose with a pale flower.**
<note>original: "Primula Veris sylvestris flore pallido." The common primrose (*Primula vulgaris*). The Latin name *Primula veris* translates to "the little first one of spring," marking it as one of the earliest bloomers in the European landscape.</note>

->IV.<-
### **Spotted lungwort.**
<note>original: "Pulmonaria maculosa." Now known as *Pulmonaria officinalis*. The "spotted" nature of its leaves led early herbalists to believe, under the "Doctrine of Signatures," that the plant was a remedy for lung diseases because the spots resembled the appearance of ulcerated lungs.</note>

<summary>This illustration features five distinct woodland plants of early spring, including the parasitic toothwort, the dog's tooth violet in two colors, the common primrose, and the medicinal spotted lungwort. The plate emphasizes the unique morphology of their roots and leaf patterns, which were essential for identification in early botanical science.</summary>
<keywords>dog's tooth violet, Erythronium dens-canis, toothwort, Lathraea squamaria, primrose, Primula vulgaris, lungwort, Pulmonaria officinalis, medicinal plants, 17th century botany</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the presentation of early spring-flowering plants. While the previous page featured the parasitic toothwort and the medicinal lungwort, page 16 focuses on alpine and woodland species: three color varieties of the prized Bear’s Ear primrose, followed by the medicinal birthwort and a decorative "double" variety of the wood anemone.</meta>

### II. Bear’s ear with a purple flower.
<note>original: "Auricula Ursi florᵉ purpureo." Now known scientifically as *Primula auricula*. The common name "Bear’s Ear" comes from the shape and texture of the leaves, which are thick, somewhat fuzzy, and shaped like the ear of a bear. The superscript "e" in "florᵉ" is a standard Latin abbreviation for "flore," meaning "with a flower."</note>

### I. Bear’s ear with a white flower.
<note>original: "Auricula Ursi flore albo." A white-flowering variety of the same alpine primrose. In the 17th century, these plants were highly sought after by "florists" (specialist flower gardeners) for their wide range of colors.</note>

### III. Bear’s ear with a yellow flower.
<note>original: "Auricula Ursi florᵉ luteo." The yellow variety. The wild version of this plant is typically yellow; the purple and white versions shown here were likely cultivated or rare color variants.</note>

### IIII. Birthwort with a white flower.
<note>original: "Aristolochia flore albo." The name *Aristolochia* comes from the Greek *aristos* (best) and *lochia* (childbirth). For centuries, the plant’s curved flowers were thought to resemble a human fetus in the womb, leading herbalists—following the "Doctrine of Signatures"—to use it as a medicine to assist in labor.</note>

### V. Wood anemone with a double white flower.
<note>original: "Ranunculus Sylvarum florᵉ albo & pleno." While *Ranunculus* is now the genus for buttercups, early botanists used the term more broadly. This plant is likely the wood anemone (*Anemone nemorosa*). The term *pleno* (full or double) indicates a flower with many more petals than the usual wild variety, a mutation often preserved in botanical gardens.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates five spring plants, focusing on three color variations of Auricula Ursi (Bear's Ear) and two woodland plants, Aristolochia (Birthwort) and a double-flowered Ranunculus.</summary>
<keywords>Auricula Ursi, Bear's Ear, Primula, Aristolochia, Birthwort, Ranunculus, Wood Anemone, botanical illustration, 17th-century gardening, medicinal plants</keywords>

It specifically highlights the contrast between the common "simple" wild forms and the "double" varieties—mutations with extra petals that were the pride of Renaissance botanical gardens.</meta>

->III.<-
->Watercress<-
->with a double flower.<-
<note>original: "Nasturtium aquaticum flore pleno." Watercress is a peppery, aquatic herb that has been used as a salad leaf and medicinal plant since antiquity. The "double" variety shown here (flore pleno) was a horticultural rarity where the reproductive parts of the flower were replaced by extra petals, creating a tiny, rose-like blossom.</note>

->I.<-
->Wood anemone<-
->with a double flower.<-
<note>original: "Ranunculus nemorosus flore pleno." Although the label uses *Ranunculus* (the genus for buttercups), this plant is what we now call the Wood Anemone (*Anemone nemorosa*). The double-flowered version was a highly prized "monstrosity" of nature, often collected from the wild and transplanted into the gardens of the wealthy.</note>

It maintains the theme of comparing "simple" wild forms with "double" varieties, a central interest for Renaissance gardeners like Basilius Besler.</meta>

->IIII.<-
->Sweet Violet with white flowers.<-
<note>original: "Viola martia flor: albo." The *Viola martia* (literally "Violet of Mars" or March Violet) is what we now call the Sweet Violet. While usually deep purple, this specimen illustrates the naturally occurring white-flowered variety.</note>

->V.<-
->Sweet Violet with reddish-purple flowers.<-
<note>original: "Viola martia flor: rubro purpurascente." This variant shows a blossom with a warmer, more wine-colored hue compared to the standard blue-violet type.</note>

->III.<-
->Dame’s Violet with purple flowers.<-
<note>original: "Viola matronalis flore purpureo." Known today as Dame’s Rocket or Sweet Rocket (*Hesperis matronalis*), this plant is actually a member of the mustard family, not a true violet. It was highly valued in the 1600s for its intoxicating fragrance, which becomes much stronger in the evening.</note>

->II.<-
->White Sweet Violet with double white flowers.<-
<note>original: "Viola martia alba flor: multiplici albo." This is a "double" variety, where the flower has been bred or selected for its extra layers of petals, giving it a tiny, pom-pom-like appearance. Such mutations were the pride of princely gardens.</note>

->I.<-

<note>original: "Viola martia purpurea multiplici flore." Similar to the specimen above, this is the double-flowered version of the common purple violet. Its density of petals made it a favorite for use in "tussie-mussies" or small fragrant bouquets.</note>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of early spring flora. Following the previous page's focus on violets, this plate introduces another classic of the spring garden: the primrose (Primula), alongside the vibrant leopard’s-bane and mountain avens. The selection continues to emphasize "double" or "full" floral varieties, which were symbols of prestige and horticultural skill in the early 17th century.</meta>

III.
->Double-flowered Cowslip.<-
<note>original: "Primula veris flore pleno." *Primula veris* is the common Cowslip. The term "flore pleno" indicates a "full" or "double" flower, a mutation where the plant produces extra layers of petals. These were highly sought after by collectors for their lush, rose-like appearance compared to the simple five-petaled wild variety.</note>

IV.
->Twin-flowered Cowslip.<-
<note>original: "Primula Veris gemino flore." This specimen is noted for its "twin" flowers. In the context of early modern botany, this often referred to a variety where the flower heads were exceptionally large or appeared in dense, paired clusters, distinct from the more sparse wild primrose.</note>

I.
->Leopard’s-bane.<-
<note>original: "Doronicum." This plant, still known by its Latin name *Doronicum*, produces bright, daisy-like yellow flowers. Its common name, "Leopard’s-bane," stems from an ancient (though likely incorrect) belief that its roots were toxic enough to kill large predators. It was a favorite in early gardens because it is one of the first large, sun-yellow flowers to bloom after winter.</note>

II.
->Mountain Avens.<-
<note>original: "Cariophyllata montana." Now generally classified within the genus *Geum*, this plant was historically called *Cariophyllata* because its dried roots have a distinct scent of cloves (the Greek word for clove being *karyophyllon*). The "mountain" designation refers to its hardiness and typical alpine habitat.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates popular spring-blooming perennials, emphasizing the "double" cowslip and the medicinal mountain avens. These plants represent the transition from wild woodland flora to the more curated specimens of a Renaissance botanical garden.</summary>
<keywords>Cowslip, Primrose, Leopard's-bane, Mountain Avens, double flowers, Renaissance botany, medicinal plants, Geum, Doronicum</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the ornamental garden varieties of the previous page to hardy woodland and meadow plants. While the previous plate featured the prized "double" primroses of the collector's garden, this selection focuses on plants often found in the wild or cultivated for their medicinal "consolidating" (healing) properties. The artist continues to provide a complete view of the specimens, including the intricate root systems that were vital for early modern pharmacists to identify.</meta>

III.
### *Wild Campion*
<note>original: "Lychnis Sylvestris." The name *Lychnis* comes from the Greek word for "lamp," likely referring to the bright colors of the flowers or the historical use of the leaves of some species as lamp wicks. *Sylvestris* identifies it as a woodland or wild variety. Today, this is commonly known as the Red Campion or Catchfly.</note>

II.
### *Pannonian Vetch*
<note>original: "Orobus Pannonicus." This member of the legume family is a type of bitter-vetch. The term "Pannonicus" refers to Pannonia, a province of the Roman Empire covering parts of modern-day Hungary, Austria, and the Balkans, indicating the plant's geographic origin or prevalence in that region during the 17th century.</note>

->I.<-
->### *Common Bugle*<-
<note>original: "Consolida media." In early botany, the name *Consolida* (from the Latin *consolidare*, "to make firm" or "to heal") was given to plants believed to have the power to knit wounds together. While "Consolida major" usually referred to Comfrey, "Consolida media" is the Common Bugle (*Ajuga reptans*). The illustration clearly shows the "stolons"—the trailing runners that allow the plant to spread across the forest floor.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three wild European perennials: the Wild Campion, the Pannonian Vetch, and the Common Bugle. These plants were valued both for their delicate flowers and, in the case of the Bugle, their traditional use in wound healing.</summary>
<keywords>Wild Campion, Pannonian Vetch, Common Bugle, Ajuga reptans, medicinal herbs, woodland flora, botanical engravings</keywords>

## II.
## Annual Greek Moonwort
<note>original: "Lunaria graeca annua." This plant is most commonly known today as **Honesty** or the **Money Plant**. The name *Lunaria* (from the Latin *luna*, meaning "moon") refers to the plant's distinctive seed pods. While labeled here as "annual," this species is typically a biennial, producing leaves in its first year and flowers and seeds in its second before dying.</note>

<column-break/>

## I.
## Perennial Greek Moonwort
<note>original: "Lunaria graeca perennis." Now known scientifically as *Lunaria rediviva*, this is the perennial cousin of the common Honesty plant. The artist has carefully distinguished it by its more elongated, elliptical <term>silicles</term> (the botanical term for these flat seed pods) and its more robust, branching root system, which allows the plant to return year after year.</note>

<summary>This page features a comparative botanical illustration of two varieties of the Moonwort or Honesty plant, emphasizing the differences in their seed pods, leaf shapes, and root systems. These plants were highly prized in 17th-century gardens for their translucent, moon-like pods, which were often dried for decoration.</summary>

<keywords>Honesty plant, Moonwort, Lunaria, botanical illustration, seed pods, silicles, perennial plants, biennial plants, garden history, 17th-century botany</keywords>

<note>The name **Geranium** comes from the Greek word *geranos*, meaning "crane." This refers to the long, beak-like shape of the plant's seed pods, which can be seen forming on the stems in these illustrations.</note>

## II. Blue Frog-leaved Crane's-bill
<note>original: "Geranium Batrachioides cærul." This plant is likely the **Meadow Crane's-bill** (*Geranium pratense*). The term *Batrachioides* (from the Greek *batrachos*, meaning "frog") was used by early botanists because the deeply lobed leaves resemble those of certain buttercups (Ranunculus), which often grow in damp areas frequented by frogs.</note>

<column-break/>

## I. Scentless Musky Crane's-bill
<note>original: "Geranium muscatum inodorum." This is a variety of the **Musk Stork's-bill** (*Erodium moschatum*). While the standard species is known for its strong musky scent, the artist has specifically identified this specimen as *inodorum*, or "without scent." Notice the delicate, feathery leaves and the thin, spreading root system described in the illustration.</note>

<column-break/>

## III. White-flowered Frog-leaved Crane's-bill
<note>original: "Geranium Batrachioides flore albo." This is a white-flowering variety of the plant shown in Figure II. In the 17th century, botanical catalogs often grouped plants by their physical form first, then by flower color, to help gardeners and herbalists identify specific cultivars.</note>

# 26.
Z 6

->II.<-
->**Globe-flowered Buttercup**<-
<note>original: "Ranunculus flore globoso." This variety is named for its distinctive spherical shape. Unlike the open-faced buttercups usually found in meadows, the petals here curve inward to create a "globe," a feature typical of the *Trollius* genus (Globe-flowers), which was historically classified alongside Ranunculus due to their similar leaves and bitter sap.</note>

---

->I.<-
->**White Buttercup with double flowers**<-
<note>original: "Ranunculus albus flo. repleno." The phrase *flo. repleno* (an abbreviation of *flore pleno*) literally means "with a full flower." This refers to a "double-flowered" variety where the plant has been bred or selected for its many layers of petals, giving it a rose-like appearance rather than the simple five-petaled shape of wild buttercups.</note>

---

->III.<-
->**Greater Bulbous Buttercup**<-
<note>original: "Ranunculus bulbosus maior." As the name suggests, this plant is defined by its "bulbus"—the swollen, onion-like base or corm visible at the bottom of the stem. This structure allows the plant to store nutrients and survive in various soil conditions. The "greater" (*maior*) designation indicates this is a larger-growing variety of the species.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three varieties of buttercups, demonstrating the 17th-century fascination with "double" flowers and the botanical classification of plants by their root structures and floral shapes.</summary>
<keywords>Ranunculus, buttercups, globe-flower, double flowers, bulbous roots, botanical illustration, 17th-century botany, floral morphology</keywords>

->II.<-
->**Double-flowered Garden Buttercup**<-
<note>original: "Ranunculus hortensis flore pleno." The term *hortensis* indicates this is a garden variety, while *flore pleno* (full flower) refers to a "double" bloom. This was a highly sought-after mutation where the plant's stamens develop into extra petals, creating the dense, rounded shape seen in the engraving.</note>

->I.<-
->**Illyrian Buttercup**<-
<note>original: "Ranunculus Illyricus." Named for its origin in Illyria (the ancient region along the Adriatic coast), this plant is notable for its silver-toned, lance-shaped leaves and its unique tuberous root system, which resembles a cluster of small claws.</note>

->III.<-
->**Snowdrop Anemone**<-
<note>original: "Anemone Sylvestris flore albo." Translated as the "white-flowered wood anemone," the name *sylvestris* tells us this plant thrives in woodland or forest settings. Although it is an anemone, early botanists grouped it here because it shares the same family characteristics as the buttercups, such as the deeply divided leaves and the structure of the central seed head.</note>

<note>original: "Ranunculus erectus minimus flore pleno luteo prolifer." This specimen is particularly interesting for being *prolifer* (proliferating), a botanical term for a rare condition where a second flower bud grows out from the center of the primary bloom. This, combined with its "double" petals (*flore pleno*), made it a prized curiosity for plant collectors.</note>

-> II. <-
-> **Double Deep Red Asian Buttercup** <-
<note>original: "Ranunculus Asiaticus grumosa radice flore pleno ruberrimo." The term *grumosa radice* (tuberous root) describes the characteristic cluster of small, finger-like tubers. The *Asiaticus* species, originating from the Eastern Mediterranean and Iran, were a sensation in 17th-century European gardens due to their intense, jewel-like colors.</note>

-> IIII. <-
-> **Scarlet Red Asian Buttercup** <-
<note>original: "Ranunculus Asiaticus grumosa radice flore Phoeniceo ruberrimo." The descriptor *Phoeniceo* refers to a deep, brilliant scarlet or crimson. Unlike the double variety above, this illustration focuses on a specimen with a specific color profile, likely a semi-double or single form that shows off the intensity of the "Phoenician" red.</note>

-> <insert>IV</insert>. <-
-> **Blood-Red Proliferating Double Asian Buttercup** <-
<note>original: "Ranunculus Asiaticus grumosa radice flore pleno Sanguineo prolifero." This variety is a botanical curiosity. The term *prolifero* (proliferating) describes a rare condition where the center of the flower continues to grow, producing a second, smaller bloom out of the heart of the first. Combined with its "blood-red" (*Sanguineo*) color, this would have been a prized specimen in a "Cabinet of Curiosities."</note>

-> III. <-
-> **Snow-White Cretan Buttercup** <-
<note>original: "Ranunculus Creticus grumosa radice flore niveo." This species hails from the island of Crete (*Creticus*). The term *niveo* literally means "snowy," indicating a pure, brilliant white petal color that distinguishes it from the surrounding red varieties.</note>

---

-> V. <-
-> **Yellow Asian Buttercup with Red Veins** <-
<note>original: "Ranunculus Asiaticus grumosa radice flore fla. rubris venis distinctis." The abbreviation *fla.* stands for *flavo* (yellow). This variety is prized for its "distinct red veins" (*rubris venis distinctis*), a feathered or striped effect that was highly fashionable among "florists"—the historical term for specialist flower breeders and hobbyists.</note>

This adaptation allows the plant to go dormant and survive the hot, dry summers of its native Mediterranean habitat.</note>

IIII.
### White False Narcissus with a yellow cup
<note>original: "Pseudo Narcissus albus calice luteo." In early modern botany, the term "Pseudo Narcissus" (False Narcissus) was used for what we now simply call the Daffodil, distinguishing it from the "True Narcissus" or Poet’s Narcissus. This specimen is described as having white outer petals and a yellow central "cup" or trumpet.</note>

### Grape Hyacinth with flesh-colored flowers
<note>original: "Hyacinthus Botryoides fl: incarnato." The name *botryoides* refers to the flower's resemblance to a "cluster of grapes." While usually blue, this variety is "incarnato"—literally "flesh-colored"—referring to a pale pink or reddish-white bloom.</note>

III.
### Entirely white False Narcissus
<note>original: "Pseudo Narcissus totus albus." This is an all-white variety of the trumpet daffodil. In the 1600s, pure white variations of common flowers were highly sought after for their perceived purity and rarity.</note>

---

I.
### Golden Hepatica with double blue flowers
<note>original: "Hepatica aurea flore pleno & coeruleo." The term "aurea" (golden) likely refers to a historical grouping rather than the petal color. "Flore pleno" (full flower) indicates a "double" flower, a mutation where the plant produces extra layers of petals, creating a denser, more ornate blossom than the wild variety.</note>

II.
### Golden Hepatica with white flowers
<note>original: "Hepatica Aurea flre albo." A white-flowered specimen of the Hepatica. The plant is commonly known as "Liverleaf" because its three-lobed leaves—clearly depicted at the base of the plant—were thought by early doctors to resemble the human liver.</note>

<summary>This page catalogs five spring flowers, illustrating specific color and structural variations of the Daffodil, Grape Hyacinth, and Hepatica. It highlights the 17th-century fascination with "double" blooms and rare color variations like the pinkish grape hyacinth.</summary>

III
**Spring Crocus**
*with violet flowers.*
<note>original: "Crocus Vernus flore violaceo." The Spring Crocus is a classic herald of the new season. The violet variety shown here was a staple of early modern European gardens, prized for its ability to bloom even while snow remained on the ground.</note>

II
**Spring Crocus**
*with white flowers.*
<note>original: "Crocus Vernus flore candido." This white variety offers a delicate contrast to the more common purple Crocus. The term "candido" implies a pure, shining white.</note>

I.
# White Squill.
<note>original: "Scilla Alba." This plant, often called the White Squill, is depicted here in its full botanical glory. The illustration emphasizes the large, thick bulb and the tall, sturdy flowering stalk (scape) terminating in a dense cluster of star-shaped flowers.</note>

V.
**Golden Hepatica**
*with red flowers.*
<note>original: "Hepatica Aurea flore rubro." The Hepatica, or "Liverleaf," was named for its three-lobed leaves, which historical herbalists thought resembled the human liver. While the name "Aurea" (Golden) is used here, it likely refers to a specific classification group in 17th-century botany rather than the petal color, which is clearly described as red.</note>

IV.
**Golden Hepatica**
*with blue flowers.*
<note>original: "Hepatica Aurea flore coeruleo." A blue-flowered variety of the liverleaf. These small perennials were among the first to bloom on the forest floor, and their arrival was a significant event for early botanists and gardeners.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates several prominent spring-blooming plants, focusing on color variations of the Spring Crocus and Golden Hepatica, centered around a large architectural drawing of a White Squill.</summary>

->II.<-
->**Greater Oriental Hyacinth**<-
->*with violet color.*<-
<note>original: "Hyacinthus Orientalis maior violaceo colore." The Oriental Hyacinth was introduced to Europe from the Ottoman Empire in the 16th century. By the time of this manuscript, it had become a symbol of horticultural status. The "violet" variety shown here features a slightly more delicate flower spike compared to its blue counterpart.</note>

<column-break/>

->I.<-
->**Greater Oriental Hyacinth**<-
->*with blue flowers.*<-
<note>original: "Hyacinthus Orientalis maior flo: coeruleo." The abbreviation "flo:" refers to "flore" (with flower). This illustration highlights the <term>raceme</term>—the thick central stalk crowded with bell-shaped blossoms—which is the defining characteristic of this species. The deep blue color was one of the most popular and sought-after varieties in early modern gardens.</note>

<summary>This page features detailed botanical engravings of two varieties of the Oriental Hyacinth, distinguishing between the violet-colored and the blue-flowered specimens. <meta>This page continues the catalog of hyacinth varieties from the previous pages. While the prior entries focused on solid violet and blue Oriental Hyacinths, this page introduces more nuanced color variations—including white and "ash" tones—and includes a smaller, starry-flowered spring species.</meta>

->I.<-
->**Oriental Hyacinth**<-
->**with purple-white flowers.**<-
<note>original: "Hyacinthus Orientalis flore purpureo albus." This variety likely refers to a flower with a pale or white base and distinct purple veins or edges, a common variation in early modern Dutch and Ottoman breeding.</note>

->II.<-
->***Oriental Hyacinth***<-
->***with snow-white flowers.***<-
<note>original: "Hyacinthus Orientalis flore niveo." The term "niveo" is derived from the Latin *nix* (snow), signifying a brilliant, pure white blossom. Pure white varieties were highly prized for their contrast against the more common blue and purple garden hues.</note>

->III.<-
->***Oriental Hyacinth***<-
->***with ash-white flowers.***<-
<note>original: "Hyacinthus Orientalis flore cinereo albus." The word "cinereo" refers to the color of ashes or a smoky grey. In botanical terms, this suggests a muted, silvery-white or off-white bloom, demonstrating the seventeenth-century obsession with cataloging every subtle gradient of color.</note>

->IV.<-
->***Small spring***<-
->***star-hyacinth.***<-
<note>original: "Hyacinthus parvus Stellatus vernus." Unlike the tall, dense <term>racemes</term>—the central flower stalks—of the Oriental varieties above, this plant is much smaller. The term "stellatus" (star-like) refers to its open, pointed petals. In modern taxonomy, this is likely a species of *Scilla* or *Chionodoxa*, often called "Glory-of-the-snow" or "Spring Squill."</note>

<summary>This page illustrates four distinct types of spring-flowering bulbs: three refined color variations of the Oriental Hyacinth (purple-white, snow-white, and ash-white) and one smaller species of starry-flowered hyacinth.</summary>

<keywords>Oriental Hyacinth, Hyacinthus Orientalis, snow-white flowers, ash-white flowers, Starry Hyacinth, Scilla, botanical engraving, seventeenth-century gardening, floral varieties</keywords>

->III.<-
->**Mixed Oriental Hyacinth**<-
<note>original: "Hyacinthus Orientalis mixtus." In seventeenth-century floriculture, "mixtus" (mixed) often referred to flowers whose petals displayed a blend of two or more colors or a "double" appearance, which was highly prized by garden enthusiasts for its complexity.</note>

->I.<-
->**Small Star-of-Bethlehem**<-
<note>original: "Ornithogalum minus." The name <term>Ornithogalum</term> is derived from the Greek words for "bird" and "milk," possibly referring to the milky-white color of the petals. Though pictured here with hyacinths, it belongs to a different genus, characterized by its star-shaped flowers and slender leaves.</note>

->II.<-
->**Variegated Oriental Hyacinth**<-
<note>original: "Hyacinthus Orientalis variegatus." A variegated flower features streaks, spots, or stripes of a different color on its petals. During the "tulip mania" era and the decades following, such mutations were often the result of viruses, but they were intensely sought after for their unique, painterly beauty.</note>

<meta>This page continues the botanical catalog of spring bulbs. -> I. <-
-> **Oriental Hyacinth** <-
-> *of a silvery color.* <-
<note>original: "Hyacinthus Orientalis argenteo colore." The "silver" designation likely refers to a very pale blue or shimmering white bloom. In the seventeenth century, such subtle, metallic lusters were highly prized by collectors for how they caught the light in a garden setting.</note>

-> II. <-
-> **Oriental Hyacinth.** <-
<note>original: "Hyacinthus Orientalis." This species was the ancestor of the modern garden hyacinth. Originally from the Eastern Mediterranean and Turkey, it became a sensation in European gardens for its intense fragrance and variety of colors.</note>

-> III. <-
-> **Wild Bulb, or** <-
-> **Yellow Star-of-Bethlehem.** <-
<note>original: "Bulbus sylvestris seu Ornithogalum luteum." The genus name <term>Ornithogalum</term> comes from the Greek for "bird’s milk," a phrase used in antiquity to describe something rare and beautiful. Unlike the common white varieties, this "yellow" star-of-Bethlehem (now often known as *Gagea lutea*) is a hardy woodland plant that signals the very beginning of the spring season.</note>

-> V. <-
-> **Early Tulip.** <-
<note>original: "Tulipa præcox." The term <term>præcox</term> means "precocious" or "ripening early." These tulips were celebrated for being the first of their kind to break through the winter soil, often blooming weeks before the more flamboyant "late" tulips.</note>

-> IV. <-
-> **Early Tulip.** <-
<note>The illustrator provides two examples of the early tulip to show variations in growth or perhaps different color patterns, though here they are captured in their elegant, closed-bud stage to highlight the broad, undulating leaves.</note>

->II.<-
->*Late-blooming Hyacinth*<-
->*with an Oriental appearance.*<-
<note>original: "Hyacinthus Serotinus facie orientali." The term <term>serotinus</term> refers to plants that bloom later in the season than their relatives. This variety was noted for having the physical characteristics (the "face") of the prized Oriental Hyacinth despite its later flowering time.</note>

->*English Hyacinth*<-
->*of an amethyst color.*<-
<note>original: "Hyacinthus Anglicus colore amethystino." What the seventeenth-century botanist called the "English Hyacinth" is what we now recognize as the common Bluebell (*Hyacinthoides non-scripta*). The "amethyst" description refers to a specific shade of purplish-blue.</note>

->I.<-
->**English Hyacinth**<-
<note>original: "Hyacinthus Anglicus." This central figure represents the standard form of the species. Native to Western Europe, it was a staple of woodland gardens, cherished for its nodding, bell-shaped flowers.</note>

->III.<-
->*English Hyacinth*<-
->*with a flesh-colored flower.*<-
<note>original: "Hyacinthus Anglicus flore incarnato." The term <term>incarnato</term> (literally "in the flesh") was used by early botanists to describe shades of soft pink or pale rose, which were prized color mutations of the usually blue wild flower.</note>

->IIII.<-
->*English Hyacinth*<-
->*of a violet color.*<-
<note>original: "Hyacinthus Anglicus colore violaceo." This variety distinguishes a deeper, more saturated purple-blue than the "amethyst" or standard varieties shown elsewhere on the page.</note>

<column-break/>

->**Starry Hyacinth of Peru**<-
<note>original: "Hyacinthus stellatus peruanus." Despite its name, this plant (known today as *Scilla peruviana* or the Portuguese Squill) is actually native to the Mediterranean. It received its "Peruvian" name by mistake because the first specimens brought to England arrived on a ship named *The Peru*.</note>

->**Hand of Christ**<-
->**with purple flowers**<-
<note>original: "Palma Christi flor[ibus] purpur[eis]." The name <term>Palma Christi</term> (Palm of Christ) was given to certain orchids because their underground tubers are shaped like a hand with spreading fingers. Today, these are generally classified in the genus *Dactylorhiza*.</note>

->**Lesser Orchid**<-
->**with flesh-colored flowers**<-
<note>original: "Orchis minor flor[e] incarnato." The term <term>incarnato</term> (literally "flesh-colored") was the standard botanical description for a soft, pale pink or rosy hue, similar to the complexion of light skin.</note>

->*Hyacinth.*<-

->*Hyacinth.*<-

This variety was noted for being brought to Europe from the regions around Constantinople (Byzantium).</note>

->**Oriental Hyacinth**<-
->**violet-colored with white lines**<-
<note>original: "Hyacinthus Orientalis violaceo colore albis lineis." This unnumbered central figure highlights a striped variety. In the 17th century, such "broken" or variegated colors were often the result of natural mutations or viruses and were extremely popular among collectors.</note>

->**II. Oriental Hyacinth with**<-
->**double flowers.**<-
<note>original: "Hyacinthus Orientalis flo. [flore] reduplici." The term <term>reduplici</term> (multiplied) describes a "double" flower, where the plant produces extra layers of petals. These lush, dense blooms were considered a peak of horticultural achievement during this period.</note>

---

->**III. Oriental Hyacinth with reflexed**<-
->**petals, violet in color.**<-
<note>original: "Hyacinthus Orientalis reflexis folijs, colore violaceo." While "folijs" usually means leaves, in early botanical Latin it was frequently used to refer to the "leaves of the flower" (petals). <term>Reflexis</term> indicates that the petals curve or bend sharply backwards, giving the flower a distinct star-like appearance.</note>

---

->**IIII. White spring crocus**<-
->**with a violet base.**<-
<note>original: "Crocus vernus albus fundo violaceo." The <term>Crocus vernus</term> is the ancestor of many of our modern large-flowering Dutch crocuses. This specific variety is noted for its "violet base," a color contrast at the throat of the flower that made it a favorite for botanical illustrators.</note>

These engravings capture the 17th-century fascination with structural mutations and intricate color patterns in spring-blooming bulbs.</summary>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page's focus on Oriental Hyacinths and early spring Crocuses, this plate introduces a distinct variety of hyacinth that blooms later in the season, flanked by two variations of the "Flame Violet," known today as the pansy.</meta>

->**II.**<-
->**Greater Flame Violet mixed with**<-
->**violet color.**<-
<note>original: "Viola flammea maior violaceo colore mixta." The <term>Viola flammea</term> (literally "flaming violet") refers to the pansy or heartsease. Early botanists used "flaming" to describe the vivid, streaked color patterns on the petals. This variety is "greater" (larger) and features deep violet hues blended with other shades.</note>

->**Greatest late-blooming hyacinth.**<-
<note>original: "Hyacinthus serotinus maximus." The term <term>serotinus</term> means "late-ripening" or "late-blooming," distinguishing this plant from the spring-flowering varieties seen previously. While classified here as a hyacinth due to its bulbous root, this plant is known to modern science as *Dipcadi serotinum*, sometimes called the Brown Bluebell or Spanish Hyacinth.</note>

->**III.**<-
->**Greater Flame Violet mixed with a**<-
->**pale color.**<-
<note>original: "Viola flammea maior pallido colore mixta." Similar to the specimen on the left, this pansy variety displays the "flaming" petal pattern, but here the secondary color is described as "pale," likely a cream or light yellow, providing a soft contrast to the darker markings.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates two color variations of the "Flame Violet" (pansy) and a central specimen of a "late-blooming hyacinth." It highlights the 17th-century fascination with color mutations and the timing of floral blooms throughout the growing season.</summary>

<keywords>Flame Violet, Pansy, Late-blooming hyacinth, Dipcadi serotinum, Viola flammea, botanical illustration, bulbous plants, horticulture</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page's focus on variations of pansies and late-blooming hyacinths, this plate shifts toward wild hyacinth varieties, a spotted orchid, and a wetland species, maintaining the theme of diverse bulbous and tuberous plants.</meta>

->**II.**<-
->**Ash-colored English Hyacinth.**<-
<note>original: "Hyacinthus Anglic, Cinericeus." The term <term>Anglic</term> (English) identifies this as a relative of the common Bluebell (*Hyacinthoides non-scripta*). <term>Cinericeus</term> means "ash-colored," indicating a specimen with pale, greyish-blue flowers rather than the typical deep violet.</note>

->**III**<-
->**Spotted Hand of Christ.**<-
<note>original: "Palma Christi maculata." The name <term>Palma Christi</term> (Hand of Christ) was a common historical name for certain orchids, specifically those in the genus *Dactylorhiza* (Marsh Orchids). It refers to the plant’s underground tuber, which is shaped like a hand with spreading fingers. <term>Maculata</term> refers to the distinctive dark spots on the leaves shown in the illustration.</note>

->**I.**<-
->**False Tassel Hyacinth.**<-
<note>original: "Hyacinthus Comosus spurius." Today known as *Leopoldia comosa*, the Tassel Hyacinth is famous for the "tassel" of violet, sterile flowers at the very top of the stalk. In early botany, the term <term>spurius</term> (false or bastard) was often used to denote a variety or a plant that appeared similar to a known species but possessed slightly different characteristics.</note>

->**IIII.**<-
->**Water Little-Dragon.**<-
<note>original: "Dracunculus Aquatilis." This is the *Calla palustris*, commonly known as the Water Dragon or Marsh Calla. The name <term>Dracunculus</term> (little dragon) was applied to several plants with snake-like speckled stems or unusual root shapes. <term>Aquatilis</term> confirms its habitat in marshes and stagnant water.</note>
