### II
->**The smallest all-yellow mountain daffodil.**<-
<note>original: "Narcissus totus luteus montanus, minimus." This refers to a dwarf wild daffodil variety. In early modern botany, <term>montanus</term> (mountain) often designated plants perceived as hardier or originating from alpine regions.</note>

### III
->**Blue Grape Hyacinth.**<-
<note>original: "Hyacinthus Botryoides cæruleus." The term <term>Botryoides</term> is derived from the Greek word for a "cluster of grapes," aptly describing the tightly packed, bell-shaped flowers. Modern gardeners know these as *Muscari*.</note>

### I.
->**The larger all-yellow mountain daffodil.**<-
<note>original: "Narcissus totus luteus montanus maior." This is the larger counterpart to the dwarf variety shown in figure II, likely representing the classic wild yellow daffodil (*Narcissus pseudonarcissus*).</note>

### III.
->**Double-flowered purple Spring Meadow Saffron.**<-
<note>original: "Colchicum Vernum flore purpureo plenum." While most species of <term>Colchicum</term> bloom in the autumn (leading to the name "Autumn Crocus"), the <term>vernum</term> variety blooms in the spring. The term <term>plenum</term> (full) signifies a "double" flower, a mutation highly prized by 17th-century collectors for its dense layers of petals.</note>

### IV
->**White-flowered Grape Hyacinth.**<-
<note>original: "Hyacinthus Botryoides floribus albis." This is the white color variant of the blue grape hyacinth depicted above. <term>Albis</term> is Latin for white.</note>

In early modern botany, the color of this "middle" part was a primary feature for distinguishing species.</note>

->III<-

<note>original: "Narcissus Juncifolius luteus flor: pleno." The descriptor <term>juncifolius</term> means "rush-leaved," referring to the thin, rounded leaves that resemble marsh rushes rather than the flat blades of common daffodils. <term>Flor: pleno</term> (full flower) is the botanical term for a double bloom where the reproductive parts have transformed into extra petals.</note>

->I.<-

The term <term>flore multiplice</term> (multiple flower) is used synonymously with "double" to describe the dense, ruffled center created by the internal multiplication of petals.</note>

<note>original: "Narcissus maior Iuncifolius." As the primary subject of the illustration, this "great" or larger variety is shown with a cluster of six blossoms on a single stalk, highlighting its vigor and abundance compared to the smaller species.</note>

<meta>Continuity note: While the previous page focused on "rush-leaved" narcissi (jonquils) with thin, reed-like foliage, this plate returns to the broader-leaved varieties. ### IV. The small creeping yellow false Narcissus.
<note>original: "Pseudo Narcissus minor luteus repens." The term <term>Pseudo Narcissus</term> (False Narcissus) was traditionally used by early botanists like Clusius to categorize the wild trumpet daffodils, distinguishing them from the "True Narcissus" of the ancients. <term>Repens</term> suggests a low-growing or "creeping" habit compared to taller varieties.</note>

### The Northern Narcissus with a full yellow flower.
<note>original: "Narcissus Septentrionalis flo: pleno luteo." The descriptor <term>Septentrionalis</term> refers to Northern Europe, indicating these varieties were found or developed in regions like the Low Countries or Germany. The term <term>flo: pleno</term> (flore pleno) signifies a "double" flower, where the bloom is densely packed with petals.</note>

### III. The Northern Narcissus with a full yellow cup and notched edges.
<note>original: "Narcissus Septentrionalis calice pleno luteo, oris incisis." In this variety, the "doubling" is concentrated within the <term>calice</term> (the central cup or corona), while <term>oris incisis</term> describes the jagged or deeply cut appearance of the petal edges, giving the flower a ruffled look.</note>

### II. The Northern Narcissus with a full yellow cup and double petals.
<note>original: "Narcissus Septentrionalis calice luteo pleno, duplicatis folijs." While "folijs" usually means leaves, in early modern botanical descriptions of flowers, it frequently refers to the "petals" or perianth segments. <term>Duplicatis folijs</term> indicates that the outer petals are layered or doubled, creating a very thick, heavy blossom.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates several varieties of "double" yellow daffodils categorized as Northern and "False" Narcissi. These plants were celebrated for their structural complexity, specifically the way their central cups and petals were multiplied into dense, ruffled blooms.</summary>
<keywords>double-flowered, Northern Narcissus, Pseudo-narcissus, yellow daffodils, botanical engraving, floral anatomy, 17th-century horticulture</keywords>

->III.<-
->Early-blooming golden false Narcissus.<-

The term <term>Pseudo Narcissus</term> (False Narcissus) was the standard botanical classification for trumpet daffodils in the 17th century. <term>Præcox</term> refers to its "precocious" or early-blooming nature, appearing at the very start of spring.</note>

->I.<-

This variety is noted for its "ample" or wide opening, and a "pallid" color that would have been a soft primrose or cream compared to the deep gold of the others.</note>

->II.<-

<note>original: "Pseudo Narcissus simplex Belga." In this context, <term>simplex</term> (simple) means a single-layered flower, lacking the denseness of the "double" varieties seen on the preceding page. <term>Belga</term> indicates it was associated with the Low Countries, which were the heart of European bulb cultivation during this era.</note>

->II.<-
->Double-flowered Fritillary.<-
<note>original: "Fritillaria flor: pleno." The term <term>flore pleno</term> (full flower) indicates a "double" variety where the plant produces extra layers of petals instead of the standard single row of six. In the 17th century, such mutations were highly sought after by collectors for their lush, crowded appearance.</note>

->I.<-
->Greatest Italian Guinea-hen flower.<-
<note>original: "Meleagris flos maximus Italicus." The name <term>Meleagris</term> refers to the Guinea fowl; early botanists believed the checkered pattern on the petals resembled the bird's spotted plumage. This "greatest Italian" variety refers to a large-flowered specimen of the Snake's Head Fritillary, likely distinguished by its size and Mediterranean provenance.</note>

->III.<-
->White Fritillary.<-
<note>original: "Fritillaria Alba." Although the species is best known for its deep purple or "checkered" hues, white varieties occur naturally. In these specimens, the characteristic checkerboard pattern is often reduced to a subtle, translucent "watermark" texture visible only upon close inspection.</note>

The label <term>inodora</term> (scentless) was an important diagnostic feature, as some Fritillaries—such as the Crown Imperial—possess a very strong, musky odor.</note>

Engraved botanical plate illustrating five varieties of Narcissus (daffodils), showing the flowers, stems, bulbs, and fibrous root systems.
1. Top Center Left: A miniature variety with a drooping flower.
2. Top Center Right: A variety with very thin, rush-like leaves and a small upright flower.
3. Bottom Left: A large narcissus with broad, strap-like leaves and a single flower with a short corona.
4. Bottom Center: A narcissus with petals reflexed backwards and a long, cylindrical corona.
5. Bottom Right: A medium-sized narcissus similar in form to the large one on the left.
</image-desc>

->IV.<-
->Smallest golden<-
->wild-type<-

->Narcissus,<-
->The Rush-leaved<-
->variety.<-
<note>original: "IV. Pseudo Narcissus minimus aureus" and "Narcissus Juncifolius." The term <term>Pseudo-narcissus</term> (false narcissus) was used historically to categorize the wild trumpet daffodils. This "smallest golden" variety is a miniature wild-type. The <term>Juncifolius</term> (rush-leaved) variety is named for its leaves which resemble the thin, cylindrical stems of rushes.</note>

---

->Largest white Narcissus<-
->with a short yellow cup.<-
<note>original: "Narcissus albus maximus calice luteo brevi." This describes a specimen with white outer petals and a small, vibrant yellow <term>corona</term> (the central "cup"). The "maximus" designation indicates it was the largest of this color pattern known to the author.</note>

---

->III.<-
->Entirely yellow Narcissus with an oblong<-
->cup and reflexed petals.<-
<note>original: "Narcissus totus luteus oblongo calice & reflexis folijs." The term <term>reflexis folijs</term> (turned-back leaves/petals) describes the distinctive way the petals sweep backward away from the central tube. This is a characteristic of the <term>Cyclamineus</term> group of daffodils, prized for their elegant, wind-swept appearance.</note>

---

->II.<-
->Medium white Narcissus with a<-
->short yellow cup.<-
<note>original: "Narcissus albus medius calice luteo brevi." Similar in coloration to the "maximus" variety above, this "medius" (medium) version represents the natural variation in size within the species or a different cultivated variety of the same color scheme.</note>

->IV.<-
->**White tulip with streaks**<-
->**of reddish-purple**<-
<note>original: "Tulipa candida virgulis ex purpura rubescentibus." The term <term>candida</term> implies a brilliant, pure white, while <term>virgulis</term> refers to the flame-like streaks or "stripes" so prized by early modern gardeners.</note>

->II.<-
->**White tulip with a yellow cup,**<-
->**marked with reddish rays**<-
<note>original: "Tulipa alba circa calicem luteum, radis rubente." This variety is distinguished by its yellow <term>calicem</term> (the cup or base of the flower) and the "rays" of red extending upward through the white petals.</note>

->V.<-
->**Yellow tulip with a prominent**<-
->**red spot in the center**<-
<note>original: "Tulipa lutea, in medijs conspicua macula rubens." A simpler variety where the primary yellow (<term>lutea</term>) color is interrupted by a "conspicua" (conspicuous or eye-catching) red mark at the heart of the bloom.</note>

->III.<-
->**Yellow tulip sprinkled with**<-
->**glowing spots of cinnabar**<-
<note>original: "Tulipa lutea irrorata ex cinnabari rutilis maculis." The word <term>irrorata</term> literally means "bedewed" or "moistened," suggesting the spots are as fine as droplets. <term>Cinnabari</term> refers to cinnabar, a bright scarlet mineral pigment used by painters.</note>

->**Yellow tulip with long cinnabar-**<-
->**colored streaks along the sides**<-
<note>original: "Tulipa lutea a virgulis oblongis ad latera, colore cinnabaris." Unlike the "sprinkled" variety, this tulip features "oblongis" (elongated) streaks running down the edges of the petals.</note>

<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the catalog of tulip varieties from the previous page, shifting focus from "streaked" and "spotted" types to those characterized by distinct colored margins and variegated patterns. The transition from pure colors to "breaking" (variegation) was a central interest for 17th-century botanists and collectors.</meta>

->**Yellow tulip variegated with red, green, and scarlet**<-
<note>original: "Tulipa lutea ex rubro viridi coccineo variegata." This variety displays a complex mix of colors. The term <term>variegata</term> was highly significant to early modern gardeners, as it often described "broken" tulips—flowers whose petals developed spectacular, unpredictable patterns due to a virus, making them rare and expensive.</note>

->III.<-
->**White tulip turning red at the edges**<-
<note>original: "Tulipa ex albo marginibus rubescens." The word <term>rubescens</term> suggests a soft transition, where the white petal appears to be "blushing" or gradually becoming red toward its outer limits.</note>

->IV.<-
->**Whitish tulip with a scarlet edge**<-
<note>original: "Tulipa albicans margine coccineo." This variety features a sharp contrast between the <term>albicans</term> (whitish or creamy) body of the petal and a vibrant <term>coccineo</term> (scarlet, derived from the color of kermes dye) border.</note>

->II.<-
->**Lush pale tulip with scarlet borders**<-
<note>original: "Tulipa floridè pallens oris coccineis." Here, <term>floridè</term> indicates a bright or flourishing appearance. The <term>oris</term> (literally "mouths" or "rims") of the petals are tipped with the same deep red found in the previous examples.</note>

->I.<-
->**Yellow tulip turning red**<-
<note>original: "Tulipa ex flavo rubescens." A simpler variety where the base color is <term>flavo</term> (a golden or yellow hue) that transitions into red as the flower matures or reaches the petal edges.</note>

<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the systematic documentation of tulip cultivars from page 72. While the previous page focused on silver and tawny-yellow varieties with cinnabar markings, this plate introduces purple-hued specimens and those with "flame-shaped" patterns, a characteristic of the "broken" tulips that fueled the 17th-century Dutch tulip craze.</meta>

->**Purple tulip with a pale cup**<-
<note>original: "Tulipa purpurea calice pallescente." The word <term>calice</term> (calyx or cup) refers to the base of the flower where the petals meet the stem. A pale base on a dark purple tulip was a notable aesthetic contrast.</note>

### IV.
->**Tulip, entirely greenish from a pale base**<-
<note>original: "Tulipa ex pallido tota virescens." <term>Virescens</term> indicates a plant that turns green or retains a green hue; these varieties were valued for their unusual, understated appearance compared to brighter flowers.</note>

### III.
->**Tulip of a yellow or sulfur color, with fading streaks and rose-colored, flame-shaped markings near the tips**<-
<note>original: "Tulipa flavo seu sulphureo colore pallescentibus lituris prope apices roseis flammiformib." The term <term>flammiformib</term> (flame-shaped) describes the feathering or "flaming" of color that occurs when a tulip "breaks," a phenomenon now known to be caused by a virus.</note>

### II.
->**Tulip with reflexed flowers, cinnabar-red underneath and grassy-green on the outside with a cinnabar-red border**<-
<note>original: "Tulipa florib: reflexis inferi, miniatis, exterius herbaceis margine ex cinnabari rubro." <term>Reflexis</term> refers to petals that curve backward. <term>Miniatis</term> and <term>cinnabari</term> both refer to bright red pigments derived from lead or mercury, often used by artists to describe a vivid, slightly orange-red.</note>

->**Tulip of a single color with reddish-purple edges**<-
<note>original: "Tulipa unici coloris, oris rubeo purpurascentibus." Though labeled as a "single color," the author notes the <term>oris</term> (borders or edges) have a distinct purplish-red tint.</note>

->**Tulip of a deep cinnabar red with a yellow cup**<-

### III
->**White tulip with rose-colored spots**<-
<note>original: "Tulipa alba maculis roseis." Unlike the "flamed" patterns seen elsewhere, these <term>maculis</term> (spots) represent a different visual manifestation of tulip breaking virus, which was highly prized by 17th-century collectors.</note>

### I
->**Yellow tulip dotted with cinnabar red**<-
<note>original: "Tulipa lutea miniate punctata." The descriptor <term>punctata</term> suggests the petals are finely speckled or stippled with red, rather than striped.</note>

### IV
->**Rose-colored tulip with yellow veins**<-
<note>original: "Tulipa rosea, venis luteis." This variety features a delicate vascular pattern where the yellow coloration follows the "veins" of the petals.</note>

### II
->**White tulip with yellow claws**<-
<note>original: "Tulipa alba, unguibus luteis." In botanical terms, the <term>unguibus</term> (claws) are the narrow, bottom-most parts of the petals where they attach to the center of the flower. Having a contrasting color at the "claw" was a specific trait used to categorize different tulip species.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates five distinct tulip varieties, detailing specific color contrasts between the petals and the "claws" or "cups" of the flowers. It highlights the 17th-century fascination with "broken" color patterns including spots, veins, and stippling.</summary>

<keywords>Tulipa, cinnabar, vermilion, botanical illustration, tulip breaking virus, petal markings, 17th-century horticulture</keywords>

While the previous page focused on single-petaled varieties with intricate vascular "veins" and "spots," this plate introduces "double-flowered" (multi-petaled) cultivars and highlights high-contrast color margins.</meta>

->**II.**<-
->**Snow-white tulip with purplish edges**<-
<note>original: "Tulipa Nivea oris purpurascente." The term <term>nivea</term> (snowy) implies a pure white base, while <term>oris purpurascente</term> describes a delicate purplish or crimson tint found specifically at the petal margins.</note>

---

**III.**
**Fully double tulip, entirely yellow**
<note>original: "Tulipa flore pleno tota lutea." A <term>flore pleno</term> (full flower) is a botanical term for a "double" blossom, where the flower has many more petals than the standard six, often giving it the appearance of a peony. This variety is noted as being entirely yellow (<term>tota lutea</term>).</note>

<column-break/>

**I.**
**Cinnabar-red tulip with a double flower**
<note>original: "Tulipa Miniata pleno flore." The descriptor <term>miniata</term> refers to the color of "minium" (red lead), a brilliant orange-red pigment used in manuscript illumination. This is another double-flowered variety, showing the diversity of "full" tulips available to 17th-century collectors.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three distinct tulip cultivars, emphasizing "double-flowered" varieties and the prized "snow-white" coloration. It reflects the horticultural transition from simple single blooms to the more complex, multi-petaled forms favored during the height of the tulip trade.</summary>

<keywords>botany, double tulip, Tulipa Nivea, Tulipa Miniata, Tulipa lutea, florilegium, 17th-century gardening</keywords>

<note>original: "Tulipa Persica non aperta." The <term>Tulipa Persica</term> (Persian tulip) refers to a species-type tulip, likely related to what we now know as *Tulipa clusiana*. In the 17th century, these were celebrated for their elegance and "wild" origin compared to the heavily bred Dutch varieties. "Non aperta" indicates the flower is shown in the bud stage, highlighting its slender, pointed profile.</note>

->**I.**<-
->**Early-blooming multi-flowered tulip**<-
->**mixed with a rosy color.**<-
<note>original: "Tulipa polyanthos præcox roseo colore mixta." A <term>polyanthos</term> (from the Greek for "many-flowered") tulip is a botanical rarity where a single stem produces multiple flower heads rather than one. <term>Præcox</term> identifies this as an "early" variety, blooming at the start of spring. The description of being "mixed with a rosy color" likely refers to pink or reddish-pink streaks or washes over a lighter base color.</note>

When open, these varieties typically display a star-like appearance with six distinct, pointed petals, differing significantly from the "cup" or "globe" shapes of common garden tulips.</note>

->**III.**<-
->**Long Victorialis.**<-
<note>original: "Victorialis longa." Now known as *Allium victorialis*, or the Victory Onion. In the 17th century, this plant was valued for its medicinal properties and as a protective charm for miners and soldiers—a tradition reflected in its name "Victorialis" (Victory). It is distinguished by its fibrous, net-like root casing and broad, ribbed leaves that resemble those of a lily of the valley more than a typical onion.</note>

<column-break/>

->**I.**<-
->**Late-blooming star-shaped tulip.**<-
<note>original: "Tulipa Serotina stellata." The term <term>Serotina</term> (late) categorizes this as a tulip that flowers in the final phase of the spring season. Unlike the "cup" or "goblet" shapes seen in many Dutch cultivars, the <term>stellata</term> (star-shaped) variety is characterized by narrow, pointed petals that splay outward. The illustration also captures the "undulating" or wavy margins of the leaves, a common trait in botanical species tulips.</note>

<column-break/>

->**II.**<-
->**Broad-leaved Moly with a yellow flower.**<-
<note>original: "Moly latifolium flore flavo." This is *Allium moly*, commonly known as Golden Garlic or Lily Leek. The name <term>Moly</term> has ancient roots, referring to the magical herb given by Hermes to Odysseus to protect him against Circe’s sorcery. In this era, the term was applied to various ornamental onions. This specific variety is prized for its bright, star-like yellow blossoms and its relatively wide leaves (*latifolium*).</note>

## Crown Imperial
## Multi-flowered.

<note>original: "Corona Imperialis Polyanthos." The Crown Imperial (*Fritillaria imperialis*) was a plant of immense prestige in 17th-century European gardens, having been introduced from the Persian region of the Ottoman Empire. The term <term>Polyanthos</term>—derived from the Greek words *poly* (many) and *anthos* (flower)—indicates that this specific variety produces a dense, globular head of numerous blossoms, rather than the single ring of flowers found on more common specimens.</note>

<summary>This page features a botanical illustration of the "Polyanthos" variety of the Crown Imperial, showcasing its distinctive cluster of bell-shaped flowers and its namesake "crown" of leafy bracts. It represents the height of botanical luxury and cultivation in the early modern era.</summary>

->The Crown Imperial.<-

## Crown Imperial
## Multi-flowered.

<note>original: "Corona Imperialis Polyanthos." The Crown Imperial (*Fritillaria imperialis*) was a plant of immense prestige in 17th-century European gardens, having been introduced to Europe from the Persian and Turkish regions of the Ottoman Empire in the late 1500s. The term <term>Polyanthos</term>—derived from the Greek words *poly* (many) and *anthos* (flower)—indicates that this specific variety produces a dense, globular head of numerous blossoms, rather than the single ring of flowers found on more common specimens. The illustration meticulously depicts the "nectaries"—the white, bead-like drops of nectar at the base of each bell-shaped flower—which were often poetically described as "tears." To the left, the artist provides a detailed study of the plant's large, distinctive bulb and fibrous roots, emphasizing the subterranean structure required to support such a heavy, majestic stem.</note>

<summary>This page features a botanical illustration of the "Polyanthos" variety of the Crown Imperial, showcasing its distinctive cluster of bell-shaped flowers and its namesake "crown" of leafy bracts. It represents the height of botanical luxury and cultivation in the early modern era.</summary>

<keywords>Crown Imperial, Fritillaria imperialis, many-flowered lily, botanical engraving, 17th-century gardening, exotic flora, bulbs</keywords>

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>Continuity note: Following the previous depiction of the "Polyanthos" or many-flowered Crown Imperial, page 82 presents a different architectural variation of the same species. While the previous page focused on the density of the blooms, this illustration highlights a "double tier" structure, a growth pattern where the flowers form two distinct rings stacked vertically on the central stalk.</meta>

<margin>Engraved by F. Cossin</margin>

->The Crown Imperial of flowers<-
->with a double tier.<-

<note>original: "Corona Imperialis florum claſſe duplici." This magnificent engraving depicts a specific variety of the Crown Imperial (*Fritillaria imperialis*) characterized by its "double tier" (Latin: *classis duplex*). In this variety, the pendulous, orange-red blossoms are arranged in two separate whorls, one above the other, rather than the single ring typically seen in wild specimens. This rare form was a prized centerpiece in the gardens of the wealthy and the curious during the 1600s. 

The artist has included two important botanical details alongside the main plant: to the left is the <term>seed capsule</term>, shown after the flowers have dropped and the fruit has turned upward to face the sky; to the right is an interior view of a single flower, revealing the six <term>tepals</term> and the prominent stamens and pistil. The signature at the base belongs to Louis Ferdinand Cossin, one of the skilled engravers tasked with bringing Bishop von Gemmingen’s garden to life on paper.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates a "double-tiered" variety of the Crown Imperial, showcasing a rare and prestigious growth habit where flowers bloom in two distinct levels. It includes detailed studies of the plant's seed pod and internal floral anatomy.</summary>

<keywords>Crown Imperial, Fritillaria imperialis, double-tier flowers, botanical engraving, seed capsule, 17th-century botany, Hortus Eystettensis, Ferdinand Cossin</keywords>

->III.<-
->**Smaller Dwarf Iris**<-
->**with narrow leaves.**<-
<note>original: "Chamæiris angustis folijs minor." The *Chamæiris* (from the Greek for "ground iris") refers to low-growing or dwarf iris species. This specific variety is noted for its slender, narrow foliage. In the 1600s, these were prized for their early spring color and their ability to thrive in the front borders of flower beds.</note>

->IV.<-
->**Bulb of the Persian Lily.**<-
<note>original: "Bulbus lilij Persici." Botanical illustrators of this era, such as those working on the *Hortus Eystettensis*, often included separate studies of bulbs and roots. This allowed scholars and gardeners to identify plants even during their dormant winter state. This large bulb shows the characteristic fleshy scales and the central cleft from which the tall flowering stalk emerges.</note>

->I.<-
->**Persian Lily.**<-
<note>original: "Lilium Persicum." Despite its historical name, this plant is actually a member of the fritillary family (*Fritillaria persica*). It is distinguished by its tall, sturdy stem—often reaching three feet in height—adorned with dozens of small, nodding, bell-shaped flowers. The blossoms are typically a deep, dusky purple or plum color, providing a somber elegance that contrasted with the bright tulips and lilies of the period.</note>

->II.<-
->**Tuberous Iris.**<-
<note>original: "Iris tuberosa." Now often classified as *Hermodactylus tuberosus*, this plant is famously known as the "Snake's-head Iris" or "Widow Iris." It is celebrated for its unusual, almost translucent green petals tipped with dark, velvety purple or black. The illustration specifically highlights the <term>tuberous</term> root system (roots that are thickened like a potato or ginger) which distinguishes it from the more common <term>rhizomatous</term> irises.</note>

<summary>This page presents a detailed botanical study of the Persian Lily and two iris varieties, showcasing the 17th-century fascination with exotic bulbous plants and their unique root structures. It provides a comprehensive view of the plants both above and below the soil line.</summary>

<keywords>Persian Lily, Fritillaria persica, Tuberous Iris, Widow Iris, Dwarf Iris, botanical bulb, 17th-century botany, Hortus Eystettensis, flower anatomy</keywords>

->II.<-

<note>original: "Lilium purpureum maius Dodonei." The name pays tribute to Rembert Dodoens, a 16th-century Flemish physician whose botanical writings were a primary source for gardeners of this era. This specimen is likely a variety of the Turk's-cap lily, known for its petals that curl elegantly backward. In early modern terminology, "purple" (*purpureum*) often referred to deep reds or crimson-oranges rather than the violet hue we associate with the word today.</note>

->I.<-

The drawing clearly depicts the overlapping fleshy scales of the lily bulb, which allowed gardeners to understand how to propagate and plant these prized specimens.</note>

->I.<-
->**Blood-red bulb-bearing Lily.**<-
<note>original: "Lilium cruentum bulbiferum." This is the Orange Lily (*Lilium bulbiferum*). The name "cruentum" (blood-red) refers to its fiery petals, while "bulbiferum" (bulb-bearing) identifies its most unique characteristic: the tiny, dark, bead-like bulbils nestled where the leaves meet the stem. These bulbils can fall to the ground and grow into entirely new plants, a fact that fascinated early modern botanists.</note>

->III<-
->**Yellow-flowered Centaury.**<-
<note>original: "Centaurium flore luteo." Known today as Yellow-wort (*Blackstonia perfoliata*), this plant was historically categorized with the centauries due to its bitter properties. In the Renaissance, such plants were highly valued in the "physic garden" for their use in treating fevers and digestive ailments.</note>

## I. Many-flowered Blood-red Lily
<note>original: "Lilium cruentum polyanthos." This magnificent specimen is a variety of the <term>Orange Lily (Lilium bulbiferum)</term>. The term <term>polyanthos</term> (from the Greek for "many flowers") describes how this specific plant produces a dense, crowded cluster of blossoms at the top of a single, exceptionally thick stem. In the 17th century, such "monstrous" or prolific blooms were highly prized by gardeners and collectors for their dramatic appearance.</note>

<column-break/>

->III.<-
->**Small white-flowered Centaury.**<-
<note>original: "Centaurium minus flore albo." This is a white-flowering version of the <term>Common Centaury</term>. While the red variety was more typical, botanical artists included white variants to help physicians and apothecaries identify all possible forms of the herb in the wild. This plant is a member of the Gentian family and, like its relatives, is known for its intense bitterness.</note>

<column-break/>

->II.<-
->**Small red-flowered Centaury.**<-
<note>original: "Centaurium minus flore rubro." Known today as *Centaurium erythraea*, this plant's name is derived from the centaur Chiron of Greek mythology, who was said to have used the herb to heal a wound in his foot. It was a staple of the "physic garden," used primarily as a digestive tonic and to reduce fevers, earning it the folk name "feverwort."</note>

<summary>This page features a spectacular "many-flowered" variety of the Orange Lily, characterized by its dense cluster of fiery blossoms, alongside two color variations of the medicinally important Common Centaury.</summary>

<keywords>Orange Lily, Lilium bulbiferum, Centaury, Centaurium erythraea, medicinal herbs, polyanthos, botanical variation, floriculture</keywords>

->II.<-
->**Byzantine Lily with many flowers**<-
<note>original: "Lilium Byzāntinum flore multiplici." This is likely a variety of the <term>Turk’s Cap Lily</term> (*Lilium chalcedonicum*). The name "Byzantine" refers to its origin in the Ottoman Empire, specifically the region around Istanbul. These lilies were highly prized for their deep red color and "reflexed" petals, which curl back tightly to resemble a turban.</note>

->I<-
->**White Lily**<-
<note>original: "Lilium album." This is the <term>Madonna Lily</term> (*Lilium candidum*). In the 17th century, it was both a symbol of purity in religious iconography and a staple of the "physic garden." Its petals and bulbs were used in oils and ointments to soothe skin irritations and heal wounds.</note>

->II.<-
->**Upright blue speedwell.**<-
<note>original: "Veronica recta cærulea." This refers to a tall, herbaceous perennial commonly known as <term>Speedwell</term>. In the 17th century, *Veronica* species were highly regarded for their medicinal virtues, particularly as a "vulnerary" (wound-healing) herb and a treatment for coughs and lung ailments.</note>

->I.<-
->**The Sultan Zambak,**<-
->**a white-flowered Martagon**<-
->**from Constantinople.**<-
<note>original: "Sultan Zambach Martagon Constantinopolitanum flore albo." The term "Zambak" (or Zambach) is the Turkish word for lily. This plant is a white-flowered variety of the <term>Martagon Lily</term> (Lilium martagon), often called the "Turk's Cap Lily" due to its petals curving backward like a turban. These plants were prized imports from the Ottoman Empire, brought to European gardens via Istanbul (Constantinople).</note>

<column-break/>

->III.<-
->**The bulb of the Sultan Zambak**<-
->**along with the lower part of the stem.**<-
<note>original: "Bulbus Sultan Zambach cum inferiori parte caulis." This anatomical illustration provides a clear view of the <term>scaly bulb</term> and the fibrous roots. Unlike the solid bulbs of tulips or onions, lily bulbs are made of individual fleshy scales, which collectors and gardeners needed to recognize for proper planting and propagation.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the "Sultan Zambak," a white Martagon lily imported from the Ottoman Empire, alongside a detailed anatomical study of its bulb and the "Upright Blue Speedwell." It highlights the 17th-century fascination with exotic Byzantine flora and the systematic study of plant root structures.</summary>

<keywords>Sultan Zambak, Martagon Lily, Speedwell, Veronica, Constantinople, botanical anatomy, lily bulb, Ottoman flora</keywords>

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>Continuity note: Following the previous page’s focus on the Sultan Zambak (Martagon Lily) and the medicinal Speedwell, this plate shifts focus to other bulbous and tuberous plants: the elegant Star-of-Bethlehem and the "Hand of Christ" orchid, notable for its unique root structure.</meta>

->IIII.<-
->**The Hand of Christ.**<-
<note>original: "Palma Christi." This name was commonly given to certain species of <term>orchids</term> (specifically the genus *Dactylorhiza*). The name derives from the shape of the plant's underground tubers, which resemble a human hand with fingers. In the 17th century, the "Doctrine of Signatures" suggested that the shape of a plant part indicated its medicinal use; because of their hand-like shape, these orchids were often associated with healing and protection.</note>

<column-break/>

->I.<-
->**The Spiked Star-of-Bethlehem**<-
->**with milk-white flowers.**<-
<note>original: "Ornithogalum spicatum flore lacteo." The genus name <term>Ornithogalum</term> translates from Greek as "bird's milk," a term used in the ancient world to describe something exceptionally beautiful or rare. This variety is known for its "spiked" arrangement, where many small, star-shaped flowers bloom along a single tall stalk.</note>

<column-break/>

->II.<-
->**The Pannonian Star-of-Bethlehem.**<-
<note>original: "Ornithogalum Pannonicum." This species is named after <term>Pannonia</term>, an ancient Roman province that covered parts of modern-day Hungary, Austria, and the Balkans. It was common for botanists of this era to identify plants by the geographic regions where they were most frequently found or first documented.</note>

<column-break/>

->III.<-
->**The Hand of Christ.**<-
<note>original: "Palma Christi." This second illustration of the orchid provides a detailed view of a different specimen, emphasizing the "palmate" or hand-shaped tuber. By showing multiple examples, the artist helps the gardener or botanist identify the species even when individual plants vary in size or leaf shape.</note>

<summary>This page features detailed botanical engravings of two Star-of-Bethlehem varieties and two "Hand of Christ" orchids. It highlights the 17th-century interest in regional European flora and the distinctive anatomical features of tuberous and bulbous plants.</summary>
<keywords>Star-of-Bethlehem, Ornithogalum, Palma Christi, Orchid, Pannonia, botanical illustration, tubers, bulbs</keywords>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the botanical survey from the previous page, moving from the orchids and Pannonian varieties of Star-of-Bethlehem to the exotic Arabian variety, the common Field Scabious, and the economically significant Dyer's Greenweed.</meta>

->II.<-
->**Small Blue**<-
->**Field Scabious**<-
<note>original: "Scabiosa Sylvestris minor cærulea." The name <term>Scabiosa</term> is derived from the Latin *scabies*, meaning "itch." In historical herbal medicine, these plants were widely used to treat skin ailments, including the sores caused by the plague. The term "Sylvestris" indicates that this variety was found growing wild in the woods or fields.</note>

->I.<-
->**The Arabian Star-of-Bethlehem**<-
<note>original: "Ornithogalum Arabicum." Building upon the different varieties of <term>Ornithogalum</term> seen on the previous page, this Arabian species is noted for its striking appearance. It features large, fragrant, creamy-white flowers with a prominent, shiny black center (the ovary), which distinguishes it from its European cousins.</note>

->III.<-
->**Dyer’s Greenweed**<-
<note>original: "Genistella Tinctorum." Now commonly known as *Genista tinctoria* or Dyer’s Broom. The name *tinctorum* comes from the Latin word for a dyer of cloth. This plant was of great economic importance in the 17th century; its stems and leaves were boiled to produce a bright yellow pigment. When this yellow was applied over fabric already dyed blue with woad, it produced the high-quality "Kendal Green" cloth.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three plants: the medicinal Field Scabious, the ornamental Arabian Star-of-Bethlehem, and Dyer's Greenweed, used for producing yellow textile dye.</summary>
<keywords>Scabiosa, Star-of-Bethlehem, Dyer's Greenweed, botanical illustration, herbal medicine, textile dyes, 17th-century botany</keywords>

<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the botanical survey of flowering bulbs and rhizomes. It transitions from the Arabian variety of the Star-of-Bethlehem (Ornithogalum) shown on the previous page to a Spanish spiked variety, and introduces the first two examples of Solomon’s Seal (Polygonatum), valued for their unique root structures and medicinal properties.</meta>

III.
->**Great Solomon’s Seal**<-
<note>original: "Polygonatum maius." The name <term>Polygonatum</term> comes from the Greek words for "many knees," referring to the numerous joints or nodes on its thick horizontal rhizome. The common name, Solomon’s Seal, refers to the circular scars left on the rootstock after the stems die back, which were thought to resemble the impression of a signet seal.</note>

I.
->**Spiked Spanish**<-
->**Star-of-Bethlehem**<-
<note>original: "Ornithogalum Hispanicum spicatum." Following the "Arabian" variety on the preceding page, this "Spiked Spanish" version is characterized by its tall, upright flower stalk (a spike or raceme) densely crowded with white, star-shaped blossoms. In the 17th century, these were popular additions to formal "pleasure gardens."</note>

II.
->**Common Solomon’s Seal**<-
<note>original: "Polygonatum vulgarius." While similar to the "Great" variety, this common version (now usually classified as *Polygonatum multiflorum*) was a staple of the woodland floor and the apothecary's garden. Herbalists of the era, such as Nicholas Culpeper, suggested that the root, when stamped and applied as a poultice, was excellent for "knitting" broken bones and healing bruises.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the spiked Spanish variety of the Star-of-Bethlehem alongside two varieties of Solomon’s Seal, highlighting their distinctive root systems and floral structures.</summary>

->IIII.<-
->**The Great Double-Flowered Yellow Rose**<-
<note>original: "Rosa lutea maxima flore pleno." Yellow roses were a rarity in European gardens of this era, originally introduced from the Near East. The term <term>flore pleno</term> tells us this is a "double" variety, bread for a high density of petals that hide the central stamens.</note>

->I.<-
->**The Red Hundred-Leaved Rose**<-
<note>original: "Rosa centifolia rubra." Known today as the Cabbage Rose or *Rosa centifolia*, the name "hundred-leaved" refers to the massive number of petals in the bloom, not the actual leaves of the plant. These roses were famous for their heavy, globular shape and intense fragrance.</note>

<column-break/>

->III.<-
->**The Double Pink Provence Rose**<-
<note>original: "Rosa provincialis flore incarnato pleno." The term <term>incarnato</term> <gloss>original gloss "na" completes "incarnato"</gloss> literally translates to "flesh-colored," a standard period term for a soft pink or blush hue. The Provence rose was a staple of European floral art and perfume.</note>

->II.<-
->**The Variegated Rose of Palestrina**<-
<note>original: "Rosa praenestina variegata." <term>Praenestina</term> refers to the ancient Italian city of Praeneste (modern-day Palestrina), which was famous in antiquity for its roses. A "variegated" rose features petals that are striped or spotted with a secondary color, usually white or pale pink against a darker red.</note>

<summary>This plate illustrates four distinct varieties of cultivated roses, including the rare yellow rose and the dense "hundred-leaved" cabbage rose, emphasizing the botanical diversity and horticultural achievement of the period.</summary>

### IV.
### White-Flowered Provence Rose
<note>original: "Rosa provincialis florē albo." The <term>Provence Rose</term>—often associated with *Rosa centifolia*—was a staple of European gardens. While typically pink, this white variant is depicted with the characteristic "cabbage" shape where numerous petals overlap in a dense, rounded bloom.</note>

<image-desc>Specimen II: A botanical engraving of a rose plant with single-petaled white flowers (a "simple" form) characterized by prominent central stamens. The illustration includes flower buds at various stages and serrated foliage.</image-desc>

### II.
### White Rose with a Simple Flower
<note>original: "Rosa alba florē simplici." In botanical terms, a <term>simple</term> flower refers to a "single" rose, possessing only the natural five petals. This allows the central crown of golden stamens (the pollen-bearing parts) to be clearly visible, a feature usually hidden in "double" roses.</note>

<column-break/>

<image-desc>Specimen III: A botanical engraving of a rose branch with two large, dense, multi-petaled red roses and a single bud at the top. The thorny stem is accompanied by serrated leaves.</image-desc>

### III.
### Fragrant Red Milesian Rose
<note>original: "Rosa Milesia florē rubro olens." The <term>Milesian Rose</term> is named after the ancient Greek city of Miletus. In antiquity, Pliny the Elder described it as having a deep red color and no more than twelve petals, though this illustration shows a much fuller, "double" version preferred by 17th-century botanists. <term>Olens</term> simply means "scented" or "fragrant."</note>

<image-desc>Specimen I: A botanical engraving of a rose branch with two large, double-flowered white roses and one smaller bloom drooping downwards. The stem is heavily thorny and bears serrated leaves.</image-desc>

### I.
### Double White Rose
<note>original: "Rosa florē albo pleno." The term <term>pleno</term> (full) indicates a "double" flower, a mutation where the plant's stamens develop into extra petals. These were the most prized specimens for ornamental gardens due to their opulent appearance.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates four distinct types of roses, highlighting the distinction between "simple" (single-petaled) and "full" (double-petaled) varieties. It features the historically significant Milesian and Provence roses in white and red forms.</summary>

->I.<-

The "blackish-red" description indicates a deep, velvety crimson color that appears nearly black in the shadows of the petals.</note>

### Cinnamon Rose
<note>original: "Rosa Cinnamomea." This species is named for the distinctive reddish-brown color of its stems, which resembles <term>cinnamon</term> bark. It was a popular garden rose in the 16th and 17th centuries, often appearing in "double" forms where the flower is full and rounded with many petals.</note>

->IV. Eglantine Rose.<-
<note>original: "Rosa Eglenteria." Commonly known as the Sweet Briar, this rose is distinctive because its leaves—not just its flowers—emit a sweet, apple-like fragrance, especially after rain. In the language of early botany and poetry, it is frequently referred to as <term>Eglantine</term>.</note>

III.
*Fuchs' Lemon Balm.*
<note>original: "Melissa Fuchsij." This variety of lemon balm is named in honor of <term>Leonhart Fuchs</term> (1501–1566), one of the "fathers of German botany." The name <term>Melissa</term> is derived from the Greek word for "honeybee," as the plant's sweet, citrus-scented leaves are famously attractive to bees.</note>

->I.<-
->*Double flesh-colored Peony.*<-
<note>original: "Pæonia flore pleno incarnato." The term <term>flore pleno</term> (full flower) indicates a "double" bloom where the plant has been cultivated to produce extra layers of petals. As with the roses on the previous page, <term>incarnato</term> describes a warm, carnation-pink or "flesh" tone.</note>

II.
*Adder's-tongue.*
<note>original: "Ophioglossum." This name comes from the Greek words *ophis* (snake) and *glossa* (tongue). It refers to the plant's distinctive appearance: a single leaf and a slender, upright spike that resembles a serpent's tongue. It was a common ingredient in "vulnerary" (wound-healing) salves in early medicine.</note>

<meta>Continuing from page 101, which depicted the "Many-petaled Peony" and the "Lesser Stonecrop," this plate continues the catalog of the Peony family by introducing a "foreign" scarlet variety. I.
# Foreign Peony with
## scarlet flowers.
<note>original: "Paeonia peregrina flore coccineo." The species name <term>peregrina</term> translates to "foreign" or "wandering," as this plant was native to the Balkans and Italy rather than Central Europe. The term <term>coccineo</term> refers to a vibrant scarlet or crimson color, traditionally derived from the kermes insect. In the 17th century, these "exotic" peonies were highly prized in pleasure gardens for their intense color and distinct, deeply lobed foliage.</note>

II.
# Wild Ginger
<note>original: "Asarum." Also known as Asarabacca, this is a low-growing, shade-loving perennial. Despite the common name "Wild Ginger," it is not related to the culinary ginger used in cooking. Early modern herbalists valued the plant primarily for its roots and leaves, which acted as a powerful emetic (to induce vomiting). The illustration captures its unique growth habit, where the small, brownish-purple flowers grow very close to the soil, often hidden by the glossy, kidney-shaped leaves.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the Foreign Peony, distinguished by its brilliant scarlet petals, alongside the low-growing Wild Ginger (Asarum). The plate highlights the contrast between the tall, showy blooms of the peony and the modest, medicinal ground-cover of the woodland floor.</summary>

<keywords>Foreign Peony, Paeonia peregrina, Scarlet Peony, Wild Ginger, Asarum, Asarabacca, medicinal plants, botanical engraving</keywords>

--- Page 114 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical sequence from the previous entries. While the previous page focused on the Foreign Peony and Wild Ginger, this plate (numbered 303 in the corner) presents the "Male" variety of the Peony, which was a cornerstone of the Renaissance garden, alongside two smaller but highly significant healing herbs: Sanicle and Lady's Mantle.</meta>

->III.<-
# Sanicle
<note>original: "Sanicula." The name is derived from the Latin verb <term>sanare</term>, meaning "to heal." In the Renaissance, this woodland plant was considered a premier "vulnerary" (wound-healing) herb. A popular saying of the time suggested that those who used Sanicle had little need for a surgeon, as it was believed to knit together both external wounds and internal ruptures.</note>

->I.<-
# Male Peony with
## purple flowers.
<note>original: "Pæonia mas flore purpureo." Early botanists classified peonies as "Male" or "Female" based on their physical characteristics rather than biological sex; the "Male" peony was distinguished by its larger, thicker roots and its simple, undivided leaves. The <term>purpureo</term> (purple) flower described here likely refers to the deep magenta or crimson-pink common to the species. Beyond its beauty, the seeds and roots were frequently used in "apothecary's magic" to protect against nightmares and seizures.</note>

->II.<-
# Lady's Mantle
<note>original: "Alchimilla." This name is a Latinized form of the Arabic word for "alchemy." The plant earned this name because its pleated, fan-like leaves collect beads of dew that do not soak in, but remain like pearls of "celestial water." Alchemists highly valued this water for their experiments, believing it possessed a purity not found in groundwater. Medicinally, it was used as a powerful astringent.</note>

<summary>This plate illustrates the robust Male Peony alongside Sanicle and Lady's Mantle, focusing on plants that were as valued for their mystical and alchemical associations as they were for their practical medicinal uses.</summary>

<keywords>Male Peony, Paeonia mas, Sanicle, Sanicula, Lady's Mantle, Alchimilla, medicinal herbs, alchemy, Renaissance botany</keywords>

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical cataloging of the garden, following the previous page's descriptions of Sanicle and Lady's Mantle. While the previous entries focused on woodland and alchemical plants, this page (104) highlights the impressive "Greater Male Peony" and two varieties of Milkwort, continuing the theme of plants valued for both their ornamental beauty and their functional roles in early modern medicine.</meta>

->I.<-
# The Greater Male Peony
## with flesh-colored flowers.
<note>original: "Paeonia mas maior flore incarnato." The descriptor <term>incarnato</term> (literally "in the flesh") refers to a delicate pink or blush-red hue. Early botanists designated this variety as "Male" due to its thick, undivided leaves and sturdy, carrot-like root system, contrasting it with the "Female" Peony, which typically featured more finely divided foliage. Beyond its garden appeal, the Peony was a staple of the Renaissance "physic garden," where its roots and seeds were dried and used to treat neurological disorders like epilepsy and "night-mare."</note>

->II.<-
# Milkwort with
## purplish-red flowers.
<note>original: "Polygalon flore rubro purpurascente." The name <term>Polygalon</term> is derived from the Greek words for "much milk." It was widely believed that if cattle grazed upon this herb, their milk production would increase, and it was similarly prescribed to nursing mothers. This variety is noted for its transition toward a deeper purple hue as it matures.</note>

->III.<-
# Milkwort with
## blue flowers.
<note>original: "Polygalon flore caeruleo." Because Milkwort blooms in various colors (white, pink, and blue) during the spring, it was often gathered for the "Rogation-tide" processions in the church calendar, earning it the folk name "Procession Flower." The blue variety, seen here, was particularly prized for the vividness of its small, winged blossoms.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the Greater Male Peony alongside two color varieties of Milkwort, highlighting plants that were essential to Renaissance herbals for their perceived influence on human and animal health.</summary>
<keywords>Peony, Milkwort, Paeonia, Polygalon, Renaissance botany, medicinal herbs, Rogation-tide</keywords>

--- Page 116 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical survey from page 104. While the previous page introduced the "Greater Male Peony" and various Milkworts, this entry presents another color variation of the "Male Peony" alongside the "Smallest Wintergreen" and the "False Ground-Box." The inclusion of these specimens highlights the early modern interest in classifying plants by both their structural similarities (such as the Box-like leaves of the Pseudochamebuxus) and their pharmaceutical applications.</meta>

->II.<-
# Smallest Wintergreen
<note>original: "Pyrola minima." The name <term>Pyrola</term> is a diminutive of the Latin *pyrus* (pear), chosen because the plant's rounded leaves resemble those of a pear tree. Wintergreen was historically valued in "vulnerary" (wound-healing) potions and was believed to possess cooling properties suitable for treating ulcers and inflammations.</note>

---

->I.<-
# Male Peony 
## with flesh-colored flowers.
<note>original: "Paeonia mas flore incarnato." The term <term>incarnato</term> refers to a delicate, pale pink or blush hue, reminiscent of flesh. Following the classification of the time, this "Male" variety is characterized by its broad, undivided leaves and carrot-like tuberous roots, as seen in the illustration. Beyond its ornamental beauty, the peony was a vital component of the "materia medica," frequently used to treat epilepsy—often by hanging a piece of the root around the neck as an amulet.</note>

---

->III.<-
# False Ground-Box
<note>original: "Pseudochamebuxus." This plant, now known as the Shrubby Milkwort (*Polygala chamaebuxus*), earned its name from its physical resemblance to a dwarf Boxwood tree (*Buxus*). Its inclusion here likely links it to the Milkworts featured on the previous page; early botanists grouped them together despite the woody stems of this particular species because of the similar structure of their flowers.</note>

<summary>This page features a botanical engraving of a flesh-colored Male Peony, the Smallest Wintergreen, and the Shrubby Milkwort (False Ground-Box), detailing their medicinal uses and naming conventions.</summary>
<keywords>Male Peony, Wintergreen, Shrubby Milkwort, Paeonia mas, Pyrola, Pseudochamebuxus, medicinal herbs, Renaissance botany, epilepsy remedies</keywords>

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical catalog, specifically focusing on white-flowered varieties and spring ephemerals. While page 105 discussed the flesh-colored Male Peony, this page presents the white-flowered version of the same species alongside the Spring Cyclamen. This sequence illustrates the early modern practice of documenting color variations within a single "kind" of plant and grouping them by their flowering season.</meta>

->I.<-
# Male Peony
## with white flowers.
<note>original: "Pæonia Mas flore albo." This is the white-flowered variation of the <term>Male Peony</term>. In the botanical traditions of the 16th and 17th centuries, the "Male" peony was distinguished by its large, thick, undivided leaves and long, carrot-like roots, whereas the "Female" peony typically had more finely divided foliage. Beyond its beauty, the peony was a cornerstone of the "materia medica" (medical materials), believed to be a potent remedy against nightmares and "falling sickness" (epilepsy).</note>

---

->II.<-
# Spring Cyclamen
<note>original: "Cyclamen Vernum." The name is derived from the Greek word *kyklos* (circle), referring either to the round, flattened shape of the tuber or the way the flower stalks coil like springs after pollination. This variety was also commonly known as <term>Sowbread</term>, based on the belief that wild swine favored the starchy underground corms as food. Though beautiful, the plant was treated with caution by herbalists due to its strong purgative effects.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates two significant early spring plants: the white-flowered variation of the Male Peony and the Spring Cyclamen. Both specimens are shown with their distinctive root systems—the peony's elongated tubers and the cyclamen's round corm—emphasizing the early modern focus on underground structures for plant identification.</summary>

<keywords>Male Peony, white peony, Spring Cyclamen, Sowbread, botanical illustration, corm, tuberous roots, medicinal plants</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the botanical catalog, moving from the native European peonies and cyclamens discussed on page 106 to varieties of Borage and exotic Peonies sourced from the East, specifically the "Byzantine" or Ottoman regions. This transition reflects the 16th and 17th-century fascination with importing ornamental plants from the Levant and Constantinople to European gardens.</meta>

V.
# Borage
## with blue flowers.
<note>original: "Borago flore cæruleo." Commonly known today as the <term>Starflower</term>, Borage was prized by herbalists for its ability to "expel pensiveness and melancholy." Its flowers were often candied or added to wine to gladden the heart.</note>

IV.
# Borage
## with white flowers.
<note>original: "Borago flore albo." While the brilliant blue variety is most common, this white-flowered form was a documented variation sought after by collectors of "florists' flowers" who valued rare color mutations.</note>

---

II.
-> # Smaller Byzantine Peony <-
<note>original: "Pæonia Bizanthina Minor." The designation <term>Byzantine</term> indicates that these specimens were brought to Europe from the gardens of the Ottoman Empire (modern-day Turkey). These peonies were distinct from the wild "Male" and "Female" peonies native to the Alps and Apennines.</note>

I.
-> # Greater Byzantine Peony <-
<note>original: "Pæonia Bizanthina Maior." This refers to a larger-flowered variety of the eastern peony. In the early modern period, plants were often categorized into "Greater" and "Smaller" (Maior and Minor) based on their overall scale and flower size rather than distinct genetic species.</note>

III.
-> # Dwarf Peony <-
<note>original: "Pæonia pumilis." From the Latin for "lowly" or "dwarf." This variety is characterized by its shorter stems and compact growth habit, making it distinct from the taller, more robust Byzantine varieties shown above it.</note>

<summary>This page presents a collection of garden plants, focusing on color variations in Borage and exotic Peonies imported from the Byzantine regions. It illustrates the early modern practice of classifying plants by size (Greater vs. Smaller) and geographic origin.</summary>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical survey of the garden, shifting focus from the exotic "Byzantine" peonies and common borage of the previous page to the classic European red peony and two medicinal or decorative herbs: Hedge hyssop and Bladder dock.</meta>

->II.<-
-> # Hedge hyssop with white flowers, also known as the Grace of God <-
<note>original: "Gratiola flor: albo et Gratia Dei." The plant <term>Gratiola officinalis</term> was widely known as *Gratia Dei* (the Grace of God) in medieval and early modern herbals. This name refers to its potent medicinal strength as a purgative, though the plant is actually quite toxic if not handled with care.</note>

->I.<-
-> # Red Peony with single flowers <-
<note>original: "Poeonia rubra flore simplici." In botanical terms, "single" refers to a flower with only one layer of petals, which allows the central cluster of yellow stamens to be seen clearly. The peony has been a staple of European gardens since antiquity, both for its showy blooms and its roots, which were used to treat "falling sickness" (epilepsy).</note>

->III.<-
-> # Foreign Bladder Dock <-
<note>original: "Acetosa Vesicaria peregrina." This variety of dock or sorrel is easily recognized by its heart-shaped, inflated seed pods. The Latin <term>vesicaria</term> means "bladder-like," and <term>peregrina</term> indicates that the plant was considered an exotic introduction from foreign lands, likely North Africa or the Mediterranean region.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three distinct botanical specimens: the medicinal "Grace of God" herb, a single-petaled red peony, and an exotic variety of dock characterized by its bladder-like seed pods.</summary>

<keywords>Red Peony, Hedge hyssop, Bladder dock, medicinal herbs, Gratiola officinalis, botanical illustration, garden history</keywords>

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical catalog, transitioning from the garden perennials of the previous page to a group of more specialized plants: a star-flowered lily relative, a forage legume from the Low Countries, and an aquatic medicinal herb found in wetlands.</meta>

->II.<-
-> ### Branched Spider-herb <-
<note>original: "Phalangium ramosum." This plant is known today as <term>Anthericum ramosum</term> or the Branched St. Bernard's Lily. In early modern botany, it was called *Phalangium* because it was believed to be a potent remedy against the bite of the "phalangia"—a term used for several types of venomous spiders. Its "branched" (ramosum) stems and star-shaped white flowers make it a distinctive garden ornamental.</note>

->I.<-
-> ### Belgian Cockshead <-
<note>original: "Onobrychis Belgarum." This is likely a variety of <term>Onobrychis viciifolia</term>, commonly known as Sainfoin or Cockshead. The name *Onobrychis* comes from the Greek words for "donkey" and "to bray," reflecting the belief that the plant was so delicious to donkeys that they would bray for it. The label "Belgarum" indicates this specific variety was associated with the Low Countries (modern Belgium and the Netherlands).</note>

->III.<-
-> ### Marsh Trefoil <-
<note>original: "Trifolium Palustre." Known to modern botanists as <term>Menyanthes trifoliata</term> or Bogbean. Despite the name "Trefoil" (three-leaf), it is not a true clover. As the name *Palustre* (marshy) suggests, it thrives in bogs and wetlands. It was highly valued in traditional medicine for its bitter properties, which were used to stimulate digestion and treat skin conditions.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three distinct flowering plants: the Branched Spider-herb (St. Bernard's Lily), the Belgian Cockshead (Sainfoin), and the Marsh Trefoil (Bogbean).</summary>

<keywords>Branched Spider-herb, St. Bernard's Lily, Belgian Cockshead, Sainfoin, Marsh Trefoil, Bogbean, Phalangium, Onobrychis, Trifolium, botanical illustration, wetland plants</keywords>

--- Page 121 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical survey from page 109. While the previous page focused on specialized herbs like the spider-herb and bogbean, this page illustrates a parasitic plant, a large toxic umbellifer, and a common wetland reed.</meta>

-> III. Bur-reed. I. Broomrape. II. Great Hemlock. <-

-> I. <-
### Broomrape
<note>original: "Orobanche." These are fascinating parasitic plants that lack chlorophyll, meaning they cannot produce their own food from sunlight. Instead, they attach to the roots of host plants to draw nutrients. The name <term>Orobanche</term> comes from the Greek words for "vetch" and "to strangle," referring to how the plant was thought to kill the legumes it fed upon. Because it lacks green leaves, early botanists often found it mysterious.</note>

-> II. <-
### Great Hemlock
<note>original: "Cicuta maxima." This name refers to a large variety of hemlock, likely the Water Hemlock or a similar member of the carrot family. Hemlock is infamous as a potent toxin; it was famously used in Ancient Greece to carry out the death sentence of the philosopher Socrates. Its <term>umbels</term> (the flat, umbrella-like clusters of flowers) and thick, fleshy root are characteristic of this dangerous group of plants.</note>

-> III. <-
### Bur-reed
<note>original: "Sparganium." A common sight in marshes and along the edges of ponds, the Bur-reed is named for its distinctive spiky, spherical seed heads that resemble burrs. The name comes from the Greek word *sparganon*, meaning a "swaddling band," which describes its long, ribbon-like leaves. It serves as an important habitat and food source for wetland birds.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three distinct botanical specimens: the parasitic Broomrape, the toxic Great Hemlock, and the aquatic Bur-reed.</summary>

<keywords>Broomrape, Orobanche, Great Hemlock, Cicuta maxima, Bur-reed, Sparganium, parasitic plants, toxic plants, wetland flora, umbellifers</keywords>

->III.<-
->*Yellow Water Lily*<-

->II.<-

->I.<-
->*Great White Water Lily*<-

-> I. <-
### Great White Water Lily
<note>original: "Nÿmphæa alba maior." This is the large European white water lily. The name <term>Nymphaea</term> comes from the Greek "nymphe," the female nature spirits of the springs and woods. This species is famous for its large, many-petaled flowers and wide, notched leaves that float on the water's surface. Below the water, it anchors itself with a thick, fleshy <term>rhizome</term> (an underground stem), which is shown here with a rough, textured surface.</note>

-> II. <-
### Lesser White Water Lily
<note>original: "Nÿmphæa alba minor." A smaller variety of the white water lily. In historical botany, "maior" and "minor" were often used to distinguish between different sizes of the same plant family before modern genetic classification. It shares the same circular, notched leaf shape and delicate white petals as its larger relative but grows in a more compact form.</note>

-> III. <-
### Yellow Water Lily
<note>original: "Nÿmphæa lutea." Commonly known today as the Nuphar lutea or Yellow Water-lily. It is easily distinguished by its globular, cup-shaped yellow flowers that sit slightly above the water. Unlike the white lilies, its petals are smaller and less numerous, and its seed pod often resembles a leather bottle, leading to its old English nickname, the "Brandy Bottle."</note>

<summary>This page features a botanical engraving of three water lily species, highlighting the differences between the large and small white varieties and the distinctive yellow water lily.</summary>

<keywords>Water Lily, Nymphaea alba, Nymphaea lutea, aquatic plants, botanical illustration, rhizome, Great White Water Lily, Yellow Water Lily, Brandy Bottle</keywords>

<meta>This page follows a discussion of terrestrial and marsh plants, presenting various species of primroses and daisies. It serves as a botanical bridge before the detailed study of aquatic lilies found on the following page (333).</meta>

->IV.<-
### Red Narrow-leaved Primrose
<note>original: "Primula Veris Angustifolia rubra." The name <term>Primula Veris</term> literally translates to "the firstling of spring," referring to the flower's early bloom. <term>Angustifolia</term> means "narrow-leaved." This variety is distinguished by its vibrant red petals and its tall, central stalk that carries a cluster of flowers (an umbel) high above the foliage.</note>

### White Narrow-leaved Primrose
<note>original: "Primula Veris Angustifolia alba." A pale counterpart to the previous specimen. These flowers were often prized in early modern gardens for their delicate color. Like its red relative, it features the characteristic wrinkled, elongated leaves typical of the primrose family.</note>

->I.<-
### Variegated Proliferous Daisy
<note>original: "Bellis prolifera variegata." The <term>Bellis</term> (daisy) takes its name from the Latin "bellus," meaning "pretty" or "charming." This specific "proliferous" variety is a botanical curiosity often called the "Hen and Chickens" daisy. It is characterized by a "childing" habit where smaller, secondary flower heads grow out of the base of the primary central flower, creating a ruffled, multi-headed appearance.</note>

->II.<-
### Lesser Red Double-flowered Daisy
<note>original: "Bellis minor flore multiplici rubra." The term <term>flore multiplici</term> refers to "double" flowers, which have been bred to have many more petals than a wild daisy, giving them a pom-pom like shape. This "lesser" variety refers to the smaller stature of the plant compared to the larger ox-eye daisies.</note>

->III.<-
### Lesser White Double-flowered Daisy
<note>original: "Bellis minor multiplici flore alba." A white-petaled version of the double daisy. In the language of 17th-century botany, these "multiplici" variations were highly sought after by collectors for their ornamental density and symmetry.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates several cultivated varieties of primroses and daisies, focusing on color variations and the unique "proliferous" growth habit of the Hen and Chickens daisy.</summary>

<keywords>Primrose, Primula veris, Daisy, Bellis, Hen and Chickens daisy, double-flowered, botanical illustration, horticulture</keywords>

->III.<-
### **Smallest Spring Gentian**
### **with a blue flower.**
<note>original: "Gentianella minima verna flore cæruleo." The <term>Gentianella</term> is a "little Gentian," a genus famous for its intense, deep-blue color that has captivated botanists for centuries. The term <term>verna</term> (spring) indicates its early flowering time, while <term>minima</term> (smallest) distinguishes this diminutive species from its larger alpine relatives.</note>

---

->I.<-
### **Marsh Marigold with a**
### **double flower.**
<note>original: "Caltha palustris flore multiplici." The <term>Caltha palustris</term> is commonly known as the Marsh Marigold or Kingcup. Its name <term>palustris</term> (derived from the Latin "palus," meaning swamp or marsh) perfectly describes its preferred damp habitat. This specific specimen is noted for being <term>flore multiplici</term> (double-flowered), a botanical mutation where the flower produces extra layers of petals instead of stamens, giving it a lush, rose-like appearance.</note>

---

->II.<-
### **March Dog Violet.**
<note>original: "Viola Martia Canina." The <term>Viola Martia</term> (Violet of Mars, or March) refers to the plant's traditional blooming month. The designation <term>Canina</term> (dog-like) was historically used by botanists to denote a plant that lacked the prized fragrance of the "Sweet Violet" (*Viola odorata*), suggesting it was "lesser" or fit only for dogs, though it remains a beautiful woodland wildflower.</note>

->I<-
### **Fox Orchid**
<note>original: "Testiculus Vulpinus." The name, which literally translates to "Fox’s Little Testicle," refers to the pair of rounded underground tubers (testiculate) visible at the base of the plant. In the Renaissance, the <term>Orchis</term> was often named in this suggestive manner due to the "Doctrine of Signatures," which held that a plant's physical form indicated its medicinal purpose—in this case, treatments related to fertility or vitality.</note>

---

->II<-
### **Greater Mountain Cornflower**
<note>original: "Cyanus Maior." The term <term>Cyanus</term> is derived from the Greek *kyanos*, meaning a deep blue color. While the common cornflower is a familiar sight in grain fields, this "Greater" variety refers to the *Centaurea montana*, a more robust perennial with larger, striking flower heads often found in sub-alpine meadows.</note>

---

->III<-
### **Austrian Ground-Pine**
<note>original: "Chamepitys Austriaca." The name <term>Chamepitys</term> translates from Greek as "ground pine" (*chamai* meaning on the ground and *pitys* meaning pine). It is so named because its low-growing habit and narrow, resinous, needle-like leaves bear a resemblance to a miniature pine tree, though it is actually a member of the mint family.</note>

---

->IIII<-
### **White-Flowered Cinquefoil**
<note>original: "Pentaphillon flor: albo." The name <term>Pentaphillon</term> (Five-Leaf) describes the plant's palmate leaves, which are divided into five distinct leaflets like the fingers of a hand. While the most common cinquefoils have yellow petals, this specific entry describes a <term>flor: albo</term> (white-flowered) variety, likely the *Potentilla alba*.</note>

<summary>This engraving illustrates four diverse herbaceous plants: a terrestrial orchid, a large blue cornflower, the resinous Ground-Pine, and a white-flowered variety of the five-leaved Cinquefoil.</summary>

Though we think of cornflowers as blue, purple variants were frequently documented by early botanists.</note>

<note>original: "Cyanus aruensis flore albo fundo purpureo." This variety is particularly striking, featuring white ray florets (the outer petals) and a dark purple center (the disk florets). The "fundo" refers to the base or heart of the flower head.</note>

->VI<-
### **Field Cornflower with Variegated Flowers**
<note>original: "Cyanus aruensis flore variegato." A <term>variegated</term> flower features petals with multiple colors, often appearing striped or splashed with different tints, a trait highly prized by early modern gardeners.</note>

---

->I<-
### **Field Cornflower with Blue Flowers**
<note>original: "Cyanus aruensis flore cœruleo." This is the "standard" cornflower, famous for its intense, brilliant blue—a color so iconic it is often called "cornflower blue." The name <term>Cyanus</term> comes directly from the Greek *kyanos*, meaning dark blue. This specimen is shown in the center of the plate with its full root system and branching stem.</note>

---

->II<-
### **Field Cornflower with Flesh-Colored Flowers**
<note>original: "Cyanus aruensis flore incarnato." The term <term>incarnato</term> (literally "in the flesh") refers to a soft, pale pink color, similar to the skin tones used in Renaissance portraiture.</note>

---

->III<-
### **Field Cornflower with White Flowers**
<note>original: "Cyanus aruensis flore albo." A pure white variety of the common field flower.</note>

# I.
# **Large-fruited Strawberry**
<note>original: "Fraga fructu magno." The name <term>Fraga</term> is the Latin root for the modern botanical genus *Fragaria*. It is derived from the word *fragrans*, referring to the fruit's powerful aroma. This "large-fruited" variety likely represents a cultivated or particularly vigorous specimen of the wild strawberry, as the modern large garden strawberry was not hybridized until the 18th century.</note>

---

## III.
## *Red-fruited Strawberry*
<note>original: "Fraga fructu rubro." This describes the common woodland strawberry (*Fragaria vesca*). Early botanists were careful to distinguish between these wild types and their color variations, noting the texture of the seeds and the "runners" (stolons) by which the plant spreads across the forest floor.</note>

## II.
## *White-fruited Strawberry*
<note>original: "Fraga fructu albo." Natural variations of strawberries with white or cream-colored fruit were popular in early modern gardens. These are not unripe berries; they remain white when fully mature and were often noted for being sweeter or less acidic than the red variety.</note>

<note>Vocabulary: Strawberry, botanical engraving, large, red, white, Fragaria</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three distinct varieties of the strawberry (Fragaria), highlighting differences in fruit size and color. It reflects the early modern botanical practice of cataloging both the standard wild species and its notable color variants.</summary>

<keywords>strawberry, Fragaria, botanical illustration, horticulture, wild strawberry, white strawberry, medicinal plants</keywords>

->IIII.<-
->**Broad-leaved dwarf iris with two flowers**<-
<note>original: "Chamaeiris latifolia biflora." The term <term>Chamaeiris</term> comes from the Greek *chamai* ("on the ground"), signifying a low-growing or dwarf iris. This specific variety is noted for its "biflora" habit, meaning it produces two blossoms on a single stalk.</note>

---

->I.<-
->**Broad-leaved dwarf iris**<-
->**Eighth variety of Clusius**<-
<note>original: "Chamaeiris latifolia VIII. Clusii." Carolus Clusius (1526–1609) was one of the most influential botanists of the Renaissance. The Roman numeral VIII refers to the eighth entry in his specific catalog of irises, which collectors used as a standard reference for identifying species.</note>

<column-break/>

->III.<-
->**Smallest broad-leaved dwarf iris**<-
<note>original: "Chamaeiris latifolia minima." In early botany, <term>minima</term> was used to describe the most diminutive version of a plant family. This specimen represents a tiny, broad-leaved variant of the ground iris.</note>

---

->II.<-
->**Broad-leaved dwarf iris**<-
->**Tenth variety of Clusius**<-
<note>original: "Chamaeiris latifolia X. Clusij." This refers to the tenth classification in Clusius’s records. During this era, many iris varieties were being imported to Europe from the Mediterranean and the Near East, requiring precise numbering to track new colors and shapes.</note>

<summary>This page features botanical engravings of four varieties of dwarf irises (Chamaeiris), distinguished by their broad leaves and specific growth habits. It highlights the influence of Carolus Clusius, whose taxonomic numbering system was essential for early modern botanical identification.</summary>

<keywords>dwarf iris, Chamaeiris, Carolus Clusius, broad-leaved iris, Renaissance botany, floral classification</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of dwarf irises (Chamaeiris) from page 117. While the previous plate categorized these plants based on leaf width and the taxonomical numbering of Carolus Clusius, this plate focuses on distinct color variations—specifically white, yellow/purple, blue/violet, and silver varieties.</meta>

->III.<-
->**Dwarf iris with a white flower**<-
<note>original: "Chamaeiris flore candido." The term <term>candido</term> refers to a bright, pure, or "dazzling" white. In early modern botany, white irises were often associated with purity and were specifically cultivated for their striking appearance against green foliage.</note>

---

->IV.<-
->**Dwarf iris with a yellow and purplish flower**<-
<note>original: "Chamaeiris flavo & purpurascente flore." This describes a bicolor variety. The use of <term>purpurascente</term> implies a color that is becoming purple or has a purplish tint, likely describing the "falls" (the drooping petals) of the iris which often differ in color from the upright "standards."</note>

<column-break/>

->II.<-
->**Dwarf iris with a blue and violet color**<-
<note>original: "Chamaeiris coeruleo & violacco colore." These represent the more traditional shades of the *Iris pumila*. The illustration emphasizes the <term>rhizome</term>—the thick, horizontal rootstock visible at the base—which was often used in historical medicine and perfumery (as "orris root").</note>

---

->I.<-
->**Dwarf iris with a silvery flower**<-
<note>original: "Chamaeiris flore argenteo." The descriptor <term>argenteo</term> (silvery) refers to a flower with a shimmering, pale luster, likely a very light lavender or a white with a cool, metallic sheen that appeared silvery under sunlight.</note>

<summary>This botanical plate illustrates four color variations of the dwarf iris (Chamaeiris), detailing their flowers, foliage, and rhizomatic root systems. These classifications highlight the transition in early botany from purely functional descriptions to the appreciation of ornamental variety and color.</summary>

<keywords>dwarf iris, Chamaeiris, botanical illustration, rhizome, iris varieties, Carolus Clusius, Renaissance botany, flower colors</keywords>
