->I.<-
->**Greater Meadow Iris**<-
<note>original: "Iris pratensis maior." The term <term>pratensis</term> indicates a plant that grows in meadows or fields. In a modern botanical context, this likely refers to a species like *Iris sibirica* or *Iris spuria*, which are significantly taller and more vigorous than the dwarf irises described previously.</note>

---

->III.<-
->**Purple Dame's-violet**<-
<note>original: "Viola matronalis flore purpureo." Although labeled here as a <term>viola</term>, this plant is actually *Hesperis matronalis*, known today as Dame’s-violet or Dame’s-rocket. It was a favorite in early modern European gardens for its sweet fragrance, which becomes particularly strong in the evening.</note>

---

->II.<-
->**White Dame's-violet**<-
<note>original: "Viola matronalis flore albo." This is the white-flowered variety of the plant listed above. The illustration carefully depicts the <term>serrated leaves</term>—those with jagged, saw-like edges—and the branching root system, which distinguishes its growth habit from the horizontal rhizomes of the iris seen in the center.</note>

<summary>This plate illustrates the Greater Meadow Iris alongside two color varieties of the Dame's-violet. It highlights the morphological differences between the iris's thick rhizome and the Dame's-violet's branching root and serrated foliage.</summary>

--- Page 131 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of the Iris genus, moving from the meadow varieties and Dame's-violets seen on the previous page to three distinct and highly prized specimens of the Iris family: the Florentine, Chalcedonian, and Illyrian varieties.</meta>

->III.<-
->**Florentine Iris**<-
<note>original: "Iris Florentina." This white-flowered iris is famous for its fragrant rhizome, known as <term>orris root</term>. In the early modern period, these roots were dried for years until they developed a scent similar to violets, at which point they were ground into powder for perfumes, hair treatments, and medicinal "sweet bags."</note>

---

->I.<-
->**Broad-leaved Chalcedonian Iris**<-
<note>original: "Iris Calcedonica latifolia." This is likely the *Iris susiana*, often called the "Mourning Iris" due to its somber, dark-speckled petals. The term <term>latifolia</term> means "broad-leaved." This species was a Great Rarity in 17th-century Europe, having been introduced from Constantinople (modern-day Istanbul) and the Levant.</note>

---

->II.<-
->**Illyrian Iris**<-
<note>original: "Iris Illyrica." Named for the ancient region of Illyria along the Adriatic coast, this plant is a relative of the *Iris pallida*. It was prized by early botanists for its hardy nature and the delicate blue or pale lilac hue of its flowers, which contrast with the thicker, more robust white and dark varieties shown alongside it.</note>

<summary>This plate features three distinct Iris species, highlighting the Broad-leaved Chalcedonian Iris as a central specimen. The illustration emphasizes the varied structures of their blooms and the thick, horizontal rhizomes characteristic of the genus.</summary>

<keywords>Florentine Iris, Chalcedonian Iris, Illyrian Iris, orris root, botanical illustration, rhizome, 17th-century gardening, exotic plants</keywords>

->III.<-
->**Greater Variegated Broad-leaved Iris**<-
<note>original: "Iris latifolia maior variegata." This variety is characterized by "variegated" or multi-colored petals. In 17th-century botany, variegation was often the result of natural mutation or viral infection (unknown at the time), making such specimens highly prized for their unique patterns.</note>

->I.<-
->**Greater Broad-leaved Violet Iris**<-
<note>original: "Iris latifolia violaceo colore maior." This is a large specimen of the violet-colored iris. The term <term>latifolia</term> (broad-leaved) refers to the wide, sword-like foliage that characterizes the "Germanica" group of irises, as opposed to the narrow, grass-like leaves of water-dwelling species.</note>

The term <term>vulgaris</term> indicates it was the most common variety known to the botanists of the era, and <term>cærulea</term> identifies its classic blue-lilac hue.</note>

->I.<-
->**Mary’s Slipper**<-
<note>original: "Calceolus Mariæ." This is the Lady's Slipper orchid (*Cypripedium calceolus*). The name derives from the Latin <term>calceolus</term>, meaning "small shoe," describing the flower’s inflated, pouch-like petal that resembles a slipper. In the medieval and early modern periods, such plants were often dedicated to the Virgin Mary.</note>

---

->III.<-
->**Variegated Pannonian Iris**<-
<note>original: "Iris Pannonica variegata." This variety is named for its origin in Pannonia, a province of the Roman Empire located in Central Europe (primarily modern-day Hungary). The term <term>variegata</term> refers to the intricate, multi-colored patterns on the petals, a feature highly sought after by early botanists and gardeners.</note>

->II.<-
->**Portuguese Iris**<-
<note>original: "Iris Portugalica." This identifies an iris species native to the Iberian Peninsula. During this era, botanical discovery was closely tied to trade and exploration; plants were often named after the regions from which they were first imported into Central European gardens.</note>

->II. **Lobel’s Variegated Yellow Iris**<-
<note>original: "Iris lutea variegata Lobelij." This specimen is named after Matthias de l'Obel (1538–1616), a Flemish physician and botanist. The term <term>variegata</term> refers to the contrasting color patterns on the petals, while <term>lutea</term> signifies a yellow hue.</note>

->I. **Clusius’s Tenth Broad-leaved Iris**<-
<note>original: "Iris latifolia decima Clusii." Carolus Clusius was one of the most influential botanists of the 16th century. His "tenth" classification follows his systematic study of irises. The term <term>latifolia</term> (broad-leaved) distinguishes this variety from those with narrower, grass-like foliage.</note>

->III. **False Marsh Iris**<-
<note>original: "Pseudo Iris palustris." Now commonly known as the Yellow Flag (*Iris pseudacorus*), the name <term>palustris</term> indicates its preference for marshy, wetland habitats. In early botany, "pseudo" (false) was often used for plants that resembled a specific genus but possessed distinct botanical differences in their fruit or seeds.</note>

<summary>This page presents three distinct iris specimens—a yellow variegated variety, a broad-leaved variety, and a marsh-dwelling variety—attributed to the pioneering botanical works of Lobelius and Clusius. The detailed engraving emphasizes the anatomical differences between the thick, horizontal rhizomes of garden irises and the fibrous root systems of water-dwelling species.</summary>

<keywords>Iris, Matthias de l'Obel, Carolus Clusius, botanical illustration, rhizome, marsh iris, broad-leaved iris, 17th-century science</keywords>

->III<-
->**Crosswort**<-
<note>original: "Cruciata." The name is derived from the Latin *crux* (cross), referring to the plant's distinctive <term>whorled</term> leaves that grow in groups of four, forming the shape of a cross. Today, this plant is typically known as *Cruciata laevipes*.</note>

->I<-
->**Stinking Iris**<-
<note>original: "Spatula fœtida." A species of iris (*Iris foetidissima*) known for its sword-shaped leaves—hence "spatula"—and the unpleasant, beef-like odor released when its leaves are crushed. Unlike the garden irises on the previous page, this variety is prized more for its bright red seeds than its muted lead-colored flowers.</note>

->II<-
->**Dodoens’s Bur-parsley**<-
<note>original: "Caucalis Dodonei." This plant belongs to the carrot family (Apiaceae). The name honors Rembert Dodoens (1517–1585), a Flemish physician and one of the most famous botanists of the Renaissance. The genus *Caucalis* usually features "burred" or prickly seeds that cling to clothing or animal fur.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three different herbaceous plants: Crosswort, characterized by its cross-like leaf pattern; the Stinking Iris, noted for its pungent foliage; and a variety of Bur-parsley named in honor of the botanist Rembert Dodoens. The detailed engravings highlight the various root types, ranging from thick rhizomes to slender taproots.</summary>

<keywords>Crosswort, Stinking Iris, Bur-parsley, Rembert Dodoens, botanical illustration, Renaissance botany, leaf morphology, root systems</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the botanical catalogue, moving from the specific irises and wild carrots of the previous pages to a group of medicinal and aromatic herbs. While the previous page focused on plants like the Stinking Iris and Bur-parsley, this page illustrates species known for their potent scents and varied growth habits, including the wetland-dwelling Sweet Flag and the woody Wall Germander.</meta>

### III.
### **Wall Germander**
<note>original: "Chamedrys." The name is derived from the Greek *chamai* ("on the ground") and *drys* ("oak"), referring to the plant's low growth habit and small leaves that resemble those of an oak tree. In early modern medicine, it was frequently used to treat gout and fevers.</note>

### I.
### **Sweet Flag with its flower spike**
<note>original: "Acorus cum suo iulo." The word *iulo* refers to the <term>spadix</term>, the finger-like, textured flower cluster seen branching from the stem. Sweet Flag (*Acorus calamus*) was prized for its aromatic <term>rhizome</term>—the thick, horizontal root shown at the bottom—which was used in perfumes, medicines, and as a "strewing herb" to scent the floors of homes.</note>

### II.
### **Water Germander**
<note>original: "Scordium." This plant (*Teucrium scordium*) takes its name from the Greek *skorodon* (garlic), due to the sharp, garlic-like odor released when its leaves are bruised. It was a famous ingredient in historical "cure-alls" and was believed to protect against the plague and various toxins.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three significant medicinal herbs: Wall Germander, Sweet Flag (featuring its distinct flower spike and aromatic root), and the garlic-scented Water Germander. These detailed engravings emphasize the specific leaf shapes and root structures used by Renaissance botanists to identify plants with healing properties.</summary>

<keywords>Sweet Flag, Wall Germander, Water Germander, Acorus calamus, botanical illustration, Renaissance medicine, aromatic herbs, rhizome, spadix, Teucrium</keywords>

--- Page 137 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical survey from the previous page's focus on aromatic and medicinal herbs like Germander and Sweet Flag. It introduces two plants known for their distinctive root systems and historical medicinal applications: the legendary Mandrake and the Lesser Celandine.</meta>

<insert>Wolf Kilian, engraver</insert> <note>original: "Wolf Kilian sculp." Wolfgang Kilian was a member of a famous family of Augsburg engravers who contributed to the "Hortus Eystettensis."</note>

->I.<-

# **Female Mandrake**
<note>original: "Mandragora foemina." In Renaissance botany, the Mandrake was often classified as "male" or "female" based on its appearance. The "female" variety typically refers to the species that flowers in autumn (*Mandragora autumnalis*). Its thick, forked <term>taproot</term> was famously thought to resemble a human figure, leading to many myths and its use in ancient anesthetics and folklore.</note>

->II.<-

# **Lesser Celandine**
<note>original: "Chelidonium minus." Known today by the scientific name *Ficaria verna*, this spring-flowering plant is distinct for its heart-shaped leaves and the small, bulbous tubers clustered at the base of the roots. Historically, it was called "pilewort" because these tubers were thought to resemble and thus treat hemorrhoids, according to the "Doctrine of Signatures"—the historical belief that a plant's physical appearance indicated its medicinal use.</note>

<summary>This page features two plants with highly characteristic root structures: the Mandrake, steeped in folklore and used as an anesthetic, and the Lesser Celandine, valued for its early spring blooms. The detailed engravings emphasize the specific root shapes which were central to identifying their medicinal properties during the Renaissance.</summary>

<keywords>Mandrake, Lesser Celandine, Mandragora foemina, Chelidonium minus, Ficaria verna, botanical illustration, Wolf Kilian, medicinal roots, Doctrine of Signatures, Renaissance botany</keywords>

->II.<-
->**Wild Sage with purple flowers**<-
<note>original: "Salvia agrestis flor: purpureo." Likely referring to *Salvia pratensis*, commonly known as Meadow Clary or Meadow Sage. The name "Salvia" comes from the Latin *salvare* (to save or heal), reflecting its long history as a medicinal herb used to treat everything from sore throats to memory loss. "Agrestis" signifies its wild nature, growing in open fields.</note>

->I.<-
->**Greater Bistort**<-
<note>original: "Bistorta Maior." Now scientifically classified as *Persicaria bistorta*. The name "Bistort" derives from the Latin *bis* (twice) and *torta* (twisted), describing the peculiar, S-shaped <term>rhizome</term>—the thick underground stem visible in the engraving. Because of its intense astringency, the root was historically used to treat wounds and stop bleeding.</note>

->III.<-
->**Wild Sage with white flowers**<-
<note>original: "Salvia agrestis flor: albo." A white-flowered color variant of the Meadow Sage. In Renaissance botanical texts, it was common practice to illustrate different color forms of the same plant side-by-side to aid in field identification and to showcase the diversity of nature’s "simple" medicines.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the Greater Bistort, named for its distinctive twisted root, alongside two color varieties of Wild Sage. The detailed engraving emphasizes the anatomical differences between the thick, woody rhizome of the bistort and the more fibrous root systems of the sage plants.</summary>

->II.<-
->**English Cowslip**<-
->**with double flowers**<-
<note>original: "Primula Veris Anglicana flore pleno." This is a "double" variety of the common Cowslip (*Primula veris*). In the language of early botanists, "flore pleno" meant "full flower," referring to blooms with extra layers of petals. These were often sought out for decorative "pleasure gardens" rather than purely for medicine.</note>

<column-break/>

->I.<-
->**Horse-tongue**<-
<note>original: "Hippoglossum." Now known scientifically as *Ruscus hypoglossum*. The name is derived from the Greek *hippos* (horse) and *glossa* (tongue). It is famous for its "tongue-on-tongue" appearance, where a smaller leaf-like structure grows out of the center of a larger one. These are actually <term>cladodes</term>—flattened stems that function like leaves—with the tiny flowers blooming right in the middle.</note>

<column-break/>

->III<-
->**Another Mountain Sanicle**<-
<note>original: "Sanicula montana altera." A species of alpine plant, possibly belonging to the *Cortusa* or *Soldanella* genus. The name "Sanicula" comes from the Latin *sanare* (to heal), as these plants were traditionally used in poultices to "solder" or heal wounds. The term "altera" simply means "the second" or "another," distinguishing it from the primary species shown elsewhere in the volume.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three distinct botanical specimens: a double-flowered English Cowslip, the structurally unique Horse-tongue, and a variety of Mountain Sanicle. The engravings focus on the specific morphological curiosities of each, such as the unusual "leaf-on-leaf" growth of the Hippoglossum.</summary>

<keywords>English Cowslip, Horse-tongue, Mountain Sanicle, Primula veris, Ruscus hypoglossum, Sanicula montana, botanical illustration, double flowers, cladodes, medicinal plants</keywords>

-> II. <-
-> ***Narrow-leaved Helleborine*** <-
<note>original: "Epipactis angustifolijs." This specimen is a member of the orchid family. The name "angustifolijs" is Latin for "narrow-leaved," describing the long, blade-like foliage seen wrapping around the stem. In early botanical works, many orchids were classified under the broad genus *Epipactis*.</note>

-> I. <-
-> **Burning Bush** <-
<note>original: "Fraxinella." Today known scientifically as *Dictamnus albus* or "False Dittany." The name "Fraxinella" (meaning "little ash") was given because its pinnate leaves closely resemble those of the Ash tree (*Fraxinus*). It is famously known as the "Burning Bush" because it produces a volatile essential oil that can ignite in hot, still weather.</note>

-> III. <-
-> ***Lily-flowered Asphodel*** <-
<note>original: "Asphodelus Liliaceus." Most likely the White Asphodel (*Asphodelus albus*). Early botanists used the descriptor "Liliaceus" to note the resemblance of its blossoms to lilies. The illustration highlights its unique tuberous root system, which was often compared to a bundle of acorns or fingers.</note>

->II.<-
->**Lily of the Valley**<-
->with a white flower.<-
<note>original: "Lilium Convallium flore: albo." This beloved woodland plant is famous for its fragrance. The Latin name *Lilium convallium* literally means "Lily of the valleys," a name famously appearing in the biblical Song of Solomon. Though called a lily, it is actually part of the asparagus family.</note>

---

->I.<-
->**Paradise Lily,**<-
->the larger variety.<-
<note>original: "Phalangium Alobrogi, cum maius." Known today as *Paradisea liliastrum* or St. Bruno's Lily. The name "Phalangium" refers to an ancient belief that the plant could cure the bite of a specific venomous spider (the *phalangium*). "Alobrogi" links the plant to the Allobroges, a Gallic people who lived in the Alpine regions where this flower grows wild.</note>

---

->III.<-
->**Lily of the Valley**<-
->with a flesh-colored flower.<-
<note>original: "Lilium Convallium flore: incarnato." This is a variety of the common Lily of the Valley with pale pink or "flesh-colored" (incarnato) blossoms. These varieties were highly sought after by 17th-century horticulturalists for their rarity compared to the standard white form.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the Paradise Lily (St. Bruno's Lily) alongside two varieties of Lily of the Valley—one with standard white flowers and one with rare pink or "flesh-colored" flowers. The engravings emphasize the entire plant structure, from the bell-shaped blooms to the dense, fibrous root systems.</summary>

<keywords>Lily of the Valley, Paradise Lily, St. Bruno's Lily, Convallaria majalis, Paradisea liliastrum, Phalangium, alpine flora, botanical illustration, seventeenth-century gardening</keywords>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical catalog from page 131. While the previous page focused on lilies of the valley and alpine lilies, plate 132 transitions to a broader variety of perennials, specifically examining the structural differences between a stately asphodel, a tuberous orchid, and the delicate, feathery foliage of a fumitory.</meta>

->II.<-
->***Purple Dog-Orchid***<-
<note>original: "Cynorchis flore purpureo." The name *Cynorchis* is derived from the Greek *kyon* (dog) and *orchis* (testicle), referring to the characteristic shape of the plant's twin root tubers, which are prominently featured in the illustration. It is likely a species of the genus *Orchis*.</note>

---

->I.<-
->**Large White Asphodel**<-
<note>original: "Asphodelus maior albus." In classical mythology, the Asphodel was the flower of the underworld, said to cover the meadows where the souls of the departed wandered. To a 17th-century botanist, it was a hardy and impressive garden perennial valued for its tall, branching spikes of white, star-like flowers.</note>

---

->III.<-
->***Fine-Leaved Red Fumitory***<-
<note>original: "Fumaria rubra tenuifolia." The name *Fumaria* comes from the Latin *fumus* (smoke). This etymology stems from the plant’s hazy, delicate foliage or the ancient belief that the plant was produced by vapors arising from the earth. The "fine-leaved" description refers to its lace-like, divided leaves.</note>

<summary>This page presents a detailed botanical engraving of three distinct plants: the Large White Asphodel, a Purple Dog-Orchid, and the Fine-Leaved Red Fumitory. The illustration highlights the diverse root systems—tuberous, fleshy, and fibrous—that were of primary interest to early modern naturalists.</summary>

<keywords>Large White Asphodel, Purple Dog-Orchid, Fine-Leaved Red Fumitory, Asphodelus albus, Orchis, Fumaria, root morphology, seventeenth-century botany, medicinal plants</keywords>

--- Page 144 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical survey from page 132. While the previous page examined the structural variety of asphodels, orchids, and fumitory, page 133 focuses on two larger flowering specimens: a robust medicinal groundsel and a species of lily traditionally distinguished by its "wild" or "foolish" nature.</meta>

->II.<-
->**Doria’s Groundsel**<-
<note>original: "Dorea Narbonensium." This plant is now scientifically known as *Senecio doria*. The name "Dorea" is believed to honor the Doria family of Genoa, while "Narbonensium" indicates its prevalence in the Narbonne region of southern France. In the 17th century, it was highly regarded as a "vulnerary" herb, meaning it was used to heal wounds and stop bleeding.</note>

---

->I.<-
->**The Fool’s Lily**<-
<note>original: "Lilium fatuum." The Latin *fatuus* means "foolish," "simple," or "tasteless." Early botanists often applied this descriptor to plants that looked like a more famous flower (in this case, the white Madonna Lily) but lacked its prized fragrance or traditional purity. This illustration likely depicts a Daylily (*Hemerocallis*) or a Turk's Cap Lily (*Lilium martagon*), which were considered "wilder" and less noble than the true garden lilies.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates two distinct tall perennials: Doria’s Groundsel, a medicinal plant from southern France, and the "Fool's Lily," a wilder relative of the common garden lily. The engraving highlights the contrast between the airy, star-shaped flower clusters of the groundsel and the heavy, drooping trumpet flowers of the lily.</summary>

<keywords>Doria’s Groundsel, Senecio doria, Fool's Lily, Lilium fatuum, Hemerocallis, Narbonne, medicinal herbs, 17th-century botany, floral morphology</keywords>

<meta>Page 134 continues the botanical catalog from page 133. While the previous page examined the medicinal groundsel and the "wild" lily, this page transitions to the Asparagus, specifically distinguishing between the mature, seeding plant and the young, edible shoots.</meta>

->I.<-
# Garden Asparagus
<note>original: "Asparagus domesticus." This is the common garden asparagus (*Asparagus officinalis*), which has been cultivated for food since antiquity. The illustration depicts the mature state of the plant, showing the <term>rhizome</term><note>A horizontal underground stem that produces the roots and shoots</note> and the feathery foliage that develops if the spears are not harvested. The small round berries shown among the leaves are the fruit, which turn red when ripe.</note>

->II.<-
# The Asparagus Spear
<note>original: "Thyrsus Asparagi." In botanical Latin, a *thyrsus* refers to a thick stalk or a compact flower cluster. Here, it describes the young, scaly shoots as they first emerge from the earth. These are the "spears" traditionally harvested for eating. The illustration specifically highlights the diversity of these shoots, including one exceptionally wide, flattened spear on the left—a condition known as <term>fasciation</term><note>A structural abnormality in plants where the growing tip becomes wide and ribbon-like rather than round</note>, which was often recorded in early botanical texts as a natural wonder.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the garden asparagus, detailing both the mature plant with its root system and berries, and the young spears harvested for consumption. The engraving specifically captures botanical variations, including a fasciated or "twin" spear, reflecting the 17th-century interest in both agriculture and natural curiosities.</summary>

<keywords>Garden Asparagus, Asparagus officinalis, Thyrsus, botanical illustration, rhizome, fasciation, edible plants, 17th-century botany</keywords>

## OF THE PLANTS
# OF THE GARDEN OF EICHSTÄTT

->*The Summer Section*<-
<note>original: "Classis Æstiva." The Hortus Eystettensis is famously organized by the seasons in which the plants bloom.</note>

->I.<-
# Garden Asparagus
<note>original: "Asparagus domesticus." This is the common garden asparagus (*Asparagus officinalis*), which has been cultivated for food since antiquity. The small round berries shown among the leaves are the fruit, which turn red when ripe.</note>

->II.<-
# The Asparagus Spear
<note>original: "Thyrsus Asparagi." In botanical Latin, a *thyrsus* refers to a thick stalk or a compact flower cluster. Here, it describes the young, scaly shoots as they first emerge from the earth. These are the "spears" traditionally harvested for eating. The illustration specifically highlights the diversity of these shoots, including one exceptionally wide, flattened spear on the left—a condition known as <term>fasciation</term><note>A structural abnormality in plants where the growing tip becomes wide and ribbon-like rather than round</note>, which was often recorded in early botanical texts as a natural wonder.</note>

<meta>This page serves as the index for the "Summer" section of the Hortus Eystettensis. It continues from the previous descriptions of spring and early summer vegetables (like asparagus) into the broader classification of flowering plants, shrubs, and citrus trees that bloom or bear fruit during the height of summer. The index is organized by folio (page) number, listing the Latin botanical name alongside the common German names used in the early 17th century.</meta>

# SECTION,
# THE SUMMER PLANTS.
<note>original: "CLASSIS, AESTIVALIS." This marks the beginning of the third part of the florilegium, dedicated to summer-blooming species.</note>

### Folio 1.
1. Tree of Life. <note>original: "Arbor vitæ. Baum deß Lebens." Likely *Thuja occidentalis*.</note>
2. Apothecary’s Self-heal. <note>original: "Prunella officinarum. Braunellen."</note>
3. Greater Ground Pine. <note>original: "Chamæpitis Maior. Je lenger je lieber/ Erdpim/ Erdkieffer."</note>

### Folio 2.
1. False Sycamore. <note>original: "Pseudocycomorus. Weiß Brustbeerlein / oder wilder Maulbeerbaum." Likely the White Jujube or a related species.</note>
2. Yarrow with red flowers. <note>original: "Millefolium flore rubro. Roth Garben/ oder Schafgarben mit rothen Blumen."</note>
3. Yarrow with white flowers. <note>original: "Millefolium flore albo. Schafgarben / mit weissen Blumen."</note>

### Folio 3.
1. Cypress tree. <note>original: "Cupressus arbor. Cypressenbaum."</note>
2. Spanish Broom. <note>original: "Spartium Hispanicum. Wolriechende Spanische Pfriemenstauden / auch Spanische Khunschroten."</note>

### Folio 4.
1. Oleander or Rosebay with white flowers. <note>original: "Nerium ſeu Rhododendron flore albo."</note>
2. Large green Indian Basil, with protruding leaves. <note>original: "Ocimum Indicum viride maius, folijs protuberantibus."</note>
3. Small narrow-leaved Basil with white flowers. <note>original: "Ocimum minus angustifolium flore albo."</note>

### Folio 5.
1. Oleander with red flowers. <note>original: "Nerion flore rubro. Oleander mit rothen Blumen/ Unholdenkraut."</note>
2. Fruit of the Oleander. <note>original: "Fructus Nerij."</note>

### Folio 6.
1. So-called dwarf Oranges. <note>original: "Poma Aurantia nana dicta. Kleine viltragende Zwergpomeranzen."</note>
2. Citrons, or more commonly Lemons. <note>original: "Mala medica, ſeu Citria. Gemeine Citronen/ oder vil mehr Limonien."</note>
3. Bitter Oranges. <note>original: "Poma Aurantia. Pomeranzen."</note>

### Folio 7.
1. Pomegranate tree with single flowers. <note>original: "Malus punica flore ſimplici."</note>
2. Rose Plantain. <note>original: "Plantago Roſea. Rosenwegrich."</note>
3. Spiked Rose Plantain. <note>original: "Plantago Roſea ſpicata. Geſpitzter Rosenwegrich."</note>

### Folio 8.
1. Wild Pomegranate, with full flowers. <note>original: "Balauſtia. Wild Granatbaum / mit vollen Blumen."</note>
2. Round Myrobalan Plum. <note>original: "Prunus Myrobalanus rotundus. Runde Myrobalanen."</note>
3. Apricots. <note>original: "Mala Armeniaca. Groß Marillen / oder Johanns Pfirschen."</note>

### Folio 9.
1. Rock-rose with white flowers. <note>original: "Cistus flore albo. Weisse Ciſtrößlein."</note>
2. Roman wild Pomegranate blossom with smaller flowers. <note>original: "Balauſtium flore minori Romanum."</note>
3. Smoke Tree. <note>original: "Cotinus. Gelb Preßilgenholz."</note>

### Folio 10.
1. Syrian Mallow. <note>original: "Alcea Syriaca. Roßinrothe Syrische Baumpappel." Now known as Rose of Sharon.</note>
2. Stinking Mayweed. <note>original: "Cotula Fœtida. Hundsdill/ Kröttendill."</note>
3. Creeping Sea Violet. <note>original: "Viola Marina repens. Klein röthlicht Meerwiolen."</note>

1. Syrian Mallow with snow-white flowers. <note>original: "Alcea Syriaca flore candido. Syrisch Baumpappel / mit weissen Blumen."</note>

3. White double-flowered Larkspur. <note>original: "Consolida regalis flore pleno albo. Weisse volle Rittersporn."</note> White double Larkspur.

### Folio 43.

1. Field Larkspur with variegated flowers. <note>original: "Consolida regalis Arvensis flore variegato. Gescheckte Aecker / oder wilde Rittersporn."</note> Dappled field or wild Larkspur.
2. Larkspur with multiplied silver flowers. <note>original: "Consolida regalis multiplici flore argenteo. Volle silberfarbe Rittersporn."</note> Double silver-colored Larkspur.
3. Larkspur with multiplied ash-colored flowers. <note>original: "Consolida regalis flore multiplici cinereo. Saffaschenfarbe volle Rittersporn."</note> Saffian-colored <note>Saffian refers to a dyed morocco leather, often a brownish-red or orange-tinted ash color.</note> double Larkspur.

### Folio 44.

1. Red double-flowered Field Larkspur. <note>original: "Consolida Arvensis flore rubro pleno. Wilde volle rothe Aecker Rittersporn."</note> Wild double red field Larkspur.
2. Blue double-flowered Larkspur. <note>original: "Consolida regalis flore pleno cæruleo. Volle blawe Rittersporn."</note> Double blue Larkspur.
3. Purple double-flowered Larkspur. <note>original: "Consolida regalis flore pleno purpureo. Volle purpurfarbe Rittersporn."</note> Double purple-colored Larkspur.

### Folio 45.

1. Shrubby Stock with green leaves and white scented flowers of Camerarius. <note>original: "Leucoium fruticosum folio viridi, flore albo odorato Camerarij." Joachim Camerarius was a famous 16th-century botanist.</note>

<column-break/>

Shrub-violet with green leaves and white, sweet-smelling flowers.
2. Scentless Wild Stock with small pale flowers. <note>original: "Leucoium Sylvestre inodorum flore paruo pallidiore. Klein wildblachgelb Veil."</note> Small, wild, pale-yellow violet.
3. Melancholic-colored Stock. <note>original: "Leucoium Melancholico colore. Blaichbraun Melancholischer farbe stauden Veil."</note> Pale brown, melancholic-colored shrub-violet.

### Folio 46.

1. Foreign Black Cumin with multiplied flowers. <note>original: "Nigella peregrina flore multiplici. Frembd schwarzer Kümel / mit voller blumen."</note> Foreign black cumin with double flowers.
2. Bittersweet with white flowers. <note>original: "Dulcis amara flore albo. Bittersüß Hirschkraut / mit weissen blumen."</note> Bittersweet nightshade with white flowers.
3. Common Bittersweet with blue flowers. <note>original: "Dulcis amara flore cæruleo vulgatior. Bittersüß Hirschkraut / mit blawichten blumen."</note> Bittersweet nightshade with bluish flowers.

### Folio 47.

1. Musky Imperial Martagon. <note>original: "Martagon Imperiale moschatum. Wolriechende frembde Keyser Lilien / oder Türckischer Bund."</note> Sweet-smelling foreign Imperial Lily, or Turk’s Cap Lily.
2. Small simple-flowered Wild Pink. <note>original: "Caryophyllus minor sylvestris flore simplici. Wilde feldneglein / oder rothe donnerneglein."</note> Wild field carnation, or red "thunder-pink."
3. Small double-flowered Wild Pink. <note>original: "Caryophyllus minor sylvestris flore pleno. Doppelte rothe Feldneglein."</note> Double red field carnation.

### Folio 48.

Largest many-flowered mountain Lily, white with scattered red spots. <note>original: "Lilium montanum maximum polyanthos album rubris maculis aspersis. Grosse viltragende weisse Goldtwurz / mit rothen gesprengten düpfeln."</note> Great many-bloomed white Martagon with red sprinkled dots.

### Folio 49.

Largest many-flowered variegated mountain Lily. <note>original: "Lilium montanum maximum polyanthos variegatum. Grosse viltragende Goldtwurz / aussen roth / inwendig weiß mit rothen düpfeln."</note> Great many-bloomed Martagon, red on the outside, white on the inside with red dots.

### Folio 50.

1. Early red mountain Lily. <note>original: "Lilium montanum rubrum præcox. Früe rothe türckische Bundlilien mit schmalen Blätern."</note> Early red Turk’s Cap Lily with narrow leaves.
2. Pale-colored Bulbous Iris. <note>original: "Iris Bulbosa pallido colore. Bleichgelbe Violwurz mit Zwiebelwurzeln."</note> Pale yellow violet-root with bulbous roots.
3. First narrow-leaved Iris of Clusius. <note>original: "Iris folijs angustis prima Clusij. Schön Blaw schmalblättericht Veilwurz des Clusij."</note> Beautiful blue narrow-leaved violet-root of Clusius. <note>Charles de l'Écluse (Clusius) was a preeminent 16th-century botanist.</note>

### Folio 51.

1. Many-flowered Chalcedonian Daylily. <note>original: "Hemerocallis Calcedonica polyanthos. Zinnaberroth viltragende Heydnische Lilien / oder türckischer Bund."</note> Cinnabar-red many-bloomed Heathen Lily, or Turk’s Cap.
2. The stalk with the bulb. <note>original: "Scapus cum bulbo."</note>
3. Larger narrow-leaved autumn Star Hyacinth. <note>original: "Hyacinthus stellatus autumnalis, angustifolijs maior. Grosser herbst Stern Hyacinth / oder Hyacinth so im Herbst blühet."</note> Great autumn star-hyacinth, or the hyacinth that blooms in autumn.

### Folio 52.

1. Mountain Lily with purplish flowers, not spotted. <note>original: "Lilium montanum flore purpurascente non punctatum. Purpurfarb Goldtwurz mit ungedüpfelten Blumen."</note> Purple-colored Martagon with un-dotted flowers.
2. Mountain Lily with purplish spotted flowers. <note>original: "Lilium montanum flore purpurascente punctatum. Purpurfarb Goldtwurz mit gedüpfelten Blumen."</note> Purple-colored Martagon with dotted flowers.

### Folio 53.

1. Enchanter’s Nightshade. <note>original: "Circæa. S. Stephans kraut."</note> St. Stephen's Herb.
2. Larger white spotted mountain Lily. <note>original: "Lilium montanum maius album punctatum. Goldtwurz mit weissen gesprengten Blumen."</note> Martagon with white sprinkled flowers.
3. Smaller white mountain Lily, not spotted. <note>original: "Lilium montanum minus album non punctatum. Weisse Goldtwurz ohne punct."</note> White Martagon without dots.

### Folio 54.

1. Pomponian Martagon. <note>original: "Martagon pomponeum. Macedonische Goldtwurz mit Minienfarben Blumen."</note> Macedonian Martagon with minium-colored <note>a bright orange-red</note> flowers.
2. Upright Hand-Orchid with variegated flowers. <note>original: "Palma Christi Erecta flore variegato. Weisse gescheckte händleinwurz."</note> White dappled "Little-hand root."
3. Upright Hand-Orchid with pure white flowers. <note>original: "Palma Christi erecta flore candido. Weiß Händleinwurz."</note> White Hand-root.

### Folio 55.

1. Smaller Spanish Iris. <note>original: "Sifyrinchium minus. Spanische Sigwurz mit violblawen Schwerdblumen."</note> Spanish "Victory-root" with violet-blue sword-flowers.
2. Mountain Lily with yellow flowers, not spotted. <note>original: "Lilium montanum flore luteo non punctatum. Gelbe Türckische Bundlilien ohne Düpfelen."</note> Yellow Turk’s Cap Lily without dots.
3. Yellow spotted mountain Lily. <note>original: "Lilium montanum flore luteo punctatum."</note>

<column-break/>

Yellow Turk’s Cap Lilies, with dotted flowers.

### Folio 56.

1. Bulbous Iris with yellow flowers. <note>original: "Iris bulbosa flore luteo. Gelbe Schwerttelilien mit Zwiebelwurz."</note> Yellow sword-lilies with bulbous roots.
2. Late Byzantine Lily. <note>original: "Lilium Byzantinum serotinum. Türckische Constantinopolitanische Ziegelfarbe Bundlilien."</note> Turkish Constantinople brick-colored Turk's Cap Lily.
3. Spring mountain Crocus. <note>original: "Crocus montanus vernalis. Berg Saffran."</note> Mountain saffron.
4. Cultivated Saffron. <note>original: "Crocus Satinus."</note> True saffron.

### Folio 57.

Greater Dragon Arum. <note>original: "Dracontium maius. Drachenwurz / oder Schlangenkraut."</note> Dragon-root or Snake-herb.

### Folio 58.

1. Lion’s Leaf. <note>original: "Leontopetalon. Löwen tappen."</note> Lion's paws.
2. Alpine Mountain Germander with yellow flowers. <note>original: "Polium Alpinum flore luteo. Steinkräutlein."</note> Little stone-herb.
3. Small blue Daisy. <note>original: "Bellis minor flore cæruleo. Blawe Margen Rößlein / oder Ruckerzu."</note> Blue morning-rose, or "Ruckerzu."

### Folio 59.

1. Round Birthwort. <note>original: "Aristolochia rotunda. Frembde runde Holwurz / oder Osterluey."</note> Foreign round hollow-root, or Birthwort.
2. Common long Birthwort. <note>original: "Aristolochia longa vulgaris. Osterluey."</note> Birthwort.

### Folio 60.

1. Broad-leaved Orchid. <note>original: "Orchis latifolia. Groß Stendelwurz."</note> Large orchid.
2. Female Dog-Orchid. <note>original: "Cynosorchis fœmina. Braunroth Knabenkraut."</note> Brown-red "Boy's-herb."
3. Bird’s-nest Orchid. <note>original: "Nidus auis. Vogelnest."</note>
4. Coral-rooted Toothwort. <note>original: "Dentaria radice coralloide. Klein kaal Zahnkraut."</note> Small bare tooth-herb.

### Folio 61.

1. Broad-leaved Helleborine. <note>original: "Epipactis latifolia. Breit blätericht Nießkraut."</note> Broad-leaved Sneeze-wort.
2. Variegated upright Hand-Orchid. <note>original: "Palma Christi erecta variegata. Händleinwurz / mit gesprengten Rosinfarben blätern."</note> Hand-root, with sprinkled raisin-colored leaves.
3. Upright Hand-Orchid with flesh-colored flowers. <note>original: "Palma Christi erecta flore incarnato. Händleinwurz / mit Rosinfarben blumen."</note> Hand-root, with raisin-colored flowers.
4. Smaller white-flowered Orchid. <note>original: "Orchis flore albo minor. Kleine breite bläterichte weisse Stendelwurz."</note> Small broad-leaved white orchid.
5. Foreign narrow-leaved Hand-Orchid with red flowers. <note>original: "Palma Christi peregrina flore rubro. Frembd Schmalblättericht Händleinwurz / mit ganz rothen Blümlein."</note> Foreign narrow-leaved hand-root with entirely red little flowers.
6. Creeping Orchid. <note>original: "Orchis repens. Kleine Kriechende weisse Stendelwurz."</note> Small creeping white orchid.

### Folio 62.

1. Second Orchid of Serapias according to Dodoens. <note>original: "Orchis Serapias secunda Dodonæi. Stendelwurz mit Blumen / so wie ein grüner Papagey / einen braunlichten Schild haltent / formiret." Rembert Dodoens was a Flemish physician and botanist.</note> Orchid with flowers formed like a green parrot holding a brownish shield.
2. Mixed yellow bulbous Iris. <note>original: "Iris bulbosa lutea mixta. Zwibel Lilien mit nidergebognen goltgelben blätern / vnnd den andern auffrichten Bleichgelb."</note> Bulbous lily with downward-curved gold-yellow leaves and others standing upright in pale yellow.
3. Mixed violet-colored bulbous Iris. <note>original: "Iris bulbosa violaceo colore mixta. Veilbraun zwibel Lilien / mit goltgelben Härlein."</note> Violet-brown bulbous lily with gold-yellow little hairs.

### Folio 63.

1. English Bulbous Iris with white flowers. <note>original: "Iris bulbosa Anglicana. Candido flore. Englische Lilien / mit schneeweissen Blumen / und Zwibelwurzeln."</note> English Lily with snow-white flowers and bulbous roots.
2. Narrow-leaved Solomon’s Seal. <note>original: "Polygonatum angustis folijs. Weißwurz mit schmalen blätern."</note> White-root with narrow leaves.

### Folio 64.

1. Greater English Iris with violet flowers. <note>original: "Iris Anglicana maior Violaceo flore. Englischer ganz veilbraun poetischer Hyacinth."</note> English entirely violet-brown "poetic hyacinth."
2. Scented narrow-leaved bulbous Iris. <note>original: "Iris bulbosa angustis folijs odorata. Liechtblawer poetischer Hyacinth."</note> Light blue poetic hyacinth.
3. Blue narrow-leaved bulbous Iris. <note>original: "Iris bulbosa angustis folijs cærulea. Liechtblawer gesprengter poetischer Hyacinth."</note> Light blue sprinkled poetic hyacinth.

### Folio 65.

1. English bulbous Iris with blue flowers. <note>original: "Iris bulbosa Anglicana flore cæruleo. Englische Lilien / mit ganz himmelblawen Blumen / vnd Zwibelwurzeln."</note> English Lily with entirely sky-blue flowers and bulbous roots.
2. White-flowered narrow-leaved bulbous Iris. <note>original: "Iris bulbosa angustis folijs flore albo. Gelbe Englische Lilien / mit purpurfarben straifflein / vnd Zwibelwurzeln."</note> Yellow English Lily <note>Note: The Latin says "white" (albo) but the German says "yellow" (gelbe); this likely refers to a cream or bicolored variety.</note> with purple-colored small stripes and bulbous roots.

<meta>catchword: 2. Iris</meta>

<summary>This page lists the summer index for Folios 36 through 65, cataloging a diverse range of ornamental and medicinal plants including Columbines, Larkspurs, Martagon Lilies, and various terrestrial Orchids. It highlights the early modern focus on flower form (single vs. double) and specific color variations like "melancholic" brown or "raisin" purple.</summary>
<keywords>Columbine (Aquilegia), Larkspur (Consolida regalis), Black Cumin (Nigella), Martagon Lily, Bulbous Iris, Orchid (Palma Christi), Saffron (Crocus), Birthwort (Aristolochia), 17th-century botany, plant taxonomy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the botanical index of the Hortus Eystettensis, moving through the summer-flowering plants. It covers a transition from bulbous Irises and Gladioli into larger sun-loving plants like Sunflowers and Marigolds, followed by a variety of Mallows, medicinal herbs like Mints and Balms, and finishing with aromatic Basils. The previous page ended with various colors of Columbines.</meta>

### Folio 66.

1. Bulbous Iris with pale blue flowers. <note>original: "Iris bulboſa flore diluto cæruleo. Purpurblaw Engliſche Lilien / mit Zwiebelwurzeln."</note> Purple-blue English lilies with bulbous roots.

### Folio 67.

2. Variegated bulbous Iris. <note>original: "Iris bulboſa Variegata. Veilfarbichter poetiſcher Hyacinth."</note> A violet-colored poetic hyacinth <note>likely an iris species formerly classified with hyacinths</note>.

### Folio 68.

1. Italian Sword-lily with red flowers. <note>original: "Gladiolus Italicus flore rubro. Roth welſch Schwert Lilien."</note> Red Italian gladiolus.

### Folio 69.

Great Sun-flower. <note>original: "Flos Solis maior. Groß Indianiſch Sonnenblumen."</note> Large Indian sunflower.

### Folio 70.

Prolific Sun-flower. <note>original: "Flos Solis prolifer. Indianiſch vieltragend Sonnenblumen / mit nebenblumen."</note> Multi-flowered Indian sunflower with side-blooms.

### Folio 71.

1. Prolific Marigold. <note>original: "Calendula prolifera. Volle Ringelblumen / mit neben Blümlein."</note> Double marigolds with small secondary flowers.
2. Yellow Marigold with double flowers. <note>original: "Calendula lutea flore pleno. Volle gelbe Ringelblumen."</note> Full, double yellow marigolds.
3. Yellow Marigold with a reddish center. <note>original: "Calendula lutea medio rufſa. Ringelblumen / mit einem rothgelben grund / oder Bußen."</note> Marigolds with
->a reddish-yellow base, or heart.<-

### Folio 72.

1. Scentless Tansy with flowers like the great Daisy. <note>original: "Tanacetum inodorum flore Bellidis maioris. Reinfahren ohne geruch."</note> Odorless tansy.
2. Succory-like plant called Blue Gum-succory with full flowers. <note>original: "Chondrilla Seſamoides dicta, cærulea flore completo. Frembd Dunckelblaw Wegwartenart."</note> A foreign, dark-blue species of chicory or wild succory.
3. Marigold with simple flowers. <note>original: "Calendula flore ſimplici. Bleichgelb Ringelblumen."</note> Pale yellow single-layered marigolds.

### Folio 73.

1. Do not touch me. <note>original: "Noli me tangere. Springſamenkraut / oder / Rühr mich nit an."</note> Touch-me-not <note>Impatiens</note> or jumping-seed herb.
2. Common Tansy. <note>original: "Tanacetum vulgare. Reinfahren."</note> Common tansy.
3. English Crested Tansy. <note>original: "Tanacetum Criſtatum Anglicanum. Krauß Engliſch Reinfahren."</note> Curled English tansy.

### Folio 74.

1. Great Daisy. <note>original: "Bellis maior. Gänßblumen."</note> Ox-eye daisy.
2. Yellow Cretan Chrysanthemum. <note>original: "Chryſanthemum Creticum luteum. Goldblumen."</note> Golden flowers from Crete.
3. Mixed Cretan Chrysanthemum. <note>original: "Chryſanthemum Creticum mixtum. Cretifche halbichte gelb vnd weiſs Goldblumen."</note> Cretan golden flowers, half yellow and half white.

### Folio 75.

1. Feverfew with simple flowers. <note>original: "Matricaria flore ſimplici. Einfach Mertram / oder Mutterkraut."</note> Simple feverfew, or mother-wort.
2. Feverfew with double flowers. <note>original: "Matricaria flore pleno. Gefüllt weiſs Mertram."</note> Filled white feverfew.

### Folio 76.

1. Everlasting. <note>original: "Ageratum. Frembd gelb Leberkraut / Leberbalſam."</note> Foreign yellow liverwort or sweet-maudlin.

### 82.

### Folio 84.

Common Marsh-mallow. <note>original: "Athæa vulgaris. Eibiſch / Ibiſch / oder Heiltraut."</note> Marsh-mallow, or healing-herb.
2. Spanish Shrubby Marsh-mallow. <note>original: "Athæa fruticans Hiſpanica. Spaniſcher Baums Eibiſch."</note> Spanish tree marsh-mallow.

### 85.

1. Thuringian Marsh-mallow. <note>original: "Athæa Thuringiaca. Leibfarber Thüringiſcher Eibiſch."</note> Flesh-colored Thuringian marsh-mallow.
2. Ivy-leaved Navelwort with blue flowers. <note>original: "Cymbalaria folijs hederaceis flore cæruleo. Kriechend Nabelkraut."</note> Creeping navelwort <note>Ivy-leaved toadflax</note>.

### Folio 86.

Tree Mallow. <note>original: "Malua Arboreſcens. Frembde groſſe Baumppeln."</note> Large foreign tree-mallows.

### Folio 87.

1. Double Rose Mallow with flesh-colored flowers. <note>original: "Malua Roſea multiplex flore incarnato. Ehren-Roſen / mit doppelt Leibfarben Blumen."</note> Roses of honor <note>Hollyhocks</note> with double flesh-colored flowers.
2. Double White Rose Mallow. <note>original: "Malua Roſea multiplex flore albo. Groſſe weiſſe doppelte Herbſt-Roſen. Groſſe weiſſe volle Pappel-Roſen."</note> Large white double autumn roses. Large white full mallow-roses.

### Folio 88.

1. Simple White Garden Mallow. <note>original: "Malua hortenſis flore ſimplici albo. Einfache weiſſe Herbſt-Pappel-Roſen."</note> Simple white autumn mallow-roses.
2. Simple Flesh-colored Garden Mallow. <note>original: "Malua hortenſis flore ſimplici incarnato. Einfache leibfarb Pappel-Roſen."</note> Simple flesh-colored mallow-roses.
3. Simple Red Garden Mallow. <note>original: "Malua hortenſis flore ſimplici rubro. Einfache rothe Pappelroſen."</note> Simple red mallow-roses.
4. Garden Mallow with dark reddish flowers. <note>original: "Malua hortenſis flore atro rubente. Volle ſchwartzrothe Pappelroen."</note> Full black-red mallow-roses.

<column-break/>

5. Garden Mallow with full red flowers. <note>original: "Malua hortenſis flore pleno rubro. Volle rothe Pappelroſen."</note> Full red mallow-roses.

### 89.

1. Curled Mallow. <note>original: "Malua Criſpa. Krauſe Pappeln."</note> Curled mallows.
2. False Dittany with whorled flowers. <note>original: "Pſeudodictamus floribus vertillatis. Wilder Cretifcher Diptam."</note> Wild Cretan dittany.
3. Narrow-leaved Cretan Horehound. <note>original: "Marrubium Creticum anguſtifolium. Spisblätterichter Cretifcher Andron."</note> Pointed-leaved Cretan horehound.

### 90.

1. Avicenna's Mallow. <note>original: "Abutilon Auicennæ. Welſche gelbe Pappeln."</note> Foreign yellow mallows <note>Velvetleaf</note>.
2. Foreign Sweet-blushing Everlasting Pea. <note>original: "Lathyrus peregrinus ſuauiter erubeſcens. Frembde breite Erbeß / mit röthlichten Blumen."</note> Foreign broad pea with reddish flowers.
3. Foreign Everlasting Pea with purple-blue flowers. <note>original: "Lathyrus peregrinus ē cæruleo purpuraſcente flore. Frembde breite Erbs mit purpurblawen Blumen."</note> Foreign broad pea with purple-blue flowers.

### Folio 91.

1. Fragrant Thorny Molucca. <note>original: "Moluca odorata ſpinoſa. Wolriechende Conſtantinopolitaniſche Meliſſen."</note> Sweet-smelling balm from Constantinople <note>Bells-of-Ireland</note>.
2. Apothecary's Balm. <note>original: "Meliſſa officinarum. Mutterkraut / oder Meliſſen."</note> Mother-wort, or lemon balm.
3. Moldavian Balm with blue flowers. <note>original: "Meliſſa Moldauica flore cæruleo. Blawe Türckiſche Meliſſen."</note> Blue Turkish balm.

### Folio 92.

1. Smooth Molucca. <note>original: "Molucca Leuis. Conſtantinopolitaniſche Meliſſen / ſo nit wolriechend iſt."</note> Constantinople balm which is not sweet-smelling.
2. Moldavian Balm with white flowers. <note>original: "Meliſſa Moldauica flore albo. Weiſs Türckiſche Meliſſen."</note> White Turkish balm.

### Folio 93.

1. Snowy English Wild-mint. <note>original: "Menthaſtrum niueum Anglicum. Geſcheckte Engliſche Münz."</note> Spotted English mint.
2. Wild Wood-mint. <note>original: "Menthaſtrum Sylueſtre. Wilde Münz / oder Roßmünz."</note> Wild mint or horse-mint.
3. Apothecary's Balsam-mint. <note>original: "Balſamita officinarum. Hertzmünz / Hertzentroſt."</note> Heart-mint, heart's ease <note>Costmary</note>.

### Folio 94.

1. Lesser Catmint. <note>original: "Nepetella. Kleine Nept."</note> Small catnip.
2. German Catmint. <note>original: "Nepeta Germanica. Frembd Katzenkraut / frembde Nept."</note> Foreign cat-herb, foreign catnip.
3. Cat Mint. <note>original: "Mentha Felina. Katzenmünz / Nept."</note> Catmint or catnip.

### Folio 95.

1. Curled Mint. <note>original: "Mentha Criſpa. Krauſe Münz / Auch Deyment."</note> Curled mint, also called spearmint.
2. Roman Nettle. <note>original: "Vrtica Romana. Welſch oder Römiſch Neſſel."</note> Foreign or Roman nettle.
3. Spiked Water-pennyroyal. <note>original: "Pulegium aquaticum ſpicatum. Wildmünz / Roßmünz."</note> Wild mint or horse-mint.

### Folio 96.

1. Common Horehound. <note>original: "Marrubium vulgare. Weiſſer Andron."</note> White horehound.
2. Scentless Narrow-leaved Cretan Horehound. <note>original: "Marrubium Creticum anguſtis folijs inodorum. Cretifcher Andron / mit kurtzen Blättern."</note> Cretan horehound with short leaves.
3. Saracen Mint. <note>original: "Mentha Sarracenica. Vnſer Frawen Münz."</note> Our Lady's mint <note>Costmary</note>.

### Folio 97.

1. Excellent Mountain Calamint. <note>original: "Calamintha montana præſtantior. Groſſe rechte Katzenmünz."</note> Great true catmint.
2. Common Mountain Calamint. <note>original: "Calamintha montana vulgaris. Gemein Katzenmünz."</note> Common catmint.

### Folio 98.

1. Syrian Sea-lavender. <note>original: "Limonium Syriacum. Syriſches Limonium."</note> Syrian sea-lavender.
2. Narrow-leaved Calamint. <note>original: "Calamentha anguſtis folijs. Wilder Poley."</note> Wild pennyroyal.
3. Buck's-horn Plantain. <note>original: "Coronopus. Kraenfuß / Hirſchhornſalat."</note> Crow's foot, or stag-horn salad.

### Folio 99.

1. Spotted Indian Basil. <note>original: "Baſilicum Indicum maculatum. Indianiſch roth Baſilien."</note> Indian red basil.
2. Middle Basil. <note>original: "Baſilicum Medium. Gemeine mittel Baſilien."</note> Common medium basil.

### Folio 100.

1. Great Broad-leaved Basil. <note>original: "Ocimum latifolium magnum. Ganz grüen breitblätterichter Baſilien."</note> Entirely green broad-leaved basil.
2. Green Curled Basil. <note>original: "Ocimum criſpum viride. Krauſe Baſilien."</note> Curled basil.
3. Smallest Clove-scented Basil. <note>original: "Ocimum minimum caryophyllatum. Klein wolriechend Negel Baſilien."</note> Small sweet-smelling clove-basil.

<meta>Catchword: Folio 101.</meta>

<summary>This index page lists a diverse array of summer plants from Folios 66 to 100, including Irises, Gladioli, Sunflowers, Marigolds, Mallows, and various aromatic or medicinal herbs like Mint and Basil. It catalogs many varieties, distinguishing them by color, leaf shape, and geographical origin (e.g., Cretan, Spanish, English).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the detailed index of the Hortus Eystettensis, specifically focusing on the mid-summer plants and medicinal herbs. It follows the previous sections on bulbous flowers like Irises and Gladioli, now moving into the broad category of "Summer-blooming herbs" which includes many members of the mint, borage, and campion families.</meta>

### Folio 101.
1. True Germander. <note>original: "Teucrium verum. Groß Barthengel."</note> Large Germander.
2. Cappadocian Pennycress with flesh-colored flowers. <note>original: "Thlaspi Cappadocium flore incarnato. Leibfarb Cretischer Baurenfenfft."</note> Flesh-colored Cretan Farmer’s-mustard.

### Folio 102.
1. Cretan Everlasting. <note>original: "Helichryson Creticum. Cretische Reinblumen."</note> Cretan Everlasting-flowers.

### Folio 103.

3. Small pinnate Sage. <note>original: "Saluia minor pinnata. Klein spritzy oder creuß Saluey / mit weissen Blumen."</note> Small sprigged or cross-leaved Sage with white flowers.

### Folio 104.

### Folio 105.

### Folio 107.

### Folio 109.
1. Greater Comfrey with purple flowers. <note>original: "Symphytum maius flore purpureo. Wallwurtz / oder Schwarzwurtz / mit purpurfarben Blumen."</note> Comfrey <note>term: Wallwurtz—literally "well-root," referring to its use in healing bones</note> or Blackwort with purple-colored flowers.

### Folio 111.

Weiss gefüllter Widerstoß / oder je lenger / je freundlicher."</note> White double-flowered Catchfly, or "the longer the friendlier" <note>An archaic folk name for these flowers, suggesting they become more beautiful with age</note>.

### Folio 141.

1. Cultivated Safflower. <note>original: "Cnicus Sativus. Wilder Saffran."</note> Wild saffron.
2. Roman Chamomile with simple flowers. <note>original: "Chamæmelum Romanum flore simplici. Einfache Römische Camillen."</note> Simple Roman chamomile.
3. Roman Chamomile with multiple flowers. <note>original: "Chamæmelum Romanum flore multiplici. Gefüllte Römische Camillen."</note> Double-petaled Roman chamomile.

### Folio 142.

1. Large Knapweed with yellow flowers. <note>original: "Iacea maior flore luteo. Gelb Flockenblumen."</note> Yellow flake-flowers.
2. Dyer’s Saw-wort. <note>original: "Serratula Tinctoris. Ferberscharten."</note> Dyer’s-notch.
3. Mattioli’s Thistle. <note>original: "Cirsium Matthioli. Frembd Ochsengangen."</note> Foreign ox-tongue.

### 143.

1. Black Knapweed with white flowers. <note>original: "Iacea nigra flore albo. Weiß Flockenblum."</note> White flake-flower.
2. Black Knapweed with purple flowers. <note>original: "Iacea nigra flore purpuræo. Purpurfarb Flockenblumen."</note> Purple-colored flake-flowers.
3. Foreign Safflower with blue flowers. <note>original: "Cricus peregrinus flore cæruleo. Blauer wilder Saffran."</note> Blue wild saffron.

### Folio 144.

Artichoke with flowers. <note>original: "Cinera cum flore. Strobildorn / blühende Artischock."</note> Pine-cone thorn, or blooming artichoke.

### Folio 145.

1. Globe-headed Thistle. <note>original: "Carduus Sphærocephalus. Spehrdistel / Bisemknöpff."</note> Spear-thistle or musk-buttons.
2. Wild Cotton-thistle with white flowers. <note>original: "Acanthium Sylvestre flore albo. Groß weiß Wegdistel."</note> Great white way-thistle.

### Folio 146.

1. Common Black Carline Thistle. <note>original: "Chamæleon niger Vulgaris. Schwarz Eberwurz."</note> Black boar-root.
2. Blessed Thistle. <note>original: "Carduus Benedictus. Cardobenedictenkraut."</note> Blessed-thistle herb.
3. Tabernaemontanus’s Thistle. <note>original: "Heracantha Tabernæmontani. Schön Härtle / Dreydistel."</note> Beautiful little-hard, or triple-thistle.
4. Entirely blue Eryngo. <note>original: "Eryngium totum cæruleum. Frembd blau Berg-Manßtreu / oder Brachdistel."</note> Foreign blue mountain sea-holly, or fallow-thistle.

### Folio 147.

1. Sea Holly. <note>original: "Eryngium marinum. Meer Brachdistel / auch Manßtreudistel."</note> Sea fallow-thistle, also man’s-fidelity thistle.

<column-break/>

2. Rock Rose with yellow flowers. <note>original: "Helianthemum flore luteo. Gelber Heiden Hyßsop / auch Sonnengunst."</note> Yellow pagan hyssop, also sun’s-favor.
3. Rock Rose with white flowers. <note>original: "Helianthemum flore albo. Weiß Heidenhyßsop / oder Sonnengunst."</note> White pagan hyssop, or sun’s-favor.

### Folio 148.

1. Broad-leaved Pannonian Eryngo. <note>original: "Eryngium pannonium latifolium. Blaue Ungerisch Mannstreuwurzel."</note> Blue Hungarian man’s-fidelity root.
2. Hyssop with white flowers. <note>original: "Hyssopus flore albo. Weisser Hyßsop."</note> White hyssop.
3. Hyssop with red flowers. <note>original: "Hyssopus flore rubro. Rother Hyßsop."</note> Red hyssop.
4. Hyssop with blue flowers. <note>original: "Hyssopus flore cæruleo. Blauer Hyßsop."</note> Blue hyssop.

### Folio 149.

1. Squirting Cucumber. <note>original: "Cucumis Asininus. Wilder Eselskürebeß."</note> Wild donkey-gourd.
2. Broad-leaved Shrubby many-flowered Hawkweed. <note>original: "Hieracium fruticescens latifolium polyanthos. Vielblumentragend gelb Habichkraut."</note> Many-flowered yellow hawk-herb.
3. Garland Rosemary. <note>original: "Rosmarinus Coronarius. Garten Roßmarin."</note> Garden rosemary.

### Folio 150.

1. Common Spurge. <note>original: "Cataputia Vulgaris. Treibkörner / Springkörner."</note> Driving-seeds or jumping-seeds.
2. Wild Pellitory. <note>original: "Parietaria Sylvestris. Waldhirschen."</note> Forest-stag.
3. Moneywort. <note>original: "Nummularia. Pfenningkraut."</note> Penny-herb.

### Folio 151.

1. Cultivated Purslane. <note>original: "Portulaca Satiua. Groß Bergelkraut."</note> Great mountain-herb.
2. Domestic Savory. <note>original: "Satureia Domestica. Zwiebel Hyßsop / Saturey / Pfefferkraut / Klosterhyßsop."</note> Onion hyssop, Savory, pepper-herb, or cloister hyssop.
3. True Savory. <note>original: "Thymbra legitima. Römische Saturey."</note> Roman savory.

### Folio 152.

1. Common Wormwood. <note>original: "Absinthium Vulgare. Gemein Wermuth."</note> Common wormwood.
2. Broad-leaved Sea Wormwood. <note>original: "Absinthium latifolium marinum. Breitblätterichter Meerwermuth."</note> Broad-leaved sea-wormwood.
3. White Pontic Wormwood. <note>original: "Absinthium ponticū album. Pontischer Wermuth."</note> Pontic wormwood.

### Folio 153.

1. Prickly Poppy. <note>original: "Papauer spinosum. Höllfeigen."</note> Hell-figs. <note>The term "Höllfeigen" often refers to the prickly seed pods.</note>
2. Foreign Goldilocks. <note>original: "Chrysocome peregrina. Frembde gelbe Rainblumen."</note> Foreign yellow tansy-flowers.
3. Common Sneezewort. <note>original: "Ptarmica vulgaris. Dorant / weiß spitzig Rainfahren / wilder Bertram."</note> Dorant, white pointed tansy, or wild pellitory.

### Folio 154.

1. Yellow Horned Poppy. <note>original: "Papauer corniculatum luteum. Gehörneter gelber Oelmagen."</note> Horned yellow oil-poppy.
2. Large Violet-colored Trinity Flower. <note>original: "Flos Trinitatis maior violaceus. Groß Freysamkraut / hoch veilbraun / Stieffmütterlein."</note> Great heartsease, deep violet-brown, little stepmother <note>A common German name for the pansy.</note>.
3. Large Pale Trinity Flower. <note>original: "Flos Trinitatis pallidus maior. Groß Freysamkraut / mit gelben Blumen."</note> Great heartsease with yellow flowers.

### Folio 155.

1. Red Poppy with double flowers. <note>original: "Papauer flore pleno rubrum. Klapperrosen / Kornrosen."</note> Rattle-roses or corn-roses.
2. Red Wild Poppy. <note>original: "Papauer Erraticum rubrum. Gefüllter rother Magsamen / oder Oelmagen."</note> Double red poppy-seed or oil-poppy.

### Folio 156.

1. Poppy with multiple flesh-colored flowers. <note>original: "Papauer flore multiplici incarnato. Gefüllter fleischfarber Magsamen / oder Oelmage."</note> Double flesh-colored poppy-seed or oil-poppy.
2. Poppy with multiple purplish flowers. <note>original: "Papauer flore multiplici purpurascente. Gefüllter purpurfarber voller Magsamen."</note> Double purple-colored full poppy-seed.

### Folio 157.

1. Cretan Tordylium. <note>original: "Tordilion Creticum. Frembd Tordilium."</note> Foreign hartwort.
2. Fringed Red Poppy with purple bases. <note>original: "Papauer laciniatum rubrum unguibus purpureis. Aufgeschnittener oder Zerkerbter brauner Magsamen."</note> Slit or notched brown poppy-seed.
3. Fringed Red Poppy with white bases. <note>original: "Papauer laciniatum rubrum unguibus albis. Roth aufgeschnitten oder Zerkerbter Magsamen."</note> Red slit or notched poppy-seed.

### Folio 158.

1. Double White Poppy with red edges. <note>original: "Papauer multiplex album oris rubicundis. Gefüllter krauser Magsamen / mit rothen enden."</note> Double ruffled poppy-seed with red tips.
2. Poppy with double vermilion flowers. <note>original: "Papauer flore miniato pleno. Gefüllter Minienfarbichter Magsamen."</note> Double cinnabar-colored poppy-seed.
3. Poppy with double silver-colored flowers. <note>original: "Papauer flore pleno argentei coloris. Gefüllter Silberfarbichter Magsamen / oder Oelmagen."</note> Double silver-colored poppy-seed or oil-poppy.

<column-break/>

### Folio 159.

1. Indian Cress. <note>original: "Nasturtium Indicum. Indianisch Kressen."</note> Indian cress <note>Known today as Nasturtium.</note>.
2. Small double red Wild Daisy. <note>original: "Bellis Sylvestris minor flore pleno rubro. Rothe gefüllte Maßliebenröstlein."</note> Red double little daisy-roses.
3. Small mixed-color Wild Daisy. <note>original: "Bellis Sylvestris minor flore mixto. Gesprengte gefüllte Maßlieben."</note> Speckled double daisies.

### Folio 160.

1. Shielded Sainfoin with sweetly reddening flowers. <note>original: "Hedyfarum clypeatum flore suauiter rubente. Rother Spanischer Hanenkoppf / oder Kamm."</note> Red Spanish cock’s-head or comb.
2. Dodoens’ Sainfoin with red flowers. <note>original: "Onobrychis Dodon: flore rubro. Roth Hanenkoppf / Widhoppfenkraut / Hanenkamm."</note> Red cock’s-head, hoopoe-herb, or cock’s-comb.
3. Oak of Jerusalem. <note>original: "Botrys. Traubenkraut / Mottenkraut / Welsch Eichen Laub."</note> Grapes-herb, moth-herb, or foreign oak leaf.

### Folio 161.

1. Large cultivated Lupine. <note>original: "Lupinus Satiuus maior. Groß Frembd purpurbraw Feigbonen."</note> Large foreign purple-brown fig-beans.
2. Narrow-leaved wild blue Lupine. <note>original: "Lupinus Sylvestris angustifolius flore cæruleo. Wilde blawe Feigbonen / mit schmalen Blättern."</note> Wild blue fig-beans with narrow leaves.
3. Fragrant wild yellow Lupine. <note>original: "Lupinus Sylvestris flore luteo odoratus. Feigbonen mit wolriechenden gelben Blumen Türkische Bell."</note> Fig-beans with sweet-smelling yellow flowers, Turkish bark.

### Folio 162.

1. Exotic White Lupine. <note>original: "Lupinus Exoticus flore albo. Weisse Feigbonen."</note> White fig-beans.
2. Common White Lupine. <note>original: "Lupinus vulgaris flore albo. Weisse Feigbonen."</note> White fig-beans.
3. Camerarius’s Four-angled Winged Pea. <note>original: "Lotus Tetragonolobus Camerarij. Frembder Schottenklee / mit blutrothen Blumē."</note> Foreign Scottish clover with blood-red flowers.

### Folio 163.

1. Common Stonecrop. <note>original: "Sedum vulgare. Groß Haußwurz."</note> Large house-root.
2. Marsh Stonecrop with flesh-colored flowers. <note>original: "Sedum palustre flore incarnato. Kleine stumpffe Haußwurz / mit leibfarbichten Blumen."</note> Small blunt house-root with body-colored flowers.
3. Small biting Stonecrop. <note>original: "Sedum minus causticatum. Maupfeffer / klein Katzenträublein."</note> Wall-pepper or little cat’s-grapes.
4. Small Stonecrop or Caterpillar-plant. <note>original: "Sedum minus, siue Vermicularis. Kleine Haußwurz / oder Katzenträublein."</note> Small house-root or little cat’s-grapes.
5. Earth-nut and False Vetch. <note>original: "Glans terræ & Pseudoapios. Erdnüß / Erckelnüß / Erdfeigen / Knolwurz."</note> Earth-nut, corner-nut, earth-fig, or tuber-root.
6. Large foreign Pea. <note>original: "Pisum magnum peregrinum. Frembde Faßelenerbeiß."</note> Foreign kidney-bean-pea.

### Folio 164.

1. Job’s Tears. <note>original: "Lachryma Iobi. Jobszehern."</note> Job's tears.
2. Narbonne Vetchling with broad leaves. <note>original: "Lathyris Narbonensis. Wilde Erbeissen / mit breiten Blättern."</note> Wild peas with broad leaves.
3. Wild Vetchling with narrow leaves. <note>original: "Lathyris Sylvestris. Wild Erbeissen mit schmalen Blättern."</note> Wild peas with narrow leaves.

### Folio 165.

1. Blue Pannonian Clematis. <note>original: "Clematis cærulea pannonica. Blawe Ungarische Waldreben."</note> Blue Hungarian forest-vines.
2. Foreign flesh-colored Clematis with simple flowers. <note>original: "Clematis peregrina incarnata simplici flore. Einfache frembde Leinen oder Waldreben / mit fleischfarben Blumen."</note> Simple foreign flax or forest-vines with flesh-colored flowers.
3. Foreign purple Clematis with simple flowers. <note>original: "Clematis peregrina purpurea simplici flore. Einfach frembd Leinen oder Waldreben / mit Blumen."</note> Simple foreign flax or forest-vines with flowers.

### Folio 166.

1. Small wild blue Bellflower. <note>original: "Campanula minor Sylvestris flore cæruleo. Kleine wilde blawe Rapunzelglöcklein."</note> Little wild blue rampion-bells.
2. Indian Bindweed with violet flowers. <note>original: "Conuoluulus Indicus flore violaceo. Frembd Indianisch Veilbraun Windglöcklein."</note> Foreign Indian violet-brown wind-bells.
3. Camerarius’s Arabian Indigo. <note>original: "Nil Arabum Camerarij. Fremd blawe Windglöckl."</note> Foreign blue wind-bells.

### Folio 167.

1. Large white Bindweed. <note>original: "Conuoluulus maior flore albo. Groß weis winden / oder Zaunglöcklein."</note> Great white bindweed or fence-bells.
2. Montpellier Scammony. <note>original: "Scammonea Monspeliensis. Scammonea von Moplir."</note> Scammony from Montpellier.
3. Sea Bindweed. <note>original: "Soldanella Marina. Meertöl."</note> Sea-töl <note>A vernacular name for sea bindweed.</note>.

### Folio 168.

1. Upright Virgin’s Bower. <note>original: "Flammula recta. Weiß Bläterzug Brennwurz."</note> White leaf-pulling burn-root.

<meta>This page continues the botanical index of the Hortus Eystettensis, moving from the previous section on clovers and legumes into a detailed catalog of decorative vines, nightshades, and an extensive collection of Marigolds (Tagetes) and Carnations (Dianthus), as well as aromatic garden herbs like Basil and Lavender.</meta>

2. Double purple foreign Clematis. <note>original: "Clematis peregrina purpurea flore pleno. Volle frembd Leinen/ oder Waldreben mit Satts braunen Blumen."</note> Full foreign flax or wood-vines with deep brown flowers.
3. Double flesh-colored foreign Clematis. <note>original: "Clematis peregrina incarnata plena. Gefüllte frembd Leinen oder Waldreben/ mit Fleisch-rothen Blumen."</note> Filled foreign flax or wood-vines with flesh-red flowers.

### Folio 169.

1. Heart-seed. <note>original: "Pisum Cordatum. Hertzsamen."</note> <term>Heart-seed</term> refers to the Balloon Vine (*Cardiospermum halicacabum*), named for the heart-shaped white mark on its seeds.
2. Common Winter Cherry. <note>original: "Halicacabum Vulgatius. Judenkirschen."</note> Literaly "Jew's cherries," a common name for the Bladder Cherry (*Physalis alkekengi*).
3. Indian Nightshade of Camerarius. <note>original: "Halicacabum seu Solanum Indicum Camer: Indianisch Schlutten / oder Judenkirschen."</note> Indian bladder-cherry or nightshade.

### Folio 170.

1. Great double yellow Indian Marigold. <note>original: "Caryophyllus Indicus flore multiplici luteo pleno maior. Groß gefüllt gelb Tuniszblumen / oder Indianische Blumen."</note> Large filled yellow Tunis flowers or Indian flowers. <note>Though called "Caryophyllus" here, these are actually African Marigolds (*Tagetes erecta*).</note>
2. Small simple yellow Indian Marigold. <note>original: "Caryophyllus Indicus flore luteo simplici minor. Einfach gelb Sammetblumen/ oder klein Tunisz Blumen."</note> Simple yellow Velvet-flowers or small Tunis flowers.
3. Small simple gold-yellow Indian Marigold. <note>original: "Caryophyllus Indicus flore aureo simplici minor. Einfach klein Goldgelb Sammetblumen/ oder Goldgelb klein Tuniszblumen."</note> Simple small golden-yellow Velvet-flowers.

### Folio 171.

1. Great double golden Indian Marigold. <note>original: "Caryophyllus maior Indicus flore multiplici aureo. Gefüllte grosse goldfarb Sammetblumen/ oder Indianische Negelein."</note> Filled large gold-colored Velvet-flowers or Indian Carnations.
2. Lemon-scented Basil. <note>original: "Ocimum citratum, seu citri odore. Groß Basilien oder Basilgram / mit Citronen geruch."</note> Large Basil or "Basil-herb" with a lemon scent.
3. Anise-scented Basil. <note>original: "Ocimum Anisatum. Basilien wie Aniß reucht."</note> Basil that smells like anise.

### Folio 172.

1. Small double Indian Marigold turning from yellow to red. <note>original: "Caryophyllus Indicus plenus flore è luteo rubescens minor. Gefüllte gelbe Sammetblumen/ mit röthlechter Farb vermischt."</note> Filled yellow Velvet-flowers mixed with a reddish color.
2. Small simple Indian Marigold turning from yellow to red. <note>original: "Caryophyllus Indicus flore è luteo rubescente simplici minor. Einfache/ gelbe/ mit röthlechter Farb vntermischte Sammetblumen."</note> Simple yellow Velvet-flowers intermixed with a reddish color.

### Folio 172. <meta>173 written in ink</meta>

1. Great double Carnation, half flesh-colored, the other half variegated with red and white stripes and spots. <note>original: "Caryophyllus flore maiore, dimidiata parte carneus, dimidiata verò alterâ rubris & albis strijs & punctis variegatus plenus. Volle Negelein/ oder Graßblumen/ so halb leibfarb/ halb weiß seindt / vnd mit rothen striemen vnnd düppeln vnterzogen."</note> Full Carnations or Grass-flowers, which are half flesh-colored and half white, underlaid with red stripes and dots.

<column-break/>

2. Small double reddish spotted Carnation. <note>original: "Caryophyllus flore minore pleno rubescens punctatus. Halb volle Minienfarb Negelein/ dessen mittel weiß ist."</note> Half-full <term>Cinnabar-colored</term> Carnations, the center of which is white.
3. Cinnabar-red Carnation whitening in the middle. <note>original: "Caryophyllus miniatus medio albescens. Klein voll Negeleinblumen/ mit röthlechter gedüppelter Farb."</note> Small full Carnation-flowers with a reddish speckled color.

### Folio 173. <meta>74 written in ink</meta>

1. Double Carnation with petals divided and spotted in red and white. <note>original: "Caryophyllus multiplex folijs florum ex rubro & albo, dimidiatim diuisis & punctatis. Halbiert rothe vnd weisse gesprengte Negelein."</note> Halved red and white sprinkled Carnations.
2. Double flesh-colored Carnation. <note>original: "Caryophyllus multiplex flore carneo. Fleischfarb gefüllte Negelein."</note> Flesh-colored filled Carnations.
3. Double purple-brown Carnation with deeply fringed petals. <note>original: "Caryophyllus purpureus flore multiplici profundè laciniato. Purpurbraun gefüllt Negelein/ dessen Bläter sehr zerschnitten."</note> Purple-brown filled Carnations, whose petals are very "cut" or jagged.

### Folio 174. <meta>75 written in ink</meta>

1. Greatest double red Carnation. <note>original: "Caryophyllus maximus plenus flore rubro. Groß doppelt rothe wolriechend Negelein / oder Graßblumen."</note> Large double red fragrant Carnations or Grass-flowers.
2. White-flowered Lavender. <note>original: "Lauendula flore albo. Lauendell mit weissen Blumen."</note> Lavender with white blossoms.
3. Blue-flowered Lavender. <note>original: "Lauendula flore cæruleo. Gemein Lauendel mit blawen Blumen."</note> Common Lavender with blue blossoms.

### Folio 175. <meta>76 written in ink</meta>

1. Double pale red Carnation like the flower of a peach tree. <note>original: "Caryophyllus plenus latè rubescens instar florum Persici mali. Gefüllte Negelein/ derer Blumen seindt an der Farb wie Pfirsingblüt."</note> Filled Carnations whose flowers are the color of peach blossoms.
2. Foreign Cat-thyme. <note>original: "Marum peregrinum. Amberkraut."</note> Amber-herb.
3. Female Southernwood, or Lavender Cotton. <note>original: "Abrotanum fœmina, siue Chamæcyparissus. Garten-Cypress."</note> Garden Cypress.

### Folio 176. <meta>77 written in ink</meta>

1. Double purple-red Carnation with spotted and fringed petals. <note>original: "Caryophyllus plenus purpurascens punctatis & laciniatis folijs. Purpurroth doppelt Negelein/ mit zerkerbten vnd gedüppelten Blumen."</note> Purple-red double Carnations with notched and speckled blossoms.
2. Water Plantain. <note>original: "Alisma. Engeltranck / Mutertwurz / oder Laugengkraut / Johannskraut."</note> Angel's drink, Mother-root, or Lye-herb, St. John's wort. <note>Early botanists often grouped several medicinal plants under these common names.</note>
3. Sundew. <note>original: "Rosella, seu ros solis. Klein Sonnentaw."</note> Small Sun-dew.

### Folio 177. <meta>78 written in ink</meta>

Greatest double variegated Carnation. <note>original: "Caryophyllus multiplex maximus variegatus. Groß gesprengt Merzen-Negelein/ oder Graß Blumen."</note> Great sprinkled March-Carnations or Grass-flowers.

### Folio 178. <meta>79 written in ink</meta>

1. Double white Carnation. <note>original: "Caryophyllus multiplex flore albo. Volle weisse Negelein."</note> Full white Carnations.
2. Double fringed flesh-colored Carnation. <note>original: "Caryophyllus multiplex laciniosus flore iacarnato. Leibfarb gefüllte Negelein / mit zerschnittenen Blätern."</note> Body-colored filled Carnations with cut petals.
3. Double purple-red Carnation. <note>original: "Caryophyllus multiplex flore ex purpureo rubescente. Purpurroth doppelt Negelein."</note> Purple-red double Carnations.

### Folio 179. <meta>80 written in ink</meta>

1. Double cinnabar-colored Carnation. <note>original: "Caryophyllus plenus miniato colore. Gefüllte Minienfarb Graßblumen/ oder Negelein."</note> Filled Cinnabar-colored Grass-flowers or Carnations.
2. Simple variegated garden Pink. <note>original: "Caryophyllus simplex hortensis versicolor. Einfache Gartennegelein / mit gescheckten Blumen."</note> Simple garden Carnations with dappled blossoms.
3. Double purple fringed Carnation. <note>original: "Caryophyllus purpureus flore multiplici laciniato. Volle oder Düppelte purpurfarb Negeleinblumen / so etwas am rand Zerschnitten sindt."</note> Full or double purple-colored Carnation flowers that are somewhat cut at the edges.

### Folio 180. <meta>81 written in ink</meta>

1. Great variegated wild Pink. <note>original: "Caryophyllus maior Sylvestris Variegatus. Gesprengte grosse frembde Negelein."</note> Sprinkled large foreign Carnations.
2. Purplish wild Pink. <note>original: "Caryophyllus Syluestris flore purpurascente. Federnegelein mit braunen Blumen."</note> <term>Feather-pinks</term> with brown flowers.
3. Flesh-colored wild Pink. <note>original: "Caryophyllus Syluestris flore incarnato. Federnegelein mit Leibfarben Blumen."</note> Feather-pinks with flesh-colored flowers.

### Folio 181. <meta>82 written in ink</meta>

1. Wild fringed flesh-colored Pink. <note>original: "Caryophyllus Sylvestris flore laciniato carneo. Wild wolriechend / Federnegele / mit braun weißlechten Blumen."</note> Wild fragrant Feather-pinks with brown-whitish blossoms.
2. Prolific wild Pink. <note>original: "Caryophyllus Syluestris prolifer. Wild klein roth Negelein / da ir vill auß einem Heutlein herfür kriechen."</note> Small wild red Carnations where many "creep out" of a single small skin or sheath.
3. Great blue Self-heal. <note>original: "Brunella cærulea magno flore. Groß purpurblaw Braunellen."</note> Large purple-blue Self-heal.
4. Fine-leaved Chrysanthemum. <note>original: "Chrysanthemum Tenuifolium. Gelbe Steinblumen / oder Streichblumen."</note> Yellow Stone-flowers or "Stroke-flowers."

### Folio 182. <meta>83 written in ink</meta>

1. Double red Sweet William. <note>original: "Armerius pleno rubro flore. Rothe doppelte Cartheusernegelein."</note> Red double <term>Carthusian Pinks</term>.
2. Wild Pink with white painted flowers. <note>original: "Caryophyllus Sylvestris flore albo picto. Wild Federnegelein / mit weissen Blumen / so mit einem röthlechten Ring gezeichnet sindt."</note> Wild Feather-pinks with white blossoms marked with a reddish ring.
3. White wild Pink. <note>original: "Caryophyllus Syluestris flore albo. Wild Federnegelein mit weissen Blumen."</note> Wild Feather-pinks with white blossoms.

<summary>This page lists a vast collection of decorative garden flowers, specifically focusing on various species of Marigolds and Pinks (Carnations), distinguished by their colors, doubling of petals, and scents. It also includes herbs like Basil, Lavender, and medicinal plants like Sundew and Self-heal.</summary>
<keywords>Carnations, Marigolds, Pinks, Tagetes, Dianthus, Basil, Lavender, Sundew, Self-heal, Botanical Index, Hortus Eystettensis</keywords>
