<meta>This page follows the discussion of Marigolds and Carnations from Folio 170. It marks a transition in the botanical catalog toward evergreen trees and specialized medicinal herbs, continuing the tradition of the Hortus Eystettensis in documenting both ornamental and functional plants within the bishop's garden.</meta>

-> II. **Self-heal of the Apothecaries.** I. **Tree of Life.** III. **Great Ground-pine.** <-

<note>original: "Prunella Officinarum." Known commonly today as <term>Self-heal</term> (*Prunella vulgaris*), this plant was a vital resource for early modern doctors. The term "Officinarum" indicates it was a standard item kept in an "officina" or apothecary's shop, used to treat wounds and inflammatory conditions.</note>

<note>original: "Arbor Vitæ." Literally the <term>Tree of Life</term>, this specimen is the *Thuja occidentalis*. It was among the first North American trees brought to Europe in the 16th century. It earned its name because its scale-like leaves stay green throughout the winter, and its medicinal extracts were famously used by explorers to treat scurvy.</note>

<note>original: "Chamæpitis Maior." This is the <term>Great Ground-pine</term> (*Ajuga chamaepitys*). Despite its name, it is a low-growing herb rather than a tree, but it possesses a resinous, pine-like scent. In the Renaissance, it was frequently prescribed for joint pain and gout.</note>

<summary>This illustration plate identifies and depicts three distinct plants: the North American Arbor Vitæ tree, the wound-healing herb Self-heal, and the resinous Ground-pine. These specimens highlight the mix of newly discovered New World species and traditional European medicinal herbs found in 17th-century botanical collections.</summary>

<keywords>Arbor Vitæ, Thuja, Self-heal, Prunella, Ground-pine, Chamæpitis, medicinal herbs, Renaissance botany, Hortus Eystettensis</keywords>

--- Page 154 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical sequence from the previous folio. While the last page focused on the "Tree of Life" and low-growing medicinal herbs like Self-heal and Ground-pine, this plate presents a larger ornamental shrub alongside two color varieties of the common Yarrow, further cataloging the diverse flora of the bishop's garden.</meta>

->I.<-
->**False Sycamore.**<-

<note>original: "Pseudoſycomorus." This plant is known today as the <term>Chinaberry tree</term> or <term>Bead-tree</term> (*Melia azedarach*). The name "False Sycamore" reflects the historical difficulty of classifying new species that resembled known plants like the sycamore or the mulberry. In the 17th century, it was highly prized for its fragrant blossoms and the hard seeds found within its yellow berries, which were often strung together to make rosaries.</note>

->III.<-
->**White-flowered Yarrow.**<-

<note>original: "Millefolium flore albo." The name <term>Millefolium</term> literally translates to "thousand-leaf," referring to the plant's extremely fine, feathery foliage. We know this plant today as **Yarrow**. Since antiquity, it has been a staple of the "physic garden" for its ability to staunch blood from wounds.</note>

->II.<-
->**Red-flowered Yarrow.**<-

<note>original: "Millefolium flore rubro." This is a vibrant red variety of the common Yarrow. While many medicinal herbs were grown for utility, the inclusion of specific color variants like this one in the *Hortus Eystettensis* demonstrates the transition of gardens from purely functional spaces to places of aesthetic beauty and botanical curiosity.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the Chinaberry tree (labeled as False Sycamore) alongside white and red varieties of Yarrow. It showcases both an exotic ornamental specimen and the diversity found within common European medicinal herbs.</summary>

<keywords>False Sycamore, Chinaberry, Melia azedarach, Yarrow, Millefolium, Achillea millefolium, medicinal plants, ornamental shrubs, Renaissance botany</keywords>

->II.<-
## Spanish Broom

<note>original: "Spartium Hispanicum." Known today by the scientific name *Spartium junceum*, this plant is a perennial shrub famous for its fragrant, pea-like yellow blossoms. In the 17th century, it was valued not only for its beauty but also for its utility; its tough, flexible stems were often processed like flax or hemp to create coarse fabrics and ropes.</note>

<column-break/>

->I.<-
## Cypress Tree

<note>original: "Cupressus arbor." This refers to the Mediterranean Cypress (*Cupressus sempervirens*). A staple of classical gardens, the cypress was a symbol of both mourning and eternal life because of its evergreen foliage and incredible longevity. The engraving specifically highlights the plant's <term>cones</term> (labeled as "galbuli" in historical texts), which are the woody, nut-like structures seen at the bottom of the branch.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the Spanish Broom and the Mediterranean Cypress, highlighting two significant woody species. It captures the 17th-century fascination with southern European plants that combined aesthetic elegance with historical and practical utility.</summary>

<keywords>Spanish Broom, Spartium junceum, Cypress tree, Cupressus sempervirens, Mediterranean plants, evergreens, botanical garden, seed cones</keywords>

->II.<-
## Large Green Indian Basil
->Large green Indian Basil with protruding leaves<-

<note>original: "Ocimum Indicum viride maius folijs protuberantibus." This likely refers to a cultivar of *Ocimum basilicum*. The description of "protruding" or "bumpy" leaves suggests a variety similar to the "lettuce-leaf" basil grown today, characterized by its large, crinkled foliage. During this period, "Indian" often denoted an exotic origin, as many aromatic herbs were introduced to Europe via trade routes from the East.</note>

->I.<-
## White Oleander
->Oleander or Rose-Laurel, with white flowers<-

<note>original: "Nerium seu Rhododendron flore albo." While we now reserve the name *Rhododendron* for a different genus of flowering shrubs, in early modern botany it was often used as a synonym for *Nerium oleander* (literally "Rose-Tree"). This Mediterranean shrub was admired for its elegant white or pink blossoms, though its high toxicity was also well-known to naturalists. The engraving by <term>Wolfgang Kilian</term> (an Augsburg-based engraver, 1581–1662) shows the plant's characteristic whorled leaves.</note>

->III.<-

<note>original: "Ocimum minus angustifolium flore albo." This is a smaller, more delicate relative of the basil shown in the first column. The "narrow-leaved" description suggests a plant similar to what is now known as Bush Basil (*Ocimum basilicum var. minimum*), which was frequently grown in pots or garden borders for its sweet scent and compact shape.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three botanical specimens: a central white-flowering Oleander flanked by two varieties of Basil, one with large crinkled leaves and another with narrow leaves. These engravings highlight the diversity of aromatic and ornamental plants cultivated in 17th-century European gardens.</summary>

->I. **Red-flowered Oleander**. II. **Fruit of the Oleander**.<-

<note>original: "Nerion flore rubro. Fructus Nerij." The term *Nerion* is the Latinized version of the Greek *nerion* (νήριον), the ancient name for the Oleander (*Nerium oleander*). This plant was often called the "Rose-Laurel" due to its rose-like blossoms and laurel-like leaves. This engraving highlights the terminal clusters of flowers (I) and the plant's distinctive fruit (II). The fruit is a long, narrow <term>follicle</term> (a dry seed pod) that splits open at maturity to release seeds equipped with "comas" or tufts of hair, which allow them to be dispersed by the wind.</note>

<meta>Following the previous page’s study of the Oleander, a flowering shrub of the Mediterranean, this plate (page 6) shifts focus to the citrus grove. It provides a detailed comparative study of three distinct members of the citrus family, known in the 17th century for both their medicinal properties and their association with the mythical "Golden Apples" of the Hesperides.</meta>

-> II. <-
-> *Medicinal Apples or Citrons.* <-

<note>original: "Mala Medica seu Citria." The fruit shown here is the Citron (*Citrus medica*). In antiquity, it was often called the "Median Apple" or "Medicinal Apple" because it was believed to have originated in Media (modern-day Iran) and was used as an antidote to various poisons. The engraving captures the characteristic thick, bumpy rind of the citron, which was more highly valued for its fragrance and zest than its sparse pulp.</note>

-> I. <-
-> Small Oranges, called "Dwarf." <-

<note>original: "Poma Aurantia nana dicta." These are dwarf orange trees, likely a variety such as the *Citrus aurantium* 'Nana'. These miniature trees were favorites in 17th-century aristocratic gardens, as they could be easily moved in terracotta pots and kept in "orangeries" during the winter. This illustration highlights their dense growth and abundance of small fruit.</note>

-> III. <-
-> *Oranges.* <-

<note>original: "Poma Aurantia." This term literally translates to "Golden Apples," reflecting the awe these bright fruits inspired when they were first introduced to Europe. This branch depicts the common orange, showing the smooth-skinned fruit and the <term>stamens</term> (the pollen-producing parts) of the blossoms, which were a source of highly prized essential oils and perfumes.</note>

<summary>This page features a botanical engraving of three citrus varieties: the thick-skinned citron, the ornamental dwarf orange, and the common orange. It highlights the diversity of the citrus family, which was prized in the early modern period for its fragrance, medicine, and ornamental beauty.</summary>

<keywords>Citron, Orange, Dwarf Orange, Citrus medica, Citrus aurantium, botanical illustration, 17th-century gardening, medicinal plants</keywords>

--- Page 159 ---
<meta>Following the study of various citrus fruits on the previous page, this plate (page 7) moves from the "Golden Apples" of the Hesperides to the "Punic Apple" or Pomegranate, alongside two curious ornamental variations of the common plantain. The focus here is on both the utility of the pomegranate and the botanical "wonders" found in mutated leaf structures.</meta>

->II.<-
->Rose Plantain<-

<note>original: "Plantago Rosea." This is not a true rose, but a "monstrosity"—a botanical mutation—of the common plantain (*Plantago major*). In this variety, the bracts (small leaf-like structures) of the flower head grow large and leafy, creating a shape that mimics a blooming rose. Such curiosities were highly prized in early modern botanical gardens as examples of nature's playful variety.</note>

->I.<-
->Pomegranate with a single flower.<-

<note>original: "Malus punica flore simplici." The Latin name literally means "Punic Apple," so named because the Romans first encountered the fruit in Carthage (modern-day Tunisia). This specimen shows the "single" flower, which typically produces the familiar red fruit, unlike the "double-flowered" varieties which were often sterile and grown only for their lush, ornamental blossoms. The pomegranate was a staple of the early modern <term>materia medica</term> (medical catalog), used for its astringent properties.</note>

->III.<-
->Spiked Rose Plantain<-

<note>original: "Plantago Rosea spicata." This is a further variation of the Rose Plantain seen in specimen II. While it retains the rose-like cluster of leaves at the base, it also produces a tall, central <term>spike</term>—the reproductive part of the plant where the tiny flowers and seeds are located. This illustration captures the plant’s transition from a leafy "rose" back into its natural seeding form.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the Pomegranate branch alongside two "rose" varieties of Plantain, highlighting the 17th-century fascination with both exotic Mediterranean fruits and unusual botanical mutations.</summary>
<keywords>Pomegranate, Rose Plantain, botanical curiosities, Malus punica, Plantago major, medicinal plants, garden mutations</keywords>

--- Page 160 ---
<meta>Building upon the study of the pomegranate and plantain variations on page 7, this plate expands the catalog of summer fruits. It moves from the single-flowered pomegranate seen previously to the lush, ornamental "double" variety, and introduces the stone fruits (drupes) of the Prunus family, specifically the cherry plum and the apricot. This transition reflects the 17th-century interest in both decorative garden blossoms and the bounty of the orchard.</meta>

### I.
# Pomegranate Flowers.

<note>original: "Balaustia." This term specifically refers to the flowers of the pomegranate tree, particularly the double-flowered variety (*Punica granatum var. pleniflora*). Unlike the single-flowered tree on the previous page which bears the familiar fruit, these "double" blossoms were prized in Baroque gardens for their dense, ruffled petals that resemble a peony or a rose. In the <term>apothecary</term> (pharmacy) of the time, these flowers were dried and used as a potent astringent to treat inflammations.</note>

### II.
## Round Cherry Plum.

<note>original: "Prunus Myrobalanus rotundus." Today we know this as the Cherry Plum or Myrobalan plum (*Prunus cerasifera*). The name *Myrobalanus* was historically used for various astringent fruits, but by the time of this manuscript, it had become associated with these small, round, cherry-like plums. They were often among the first fruits to ripen in the summer and were valued for their hardiness and early appearance.</note>

### III.
## Armenian Apples.

<note>original: "Mala Armeniaca." This is the historical Latin name for the Apricot (*Prunus armeniaca*). Early botanists believed the fruit originated in Armenia, though it likely came from further east in Central Asia or China. The illustration captures the clustered growth habit of the fruit on the branch. In the early modern period, apricots were considered a luxury, often grown against warm, south-facing walls in cooler climates to ensure they ripened into their characteristic sweetness.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the ornamental double-flowered pomegranate alongside two early-ripening stone fruits, the cherry plum and the apricot. It highlights the Renaissance botanical focus on both the medicinal utility of blossoms and the geographic origins of cultivated orchard fruits.</summary>

<keywords>pomegranate, cherry plum, apricot, balaustia, botanical illustration, horticulture, Materia Medica, stone fruit</keywords>

--- Page 161 ---
<meta>Continuing from the discussion of pomegranate varieties and stone fruits on the previous page, this plate provides further detail on Mediterranean shrubs. It shifts focus from the larger fruit-bearing trees to ornamental and flowering shrubs like the rockrose and the smoke tree, while returning to a specific "Roman" variety of the pomegranate blossom seen earlier in the volume.</meta>

->II.<-
->Smaller Roman<-
->Double Pomegranate Flower.<-

<note>original: "Balaustium florę minori Romanum." The term *Balaustium* refers specifically to the flowers of the pomegranate tree (*Punica granatum*), particularly the "double" varieties that do not bear fruit. These were highly valued by the <term>apothecary</term> (a historical pharmacist) as an astringent to treat inflammation. The "Roman" designation often suggested a variety either sourced from or popular in Italian gardens, characterized here by its slightly smaller, ruffled bloom.</note>

->I.<-
->White Rockrose.<-

<note>original: "Cistus flore albo." The *Cistus*, or Rockrose, is a hardy Mediterranean shrub. Its blossoms are famous for their delicate, tissue-paper texture and their tendency to last only a single day. In the 17th century, certain species of *Cistus* were economically important for producing <term>ladanum</term>, a sticky, aromatic resin used in perfumes and traditional medicine.</note>

->III.<-
->Smoke Tree.<-

<note>original: "Cotinus." Known today as *Cotinus coggygria*, or the Venetian Sumach. This shrub is easily identified by its "smoke"—the airy, feathery plumes shown in the engraving. These clouds are not the flowers themselves, but the elongated, hairy stalks of sterile flowers. Historically, the plant was used both as a garden ornament and in the tanning and dyeing industries for the yellow pigment found in its wood.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three ornamental Mediterranean shrubs: the Roman double pomegranate flower, the white rockrose, and the smoke tree, focusing on their unique floral structures and historical medicinal uses.</summary>
<keywords>pomegranate flowers, Cistus, Rockrose, Cotinus, Smoke Tree, botanical medicine, Baroque gardening, Roman flora</keywords>

->III.<-
### Creeping Sea Violet.

<note>original: "Viola Marina repens." Despite the name "Violet," this likely refers to a coastal plant such as the sea-heath or perhaps a variety of sweet alyssum. The term *marina* signifies its coastal habitat, while *repens* describes its "creeping" growth habit, forming low mats of foliage as shown in the illustration.</note>

->I.<-
# Syrian Mallow.

<note>original: "Alcea Syriaca." Known to modern gardeners as the Rose of Sharon (*Hibiscus syriacus*). Although native to Asia, it reached Europe through trade routes in the Levant, hence the "Syrian" designation. It was a centerpiece of early modern botanical gardens, admired for its large, exotic blooms and its relation to the common mallow and hollyhock.</note>

->II.<-
### Stinking Mayweed.

<note>original: "Cotula Fœtida." Now known as *Anthemis cotula*, this is a wild relative of the chamomile. The name *fœtida* (stinking) was given because of its pungent, often disagreeable odor, which distinguishes it from the fragrant "true" chamomile. Historically, it was used in herbal medicine as a treatment for various "vapors" and inflammatory conditions, though it was also known as a troublesome weed in grain fields.</note>

It showcases the variety of 17th-century botanical study, ranging from prized garden imports to common wild herbs.</summary>

### II.
## Tarentine Myrtle.

<note>original: "Mÿrtus Tarentina." This is a small-leaved variety of the common myrtle (*Myrtus communis*). It takes its name from Tarentum, an ancient Greek city in Southern Italy (modern-day Taranto), where it was famously cultivated. Its dense, petite foliage and compact growth made it a favorite for ornamental gardening and topiary during the Renaissance and Baroque periods.</note>

<column-break/>

### I.
## Broad-leaved Myrtle.

<note>original: "Mÿrtus latifolijs." A variation of the Latin *latifolia*, meaning "broad-leaved." In contrast to the Tarentine variety, this specimen is depicted with larger, more prominent foliage and is shown in full bloom. The illustration captures the delicate, brush-like stamens that are characteristic of myrtle flowers, which were often associated with the goddess Venus and used in bridal wreaths.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates two distinct varieties of the Myrtle plant: the small-leaved Tarentine Myrtle and the Broad-leaved Myrtle. These engravings highlight the subtle morphological differences between cultivars that were highly valued in early modern botanical collections.</summary>

<keywords>Tarentine Myrtle, Broad-leaved Myrtle, Myrtus communis, botanical engraving, 17th-century gardening, medicinal plants, ornamental shrubs</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the botanical survey, moving from the woody shrubs of the previous page into a display of fragrant garden favorites like jasmine and marjoram, alongside wild medicinal herbs like eyebright. The transition highlights the dual focus of early modern herbaria on both aesthetic beauty and practical pharmaceutical use.</meta>

->II<-
->**Broad-leaved Marjoram.**<-

<note>original: "Mairana latifolia." This is an archaic spelling of *Majorana*. It refers to a broad-leaved variety of Sweet Marjoram (*Origanum majorana*). Marjoram has been cultivated since antiquity for its aromatic oils, used in cooking, medicine, and as a "strewing herb" to scent the floors of homes.</note>

->**Catalan Jasmine.**<-

<note>original: "Gelsiminum Catalonicum." Now known as *Jasminum grandiflorum* or Spanish Jasmine. Despite the name, it likely originated in South Asia but was famously cultivated in Catalonia and the Mediterranean. It is the primary species used in the traditional perfume industry due to its intense, sweet fragrance.</note>

->III<-
->**Branched Meadow Eyebright**<-
->**with white flowers**<-

<note>original: "Euphrasia ramosa pratensis flore albo." The name *Euphrasia* comes from the Greek word for "gladness." In traditional herbal medicine, this plant was used to treat eye infections and strain—a practice based on the "Doctrine of Signatures," as the flower's markings were thought to resemble an inflamed eye.</note>

->IV.<-
->**Less-branched Eyebright with flowers**<-
->**turning from blue to purple**<-

<note>original: "Euphrasia minus ramosa flore ex caeruleo purpurascente." This entry identifies a different growth habit (less branched) and a specific color variation (bluish-purple) of the eyebright plant. Early botanists were deeply interested in these subtle variations in color and structure to distinguish between subspecies.</note>

These illustrations emphasize the variety of growth habits and flower colors found within the same plant families.</summary>

<keywords>Catalan Jasmine, Marjoram, Eyebright, Euphrasia, botanical illustration, 17th-century medicine, perfumery, medicinal herbs, floral morphology</keywords>

<meta>This page follows a series of aromatic and medicinal herbs (jasmine, marjoram, and eyebright) from page 13. ### I.
### Nightshade Tree.

<note>original: "Strichnodendron." This name is derived from the Greek *strychnos* (referring to various nightshades) and *dendron* (tree). In the early modern period, this likely referred to the Jerusalem Cherry (*Solanum pseudocapsicum*). This plant was often grown in "winter gardens" and orangeries for its bright, orange-red berries, which remain on the branch long after the leaves have thinned, though the fruit is toxic if ingested.</note>

### II.
### Styrian Toadflax.

<note>original: "Linaria Styriaca." The genus name *Linaria* comes from the Latin *linum* (flax), owing to the similarity of the foliage to the flax plant. The epithet *Styriaca* identifies this specimen as being native to Styria, a historic duchy in present-day Austria and Slovenia. This reflects the 16th and 17th-century botanical interest in cataloging "Alpine" flora and the specific biodiversity of the Holy Roman Empire.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates two distinct botanical specimens: the woody Nightshade Tree, likely an ornamental Solanum, and the Styrian Toadflax, a regional wildflower from the Eastern Alps. The inclusion of these plants highlights the era's focus on both decorative "winter" greenery and the systematic documentation of regional European flora.</summary>

--- Page 167 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical survey from page 14. While the previous page featured the woody Nightshade Tree and the Alpine Styrian Toadflax, page 15 moves toward forest-dwelling plants known for their potent medicinal properties and striking symmetry: Herb Paris and two varieties of Foxglove.</meta>

->### I.<-
->### Herb Paris.<-

<note>original: "Herba Paris." The name is derived from the Latin *par* (equal), referring to the precise symmetry of its four leaves. Despite its name, it has no relation to the French city. In folklore, it was sometimes called "True Lover's Knot." Historically, it was used in small doses to treat inflammation or as a supposed antidote to poisons like arsenic, though the entire plant—especially the berry—is toxic.</note>

### III.
### Yellow-flowered Foxglove.

<note>original: "Digitalis flore luteo." This likely refers to *Digitalis lutea* or *Digitalis grandiflora*. The genus name *Digitalis* comes from the Latin *digitus* (finger), because the blossoms look like the fingers of a glove. This yellow variety is often found in the mountainous regions of Central Europe.</note>

### II.
### Flesh-colored Foxglove.

<note>original: "Digitalis flor: incarnato." The term *incarnato* literally means "flesh-colored," describing the pinkish-purple hue of the Common Foxglove (*Digitalis purpurea*). While it was used in folk medicine for centuries to treat "dropsy" (swelling), it was later discovered to contain digitalis glycosides, which are still used in modern medicine to regulate heart rhythm, though the raw plant remains dangerously poisonous.</note>

<summary>This page presents three woodland plants: the symmetrical Herb Paris and two distinct species of Foxglove (yellow and purple/flesh-colored). These specimens reflect the transition in early botanical science from folklore and herbalism to the formal classification of plants with powerful, often toxic, pharmacological properties.</summary>

<keywords>Herb Paris, Paris quadrifolia, Digitalis, Foxglove, Digitalis flore luteo, Digitalis purpurea, medicinal plants, botanical illustration, toxicology</keywords>

->III.<-
# Foreign Hawkweed.

<note>original: "Hieracium peregrinum." The genus name *Hieracium* is derived from the Greek word *hierax* (hawk). According to ancient folklore, hawks would tear the plant open and use the milky sap to sharpen their eyesight. The epithet *peregrinum* suggests this specific variety was considered "foreign" or "exotic" to the author's region, likely originating from southern Europe.</note>

<image-desc>A botanical plate featuring three detailed plant engravings. 
In the center is 'Foreign Hawkweed' (Hieracium peregrinum), which has a tall, slender, slightly hairy stem with one lance-shaped leaf and several circular, composite flower heads at its tip. 
To the left is 'Red Foxglove' (Digitalis flore rubro), a plant with a thick stalk and a dense spike of large, hanging, bell-shaped flowers. The interior of the lower blossoms shows characteristic spots. A small Roman numeral 'II' is engraved near the base of the stem. 
To the right is 'White Foxglove' (Digitalis flore albo), displaying a similar tall flowering spike of bell-shaped blossoms, though the arrangement at the top is more tapered and the flowers appear more tightly packed. A small Roman numeral 'I' is engraved near the base of the stem.</image-desc>

## II. Red Foxglove.

<note>original: "Digitalis flore rubro." This refers to the common *Digitalis purpurea*. While the text calls it "red," these flowers typically range from deep pink to purple. In the 18th century, William Withering famously documented the use of this plant's leaves to treat dropsy, leading to the discovery of digitalis as a powerful cardiac stimulant.</note>

## I. White Foxglove.

<note>original: "Digitalis flore albo." This is the white-flowered variant of the common Foxglove. In early modern botany, color variations were often classified as separate species or distinct "types" before modern genetics explained them as simple color mutations within the same species.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the study of the Foxglove family by illustrating the red and white varieties, while also introducing the Foreign Hawkweed. These illustrations highlight the plant's physical structures and reflect the era's fascination with classifying medicinal and wild flora.</summary>

<keywords>Foreign Hawkweed, Hieracium peregrinum, Red Foxglove, Digitalis flore rubro, White Foxglove, Digitalis flore albo, medicinal plants, 18th-century botany</keywords>

### III. 
### Blue Canterbury Bells.

<note>original: "Medium flore cœruleo." The term *Medium* (derived from the Greek *mēdion*) was used by early botanists like Dioscorides and later Carolus Clusius to describe certain bellflowers. Today, this is known as *Campanula medium*. The name "Canterbury Bell" likely comes from the resemblance of the flowers to the small bells worn by horses on the road to the shrine of Thomas Becket.</note>

### I.
### Red Valerian.

<note>original: "Valeriana Rubra." Known today as *Centranthus ruber*, this hardy perennial is famous for its clusters of tiny, star-shaped flowers. Although called "valerian," it is distinct from the medicinal *Valeriana officinalis*. In the 17th century, it was frequently planted in stone walls and cottage gardens across Europe.</note>

### II.
### White Canterbury Bells.

<note>original: "Medium flore albo." This is the white-flowering variant of the Canterbury Bell. Early botanical works often presented different color variations of the same species side-by-side to assist gardeners and collectors in identifying the "sports" or cultivars available for their collections.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three ornamental garden plants: the blue and white varieties of the Canterbury Bell (Campanula medium) and the vibrant Red Valerian.</summary>
<keywords>Canterbury Bells, Campanula medium, Red Valerian, Centranthus ruber, botanical illustration, garden flowers, blue flowers, white flowers</keywords>

### II.
### Reddish-flowered Corn Cockle.

<note>original: "Pseudomelanthium flore rubescente." The name *Pseudomelanthium* (literally "false black-flower") was used by early botanists to distinguish this plant from *Melanthium* (black cumin). We know it today as *Agrostemma githago*. Though a common sight in grain fields, its seeds are toxic, and historically, farmers went to great lengths to "weed" it out of their harvests.</note>

->I.<-
->Parisian Pyramidal Bellflower<-

<note>original: "Pyramidalis Lutetiana." Today known as the Chimney Bellflower (*Campanula pyramidalis*), this plant is depicted in the center of the plate. It is named for its tall, tapering (pyramidal) flowering spike, which can reach great heights. The star-shaped flowers and lance-like leaves are characteristic of this species, which remained a popular decorative plant in European grand gardens for centuries.</note>

***

->II.<-
->Catchfly with a white flower<-

<note>original: "Muscipula flore albo." The name *Muscipula* translates to "mousetrap" or "flytrap." Many plants in this group (typically the genus *Silene*) secrete a sticky, resinous substance on their stems. This "glue" serves as a defense mechanism, trapping ants and other crawling insects that might otherwise steal nectar without providing the service of pollination. This specimen represents a variety with white blossoms.</note>

These plants were often found in meadows and were valued by early botanists for their unique physical defenses and bright, five-petaled flowers.</note>

-> III. **Variegated Monkshood** <-
<note>original: "Napellus flor:e variegato." The term *Napellus* means "little turnip," referring to the shape of the plant's poisonous tuberous roots. This variety is distinguished by its "variegated" flowers, which display a mix of colors—usually blue and white—rather than a solid deep purple.</note>

-> I. **Healing Wolfsbane** <-
<note>original: "Antora." Also known as *Aconitum anthora* or "Yellow Monkshood." In early modern medicine, it was called "Wholesome Wolfsbane" because it was mistakenly believed to be an antidote to the more lethal species of Aconite. The name *Antora* is derived from the idea that it works "against" (*anti*) the poison known as *thora*.</note>

-> II. **Tree-like Monkshood** <-
<note>original: "Napellus Arborescens." The descriptor *arborescens* (becoming tree-like) refers to the plant's impressive height and robust, woody stem compared to other low-growing herbs. This variety was prized for its tall spikes of large, architectural blooms.</note>

->(III.) **Hart’s Pennyroyal** &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; I. **False Soapwort** &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; II. **Royal Pennyroyal**<-

->I.<-
->**False Soapwort**<-
<note>original: "Pseudo Struthium." The name refers to a plant that resembles the true "Struthium" or "Soapwort" of antiquity, which was famous for its ability to produce a lather in water. It was widely used by fullers for cleaning and scouring raw wool. This variety is shown with its long, slender leaves and prominent, tapering flower spikes.</note>

->II.<-
->**Royal Pennyroyal**<-
<note>original: "Pulegium Regium." This is the common Pennyroyal, a well-known member of the mint family. Its Latin name is derived from "pulex" (flea), as the plant was traditionally used to repel pests. This illustration highlights its branched stem and the round flower heads that appear at intervals along the branches.</note>

->III.<-
->**Hart’s Pennyroyal**<-
<note>original: "Pulegium Cervinum." Also known as "Stag Pennyroyal," this plant is a rarer relative of the common pennyroyal. It is characterized by its very narrow leaves and the dense, ball-like clusters of flowers that encircle the stem at the base of the leaves (whorls), giving it a distinctively tiered appearance.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three plants valued for their aromatic and practical uses: two varieties of Pennyroyal and the "False Soapwort." The detailed engravings showcase the diverse growth habits of these species, ranging from the spiked flowers of the Soapwort to the whorled clusters of the Pennyroyals.</summary>

<meta>The previous page concluded the study of aromatic mints and cleansing herbs, specifically Pennyroyal and Soapwort. This sequence demonstrates the herbalist's transition from soft-stemmed field herbs to more substantial, woody garden specimens.</meta>

->III.<-
->**Southernwood**<-
<note>original: "Abrotanum Mas." Historically known as "Male Southernwood" or "Old Man," this woody shrub is a relative of wormwood. In the early modern period, the designation "Mas" (male) was often used to distinguish a larger or more robust variety of a plant from a smaller "female" counterpart, rather than reflecting biological sex. It was highly valued for its intense, lemon-like fragrance and its ability to ward off insects.</note>

<column-break/>

->I.<-
->**Pliny’s Mignonette**<-
<note>original: "Reseda Plinij." This plant was identified and named in reference to the writings of the Roman naturalist Pliny the Elder. The genus name *Reseda* is derived from the Latin verb *resedare*, meaning "to soothe" or "to calm." True to its name, it was traditionally used in external applications to "soothe" inflammations and heal bruises.</note>

<column-break/>

->II.<-
->**Garden Tarragon**<-
<note>original: "Dracuncellus Hortensis." The name literally translates to "Little Garden Dragon." This refers to the plant we know today as Tarragon. The "dragon" association stems from its serpentine, coiled root structure, which led historical healers to believe—under the Doctrine of Signatures—that the plant was an effective remedy for the bites of snakes and other venomous beasts.</note>

<summary>This page presents three distinct garden plants: Southernwood, Pliny’s Mignonette, and Garden Tarragon. Each entry highlights the plant's transition from classical Roman botanical lore to practical use in the early modern apothecary garden.</summary>
<keywords>Southernwood, Mignonette, Tarragon, Pliny the Elder, aromatic herbs, medicinal plants, Doctrine of Signatures, botanical illustration</keywords>

<note>original: "Consolida regalis flore pleno purpureo." This variety completes the trio of primary garden colors. The <term>purpureo</term> (purple) of the 1600s often leaned toward a rich, reddish-violet. These tall, spiked flowers were popular in "knot gardens," where their height provided a dramatic vertical element against lower-growing herbs.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three "double-flowered" varieties of Larkspur in blue, red, and purple, highlighting the transition from wild field plants to ornate garden cultivars. The detailed engravings emphasize the dense petal structure and feathery foliage characteristic of the species.</summary>
<keywords>Larkspur, Consolida regalis, Consolida arvensis, double flowers, botanical illustration, floriculture, 17th-century gardening</keywords>

--- Page 197 ---
<meta>Transitioning from the Larkspur (Consolida) genus featured on the previous pages, this page introduces the Leucoium genus, encompassing plants known today as wallflowers and stocks. While the previous page focused on the vibrant blues and purples of garden cultivars, this page explores wild, shrubby, and uniquely colored varieties of these fragrant spring flowers.</meta>

->**Middle-sized wild wallflower**<-
->**with a smaller, paler flower.**<-
<note>original: "Leucoium Sylvestre medium flore parvo pallidiore." The term <term>Leucoium</term> was historically used to describe a broad group of cruciferous plants, primarily wallflowers (*Erysimum*) and stocks (*Matthiola*). The descriptor <term>Sylvestre</term> indicates this is a wild variety, which typically possesses smaller, less showy blossoms than its garden-bred relatives.</note>

<column-break/>

->**Shrubby wallflower with green leaves**<-
->**and a fragrant white flower, as noted by Camerarius.**<-
<note>original: "Leucoium fruticosum folio viridi flore albo odorato Camerarij." The word <term>fruticosum</term> (shrubby or woody) refers to the plant's sturdy, perennial stem. This specific variety is attributed to Joachim Camerarius the Younger (1534–1598), a leading botanist of the Renaissance whose "Hortus Medicus" was a vital reference for identifying plants and their medicinal properties.</note>

<column-break/>

->III.<-
->**Wallflower of a melancholy color.**<-
<note>original: "Leucoium Melancholico colore." In early modern botany, "melancholy" described plants with somber, muted, or "sad" colors, such as brownish-purples or dull yellows. This likely represents the "Sad Stock" (*Matthiola tristis*), a plant that may appear drab during the day but is prized for the intense, sweet fragrance it releases at night.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three distinct varieties of wallflowers and stocks, ranging from a pale wild form to a sturdy white-flowered shrubby variety and a somber "melancholy" colored specimen. These engravings reflect the 17th-century interest in both the botanical classifications of experts like Camerarius and the sensory appeal of fragrant garden plants.</summary>

<keywords>Leucoium, wallflower, stock, wild flowers, Joachim Camerarius, fragrant plants, Matthiola, botanical illustration</keywords>

--- Page 198 ---
<meta>Transitioning from the various stocks and wallflowers (Leucoium) described on the previous page, the focus shifts here to two distinct botanical types: the climbing Bittersweet nightshade and the feathery, ornamental Love-in-a-mist.</meta>

<column-break/>

->II.<-
->**Bittersweet with white flowers.**<-
<note>original: "Dulcis amara flore albo." This is a white-flowering variant of the <term>Bittersweet</term> or Woody Nightshade (*Solanum dulcamara*). The name "Bittersweet" reflects the plant's unique properties—the bark of the stem is said to taste first bitter upon the tongue and then sweet. While beautiful, all parts of this climbing plant are toxic if ingested.</note>

<column-break/>

->I.<-
->**Foreign Love-in-a-mist with double flowers.**<-
<note>original: "Nigella peregrina flore multiplici." <term>Nigella</term> is named for its jet-black seeds (from the Latin *niger*). This "foreign" variety is shown with "double" flowers (*flore multiplici*), a term used by early botanists to describe blossoms with extra layers of petals, often bred for garden displays. Today, this genus is commonly known as "Love-in-a-mist" or "Devil-in-the-bush" due to its feathery, fennel-like foliage.</note>

<column-break/>

->III.<-
->**Common bittersweet with blue flowers.**<-
<note>original: "Dulcis amara flore caeruleo vulgatior." This is the more "common" (*vulgatior*) form of the Bittersweet nightshade. It is characterized by its violet-blue petals and a prominent yellow cone of stamens in the center. In the early modern period, it was frequently used in folk medicine for skin conditions, though with great caution due to its poisonous nature.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates two varieties of Bittersweet nightshade—the rarer white-flowered and the common blue-flowered types—arranged around a central, highly ornate "double" variety of Nigella or Love-in-a-mist.</summary>
<keywords>Bittersweet nightshade, Solanum dulcamara, Nigella, Love-in-a-mist, double flowers, botanical engraving, medicinal plants, climbing plants</keywords>

Early botanists used the term "single flower" (*flore simplici*) to describe blossoms with the natural arrangement of petals (usually five), as found in the wild before selective breeding for garden display.</note>

1.
## Greatest Mountain Lily,
## many-flowered and
## variegated.

->**The greatest mountain lily<gloss>I</gloss> with many white flowers,**<-
->**sprinkled with red spots.**<-
<note>original: "Lilium Montanum maximum polyanthos album rubris maculis aspersis." This describes a particularly large and floriferous variety of the <term>Martagon lily</term> (also known as the Turk's cap lily). The term "polyanthos" — from the Greek for "many flowers" — highlights the plant's ability to produce a high volume of blossoms on a single stalk, a trait highly prized by 17th-century collectors.</note>

->ii<-
->**Smaller wild carnation with single flowers.**<-
<note>original: "Caryophyllus minor sylvestris flore simplici." This refers to a wild variety of the <term>Carnation</term> or "pink" (genus *Dianthus*). Early botanists used the term "single flower" to describe blossoms with the natural arrangement of petals—usually five—as found in the wild before selective breeding for garden display transformed them into the "double" varieties we often see today.</note>

<column-break/>

->i<-
->**Imperial Musk Martagon.**<-
<note>original: "Martagon Imperiale Moschatum." The <term>Martagon lily</term> is described here as an "Imperial" variety with a "musk" scent. This illustration is notable for its focus on the plant's underground anatomy; the large, scaly bulb and the thick, wandering roots are drawn with as much detail as the leaves, reflecting the early modern fascination with how plants store energy and grow beneath the soil.</note>

<column-break/>

->iii<-
->**Smaller wild carnation with double flowers.**<-
<note>original: "Caryophyllus minor sylvestris flore pleno." This is a "double-flowered" version of the same wild carnation shown in the first column. In these varieties, the reproductive parts of the flower—the stamens—often transform into extra petals through a natural mutation. To a 17th-century gardener, "flore pleno" (full flower) was a mark of luxury and horticultural success.</note>

It emphasizes both the aesthetic beauty of the blooms and the scientific importance of the root and bulb systems.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the botanical catalogue, moving from the lilies and carnations of the previous page to specific varieties of Iris and the early-blooming Mountain Lily. The monogram "MV" in the bottom right corner likely identifies the engraver or artist of this specific plate.</meta>

->II.<-
->**Pale-colored Bulbous Iris.**<-
<note>original: "Iris Bulbosa pallido colore." Early modern botanists carefully distinguished between <term>bulbous</term> irises—which grow from a rounded, onion-like underground storage organ—and the more common types that grow from creeping stems. This specimen, noted for its light or "pale" hue, reflects the 17th-century fascination with color variations in garden flowers.</note>

<column-break/>

->I.<-
->**Early red**<-
->**Mountain Lily.**<-
<note>original: "Lilium Montanum rubrum præcox." This is a variety of the <term>Martagon lily</term>, often called the Turk's cap lily because of its curled-back petals. The descriptor "praecox" means "early," indicating that this specific red variety was known for blooming sooner than other mountain lilies, a vital detail for gardeners planning their seasons.</note>

<column-break/>

->**The first narrow-leaved Iris**<-
->**of Clusius.**<-
<note>original: "Iris folijs angustis prima Clusij." This plant is named in reference to <term>Carolus Clusius</term> (Charles de l'Écluse), the legendary 16th-century botanist whose work laid the foundations for modern plant science. Clusius was famous for documenting the flora of Europe and Turkey; this "narrow-leaved" species is shown with a <term>rhizome</term> (a thick horizontal root), contrasting with the bulbous iris on the far left.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three distinct ornamental plants: a pale bulbous iris, an early-flowering red Martagon lily, and a narrow-leaved iris classified by the botanist Carolus Clusius. The plate highlights the anatomical differences between various root systems, such as bulbs and rhizomes.</summary>

<keywords>Iris, Mountain Lily, Martagon Lily, Carolus Clusius, bulbous plants, rhizome, botanical illustration, 17th-century botany</keywords>

--- Page 204 ---
<meta>This page continues the presentation of ornamental garden plants, shifting focus from the irises and mountain lilies of the previous plate to the dramatic Chalcedonian lily and late-blooming hyacinth varieties.</meta>

->II.<-
->**Stalk with bulb.**<-
<note>original: "Scapus cum bulbo." This illustration provides a botanical detail of the lily's base. The <term>scapus</term> refers to the leafless flowering stem (though here shown with its lower foliage), while the <term>bulb</term> is the scaly underground organ that stores nutrients for the plant.</note>

<column-break/>

->I.<-
->**Many-flowered**<-
->**Chalcedonian Day-lily.**<-
<note>original: "Hemerocallis Calcedonica polyanthos." In the 17th century, "Hemerocallis" was often used to describe various lilies, but this plant is specifically the <term>Chalcedonian Lily</term> (*Lilium chalcedonicum*), prized for its brilliant scarlet flowers with recurved petals. "Polyanthos" indicates a "many-flowered" variety, a characteristic highly sought after by collectors of the era.</note>

<column-break/>

->III.<-
->**Greater narrow-leaved**<-
->**autumn star-hyacinth.**<-
<note>original: "Hyacinthus stellatus autumnalis angustifolijs maior." This specimen is what we today might call an <term>Autumn Squill</term> (*Prospero autumnale*). While most hyacinths are synonymous with spring, this "star-hyacinth" was noted for blooming in the autumn. The description "angustifolijs" (narrow-leaved) and "maior" (greater) helped gardeners distinguish it from smaller or broader-leaved cousins.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates a spectacular many-flowered Chalcedonian lily, including a detailed view of its bulbous root system, alongside a larger variety of the narrow-leaved autumn star-hyacinth. These engravings emphasize the diversity of lily and hyacinth species available to 17th-century European collectors, specifically highlighting those originating from the Eastern Mediterranean.</summary>

<keywords>Chalcedonian Lily, Lilium chalcedonicum, Autumn Star-Hyacinth, Scilla, botanical illustration, bulbous plants, polyanthos, 17th-century gardening</keywords>

->**Mountain Lily with a purplish**<-
->**spotted flower.**<-
<note>original: "Lilium montanum flore purpurascente punctatum." This plant is the <term>Martagon Lily</term> (*Lilium martagon*). The name "purpurascente" refers to its pinkish-purple hue, and "punctatum" describes the dark, recessed spots on the petals. This variety is prized for its "recurved" petals that curl back toward the stem, resembling a turban.</note>

<column-break/>

->**Mountain Lily with a**<-
->**purplish unspotted**<-
->**flower.**<-
<note>original: "Lilium montanum flore purpurascente non punctatum." This is a plain-petaled variety of the same species. Early botanical works often showcased these variations side-by-side to help collectors and scholars distinguish between subtle natural mutations in color and patterning.</note>

<note>The engravings on this page provide a complete botanical study, from the <term>scaly bulb</term>—a characteristic feature of true lilies where the bulb is composed of overlapping fleshy scales—to the whorled leaf arrangement and the nodding, downward-facing blooms.</note>

->I.<-
->**Enchanter’s Nightshade**<-
<note>original: "Circæa." This plant, known today as *Circaea lutetiana*, is actually a member of the willowherb family rather than a true nightshade. Its name refers to Circe, the sorceress of Greek myth, likely due to its preference for growing in the dark, damp woods where one might imagine a witch’s garden.</note>

->III.<-
->**Smaller White Mountain Lily,**<-
->**without spots.**<-
<note>original: "Lilium Montanum minus album non punctatum." This is a white variety of the <term>Martagon Lily</term> (*Lilium martagon var. album*). The term "minus" (smaller) identifies it as a more delicate specimen or perhaps a younger plant compared to the neighboring figure.</note>

->II.<-
->**Larger White Mountain Lily,**<-
->**with spots.**<-
<note>original: "Lilium Montanum maius album punctatum." This specimen displays the characteristic dark speckles common to the species, but on a white petal background. Like all true lilies shown here, it features a <term>scaly bulb</term>—a root structure composed of overlapping, fleshy leaves that store nutrients for the plant.</note>

<note>The engraving captures the "nodding" habit of the flowers, where the heads hang downward and the petals curve sharply back (reflexed), a distinctive trait of the Turk’s Cap lily group.</note>

->III.<-
->**Upright Hand-of-Christ Orchid**<-
->**with pure white flowers.**<-
<note>original: "Palma Christi erecta flore candido." The name <term>Palma Christi</term> (Hand of Christ) was given to these orchids because of their tubers, which are "palmate" or shaped like a hand with spreading fingers. This specimen is likely a white-flowered variant of a Marsh Orchid.</note>

->I.<-
->**Pompon Lily**<-
<note>original: "Martagon Pomponeum." Known today as *Lilium pomponium* or the Turban Lily, this plant is native to the western Alps. It is distinguished from the common Mountain Lily by its exceptionally dense, narrow, needle-like leaves and its bright, "nodding" flowers that curve back into a tight turban shape.</note>

->II.<-
->**Upright Hand-of-Christ Orchid**<-
->**with variegated flowers.**<-
<note>original: "Palma Christi Erecta flore variegato." In early botany, "variegated" often referred to the delicate spots, streaks, or marbling found on the petals. The artist has carefully rendered the hand-shaped tubers at the base to distinguish this from the bulb-bearing lilies.</note>

<note>The illustration highlights the two primary types of storage roots known to early modern botanists: the <term>scaly bulb</term> of the lily (center), composed of fleshy layers, and the <term>palmate tuber</term> of the orchids (left and right), which resembles a human hand.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the Pompon Lily alongside two varieties of "Hand-of-Christ" orchids, focusing on the contrast between the lily’s scaly bulb and the orchids’ hand-shaped tubers. It serves as a study in both floral morphology and the diverse underground structures of flowering plants.</summary>

->I.<-
->**Lesser Sisyrinchium**<-
<note>original: "Sifyrinchium minus." In the early modern period, the name <term>Sisyrinchium</term>—borrowed from the ancient Greek naturalist Theophrastus—was used to describe various small, bulbous plants in the iris family. The plant shown here, with its iris-like bloom and round corm, is likely the <term>Barbary Nut</term> (*Moraea sisyrinchium*), a Mediterranean species prized for its delicate flowers and edible bulb.</note>

->III.<-
->**Yellow Spotted Mountain Lily**<-
<note>original: "Lilium montanum flore luteo punctato." This specimen represents the <term>Yellow Turk’s Cap Lily</term> (*Lilium pyrenaicum*), which is native to the Pyrenees and surrounding mountains. The artist highlights the characteristic "nodding" posture of the flowers and the dark speckles (the "punctato" of the Latin name) that adorn the yellow petals.</note>

---

->II.<-
->**Unspotted Yellow Mountain Lily**<-
<note>original: "Lilium montanum flore luteo non punctato." This is a variant of the Yellow Turk’s Cap Lily that lacks the dark spots on its petals. Early botanists carefully recorded these variations, as they were often debated as being either distinct species or merely "sports" (natural variations) of the same plant.</note>

<note>The engraving provides a detailed comparison of the <term>scaly bulb</term> typical of true lilies (seen in items II and III) versus the solid, rounded <term>corm</term> of the Sisyrinchium (item I). This visual focus on the underground anatomy reflects the 17th-century interest in classifying plants by their root structures as much as their blossoms.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the Lesser Sisyrinchium alongside two varieties of Yellow Mountain Lilies, distinguishing them by the presence or absence of spots. The plate emphasizes the differences between scaly lily bulbs and the solid corms of iris-like plants.</summary>

->I.<-
->**Yellow Bulbous Iris**<-
<note>original: "Iris bulbosa flore luteo." In the 17th century, botanists used the term "bulbous" to distinguish species like this one—likely the <term>Spanish Iris</term> (*Iris xiphium*)—from the more common "flag" irises that grow from thick, horizontal rhizomes. This specimen is noted for its grass-like leaves and vibrant yellow bloom.</note>

->II.<-
->**Late-blooming Byzantine Lily**<-
<note>original: "Lilium Bizantinum Serot:" (an abbreviation for *serotinum*, meaning late-blooming). Known today as the <term>Scarlet Turk’s Cap Lily</term> (*Lilium chalcedonicum*), this plant was introduced to Western Europe via Constantinople (Byzantium). The illustration captures its distinctive "recurved" petals, which curl back toward the stem to form a shape resembling a Turkish turban.</note>

---

->III.<-
->**Spring Mountain Crocus**<-
<note>original: "Crocus Montanus Vernalis." This refers to the <term>Spring Crocus</term> (*Crocus vernus*), a hardy mountain wildflower. Unlike the autumn-blooming saffron, this species was celebrated as one of the first signs of the retreating winter snows in alpine regions.</note>

---

->IV.<-
->**Saffron Crocus**<-
<note>original: "Crocus Sativus." This is perhaps the most famous of all crocuses, cultivated since antiquity for its threads of saffron. The artist has carefully detailed the three long, drooping red stigmas within the purple petals, which are harvested to produce the precious spice and dye.</note>

<note>This plate provides a clear visual lesson in <term>underground morphology</term>. It contrasts the <term>scaly bulb</term> of the Byzantine Lily (II), which is composed of overlapping fleshy leaves, with the <term>corms</term> of the crocuses (III and IV). Notice the "fibrous-coated" tunic of the Saffron Crocus bulb, which protects the plant during its summer dormancy.</note>

<summary>This page presents a comparative study of bulbous flowering plants, highlighting the Saffron Crocus, the Byzantine Lily, and the Yellow Iris. It emphasizes the structural differences between their flowers and their various underground storage organs, such as corms and scaly bulbs.</summary>

<meta>The previous page concluded a study of various bulbous plants, including the Spanish Iris, the Scarlet Turk’s Cap Lily, and two varieties of Crocus. This page transitions from those delicate garden flowers to a single, more dramatic and exotic specimen: the Great Dragon Arum.</meta>

->1.<-
->**Great Dragon Arum**<-
<note>original: "Dracontium maius." Today this plant is known by the scientific name *Dracunculus vulgaris*. Its historical name, "Great Dracontium," refers to its impressive size and the snake-like markings on its stem.</note>

This illustration depicts a specimen of the Great Dragon Arum, a plant belonging to the <term>Araceae</term> (Arum) family. The engraving highlights the plant’s most distinctive features:

*   **The Inflorescence:** At the center of the image is a large, tapering **spadix** (the central spike) surrounded by a wide, ruffled **spathe**. In life, this spathe is often a deep, velvety maroon or purple color.
*   **The Foliage:** Several large leaves are shown at the base and to the left. These are <term>pedately divided</term>, meaning they spread out like the toes of a bird's foot, with wavy or undulating edges.
*   **The Root System:** In the upper right-hand corner, a detailed view shows the thick, lumpy **tuber**—the underground storage organ from which the plant grows each year.
*   **The Mottled Stem:** Notice the base of the stem in the detail drawing; it is covered in dark, irregular spots. To early botanists, this resembled the scales of a serpent or a dragon, which led to the plant's common names and its reputation in folklore as a cure for snakebites.

<note>While beautiful to look at, this plant is famous for a "deathly" scent it emits when in bloom. This odor attracts carrion flies, which act as pollinators, a fascinating example of how plants adapt to their environment through mimicry.</note>

<summary>This page features a detailed copperplate engraving of the Great Dragon Arum (Dracunculus vulgaris), focusing on its dramatic spathe, its pedate leaves, and its characteristic mottled, snake-like stem.</summary>
<keywords>Great Dragon Arum, Dracontium maius, Dracunculus vulgaris, spadix, spathe, tuber, botanical engraving, Araceae, snakeskin stem</keywords>

<meta>The previous page detailed the exotic and large-scale Great Dragon Arum. This page moves to a more varied composition, contrasting the bulky, tuberous Lion’s Leaf with two smaller, delicate flowering plants from different environments.</meta>

->III.<-
->**Small blue-flowered daisy**<-
<note>original: "Bellis minor flore cæruleo." While the common daisy (*Bellis perennis*) is usually white or pink, the "blue" variety described here often referred to plants like the Globe Daisy (*Globularia vulgaris*) or certain types of Asters that shared a similar low-growing, rosette-shaped form.</note>

->I.<-
# Lion’s Leaf
<note>original: "Leontopetalon." Now known as *Leontice leontopetalum*, this plant’s name literally translates from Greek as "lion’s petal" or "lion’s leaf," likely referring to the shape or perceived strength of its foliage.</note>

->II.<-
->**Yellow-flowered Alpine Poly**<-
<note>original: "Polium Alpinum flore luteo." This is likely a species of Mountain Germander (*Teucrium montanum*). The name "Polium" comes from the Greek word for "gray" or "whitish," referring to the pale, downy hair often found on the leaves of this genus.</note>

This plate illustrates three distinct botanical specimens, emphasizing their root structures and flowering habits:

*   **The Lion’s Leaf (Center):** This is the most prominent specimen on the page. It features a large, <term>tuberous</term> root (an underground storage organ) that is deeply textured. From this root rises a thick, branching stem. The leaves are broad and rounded, and the plant is topped with clusters of small, delicate flowers. In historical medicine, the root of the Lion's Leaf was often prepared as a treatment for sciatica or even as an antidote to snake venoms.

*   **Small Blue-flowered Daisy (Bottom Left):** This smaller plant is shown with a <term>basal rosette</term> (leaves radiating from the base) and several long, leafless stalks ending in composite flower heads. Its roots are fibrous and shallow, typical of many field-growing species.

*   **Yellow-flowered Alpine Poly (Bottom Right):** This plant represents a hardy, high-altitude species. It has a woody base and many small, narrow leaves. The flowers are clustered at the tips of the stems. <note>Mountain plants like this were often prized by early modern botanists for their aromatic properties and perceived potency in herbal remedies.</note>

<summary>This page presents a botanical study of three plants: the tuberous Lion's Leaf, a blue-flowered variety of small daisy, and a yellow-flowered Alpine Poly. The engraving focuses on the contrast between the large, lumpy root of the Lion's Leaf and the more delicate structures of the surrounding flora.</summary>

<keywords>Lion's Leaf, Leontopetalon, Small Daisy, Bellis minor, Alpine Poly, Polium Alpinum, medicinal plants, tuberous roots, Alpine flora, 17th-century botany</keywords>

->II. Common Long Birthwort. I. Round Birthwort. III. Thorough-wax.<-

<note>original: "II. Aristolochia longa vulgaris. I. Aristolochia rotunda. III. Perfoliata."</note>

This copperplate engraving displays three botanical specimens, each chosen for their unique underground structures and leaf formations.

->I.<-
# Round Birthwort
<note>original: "Aristolochia rotunda." The name *Aristolochia* is derived from the Greek words *aristos* (best) and *locheia* (childbirth), reflecting its ancient use in midwifery. The "round" designation refers to its distinctive root.</note>

This central plant is characterized by its massive, <term>tuberous</term> root (a thick, underground storage organ), which is depicted with intricate cracks and fibrous offshoots. The leaves are heart-shaped and grow closely to the stem. On the body of the root, the engraver has left his mark: <margin>Serwouters engraved this</margin> <note>original: "Serwouters sculp." Pieter Serwouters was a noted 17th-century Dutch engraver known for his precision in scientific illustration.</note>

->II.<-
# Common Long Birthwort
<note>original: "Aristolochia longa vulgaris." While related to the central specimen, this variety is distinguished by its root shape.</note>

Shown on the left, this plant features a long, vertical taproot rather than a round bulb. Its leaves are similarly heart-shaped, and small, tubular flowers can be seen emerging from the <term>axils</term> (the angle between the leaf stalk and the stem). In historical medicine, the <term>Doctrine of Signatures</term> suggested that the shape of these roots—resembling a birth canal or a fetus—indicated their effectiveness in aiding delivery.

->III.<-
# Thorough-wax
<note>original: "Perfoliata." Likely *Bupleurum rotundifolium* (Hare’s-ear). The common name "Thorough-wax" comes from the Old English "thorough" (through) and "wax" (to grow), describing the stem growing through the leaf.</note>

The specimen on the right is notable for its <term>perfoliate</term> leaves. Unlike most plants where the leaf attaches to the side of the stem, here the stem appears to pierce directly through the center of the leaf blade. It is topped with delicate clusters of star-shaped flowers. Historically, this plant was highly regarded as a "vulnerary," a herb used to knit together or heal external wounds and fractures.

<summary>This page illustrates the anatomical differences between Round and Long Birthworts alongside the unique "pierced" leaf structure of the Thorough-wax.</summary>
<keywords>Birthwort, Aristolochia, Thorough-wax, Perfoliata, botanical illustration, Pieter Serwouters, medicinal herbs, taproot, tuber</keywords>

IIII.
## Coral-rooted Toothwort
<note>original: "Dentaria radice coralloidè." Likely referring to *Corallorhiza trifida* (Coralroot) or a species of *Cardamine*. The name "Dentaria" refers to the tooth-like appearance of some roots, while "coralloide" describes the branching, coral-like structure of the underground stem.</note>

III.
## Bird’s Nest Orchid
<note>original: "Nidus Avis." Modern botanical name: *Neottia nidus-avis*.</note>

II.
## Female Dog-Orchid
<note>original: "Cynosorchis fœmina." The term *Cynosorchis* is derived from the Greek *kyon* (dog) and *orchis* (testicle), a common historical naming convention for orchids based on the shape of their twin tubers.</note>

I.
# Broad-leaved Orchid
<note>original: "Orchis latifolia." Now commonly known as the Broad-leaved Marsh Orchid.</note>

This plate illustrates four botanical specimens notable for their unique underground anatomy, a key focus for 17th-century botanists who used these structures for classification.

->I.<-
# Broad-leaved Orchid
Occupying the center of the engraving, this orchid features broad, lance-shaped leaves and a dense, cone-like spike of flowers. Its most striking feature is its root system, consisting of two smooth, oval <term>tubers</term> (thickened underground parts of a stem or rhizome used by the plant for food storage).

->II.<-
## Female Dog-Orchid
To the right is a smaller, more delicate orchid. Unlike the central specimen, it is shown with narrower leaves and a single prominent root tuber.

->III.<-
## Bird’s Nest Orchid
In the bottom left, the *Bird’s Nest Orchid* is shown as a scaly, leafless stem. Because this plant lacks green leaves to capture sunlight, it was a curiosity to early botanists. Its name is derived from the thick, tangled mass of roots which resembles a bird's nest. <note>We now know this plant is <term>saprophytic</term>, meaning it obtains its nutrients from decaying organic matter through a relationship with fungi.</note>

->IIII.<-
## Coral-rooted Toothwort
At the top left, this slender plant features a sparse cluster of bell-shaped flowers. It is distinguished by its unique <term>rhizome</term> (a horizontal underground stem), which is intricately branched and resembles the growth patterns of sea coral.

It serves as a study in 17th-century botanical classification based on underground morphology.</summary>

**Broad-leaved Helleborine**
<note>original: "Epipactis latifolia." This species is still known by this scientific name today. It is a robust orchid found in woodlands across Europe.</note>

->V.<-
**Foreign Palm of Christ with red flowers**
<note>original: "Palma Christi peregrina fl. rubro." The name <term>Palma Christi</term> (Palm of Christ) was given to certain orchids, particularly those now in the genus *Dactylorhiza*, because their underground tubers are split into finger-like lobes resembling a human hand. "Peregrina" indicates it was considered a foreign or exotic variety by the author.</note>

<note>original: "Palma Christi erecta flo: incarnato." The term "incarnato" refers to a pale pink or "flesh" tone, typical of the Marsh-orchids.</note>

<note>original: "Orchis Repens." Now identified as *Goodyera repens*, commonly called Creeping Lady's-tresses. Unlike the other orchids on this page which grow from tubers, this species spreads via a creeping <term>rhizome</term> (a horizontal underground stem).</note>

# **I.**
# **English Bulbous Iris with a white flower**
<note>original: "Iris bulbosa Anglicana candido flore." Despite being called the "English Iris" (*Iris latifolia*), this plant is actually native to the Pyrenees. It earned its common name because it was first sent to continental botanists from gardens in England. The term <term>bulbous</term> distinguishes it from the more common garden irises that grow from thick, surface-creeping stems.</note>

<column-break/>

# **II.**
# **Narrow-leaved Solomon’s Seal**
<note>original: "Polygonatum angustifolijs." The name <term>Polygonatum</term> is derived from the Greek words for "many" and "knee," referring to the numerous joints or scars found on the plant's horizontal rootstock. The common name "Solomon's Seal" arises from these circular scars, which were thought to resemble the impression of a signet seal in wax.</note>

<summary>This botanical plate illustrates two distinct types of perennial plants: the white English Bulbous Iris and the Narrow-leaved Solomon's Seal. The engraving emphasizes their underground structures, showing the contrast between a true bulb and a jointed rhizome.</summary>

<keywords>English Iris, Solomon's Seal, Polygonatum, Iris bulbosa, botanical illustration, bulb, rhizome, Renaissance botany</keywords>

->1.<-

# Proliferous Sunflower
<note>original: "Flos Solis prolifer." The term "proliferous" refers to the plant's unusual habit of producing multiple secondary flower heads branching off from the main stem, rather than a single large blossom.</note>

<insert>Heinrich Ulrich</insert>
<note>Heinrich Ulrich (c. 1572–1621) was a noted German engraver and goldsmith from Nuremberg, responsible for the detailed copperplate illustrations in this volume.</note>

->I.<-

### The Great Sunflower
<note>original: "Flos Solis maior." Literally "The Great Flower of the Sun," this refers to the *Helianthus annuus*. At the time this was documented, the sunflower was a botanical wonder recently brought to Europe from the Americas. It was celebrated for its height and its "heliotropic" nature—the way the flower head appears to follow the sun.</note>

->II.<-
->**White-flowered Spanish Sword Lily**<-
<note>original: "Gladiolus Hispanicus flore albo." The name <term>Gladiolus</term> comes from the Latin word for "little sword," describing the plant’s sharp, blade-like leaves. This variety was identified by its pure white petals and its native habitat in Spain.</note>

->I.<-
->**Red-flowered Italian Sword Lily**<-
<note>original: "Gladiolus Italicus flore rubro." This describes the *Gladiolus italicus*, a common Mediterranean wildflower. In the early modern period, it was a familiar sight in the Italian countryside, often found growing vibrantly among fields of grain.</note>

->III.<-
->**Flesh-colored Narbonne Sword Lily**<-
<note>original: "Gladiolus Narbonensis flore incarnato." The term <term>incarnato</term> (literally "in the flesh") refers to a pale, pinkish-peach color. Narbonne, in Southern France, was a vital hub for botanical study and trade, and many Mediterranean specimens were first classified there.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates a "proliferous" variety of the Great Sunflower, a prominent arrival from the Americas, alongside a classification of three Sword Lilies (Gladioli) from Spain, Italy, and Southern France.</summary>
<keywords>Sunflower, Helianthus annuus, Gladiolus, botanical illustration, Heinrich Ulrich, New World plants, Sword Lily, Narbonne, Spanish flora, Italian flora</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the botanical catalog of garden flowers. Following the previous page's focus on the newly discovered Sunflower and various Mediterranean Gladioli (Sword Lilies), the collection now turns to the Calendula, commonly known as the Marigold, a staple of European flower gardens and herbal medicine.</meta>

->II.<-
->**Double Yellow Marigold**<-
<note>original: "Calendula lutea flore pleno." The phrase <term>flore pleno</term> (full flower) refers to what modern gardeners call a "double flower," where the plant produces an abundance of extra petals instead of the usual single row. This variety was highly sought after for its decorative, pom-pom-like appearance.</note>

->I.<-
->**Proliferous Marigold**<-
<note>original: "Calendula prolifera." This is a botanical curiosity where the primary flower head produces several smaller flowers on their own tiny stalks from its base. Historically, this was often called the "Hen and Chickens" marigold because the central bloom appears to be surrounded by its "offspring."</note>

->III.<-
->**Yellow Marigold with a Reddish Center**<-
<note>original: "Calendula lutea medioruffa." This variety represents the "single" form of the marigold, distinguished by its color contrast: bright yellow outer petals surrounding a dark, reddish-brown central disk or "eye."</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three varieties of the Marigold (Calendula), showcasing the diversity of the species through its petal density, color variations, and unusual growth patterns like the proliferous "hen and chickens" form.</summary>
<keywords>Marigold, Calendula, double flowers, proliferous plants, botanical illustration, horticulture, Renaissance gardening</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the botanical catalog of garden flora. <note>original: "Chondrilla Sesamoides dicta caerulea flore completo." While categorized here as a <term>Chondrilla</term> (Skeletonweed), the description and blue color suggest this is likely Cupid’s Dart (*Catananche caerulea*). The phrase <term>flore completo</term> (full or complete flower) indicates a "double" variety where the flower head is densely packed with petals.</note>

->I.<-

Unlike the "double" or "proliferous" (hen-and-chickens) varieties shown previously, the <term>flore simplici</term> has only a single row of petals surrounding the central pollen-bearing disk.</note>

->**III.** &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; **I.** &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; **II.**<-
->**English Crested Tansy. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Touch-Me-Not. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Common Tansy.**<-

***

->**I.**<-
->**Touch-Me-Not**<-
<note>original: "Noli me tangere." This plant, known scientifically today as *Impatiens noli-tangere* or the <term>Yellow Balsam</term>, takes its name from the Latin phrase meaning "do not touch me." This refers to the plant's mature seed capsules, which explode at the slightest touch, flinging seeds a considerable distance. The illustration shows its distinctive pendant, tube-like flowers.</note>

***

->**II.**<-
->**Common Tansy**<-
<note>original: "Tanacetum vulgare." A hardy perennial with a strong, spicy scent. In the Renaissance, the <term>Common Tansy</term> was essential in "physic gardens" (medicinal gardens); it was used to treat intestinal worms and was also rubbed on meat to repel flies. Its flowers are described as "buttons" because they lack the long outer petals seen on daisies.</note>

***

->**III.**<-
->**English Crested Tansy**<-
<note>original: "Tanacetum Cristatum Anglicum." This is a "double" or "curled" variety of the tansy plant. The term <term>cristatum</term> (crested) refers to the way the leaves are more intricately divided and ruffled compared to the common variety, making it a popular choice for ornamental garden borders.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the "Touch-me-not" plant alongside two varieties of Tansy, highlighting the distinction between the common medicinal herb and its decorative "crested" English relative.</summary>
<keywords>Tansy, Tanacetum, Touch-me-not, Impatiens, English Crested Tansy, medicinal herbs, botanical engraving, Renaissance gardening</keywords>

It presents the Great Oxeye Daisy alongside two exotic varieties of Corn Marigolds brought from the Mediterranean to the gardens of Eichstätt. The layout continues the comparative botanical study typical of this volume.</meta>

->II.<-
->Yellow Cretan Corn Marigold<-
<note>original: "Chrysanthemum Creticum luteum." This is the yellow variety of the <term>Crown Daisy</term>. The name "Chrysanthemum" comes from the Greek words for "gold flower," while "Creticum" indicates its origin on the island of Crete. In the early 17th century, these Mediterranean imports were highly fashionable in European aristocratic gardens.</note>

***

->I<-
->Greater Daisy<-
<note>original: "Bellis maior." Known today as the <term>Oxeye Daisy</term> (*Leucanthemum vulgare*), this plant is a larger relative of the common lawn daisy. In folklore, it was often dedicated to St. John and was used in traditional medicine to treat respiratory ailments, though here it is celebrated for its height and clear, white flowering heads.</note>

***

->III.<-
->Mixed Cretan Corn Marigold<-
<note>original: "Chrysanthemum Creticum mixtum." This refers to the bicolor variety of the Cretan Corn Marigold. Unlike the solid yellow version (II), this "mixed" variety features petals that are yellow at the base and creamy white at the tips. The illustration captures the deeply lobed, feathery foliage that distinguishes these plants from the simpler leaves of the Oxeye Daisy.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three large-flowered members of the daisy family, highlighting the distinction between the native European Oxeye Daisy and the more exotic, decorative Corn Marigolds from Crete. It reflects the Renaissance interest in "improving" gardens with colorful Mediterranean species.</summary>
<keywords>Oxeye Daisy, Crown Daisy, Chrysanthemum, Cretan flora, Asteraceae, botanical illustration, 17th-century gardening</keywords>
