### II.
### *Double-flowered Feverfew*
<note>original: "Matricaria flor: pleno." The name <term>Matricaria</term> is derived from the Latin *matrix*, meaning "womb," highlighting the plant's historical use in treating feminine ailments. The "double" variety shown here (*flore pleno*) was a popular horticultural variation where the central disc flowers are replaced by additional rows of petals, creating a lush, pom-pom effect.</note>

<column-break/>

### I.
### Single-flowered
### Feverfew
<note>original: "Matricaria flore simplici." This is the common or "simple" form of the plant, often found in old cottage gardens. Its common English name, <term>Feverfew</term>, comes from the Latin *febrifugia*, meaning "fever-driver," referring to its use as a tonic to reduce temperatures. The illustration clearly shows the difference between this wild-type flower, with its single row of white petals, and the cultivated double variety on the left.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates two varieties of Feverfew, contrasting the natural single-flowered form with a "double" horticultural variety. It highlights the plant's dual role in the 17th century as both a decorative garden flower and an essential medicinal herb for treating fevers and women's health.</summary>
<keywords>Feverfew, Matricaria, botanical illustration, medicinal herbs, double-flowered, Asteraceae, 17th-century gardening</keywords>

--- Page 229 ---
<meta>Following the discussion of Feverfew on the previous page—a plant prized for its role in women's health and its "double" garden varieties—the manuscript now moves to three other members of the Daisy family (Asteraceae). These plants were valued less for their showy petals and more for their aromatic oils and their practical utility in the early modern household, particularly as insect repellents.</meta>

III.
### Small Fleabane
<note>original: "Coniza minor." The name <term>Coniza</term> (or *Conyza*) comes from the Greek word for "gnat," referring to the plant's pungent smell which was said to drive away biting insects. This "minor" or smaller variety was often dried and strewn across floors or stuffed into mattresses to repel fleas, giving it the common English name "Fleabane."</note>

I.
# Sweet Maudlin
<note>original: "Ageratum." In the 16th and 17th centuries, this name referred to *Achillea ageratum*, an aromatic herb also known as Sweet Maudlin. The name is derived from the Greek *ageratos*, meaning "not growing old," because the yellow flower heads remain vibrant and colorful long after they have been cut. It was a favorite in Renaissance gardens for its balsamic fragrance and was used in medicine to "warm" a cold stomach.</note>

II.
### Marsh Fleabane
<note>original: "Coniza Palustris." This is the wetland-dwelling cousin of the Small Fleabane. The descriptor <term>palustris</term> tells the reader that this plant is found in marshes or boggy ground. As seen in the illustration, it is much larger and more robust than the dry-ground variety (III), but it shared the same reputation for being "fatal to gnats and fleas" when burned as smudge or hung in bundles.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three functional herbs: two types of Fleabane used for pest control and Sweet Maudlin, a fragrant medicinal plant. The detailed engravings emphasize the differences in their growth habits, from the delicate Small Fleabane to the sturdy, water-loving Marsh variety.</summary>
<keywords>Small Fleabane, Sweet Maudlin, Marsh Fleabane, Conyza, Ageratum, medicinal herbs, insect repellents, 17th-century botany, Asteraceae</keywords>

--- Page 230 ---
<meta>Continuing the survey of the Daisy family (Asteraceae) from the previous page, which focused on aromatic herbs like Fleabane and Sweet Maudlin, this page illustrates two varieties distinguished by their "woolly" or "silvery" appearance. These plants were often grouped together in early modern herbals because of their distinctive felt-like textures, a physical trait thought to reflect their medicinal properties.</meta>

->II.<-
->**Sea Ragwort**<-
<note>original: "Cineraria Marina." The name <term>Cineraria</term> comes from the Latin *cinis*, meaning "ash," a direct reference to the dusty, silvery-white coating on the leaves. Today known scientifically as *Jacobaea maritima*, it was frequently mentioned by early botanists for its resilience in coastal air and its use in cooling ointments for inflammation.</note>

->I.<-
->**Foreign Broad-leaved Cudweed**<-
<note>original: "Gnaphalium latifolium peregrinum." The name <term>Gnaphalium</term> is derived from the Greek word for a "tuft of wool," describing the soft, downy hair covering the plant. These plants were commonly called "Everlastings" or "Cudweeds" because their flower heads retain their shape and color long after drying. The term <term>peregrinum</term> indicates that this specific broad-leaved variety was considered a "foreign" or exotic introduction to the local landscape.</note>

<summary>This page features two "silver-leaved" plants: Sea Ragwort and a foreign variety of Cudweed. These botanical illustrations highlight the 17th-century interest in plants with distinctive textures, often used for both garden ornament and topical medicinal remedies.</summary>

<keywords>Sea Ragwort, Cudweed, Gnaphalium, Cineraria, silver-leaved plants, botanical engraving, Asteraceae, medicinal herbs</keywords>

--- Page 231 ---
<meta>Following the previous page’s discussion on the silvery-leaved members of the Daisy family (Asteraceae), this page continues the botanical survey with the Pellitory of Spain—another member of the same family—alongside a "lesser" variety of succulent. While the previous page focused on plants with a woolly appearance, this page highlights plants valued for their specific medicinal "heat" and "cooling" properties.</meta>

->II.<-
->**Pellitory of Spain**<-
<note>original: "Pyrethrum Officinarum." The name <term>Pyrethrum</term> is derived from the Greek word for "fire," a reference to the intense, burning sensation the root produces when chewed. The label <term>Officinarum</term> signifies its status as an "official" medicinal herb kept in the apothecary's shop. In early modern medicine, it was a primary remedy for toothaches and for "purging the head" by stimulating the flow of saliva.</note>

->I.<-
->**Lesser Navelwort**<-
<note>original: "Cotyledon minus." The name <term>Cotyledon</term> comes from the Greek word for "cup," referring to the shallow, dish-like shape of the leaves in certain species. During this period, the name was used for various succulents, including Stonecrops and Houseleeks. This "lesser" (<term>minus</term>) variety was often applied topically to cool "Saint Anthony's Fire" (erysipelas) and other inflammatory skin conditions.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the Pellitory of Spain, known for its fiery medicinal root, and the Lesser Navelwort, a succulent used for its cooling properties. These engravings demonstrate the 17th-century botanical practice of grouping plants by their pharmaceutical utility as much as their physical appearance.</summary>

<keywords>Pellitory of Spain, Pyrethrum, Navelwort, Cotyledon, medicinal plants, succulents, Asteraceae, botanical illustration</keywords>

--- Page 232 ---
<meta>Following the previous page’s discussion on the "fiery" Pellitory of Spain and the "cooling" Lesser Navelwort, this page moves toward plants found in woodland and meadow environments. While the previous entries focused on the pharmaceutical "heat" of the specimens, this page highlights the evergreen nature of the Wintergreen and the vibrant, seasonal pigments of the Pheasant’s Eye.</meta>

->III.<-
->**Flame-colored Pheasant’s Eye**<-
<note>original: "Eranthemum flor: flammeo." The name <term>Eranthemum</term> is derived from the Greek words <term>er</term> (spring) and <term>anthemon</term> (flower). In early modern botany, this name was typically used for the plant we now call *Adonis* (Pheasant’s Eye). The descriptor <term>flammeo</term> refers to the brilliant, orange-red hue of the petals, which were often compared to flickering flames in a field.</note>

->I.<-
->**Wintergreen**<-
<note>original: "Pyrola." The name is a diminutive form of the Latin word for a pear tree (<term>Pyrus</term>), a reference to the shape of its leaves, which resemble small pear leaves. Known as "Wintergreen" because it retains its lush green foliage throughout the cold months, it was historically used in "vulnerary" (wound-healing) potions and distilled waters to treat internal inflammations.</note>

->II.<-
->**Red-flowered Pheasant’s Eye**<-
<note>original: "Eranthemum flor: rubro." A variety of the same species as figure III, but with a deeper red (<term>rubro</term>) coloration. In the floral symbolism of the 17th century, these plants were often associated with the myth of Adonis, whose blood was said to have stained the earth and given rise to these jewel-toned flowers.</note>

<summary>This page features botanical illustrations of the Wintergreen and two color variations of the Pheasant's Eye. These engravings highlight the period's fascination with evergreen foliage and the mythological associations of vibrant field flowers.</summary>

<keywords>Wintergreen, Pyrola, Pheasant's Eye, Eranthemum, Adonis, medicinal plants, woodland flora, 17th-century botany</keywords>

--- Page 233 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded the section on field flowers like Pheasant's Eye and Wintergreen. This page continues the botanical survey with members of the Aster family, focusing on starworts and fleabanes, transitioning from the vibrant reds of the previous entries to the cool blues and yellows of these meadow-dwelling composites.</meta>

->II.<-
->**Small Blue Athenian Starwort**<-
<note>original: "Aster Atticus minor flor: cæruleo." The name <term>Aster</term> is derived from the Greek word for "star," referring to the radiating petals of the flower. This "Athenian" variety—likely the species *Aster amellus*—was highly praised by ancient writers like Virgil for its golden center and purple-blue petals, which were thought to resemble a crown or a star.</note>

<column-break/>

->I.<-
->**Middle Fleabane**<-
<note>original: "Conyza media." The name <term>Conyza</term> is derived from the Greek word for "dust" or "gnat" (<term>konis</term>). Historically, these pungent plants were dried and hung in houses or burned as incense to drive away fleas and gnats, earning them the common name "Fleabane." The "middle" (<term>media</term>) designation refers to its size relative to other known species in the botanical classification of the time.</note>

<column-break/>

->III.<-
->**Large Blue Athenian Starwort**<-
<note>original: "Aster Atticus maior flor: cæruleo." A larger (<term>maior</term>) variety of the same blue starwort shown in figure II. In the herbal traditions of this era, these plants were not merely ornamental; they were often used in poultices to treat swellings and inflammations, and were sometimes associated with protective qualities due to their star-like appearance.</note>

<summary>This page presents detailed engravings of three composite flowers: the Middle Fleabane and two size variations of the Athenian Starwort. These plants were significant in early modern botany for their medicinal uses as insecticides and anti-inflammatories, as well as their classical associations with Greek and Roman literature.</summary>

<keywords>Aster Atticus, Starwort, Conyza, Fleabane, botanical illustration, Athenian Starwort, medicinal herbs, 17th-century flora</keywords>

-> III. Small Glasswort. I. Roselle. II. Blue Bellflower with Saw-Toothed Leaves. <-

<column-break/>

->I.<-
->**Roselle**<-
<note>original: "Sabdariffa." Now known as *Hibiscus sabdariffa*, this plant is a member of the mallow family. It is easily recognized by its deeply lobed leaves and large, pale flowers with dark centers. In historical botany, it was often classified as a "shrubby mallow." It is famous today for its fleshy red calyxes used to make tart herbal teas, though in this era, it was primarily a prized exotic specimen in botanical gardens.</note>

<column-break/>

->II.<-
->**Blue Bellflower with Saw-Toothed Leaves**<-
<note>original: "Campanula folijs serratis cærulea." The name <term>Campanula</term> comes from the Latin for "little bell," referring to the shape of the blossoms. The description "saw-toothed" (<term>serratis</term>) refers to the jagged edges of the leaves. This specific variety is likely the *Campanula rotundifolia* or a similar species, common across Europe and admired for its nodding, azure flowers.</note>

<column-break/>

->III.<-
->**Small Glasswort**<-
<note>original: "Kali Minus." Also known as Saltwort. The term <term>Kali</term> is derived from the Arabic word for "alkali." These plants grow in salty soils and were historically harvested and burned to produce soda ash. This ash was a critical ingredient in the ancient and early modern production of glass and soap, giving the plant great economic value.</note>

<summary>This page features a trio of botanically and economically diverse plants: the tropical Roselle, a common serrated Blue Bellflower, and the Small Glasswort. The inclusion of Glasswort highlights the practical applications of botany in the production of glass and soap during the period.</summary>

<keywords>Roselle, Hibiscus, Bellflower, Campanula, Glasswort, Saltwort, Alkali, Early Modern Botany, Economic Plants</keywords>

->III.<-
->**Smallest Double-Flowered**<-
->**Yellow Buttercup**<-
<note>original: "Ranunculus Minor flore luteo plenus minimus." The genus name <term>Ranunculus</term> comes from the Latin for "little frog," a name applied because many species in this family thrive in damp areas where frogs are found. This specific variety is "double-flowered" (<term>flore plenus</term>), an ornamental form where the plant's stamens have been replaced by extra petals, creating a dense, spherical appearance.</note>

->I.<-
# Venetian Mallow
<note>original: "Alcea Veneta." Known today as *Hibiscus trionum* or "Flower-of-an-hour," this plant is a member of the mallow family. The name "Venetian" points to its popularity in the botanical gardens of Italy during the Renaissance. It is characterized by its deeply lobed leaves and striking flowers that typically open only for a few hours in the midday sun before closing.</note>

->II.<-
->**Large Double-Flowered**<-
->**Yellow Buttercup**<-
<note>original: "Ranunculus Minor flore luteo plenus maximus." While categorized as a "Minor" ranunculus (distinguishing the species from larger meadow varieties), this specimen is the "maximus" or largest of the double-petaled garden varieties shown here. In the 17th century, these "full" flowers were highly sought after by collectors for their resemblance to miniature roses or pompons.</note>

<meta>This page continues the botanical survey of the Malvaceae (mallow) family introduced on the previous page. While page 82 featured the ornamental "Venetian Mallow," this page focuses on the more standard and shrub-like varieties used in both decorative gardening and traditional herbalism.</meta>

I.
->Common Mallow<-
<note>original: "Alcea vulgaris." In the 17th century, the name <term>Alcea</term> was a general term for various plants in the mallow family. The descriptor <term>vulgaris</term> indicates that this was the "common" or most well-known variety. These plants were often found in cottage gardens and were prized for their ability to soothe inflammations, a quality reflected in the soft, velvety texture of their broad, hand-shaped leaves.</note>

II.
->*Shrubby Mallow*<-
<note>original: "Alcea Fruticans." This variety is distinguished from the common mallow by its growth habit; <term>fruticans</term> means "shrub-like" or woody. The artist has carefully illustrated the "laciniate" leaves—meaning they are deeply divided into jagged lobes—which provided a more delicate and decorative appearance in a formal garden compared to the rounder leaves of the common variety.</note>

<summary>This page features detailed engravings of two mallow species: the Common Mallow and the Shrubby Mallow. It highlights the botanical distinctions in leaf shape and stem woodiness that were used to categorize plants in the early modern period.</summary>
<keywords>Common Mallow, Shrubby Mallow, Alcea vulgaris, Alcea Fruticans, botanical illustration, Malvaceae, 17th-century gardening, medicinal plants</keywords>

# Mallow <gloss>II.</gloss> Spanish Shrubby.

<column-break/>

# Mallow <gloss>I.</gloss> Common.

II.
->*Spanish Shrubby Mallow*<-
<note>original: "Althæa Fruticans Hispanica." The name identifies this as a woody, shrub-like plant (<term>fruticans</term>) originating from Spain (<term>Hispanica</term>). Unlike the soft-stemmed common varieties, this plant possesses a more rigid, branched structure. The illustration highlights its "serrated ovate" leaves—meaning they are egg-shaped with tooth-like edges—and flowers shown at various points of development, from tight buds to full blossoms.</note>

<column-break/>

I.
->Common Marsh-mallow<-
<note>original: "Althæa Vulgaris." The term <term>vulgaris</term> denotes that this was the most "common" or standard variety known to 17th-century botanists. Known today as the Marsh-mallow, its name <term>Althæa</term> stems from the Greek word *altho*, meaning "to heal." This plant was a staple of the apothecary's garden; its thick, fleshy roots and velvety leaves were used to produce mucilage for soothing coughs and inflammations.</note>

<summary>This page provides a comparative botanical illustration of two mallow species, distinguishing between the woody Spanish Shrubby Mallow and the medicinal Common Marsh-mallow. These engravings served both as a scientific record of leaf and root structures and as a guide for gardeners and herbalists.</summary>

<keywords>Common Marsh-mallow, Spanish Shrubby Mallow, Althaea vulgaris, Althaea fruticans, botanical illustration, 17th-century medicine, Malvaceae, herbalism</keywords>

# Ivy-leaved Toadflax <gloss>II.</gloss>
<column-break/>
# Thuringian Mallow <gloss>I.</gloss>
<column-break/>
# Traveler’s Joy <gloss>III.</gloss>

II.
->*Ivy-leaved toadflax with blue flowers*<-
<note>original: "Cymbalaria folijs hederaceis flore cæruleo." This delicate, trailing plant is named for its leaves which resemble ivy (<term>hederaceous</term>). Today known as *Cymbalaria muralis*, it was often called "Coliseum Ivy" or "Pennywort" in early modern herbals due to its habit of growing in the mortar of old stone walls and ruins.</note>

<column-break/>

I.
->*Thuringian Mallow*<-
<note>original: "Althæa Thuringiaca." This species, known today as *Lavatera thuringiaca* or the Garden Tree Mallow, is named for the Thuringia region of Germany. It stands as the central figure of the plate, showcasing the family's characteristic large, five-petaled blossoms and deeply lobed foliage. Like other members of the <term>Althæa</term> genus, it was recognized for its relationship to the medicinal marsh-mallow.</note>

<column-break/>

III.
->*Clematis, or the common Traveler’s Joy of de l'Obel*<-
<note>original: "Clematis sive Viorna Vulgi lobelij." This refers to the *Clematis vitalba*, a vigorous climbing vine. The name <term>Viorna</term> was a traditional Latin term for the plant, while <term>lobelij</term> attributes the classification to Matthias de l'Obel (1538–1616), a renowned Flemish botanist whose system of classification based on leaf shape was highly influential during the 17th century.</note>

<summary>This page features a trio of European plants: the creeping Ivy-leaved Toadflax, the tall Thuringian Mallow, and the climbing Traveler’s Joy. The collection demonstrates the 17th-century interest in documenting both common wild vines and regional variations of the mallow family.</summary>

### Narrow-leaved Cretan 
### Horehound,
<note>original: "Marubium Creticum angustifolium." This plant is a variety of Horehound, a bitter aromatic herb traditionally used in herbal medicine. "Creticum" indicates it was sourced from the island of Crete, and <term>angustifolium</term> is Latin for "narrow-leaved," distinguishing it from broader-leaved relatives.</note>

### I.
### Curled Mallow.
<note>original: "Malua crispa." Today classified as *Malva verticillata* var. *crispa*, this plant was a favorite in early modern gardens for its striking, architectural foliage. The term <term>crispa</term> refers to the "crisped" or highly ruffled edges of the leaves, which give the plant a frilled appearance.</note>

### II.
### False Dittany with whorled 
### flowers.
<note>original: "Pseudodictamus floribus vertillatis." Known today as *Ballota pseudodictamnus*, this "False Dittany" was often grown for its woolly, silver-green foliage. The term <term>vertillatis</term> (verticillate) describes a botanical structure where flowers or leaves grow in a circle around a single point on the stem, resembling the spokes of a wheel.</note>

### Avicenna’s Velvetleaf
<note>original: "Abutilon Auicenna." This plant, now known as *Abutilon theophrasti*, belongs to the mallow family. It was named after the Persian philosopher and physician Avicenna (Ibn Sina). Its heart-shaped leaves are notably soft to the touch, and its unique, star-shaped seed pods were a point of fascination for early botanists.</note>

->III.<-
### Moldavian Balm with
### blue flowers
<note>original: "Melissa Moldavica flore coeruleo." Known today as the Moldavian Dragonhead (*Dracocephalum moldavica*), this plant is native to central Asia. It was a popular addition to 17th-century gardens for its refreshing lemon-scented leaves and its ability to attract bees with its vibrant blue-purple flowers.</note>

<column-break/>

->I.<-
### Fragrant Spiny
### Shellflower
<note>original: "Moluca odorata spinosa." This is an early description of the plant now called Bells of Ireland (*Moluccella laevis*). The "shellflower" name comes from the large, cup-shaped green calyxes that surround the small, fragrant white flowers. Though the name "Moluca" suggests the Molucca Islands, the plant is actually native to the Mediterranean region.</note>

<column-break/>

### Common 
### Lemon Balm
<note>original: "Melissa Officinarum." This is the classic lemon balm (*Melissa officinalis*), a staple of European herb gardens. The term <term>officinarum</term> signifies its status as an "officinal" herb, meaning it was a standard item kept in an apothecary's shop for its recognized medicinal properties, particularly for soothing the nerves and heart.</note>

->II.<-
## Moldavian Balm with white flowers
<note>original: "Melissa Moldavica flor: albo." This is the white-flowering variety of the Moldavian Dragonhead (*Dracocephalum moldavica*). Early modern botanists often grouped these by color; like its blue-flowered relative, it was highly prized for its lemony fragrance and its leaves, which were used to create "cordial" waters intended to lift the spirits.</note>

<column-break/>

->I.<-
## Smooth Shellflower
<note>original: "Molucca Levis." Known today as Bells of Ireland (*Moluccella laevis*). The Latin term <term>levis</term> translates to "smooth," distinguishing this plant from the spiny version shown previously. The "bells" are actually enlarged green <term>calyxes</term> (the outer part of a flower) that surround small, fragrant white blossoms. Although the name suggests an Indonesian origin, the plant actually hails from the Mediterranean.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates two specific botanical variations: a white-flowering version of the Moldavian Balm and the smooth-stemmed variety of the Shellflower, known today as Bells of Ireland.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the decorative garden balsams and exotic "bells" of the previous page to the more robust, medicinal herbs of the mint family and their relatives. ->III.<-
## Costmary of the Apothecaries
<note>original: "Balsamita Officinarum." This plant, now known scientifically as *Tanacetum balsamita*, was a staple in medieval and Renaissance gardens. Although it resembles the mints in fragrance, it is actually a member of the daisy family. It was famously called "Alecost" because its bitter, aromatic leaves were used to flavor ale before hops became standard. The term <term>Officinarum</term> confirms its status as an "official" medicinal herb sold in apothecary shops.</note>

<column-break/>

->I.<-
## English Snowy Horse-mint
<note>original: "Mentaſtrum niveum Angl." A variety of wild mint (Mentastrum) noted for being "snowy" (*niveum*), which likely refers to the silvery-white downy hair on its leaves or its pale flowers. Early botanists used the name "English" to denote varieties common to the British Isles. Mint varieties were often difficult to categorize due to their tendency to hybridize.</note>

<column-break/>

->II.<-
## Wild Horse-mint
<note>original: "Mentaſtrum Sylveſtre." This represents the common wild mint (likely *Mentha longifolia*). The illustration particularly highlights the horizontal <term>stolons</term>—underground runners—that connect the plants, demonstrating the aggressive spreading habit that makes the mint family so resilient in the wild.</note>

<summary>This botanical plate illustrates three aromatic herbs: Costmary (used in brewing) and two varieties of wild "Horse-mint," specifically highlighting their interconnected root systems and medicinal status.</summary>

->II. German Catmint. &nbsp;&nbsp; I. Lesser Catmint. &nbsp;&nbsp; III. Cat's Mint.<-

->I.<-
## Lesser Catmint
<note>original: "Nepetella." The name is a diminutive form of <term>Nepeta</term>, indicating a smaller or more delicate variety. This specimen is depicted in its entirety, including the <term>fibrous root</term> system, which was often included in botanical engravings to aid collectors in identifying the plant in various stages of growth. Historically, these smaller varieties were used in herbal teas to treat "vapors" or nervous exhaustion.</note>

<column-break/>

->II.<-
## German Catmint
<note>original: "Nepeta Germanica." A tall, hardy variety of catmint. The illustration clearly shows the <term>lanceolate</term> (spear-shaped) leaves and the <term>verticillasters</term>—the specific way the flowers grow in whorls or rings around the stem. The "German" designation in early modern botany often referred to plants common in Central European forests and meadows, noted for their sturdy, upright habit.</note>

<column-break/>

->III.<-
## Cat’s Mint
<note>original: "Menta Felina." More commonly known today as Catnip (*Nepeta cataria*). The name "Feline Mint" refers to the well-known reaction domestic cats have to the plant's essential oils. This illustration emphasizes the plant's vigor, showing a much thicker stem and larger, heart-shaped leaves compared to its relatives. In the apothecary’s shop, it was valued for its ability to induce sweating and break fevers.</note>

<summary>This botanical plate illustrates three members of the catmint family (Nepeta), detailing their variations in size and structure, from the slender "Lesser" variety to the robust "Cat's Mint" used in medicine.</summary>

<keywords>Catmint, Nepeta, Nepetella, Catnip, German Catmint, medicinal herbs, Lamiaceae, botany</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the Catmints (Nepeta) of the previous plate to other members of the Lamiaceae family and a distinctive variety of nettle. ->III. Spiked Water Pennyroyal. &nbsp;&nbsp; I. Curled Mint. &nbsp;&nbsp; II. Roman Nettle.<-

->I.<-
## Curled Mint
<note>original: "Mentha Crispa." The name "crispa" refers to the highly ruffled or "crisped" margins of the leaves. This variety was a staple in apothecary gardens, prized more for its high concentration of essential oils than the common garden mint. It was frequently used in distilled waters to treat stomach ailments and "windy" complaints.</note>

<column-break/>

->II.<-
## Roman Nettle
<note>original: "Vrtica Romana." Unlike the common stinging nettle, the Roman Nettle (*Urtica pilulifera*) is distinguished by its fruit—shown here as small, bristly green spheres or "pills" at the leaf joints. Legend held that Roman soldiers brought the seeds to Britain to sow so they could rub the stinging leaves on their limbs to keep warm in the cold northern climate. In early medicine, "urtication" (purposeful stinging) was used to treat paralysis and rheumatism.</note>

<column-break/>

->III.<-
## Spiked Water Pennyroyal
<note>original: "Pulegium aquaticum spicatum." The term <term>Pulegium</term> is derived from the Latin *pulex* (flea), referring to the plant's traditional use as a scorched or fresh herb to repel insects. This "spiked" variety is shown with its elongated flower clusters, growing in its preferred damp or marshy habitat. It was considered a "hot" herb in the humoral system, used to provoke menstruation and clear the lungs.</note>

<summary>This botanical plate presents three distinct medicinal herbs: Curled Mint, the globular-fruited Roman Nettle, and Spiked Water Pennyroyal. It highlights the morphological diversity of garden herbs used for everything from digestive aids to pest repellents.</summary>

<keywords>Curled Mint, Roman Nettle, Pennyroyal, Mentha Crispa, Urtica Romana, medicinal botany, apothecary garden, insect repellent</keywords>

-> II <-
-> **Cretan Horehound with somewhat narrow leaves** <-
<note>original: "Marrubium Creticum angustis folijs modicum." This variety, sourced from the island of Crete, was highly regarded by Renaissance botanists for its perceived superior potency compared to local varieties. The "narrow leaves" mentioned in the title distinguish it from its broader-leaved cousins.</note>

<column-break/>

-> I <-
-> **Common Horehound** <-
<note>original: "Marrubium vulgare." A rugged, woolly herb with a distinctively bitter taste. In the 16th and 17th centuries, it was the primary remedy for "tough phlegm" and chest complaints. It remains well-known today as the flavoring for traditional horehound cough drops. Its name may derive from the Old English *har* (hoary/white) and *hune* (herb), referring to the white, downy hairs covering its stems.</note>

<column-break/>

-> III <-
-> ***Saracen Mint*** <-
<note>original: "Mentha Sarracenica." Despite the name, this plant is often identified as Costmary or "Alecost" (*Tanacetum balsamita*). It was called "Saracen" because it was believed to have been brought to Europe from the East by Crusaders. It has a sweet, balsamic scent and was commonly used to flavor ale and as a fragrant bookmark in Bibles, earning it the nickname "Bible Leaf."</note>

<summary>This page illustrates two varieties of Horehound—valued for their respiratory healing properties—and Saracen Mint, a fragrant herb used in brewing and as a perfume.</summary>

-> II <-
-> **Common Mountain Calamint** <-
<note>original: "Calamintha montana vulgaris." The name Calamint derives from the Greek *kalos* (beautiful) and *minthe* (mint). This "common" variety was a frequent sight in hilly pastures. Early herbalists classified it as a "hot and dry" herb, recommending it to "purge the brain of phlegm" and to help with shortness of breath. Its small, rounded leaves and delicate clusters of blossoms distinguish it from its larger relative.</note>

<column-break/>

-> I <-
-> **Superior Mountain Calamint** <-
<note>original: "Calamintha montana præstantior." Termed "superior" or "more excellent" due to its larger size and more vigorous growth, this variety features the broader, serrated leaves and prominent, bell-shaped flowers depicted on the right of the plate. Because of its stronger aromatic scent—resembling a mix of mint and pennyroyal—it was often preferred by apothecaries for making medicinal syrups and cordials to aid digestion.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates two varieties of Mountain Calamint, a medicinal herb used since antiquity to treat respiratory and digestive ailments.</summary>

-> III <-
-> **Buck's-horn Plantain** <-
<note>original: "Coronopus." The name is a compound of the Greek words for "crow" and "foot," aptly describing the narrow, deeply divided leaves that spread out at the base of the plant like a bird's footprint. In the Renaissance, this plant was frequently used in cooling salads or applied as a poultice to soothe inflammatory conditions and "hot" tumors.</note>

<column-break/>

-> I <-
-> **Syrian Sea-Lavender** <-
<note>original: "Limonium Syriacum." While not a true lavender, this plant belongs to the *Limonium* genus, known for its "everlasting" papery flowers. The "Syrian" epithet marks it as an exotic specimen brought to European botanical gardens from the Near East. It is depicted here with its characteristic broad, wavy leaves and a tall, branching flower stalk.</note>

<column-break/>

-> II <-
-> **Narrow-leaved Calamint** <-
<note>original: "Calamentha Angustifolijs." Following the Calamints described on the previous page, this variety is distinguished by its more slender, "narrow" (*angustifolia*) foliage. Like its cousins, it was a staple of the apothecary's garden, prized for its sharp, aromatic scent and its ability to "open" the chest and stomach by clearing away phlegm and internal coldness.</note>

<summary>This illustrated plate presents three distinct medicinal and ornamental plants: the Buck's-horn Plantain, the Syrian Sea-Lavender, and the Narrow-leaved Calamint, highlighting their unique leaf structures and growth habits.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the botanical survey from the previous plates. Having concluded the discussion on plantains and calamints, the work now transitions to varieties of Basil (Ocimum), specifically focusing on exotic and intermediate forms.</meta>

->I.<-
->**Indian Spotted Basil**<-
<note>original: "Basilicum Indicum maculatum." The term *maculatum* refers to the irregular dark patches or "spots" on the broad leaves, a distinctive feature of this variety. During this period, "Indian" (*Indicum*) was a broad descriptor for plants introduced from the East Indies or surrounding tropical regions, prized in European gardens for their exotic appearance and intense aroma.</note>

---

->II.<-
->**Intermediate Basil**<-
<note>original: "Basilicum Medium." This variety, also known as "Middle Basil," was classified by Renaissance botanists as being of moderate height and leaf size, sitting between the larger "Great Basil" and the smaller, fine-leaved "Bush Basil." Like its relatives, it was used to treat "melancholy" and to provide a sweet fragrance to the household.</note>

<summary>This illustrated page depicts two varieties of basil: the Indian Spotted Basil, notable for its variegated foliage, and the Intermediate Basil, shown with its characteristic serrated leaves and flowering spikes.</summary>

<keywords>Indian Spotted Basil, Intermediate Basil, Ocimum basilicum, botanical illustration, aromatic herbs, Renaissance botany</keywords>

--- Page 253 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of basil varieties (Ocimum). While the previous page focused on "Indian" and "Intermediate" types, this plate illustrates three distinct cultivars distinguished by their leaf shape and scent: the large-leaved variety, the ruffled or curled variety, and the small-leaved clove-scented variety.</meta>

->II.<-
->**Green Curled Basil**<-
<note>original: "Ocimum Crispum viride." The descriptor *crispum* refers to the distinctively ruffled or "frizzled" margins of the leaves. This variety was highly valued in Renaissance gardens for its ornamental texture and was often used as a decorative border plant.</note>

->I.<-
->**Great Broad-leaved Basil**<-
<note>original: "Ocimum latifolium magnum." This is the "Great Basil" that remains the ancestor of many modern culinary basils. The term *latifolium* (broad-leaved) distinguishes it from the narrower or smaller varieties. It was prized for its vigorous growth and its ability to produce many flowering spikes, as seen in the five vertical stems depicted here.</note>

->III.<-
->**Small Clove-scented Basil**<-
<note>original: "Ocimum minimum gariophyllatum." This variety is known today as "Bush Basil" or "Greek Basil." The name *gariophyllatum* indicates a scent reminiscent of cloves (*Caryophyllus*). Because of its compact, rounded growth habit (the "minimum" or "smallest" type), it was traditionally grown in pots and kept on windowsills to refresh the air and deter flies.</note>

<summary>This plate illustrates three distinct varieties of basil, showcasing the diversity of the Ocimum genus through its leaf structures—ruffled, broad, and miniature—and its specific fragrance profiles, such as the clove-scented cultivar.</summary>

<keywords>Green Curled Basil, Great Broad-leaved Basil, Small Clove Basil, Ocimum basilicum, botanical illustration, herb gardens, clove scent, Lamiaceae</keywords>

--- Page 254 ---
<meta>Following the previous page's focus on the diverse varieties of Basil (Ocimum), this plate shifts attention to two different genera of medicinal and ornamental herbs: Teucrium (Germander) and Thlaspi (Penny-cress). The transition illustrates the collector's interest in grouping plants by their geographic origins or visual similarities, moving from culinary herbs to those often found in rock gardens or used in early pharmacy.</meta>

->III.<-
->**Cappadocian Penny-cress with white flowers**<-
<note>original: "Thlaspi Cappadocicum flore albo." The name *Thlaspi* was used by early botanists to describe a wide range of "cress" plants in the mustard family, often including what we now call Candytuft (*Iberis*). This specific variety is noted as coming from Cappadocia, an ancient region in modern-day Turkey, reflecting the Renaissance fascination with "exotic" flora from the Levant.</note>

->I.<-
->**True Germander**<-
<note>original: "Teucrium Verum." This plant, likely *Teucrium marum* (Cat Thyme), is not a true thyme but a member of the Germander genus. It was called "True" to distinguish it from other wood-sage varieties. It is famous for its intense, pungent aroma that—much like catnip—is known to provoke a strong reaction in cats.</note>

->II.<-
->**Cappadocian Penny-cress with pink flowers**<-
<note>original: "Thlaspi Cappadocicum flore incarnat." The descriptor *incarnatum* (literally "flesh-colored") refers to the soft pink or blush hue of the petals. Shown here alongside its white-flowered relative (Item III), it demonstrates how early botanical catalogs categorized plants primarily by their color variations and their place of origin.</note>

<sig>JOZ</sig>

<summary>This botanical plate illustrates two distinct species: the aromatic True Germander and two color variations of Penny-cress (or Candytuft) sourced from Cappadocia. These plants were valued in the Early Modern period for both their ornamental beauty in rockeries and their potential medicinal uses as stimulants or treatments for scurvy.</summary>

<keywords>True Germander, Teucrium marum, Penny-cress, Thlaspi, Cappadocia, botanical illustration, Mediterranean flora, medicinal herbs, 17th-century botany</keywords>

--- Page 255 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page's description of Germander and Penny-cress, this plate introduces three distinct species from different families: a night-blooming herb, an aromatic flower from Crete, and a specialized variety of Toothwort. The transition highlights the Renaissance botanist's focus on geographic origins—moving from Cappadocia to Crete—and the specific physiological traits of plants, such as night-blooming cycles and unusual root structures.</meta>

II.
**Special narrow-leaved berry-bearing Toothwort**
<note>original: "Dentaria angustifolia baccifera peculiaris." The genus name *Dentaria* (now categorized under *Cardamine*) comes from the Latin *dens* (tooth), referring to the tooth-like scales on the rhizome, seen here in the knobby root system. While the author calls it "berry-bearing" (*baccifera*), these are actually small bulbils found in the leaf axils that the plant uses to reproduce. It is likely the Coralroot Bittercress.</note>

->I.<-
->**Cretan Gold-flower**<-
<note>original: "Helichryson Creticum." Known today as "Everlasting" or "Strawflower," the name comes from the Greek words for "sun" and "gold." This species from the island of Crete was celebrated for its clusters of yellow flower heads that maintain their color and shape long after being dried, symbolizing immortality in the early modern period.</note>

III.
**White-flowered night-blooming Campion**
<note>original: "Ocimastrum noctiflorum flore albo." The name *Ocimastrum* suggests a plant that resembles basil (*Ocimum*) in its leaf shape. This specific "night-flowering" (*noctiflorum*) variety is famous for its blossoms that open in the late afternoon or evening, releasing a potent fragrance to attract moths for pollination. In modern botany, this is likely *Silene noctiflora*.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three distinct botanical specimens: the Cretan Gold-flower (Helichrysum), a specialized variety of Toothwort with reproductive bulbils, and a night-blooming white campion. The plate emphasizes the physical diversity of root systems and the specific environmental adaptations of the plants, such as nocturnal blooming.</summary>

<keywords>Toothwort, Helichrysum, Cretan Gold-flower, Night-flowering Campion, botanical illustration, rhizomes, bulbils, Mediterranean flora</keywords>

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; III. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; II. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; I.

*Small pinnate Sage. Spanish Sage with white flowers. Large broad-leaved Sage*
<note>original: "Salvia minor pinnata. Salvia Hispanica flore albo. Salvia maior latifoliis flore cæruleo." The word *Salvia* comes from the Latin *salvere*, meaning "to heal" or "to be healthy," reflecting the plant's ancient reputation as a medicinal powerhouse. "Pinnate" (III) refers to leaves arranged like a feather; "flore albo" (II) means white-flowered; and "flore cæruleo" (I) means blue-flowered.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; *with blue flowers.*

### III. Small pinnate Sage
<note>Likely *Salvia pinnata*. Unlike the common garden sage, this variety has deeply lobed or "pinnate" leaves, where the leaf is divided into smaller leaflets along a central stalk. In the Renaissance, such distinctions were vital for identifying specific medicinal properties associated with different leaf morphologies.</note>

### II. Spanish Sage with white flowers
<note>Likely a white-flowering variant of *Salvia lavandulifolia* or *Salvia hispanica*. While Spanish Sage is typically known for its lavender-blue blossoms, early modern botanists meticulously documented color variations (albinism in plants) as distinct horticultural entries.</note>

### I. Large broad-leaved Sage with blue flowers
<note>This is the classic Common Sage (*Salvia officinalis*), the most familiar variety used in both the kitchen and the apothecary. The label "maior latifoliis" (greater broad-leaved) distinguishes it from the narrower-leaved varieties, emphasizing its robust growth and the vibrant blue-purple flowers characteristic of the species.</note>

<summary>This page presents a comparative botanical illustration of three Sage (Salvia) varieties, distinguishing them by their leaf structures—pinnate, lanceolate, and broad-leaved—and their flower colors.</summary>
<keywords>Sage, Salvia, botanical illustration, pinnate leaves, Spanish Sage, Salvia officinalis, medicinal herbs, Renaissance botany</keywords>

Clary was frequently referred to as "Clear-eye" (or *Oculus Christi*) during the Renaissance because its seeds produce a thick mucilage when soaked, which was used to safely catch and remove dust or seeds from the eye.</note>

->I.<-
->Ethiopian Sage<-
<note>original: "Aethiopis." This plant, known today as *Salvia aethiopis* or Mediterranean Sage, was named for its supposed origin in Ethiopia. Ancient authorities like Dioscorides and Pliny the Elder described it as having woolly, jagged leaves and a thick root, features that are meticulously rendered in this engraving.</note>

<term>Aethiopis: A species of sage often called "Woolly Sage" due to the dense, white hairs covering its foliage. Historically, it was believed to have drying properties and was used in various medicinal preparations.</term>

### I.
## Jupiter's Distaff.
<note>original: "Colus Iovis." This plant is now scientifically classified as *Salvia glutinosa*. The common name "Jupiter's Distaff" refers to the tall, thin flowering spikes which were thought to resemble the distaff used in ancient spinning.</note>

<term>Vocabulary: Jupiter's Distaff, Sticky Sage <note>Latin: *Salvia glutinosa*</note>, botanical illustration, engraving.</term>

->I.<-
->Ethiopian Sage<-
<note>original: "Aethiopis." This plant, known today as *Salvia aethiopis* or Mediterranean Sage, was named for its supposed origin in Ethiopia. Ancient botanical authorities like Dioscorides and Pliny the Elder described it as having woolly, jagged leaves and a thick root—features that are meticulously rendered in this copperplate engraving.</note>

<term>Ethiopian Sage: A species of sage often called "Woolly Sage" due to the dense, white hairs covering its foliage. Historically, it was believed to have drying properties and was used in various medicinal preparations to treat "moist" ailments.</term>

<meta>This page continues the botanical survey from the previous entries on Sages (Salvia). In early modern botany, these were often grouped nearby due to their similar hairy leaves and clusters of small, five-petaled flowers.</meta>

# Varieties of Bugloss

<note>The name "Bugloss" comes from the Greek *bous* (ox) and *glossa* (tongue), referring to the plant's rough, rasping leaves which were thought to resemble the tongue of an ox. Today, these plants are generally classified under the genus *Anchusa* or *Pentaglottis*.</note>

->II.<-
->Italian Bugloss with white flowers.<-
<note>original: "Bugloßum Italicum flore albo." This is likely a white-flowering variety of *Anchusa italica* (now *Anchusa azurea*). Although the species is famous for its vibrant blue, early botanists carefully documented rare color variations like this white form.</note>

<column-break/>

->I.<-
->Broad-leaved Spanish Evergreen Bugloss<-
<note>original: "Bugloßum semper virens Hispanicum latifolium." This plant is likely *Pentaglottis sempervirens*, known today as Green Alkanet. Unlike many other buglosses that wither after flowering, this variety stays green throughout the year (semper virens) and is distinguished by its wider (latifolium) leaves.</note>

<column-break/>

->III.<-
->Italian Bugloss with blue flowers.<-
<note>original: "Bugloßum Italicum flore coeruleo." This represents the classic blue-flowering Italian Bugloss (*Anchusa azurea*). It was highly valued in Renaissance gardens for the intensity of its blue pigment, which was rare in the floral world.</note>

***

### Botanical Glossary

**Bugloss** 
<term>Bugloßum: A group of plants known for their bristly stems and leaves. In the 17th century, they were frequently used in "cordials" (medicines intended to comfort the heart) and were said to drive away sadness and promote cheerfulness.</term>

**Geographic Origins**
<term>Italian (Italicum) and Spanish (Hispanicum): These labels indicate the regions where these specific varieties were first identified or where they were known to grow most vigorously. Early modern herbals often categorized plants by their country of origin.</term>

**Descriptive Characteristics**
<term>Broad-leaved (latifolium): A term used to distinguish a plant from narrower-leaved relatives.</term>
<term>Evergreen (semper virens): Literally "always green," referring to plants that do not lose their foliage in winter.</term>
<term>White-flowered (flore albo) and Blue-flowered (flore coeruleo): Color was a primary means of distinguishing varieties before the advent of modern genetic classification.</term>

<summary>This page illustrates three distinct varieties of Bugloss, focusing on the Italian and Spanish types. It highlights the 17th-century botanical emphasis on flower color and leaf shape as primary markers for plant identification.</summary>

<keywords>Bugloss, Anchusa, Italian Bugloss, Spanish Bugloss, Green Alkanet, botanical illustration, 17th-century herbal, medicinal plants</keywords>

These plants were traditionally grouped together because of their shared characteristics: rough, hairy foliage and clusters of small, five-petaled flowers.</meta>

# Varieties of Hound’s-tongue and Bugloss

<note>The plants on this page belong to the Borage family. They are characterized by their "scorpioid cymes"—flower spikes that curl like a scorpion's tail—and leaves that are often covered in soft or bristly hairs.</note>

->II.<-
->Common Hound’s-tongue<-
<note>original: "Cynogloßum vulgare." Now known scientifically as *Cynoglossum officinale*. The name derives from the Greek *kynos* (dog) and *glossa* (tongue), because the leaf's shape and soft, downy texture were thought to resemble a dog's tongue. In traditional herbalism, it was used as a cooling herb for inflammatory "hot" ailments.</note>

<column-break/>

->I.<-
->Common Bugloss in violet and white colors<-
<note>original: "Bugloßum vulgare violaceo & albo colore." This is likely *Anchusa officinalis*. It is noted here for its color variety; the flowers of this family often change hue as they age, shifting from white or pink to a deep violet-blue, sometimes appearing in multiple colors on a single plant.</note>

<column-break/>

->III.<-
->Hound’s-tongue of Narbonne<-
<note>original: "Cynogloßum Narbonense." This refers to a Mediterranean variety, likely *Cynoglossum creticum* (Blue Hound's-tongue). It was common for early modern botanists to distinguish species by the specific region where they were found, such as Narbonne in Southern France.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three members of the Borage family, focusing on the Common Hound's-tongue, the Narbonne variety of Hound's-tongue, and the color-shifting Common Bugloss. These plants were valued both for their unique textures and their various applications in early pharmaceutical preparations.</summary>

<keywords>Hound's-tongue, Bugloss, Cynoglossum officinale, Anchusa officinalis, Narbonne, botanical illustration, herbal medicine, Boraginaceae</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the study of the Borage family (Boraginaceae), moving from the Hound's-tongue and Bugloss of the previous page to the genus Symphytum, commonly known as Comfrey. Comfrey was one of the most esteemed medicinal plants in the early modern "physic garden" due to its reputation for healing.</meta>

# Varieties of Comfrey

<note>Comfrey plants are easily recognized by their large, hairy, pointed leaves and clusters of bell-shaped flowers that droop toward the ground. The genus name *Symphytum* is derived from the Greek *symphyo*, meaning "to grow together," a reference to the plant's long-standing use in traditional medicine to "knit" together broken bones and heal deep wounds.</note>

->III.<-
->Comfrey with pale flowers<-
<note>original: "Symphÿtum flore pallido." This describes a light-colored or cream-white variety of *Symphytum officinale*. Early botanists often classified plants primarily by their flower color and size before the modern system of binomial nomenclature was fully established.</note>

<column-break/>

->I.<-
->Greater Comfrey with purple flowers<-
<note>original: "Symphÿtum maius flore purpureo." This is the common purple-flowered variety of *Symphytum officinale*. The designation "Greater" (Latin: *maius*) distinguishes it from other plants that were thought to have similar healing properties but grew smaller or had different leaf structures.</note>

<column-break/>

->II.<-
->Greater Comfrey with red flowers<-
<note>original: "Symphÿtum maius flore rubro." A reddish or deep rose-colored variant. Like many members of this family, the pigments in the petals can change slightly depending on the age of the blossom or the acidity of the soil.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three color varieties of Comfrey (Symphytum), an essential medicinal herb known in the Renaissance as "knit-bone" for its supposed ability to heal fractures and wounds. The detailed engravings emphasize the plant's distinctive hairy leaves and drooping, bell-like blossoms.</summary>
<keywords>Comfrey, Symphytum, medicinal plants, botany, Renaissance herbalism, bone-setting, purple flowers, red flowers</keywords>

--- Page 263 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the medicinal Comfrey of the previous section to the genus *Hyoscyamus*, commonly known as Henbane. While Comfrey was used for healing wounds, Henbane belongs to the Solanaceae (Nightshade) family and was known for its much more potent, narcotic, and toxic properties.</meta>

# Varieties of Henbane

<note>Henbane has a long and storied history in herbal medicine and folklore. Containing powerful alkaloids such as hyoscyamine and scopolamine, it was used historically as a sedative and painkiller, but also gained notoriety as a key ingredient in "flying ointments" associated with witchcraft. The name "Henbane" reflects its toxicity to domestic fowl.</note>

->II.<-
->*White Henbane*<-
<note>original: "Hyosciamus albus." This species is distinguished by its pale, creamy-yellow flowers and lacks the dark purple veining often seen in the common variety. In historical medicine, the white variety was often preferred over the black or common variety because it was considered slightly milder and less dangerous to use.</note>

<column-break/>

->I.<-
->*Common Henbane*<-
<note>original: "Hyosciamus vulgaris." Also known as Black Henbane due to the dark purple centers and veins of its flowers. It is a robust, foul-smelling plant covered in sticky, glandular hairs. The illustration captures the plant's characteristic seed pods, which are shaped like little pots with lids.</note>

<note>The initials "D.K." found on the stem of the Common Henbane refer to Dominicus Custos, a master engraver from Augsburg who was instrumental in producing the high-quality copperplates for Besler's *Hortus Eystettensis*.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates two species of Henbane, White and Common, highlighting their distinct leaf shapes and flowering patterns. These plants were significant in early modern botany for their powerful narcotic effects and their dual role as both a medicine and a poison.</summary>

<keywords>Henbane, Hyoscyamus, medicinal plants, Nightshade family, botanical engraving, Dominicus Custos, toxicity, narcotic herbs</keywords>

Although sharing the name "Hellebore" with the Christmas Rose, these plants (*Veratrum*) were historically categorized by their medicinal properties as powerful purgatives. # Varieties of White Hellebore

<note>Despite the name "Hellebore," the plants on this page are known today as *Veratrum album* or False Hellebore. In the early modern period, they were classified by their roots rather than their floral structure. These plants are highly toxic and contain alkaloids that can slow the heart; historically, they were used in very small doses to treat "melancholy" or as a powder to induce sneezing.</note>

->I.<-
->*White Hellebore with*<-
->*greenish flowers*<-
<note>original: "Heleborus albus flore herbaceo." This variety is characterized by its star-shaped blossoms that match the pale green color of the plant's stems. The large, pleated leaves are a distinctive feature of the *Veratrum* genus.</note>

<column-break/>

->II.<-
->*White Hellebore with flowers*<-
->*turning dark red*<-
<note>original: "Helleborus albus flore atro rubescens." A color variant of the species where the flowers take on a deep, dusky red or brownish hue. The illustrator has carefully rendered the fibrous root system on the left, which was the most important part of the plant for the apothecaries of the time.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates two varieties of White Hellebore, a plant used cautiously in Renaissance medicine as a potent purgative and treatment for mental ailments. The engraving highlights the plant's unique pleated leaves and the distinction between its greenish and dark-red flowering forms.</summary>

<keywords>White Hellebore, Veratrum album, medicinal plants, purgatives, botanical illustration, toxic herbs</keywords>

While the previous plants were associated with purging, these plants were traditionally valued for their ability to heal wounds and their resinous, berry-like fruits.</meta>

# Varieties of St. John’s Wort and Tutsan

<note>The plants on this page belong to the genus *Hypericum*. In the Renaissance, these were often called "Androsaemum" (from the Greek for "man's blood") because the berries of some species produce a deep red juice that resembles blood. They were staples of the "vulnerary" or wound-healing pharmacopeia.</note>

->II. Stinking Tutsan<-
->also known as Goat-weed.<-
<note>original: "Endrosæmum fæditum seu Tragodes." The name *Androsaemum* is a Latinization of the Greek word for Tutsan. The terms "fæditum" (stinking) and "Tragodes" (goat-like) refer to the strong, often unpleasant scent of the foliage, characteristic of the species *Hypericum hircinum*.</note>

->I. Sicilian St. John’s Wort.<-
<note>original: "Siciliana." This refers to a large-leaved variety of Tutsan. The artist has emphasized the "runners" or stolons at the bottom of the plant, showing how it spreads across the ground. It is shown here with both flowers and the round berries that will eventually turn black.</note>

->III. St. John’s Wort<-
<note>original: "Hypericon." This is the common St. John’s Wort (*Hypericum perforatum*). It is distinguished from the Tutsans by its more delicate, upright habit and its many long, hair-like stamens that give the yellow flowers a fuzzy appearance. It was famously used to treat "melancholy" and as a balm for burns.</note>

<summary>This botanical plate illustrates three species of the St. John's Wort family, highlighting the physical differences between the berry-bearing Tutsan and the common flowering St. John's Wort. These plants were vital in early modern medicine for treating both physical wounds and emotional ailments.</summary>

<keywords>St. John’s Wort, Tutsan, Hypericum, medicinal herbs, botanical illustration, wound healing, Hypericaceae</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: This page transitions from the healing and resinous herbs of the St. John’s Wort family to a selection of distinct flowering plants. These include the bog-dwelling Grass of Parnassus, the medicinal but toxic Castor plant, and a late-blooming bellflower associated with the renowned botanist Rembert Dodoens.</meta>

# Grass of Parnassus, Castor Bean, and Autumn Bellflower

<page-num>JJ4</page-num>
<sig>NN</sig>

->III.<-
->Grass of Parnassus.<-
<note>original: "Gramen Pernassi." Despite its name, this is not a grass but *Parnassia palustris*. It was named after Mount Parnassus in Greece, the home of the Muses; in the 16th century, it was admired for its elegant white flowers with distinct green veins, which were thought to represent purity and poetic inspiration.</note>

->I.<-
->Smaller Castor Bean.<-
<note>original: "Ricinus Minor." This is a smaller variety of the Castor Oil plant (*Ricinus communis*). The Latin name *Ricinus* literally means "tick," because the mottled seeds resemble the Mediterranean sheep tick. While the oil extracted from the seeds has long been used as a potent purgative, the seeds themselves contain ricin, a deadly toxin.</note>

->II.<-
->Dodoens’ Autumn Bellflower.<-
<note>original: "Campanula Autumnalis Dodonæi." This refers to a plant described by the influential Flemish physician Rembert Dodoens (Dodonæus). While called a "bellflower" here, modern botanists identify this as the Marsh Gentian (*Gentiana pneumonanthe*), prized for its brilliant blue, trumpet-shaped flowers that bloom in late summer and autumn.</note>

<summary>This botanical plate presents a trio of anatomically detailed plants, ranging from the delicate Grass of Parnassus to the medicinal Castor Bean and the Marsh Gentian. It highlights the Renaissance practice of naming plants after classical landmarks and contemporary scholars like Rembert Dodoens.</summary>

<keywords>Grass of Parnassus, Parnassia palustris, Ricinus Minor, Castor Oil Plant, Rembert Dodoens, Marsh Gentian, Gentiana pneumonanthe, botanical illustration, Renaissance medicine</keywords>

--- Page 267 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page’s description of the "Smaller Castor Bean" (Ricinus Minor), this plate illustrates its larger counterpart alongside a species of creeping clubmoss. The numbering on this plate focuses on these two specific botanical specimens, continuing the transition from decorative meadow flowers to plants with significant medicinal and pharmaceutical histories.</meta>

# Greater Castor Bean and Creeping Clubmoss

<sig>N N Jj 5</sig>

->II.<-
->Creeping Clubmoss<-
<note>original: "Muscus clavatus procubens" (typically spelled *procumbens*). Known today as *Lycopodium clavatum*, this is a primitive vascular plant rather than a true moss. It was historically valued for its spores, a fine yellow powder that is highly flammable and was used in early flash photography and as a coating for medicinal pills to prevent them from sticking together.</note>

->I.<-
->Greater Castor Bean<-
<note>original: "Ricinus maior." This is the primary species of the Castor Oil plant (*Ricinus communis*). In the 16th and 17th centuries, it was frequently called "Palma Christi" (the Palm of Christ) because its large, deeply lobed leaves resemble the palm of a hand. While the oil is a famous laxative, the plant is also the source of the potent toxin ricin.</note>

<margin>Heinrich Ulrich</margin>
<note>Heinrich Ulrich (c. 1572–1621) was a skilled German engraver from Nuremberg. His signature here marks the artistic contribution to the scientific accuracy of this botanical compendium.</note>

<summary>This illustrated page features two distinct botanical specimens: the "Greater" variety of the Castor Bean plant and a species of Creeping Clubmoss. The plate serves to document the anatomical structures of these plants, particularly the "tick-like" seeds of the Castor and the spore-bearing spikes of the Clubmoss.</summary>

<keywords>Greater Castor Bean, Ricinus maior, Creeping Clubmoss, Muscus clavatus, Lycopodium clavatum, Heinrich Ulrich, botanical illustration, Renaissance medicine</keywords>

->II.<-

### *White Double-flowered Wild Catchfly*
<note>original: "Lichnis Sylvestris fl: pleno albo." This specimen is a double-flowered variety of the White Campion (now classified as *Silene latifolia*). In botanical terms, "double-flowered" (*flore pleno*) refers to a mutation where the plant's stamens develop into extra petals, creating a lush, rose-like appearance that was highly sought after by 17th-century gardeners.</note>

<column-break/>

->I.<-

### Red Double-flowered 
### Wild Catchfly
<note>original: "Lichnis sylvestris flore pleno rubro." This is the double-flowered form of the Red Campion (*Silene dioica*). While the wild single-flowered version was common in meadows and hedgerows, this dense, multi-petaled version was considered a "garden wonder." The name "Catchfly" refers to the sticky secretions on the stems of some species in this family, which can trap small insects.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates two decorative varieties of Wild Catchfly, featuring white and red "double" blooms. These specimens represent the botanical fascination with horticultural sports—natural mutations that produced fuller, more complex flowers than their wild counterparts.</summary>

<keywords>Catchfly, Campion, Lychnis sylvestris, Silene dioica, Silene latifolia, double-flowered, flore pleno, botanical illustration, ornamental plants</keywords>

The "double" (*pleno*) mutation shown here creates a dense, many-petaled flower that was a centerpiece of 17th-century decorative gardens.</note>

The Rose Campion, now known scientifically as *Silene coronaria*, was prized for its luminous white or red petals which contrast sharply against its woolly, silver-gray stems and leaves. The term "simple" indicates a single row of five petals, the natural form of the wild plant.</note>

Heaths were often included in florilegiums for their fine, needle-like foliage and tiny, bell-shaped blossoms.</note>

->II.<-
->**Flesh-colored Maltese Cross**<-
<note>original: "Flos Constantinopolitanus incarnatus." Known in the 17th century as the "Flower of Constantinople," this species is recognized today as the Maltese Cross or Jerusalem Cross. The term "incarnatus" (flesh-colored) describes a soft pink or pale rose variety.</note>

->I.<-

<note>original: "Flos Constantinopolitanus miniatus." This is the most famous variety of the species. The term "miniatus" refers to a bright vermilion or cinnabar red. Each petal is deeply notched, creating a shape reminiscent of the cross worn by the Knights of Malta.</note>

->II.<-
->**Goat’s Beard**<-
<note>original: "Barba Capræ." This plant is known today by the scientific name *Aruncus dioicus*. It is a tall, woodland perennial easily recognized by its massive, branching plumes of tiny white flowers, which early botanists thought resembled the beard of a goat. In the 17th century, it was often grown in large gardens for its architectural presence.</note>

---

->I.<-
->**Broad-leaved Viper’s Grass**<-
<note>original: "Scorfonera latifolia." Known today as *Scorzonera hispanica* or Black Salsify. The name "Scorzonera" is thought to come from the Old French word *escorce* (bark) or the Italian *scorzone* (a venomous serpent), as the root was traditionally used as an antidote for snake bites. The term "latifolia" refers to its wide, lance-shaped leaves. While illustrated here for its beauty, the long, dark-skinned root remains a popular root vegetable in Europe.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates two hardy perennials: the plume-like Goat’s Beard and the Broad-leaved Viper’s Grass, the latter of which was valued both as a medicinal remedy for snakebites and as an edible root vegetable.</summary>

<keywords>Goat’s Beard, Aruncus dioicus, Viper’s Grass, Scorzonera hispanica, Black Salsify, botanical illustration, 17th-century gardening, medicinal plants, edible roots</keywords>

--- Page 274 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page's description of Goat’s Beard and Viper’s Grass, this page presents two more perennial herbs: Pliny’s Tooth-wort and the pungent Stinking Gum-succory.</meta>

->II.<-
->**Pliny’s Tooth-wort**<-
<note>original: "Odontitis Plinij." The name is derived from the Greek word for tooth (*odous*). The Roman naturalist Pliny the Elder claimed that this plant, when decocted in wine, was a powerful remedy for toothaches. While its exact identity was often debated by early modern botanists, it is frequently associated with species like Red Bartsia or Great Burnet. The illustration displays its branching habit and the small, spherical flower heads that distinguish it.</note>

->I.<-
->**Stinking Gum-succory**<-
<note>original: "Condrilla fœtida." Known today as Stinking Hawksbeard (*Crepis foetida*). The genus name "Condrilla" (or *Chondrilla*) refers to the gummy or granular exudate found on the stems of similar plants. As the name "fœtida" suggests, the plant is known for its strong, somewhat unpleasant odor, often compared to bitter almonds. It is shown here with its basal rosette of narrow leaves and its large, dandelion-like composite flowers.</note>

<summary>This page features two botanical specimens: Pliny's Tooth-wort, historically prized as a remedy for dental pain, and the Stinking Gum-succory, a member of the chicory family known for its distinctively sharp scent.</summary>

<keywords>Pliny’s Tooth-wort, Odontitis, Stinking Gum-succory, Condrilla foetida, Crepis foetida, botanical illustration, 17th-century herbal, medicinal plants, Pliny the Elder</keywords>

--- Page 275 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page's descriptions of Pliny’s Tooth-wort and Stinking Gum-succory, this page illustrates three varieties of Scabious, a group of flowering plants highly regarded in early modern medicine for treating skin ailments.</meta>

->II.<-
->**White-flowered Belgian Scabious**<-
<note>original: "Scabiosa Belgica flore albo." This variety of the pincushion flower was distinguished by early botanists for its origins in the Low Countries (modern-day Belgium and the Netherlands) and its pure white blossoms. The genus name *Scabiosa* is derived from the Latin *scabies* (the itch), reflecting the plant's traditional use in decoctions to treat skin diseases, including the plague and various rashes.</note>

<column-break/>

->I.<-
->**Alpine Centaury-like Scabious**<-
<note>original: "Scabiosa Alpina Centauroides." This is the most prominent specimen on the page, a robust mountain-dwelling species. The term "Centauroides" suggests its resemblance to the *Centauria* (Knapweed) family, particularly in the shape of its broad, serrated leaves. Known today as *Cephalaria alpina* or Giant Scabious, it was valued for its impressive height and large, spherical yellow or cream-colored flower heads.</note>

<column-break/>

->III.<-
->**Common Scabious**<-
<note>original: "Scabiosa Vulgaris." The "vulgaris" or common variety is likely the Field Scabious (*Knautia arvensis*). This wild-growing herb was a staple of the European landscape. Its flowers, which resemble small cushions stuck with pins (hence the folk name "Pincushion Flower"), were often used in syrups to treat chest complaints and respiratory issues.</note>

<summary>This botanical plate illustrates three species of Scabious, plants named for their historical role in curing skin conditions like scabies. The specimens range from the common field variety to the more rare Alpine and Belgian types.</summary>

<keywords>Scabious, Scabiosa, Pincushion Flower, Belgian Scabious, Alpine Scabious, Field Scabious, Cephalaria alpina, Knautia arvensis, herbal medicine, botanical engraving</keywords>

->III.<-
->**White-flowered Hungarian Scabious**<-
<note>original: "Scabiosa Pannonica flore albo." The term "Pannonica" refers to Pannonia, a province of the Roman Empire covering modern-day Hungary and parts of Austria and the Balkans. This variety is noted for its deeply lobed leaves and delicate white blossoms, standing in contrast to the typical purple or blue hues of the genus.</note>

<column-break/>

->I.<-
->**Great Spanish Scabious**<-
<note>original: "Scabiosa Hispanica maior." This is the central and most robust specimen on the plate. The name *Scabiosa* is derived from the Latin *scabies* (the itch), as the plant was famously used in traditional medicine to treat skin diseases, including scabies and even the sores of the plague. The "Great" (*maior*) designation refers to its significant size and large, rounded flower head.</note>

<column-break/>

->II.<-
->**Narrow-leaved Scabious with pale blue flowers**<-
<note>original: "Scabiosa Angustifolia flore subcaerul." The descriptive name *angustifolia* (narrow-leaved) highlights the plant's distinctive thin, needle-like foliage. The flowers are described as *subcaeruleo*, a Latin term for a light or sky-blue color, often appearing as a soft lavender in the field.</note>

<summary>This page provides a detailed botanical illustration of three Scabious varieties from different European regions—Hungary and Spain—as well as a narrow-leaved variety. These plants were highly valued in early modern medicine for their supposed ability to cure various skin ailments and respiratory conditions.</summary>

->II.<-
->**Indian Scabious with pale red flowers**<-
<note>original: "Scabiosa Indica flore dilute rubro." In the botanical language of the time, "Indica" (Indian) was a broad term often used for plants brought from overseas, including the East or West Indies and the Americas. The "pale red" flowers likely refer to a soft pink or rose-colored variety of what we now know as *Scabiosa atropurpurea*.</note>

<column-break/>

->I.<-
->**Indian Scabious with deep red flowers**<-
<note>original: "Scabiosa Indica flore saturate rubro." This is the central, most prominent specimen. The "saturated red" describes a dark, velvety crimson or maroon. Because of its dark, somber color, this specific plant later became known in English folklore as the "Mourning Bride" or "Pincushion Flower," the latter due to the way the stamens protrude from the flower head like pins in a cushion.</note>

<column-break/>

->III.<-
->**Fine-leaved Scabious with ash-gray flowers**<-
<note>original: "Scabiosa tenuifolia flore cinereo." The name *tenuifolia* (fine-leaved) refers to the highly dissected, lace-like foliage shown on the right. The flower color is described as *cinereo* (ash-like), suggesting a pale, silvery-grey or muted lavender hue.</note>

<meta>The monogram JZS in the bottom right corner likely identifies the engraver or artist responsible for the plate, possibly a member of the Sadeler family of engravers or a contemporary botanical artist like Johannes Zaluziansky, though such signatures were common to establish copyright and quality.</meta>

<note>original: "Wolf: Kilian sculp." Wolfgang Kilian (1581–1662) was a prominent German engraver from a famous family of artists in Augsburg, known for his high-quality botanical and anatomical plates.</note>

->I.<-
->**Female Mandrake**<-
<note>original: "Mandragora foemina." In early botanical traditions, mandrakes were often categorized by "gender" based on their appearance rather than biological sex. The "female" mandrake usually refers to *Mandragora autumnalis*, which has darker, broader leaves and a more complexly branched root system that was often mythologized to resemble a human figure.</note>

<column-break/>

->II.<-
->**Lesser Celandine**<-
<note>original: "Chelidonium minus." Today classified as *Ficaria verna* or the Lesser Celandine. In the 17th century, it was grouped with the "Greater Celandine" because both were used to treat eye ailments, though we now know they are from different botanical families (Buttercup vs. Poppy).</note>

<summary>A botanical copperplate engraving featuring the Female Mandrake with its distinctive anthropomorphic root and the Lesser Celandine, both significant in early modern herbal medicine.</summary>

--- Page 279 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page's focus on the Mandrake and Lesser Celandine, this page introduces the Valerian family. While the Mandrake was steeped in folklore, Valerians were prized in the early modern period for their more practical, observable medicinal properties, particularly as sedatives.</meta>

<page-type>illustration</page-type>

->II.<-
->**Greek Valerian with white flowers**<-
<note>original: "Valeriana Græca flore albo." Today known as Jacob’s Ladder (*Polemonium caeruleum*). Although called "Greek Valerian" by early botanists, it belongs to a different family than the true Valerians, but was grouped here due to the similar shape of its pinnate (feather-like) leaves.</note>

<column-break/>

->I.<-
->**Great Valerian**<-
<note>original: "Valeriana maior." This is the "True Valerian" (*Valeriana officinalis*). Since the Middle Ages, its root—depicted here as a thick, gnarled rhizome—has been used to treat nervousness and insomnia. The name "Valeriana" is derived from the Latin verb *valere*, meaning "to be strong" or "to be healthy."</note>

<column-break/>

->III.<-
->**Greek Valerian with blue flowers**<-
<note>original: "Valeriana Græca flore coeruleo." The blue-flowered variety of Jacob's Ladder. The name "Jacob's Ladder" refers to the ladder-like appearance of the leaflets, evoking the biblical story of Jacob's dream of a ladder reaching to heaven.</note>

<summary>A botanical illustration presenting three varieties of Valerian, distinguishing between the medicinal "Great Valerian" and the ornamental "Greek Valerian" (Jacob's Ladder) in both its white and blue forms.</summary>

<keywords>Great Valerian, Valeriana officinalis, Greek Valerian, Jacob's Ladder, Polemonium caeruleum, botanical illustration, herbal medicine, medicinal roots</keywords>

->III.<-

->**Black Hellebore of Dioscorides**<-
<note>original: "Veratrum nigrum Discoridis." Known today as Black False Hellebore. The reference to Dioscorides, a 1st-century Greek physician, signals the plant's long history in ancient medicine. Despite being highly toxic, it was used in controlled doses by early physicians to treat a variety of ailments, including "melancholy."</note>

<column-break/>

->I.<-

->**Soapwort**<-
<note>original: "Saponaria." This plant (*Saponaria officinalis*) takes its name from the Latin word *sapo* (soap). Its roots and leaves contain natural compounds called saponins, which produce a soapy lather when agitated in water. It was a staple in households and textile workshops for cleaning delicate fabrics.</note>

<note>The initials D.K. found on the leaf likely refer to the engraver Dominicus Custos, a Flemish artist who worked extensively in Augsburg.</note>

<column-break/>

->II.<-

->**Wild Valerian**<-
<note>original: "Valeriana Sylvestris." This refers to the wild-growing variety of the Valerian plant. Early botanists distinguished between the "Great Valerian" (often cultivated in gardens for medicine) and this "Wild" variety, though both were recognized for their ability to soothe the nervous system and aid sleep.</note>

<summary>A botanical plate featuring three plants with distinct uses: the medicinal yet toxic Black Hellebore, the cleansing Soapwort used for washing, and the sedative Wild Valerian.</summary>

<keywords>Black Hellebore, Veratrum nigrum, Soapwort, Saponaria officinalis, Wild Valerian, Valeriana sylvestris, Dioscorides, Dominicus Custos, botanical engraving, medicinal herbs</keywords>
