->**Spineless Yellow-Variegated Restharrow**<-
<note>original: "Ononis flore luteo variegata, non spinosa." Today, this is likely a variety of *Ononis*, commonly known as Restharrow. The name "Restharrow" comes from the plant’s tough, woody roots, which were said to be strong enough to "arrest" or stop a horse-drawn harrow in the fields. This specific variety is noted for its lack of thorns and its yellow-streaked blossoms.</note>

---

->**Syrian Bean-caper**<-
<note>original: "Capparis Fabago." Known scientifically today as *Zygophyllum fabago*. Despite the name, it is not a true caper, though its flower buds were historically pickled and used as a substitute for them. The name *Fabago* refers to the leaves, which resemble those of a broad bean (*Faba*).</note>

<summary>A detailed botanical plate illustrating the Restharrow, known for its field-stopping roots, and the Syrian Bean-caper, a plant whose buds served as a historical culinary substitute for true capers.</summary>

<keywords>Ononis, Restharrow, Capparis Fabago, Syrian Bean-caper, botanical illustration, medicinal plants, 17th-century botany</keywords>

->III.<-
->**Thorny Restharrow**<-
<note>original: "Anonis spinosa." Today scientifically known as *Ononis spinosa*. This specimen is characterized by the sharp spines along its stems, a natural defense that, combined with its tough roots, made it a notorious "rest-harrow"—a plant capable of stopping a horse-drawn harrow in its tracks.</note>

->I.<-
->**Yellow-flowered Restharrow**<-
<note>original: "Ononis luteo flore." A variety distinguished by its yellow blossoms. The engraving highlights its upright growth habit and the distinct, elongated seed pods that follow the flowering stage.</note>

->II.<-
->**Spineless Restharrow**<-
<note>original: "Anonis non spinosa." Now usually classified as *Ononis arvensis* or a smooth variety of *Ononis repens*. Unlike the thorny variety shown on the left, this plant lacks prickles, though it retains the signature thick, gnarled root system common to the genus.</note>

<summary>A botanical engraving illustrating three varieties of Restharrow, highlighting the physical differences between thorny, spineless, and yellow-flowered specimens and their characteristic hardy root systems.</summary>

<keywords>Restharrow, Ononis spinosa, Ononis luteo flore, Anonis non spinosa, botanical illustration, 17th-century herbal, medicinal plants, root systems</keywords>

->II.<-
->**Common Dropwort**<-
<note>original: "Filipendula vulgaris." This species remains known by this name today. The name "Filipendula" is a compound of the Latin *filum* (thread) and *pendulus* (hanging), which describes the way the plant's small, almond-shaped tubers appear to hang from the main root fibers—a feature clearly depicted in the engraving.</note>

->I.<-
->**Herb Christopher**<-
<note>original: "Christophoriana." Currently known as *Actaea spicata* or Baneberry. In the 17th century, it was frequently named after Saint Christopher. The illustration captures its characteristic serrated, compound leaves and the clusters of berries that distinguish it from the more delicate meadow-dwelling plants.</note>

<summary>A botanical illustration featuring two distinct plants: the Common Dropwort, noted for its tuberous hanging roots, and Herb Christopher (Baneberry), shown with its broad foliage and berry clusters.</summary>

<keywords>Common Dropwort, Filipendula vulgaris, Herb Christopher, Christophoriana, Actaea spicata, botanical engraving, 17th-century botany, medicinal plants, root morphology</keywords>

--- Page 284 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following page 131’s focus on the Common Dropwort and Herb Christopher, page 132 presents three varieties of Mullein and Moth Mullein. This transition shifts from the tuberous roots of the previous page to the tall, slender flowering spikes and broad foliage characteristic of the Verbascum and Blattaria genera.</meta>

->II.<-
->**Yellow-flowered Moth Mullein**<-
<note>original: "Blattaria flore luteo." The name *Blattaria* is derived from the Latin word *blatta*, meaning "moth" or "cockroach," as it was historically believed that these plants either repelled or attracted such insects. This species is known today as *Verbascum blattaria* f. *luteum*.</note>

->I.<-
->**White-flowered Moth Mullein**<-
<note>original: "Blattaria flore albo." This represents the white-flowering form of the Moth Mullein. In 17th-century botany, different color variations of the same plant were often categorized as distinct types.</note>

->III.<-
->**Pliny’s Moth Mullein, or**<-
->**Dark Mullein**<-
<note>original: "Blattaria Plinij, Verbascum nigrum." The label references Pliny the Elder, the Roman naturalist whose ancient works were the primary authority for Renaissance botanists. *Verbascum nigrum*, or Dark Mullein, is characterized by its heart-shaped leaves and yellow flowers with distinctive purple-haired filaments.</note>

<summary>A botanical plate illustrating three members of the Mullein family, focusing on color variations of the Moth Mullein and the Dark Mullein associated with the writings of Pliny the Elder.</summary>

<keywords>Moth Mullein, Blattaria, Verbascum nigrum, Pliny the Elder, Dark Mullein, botanical illustration, 17th-century botany, yellow flowers, white flowers</keywords>

--- Page 285 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following page 132’s focus on the Moth Mullein and Dark Mullein, page 133 continues the study of the Verbascum genus with the Great Broad-leaved Mullein and a variety described by Cesalpino. It also introduces the Sea Starwort (Tripolium), which, though not a mullein, shares the page due to its similar upright growth habit.</meta>

-> II. <-
-> **Great Broad-leaved Male Mullein** <-
<note>original: "Verbascum latifolium mas." In Renaissance botany, the term "male" (mas) was frequently used to describe the more robust, larger, or more vigorous-looking specimen of a plant group, rather than indicating its biological sex. This is likely the Great Mullein (*Verbascum thapsus*), famous for its thick, felt-like leaves.</note>

-> I. <-
-> **Common Sea Starwort of L'Obel** <-
<note>original: "Tripolium vulgare Lob." The abbreviation "Lob." refers to Matthias de L'Obel (1538–1616), a prominent Flemish botanist. This plant is known today as the Sea Aster (*Aster tripolium*), a perennial that thrives in salt marshes and along coastlines.</note>

-> III. <-
-> **Caesalpino's Fourth Mullein: with** <-
-> **pale flowers.** <-
<note>original: "Verbascum quartum Caesalpin: floribus pallidis." The label cites Andrea Cesalpino (1524–1603), an Italian physician and botanist who was one of the first to attempt a systematic classification of plants based on their physical structures. This specific variety was the fourth in his personal categorization of mulleins.</note>

<summary>This page features a botanical engraving of two varieties of Mullein and the Sea Starwort, highlighting the classification systems of influential 16th-century botanists like L'Obel and Cesalpino.</summary>

<keywords>Great Mullein, Sea Starwort, Sea Aster, Verbascum thapsus, Aster tripolium, Matthias de L'Obel, Andrea Cesalpino, botanical classification, 17th-century engraving</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: Following page 133’s exploration of Mulleins and the Sea Starwort, page 134 shifts to other tall-growing, yellow-flowering herbs frequently used in Renaissance medicine. The artist's signature "Swaertius. fe." (Emanuel Sweert made this) is visible on the plate, identifying the work as part of his famous 1612 Florilegium.</meta>

#### II
### ***Goldenrod***
<note>original: "Virga aurea." This plant, known today in the genus *Solidago*, was a staple of the medieval and Renaissance apothecary. Its name literally translates to "golden rod," a reference to its tall, bright yellow flowering spikes.</note>

#### I
### ***Saracen’s Woundwort***
<note>original: "Consolida Saracenica." The name "Consolida" is derived from the Latin *consolidare* ("to make whole" or "to make solid"), signifying its use in binding and healing wounds. The term "Saracen" suggests a historical belief that the plant's medicinal uses were learned from Middle Eastern healers during the Crusades.</note>

#### III
### ***Yellow Loosestrife***
<note>original: "Lysimachia lutea." Known today as *Lysimachia vulgaris*. According to ancient lore mentioned by Pliny, the name derives from King Lysimachus of Sicily, who reportedly placed the plant on the yokes of unruly oxen to "loose" their "strife" and keep them calm.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three significant medicinal herbs—Goldenrod, Saracen's Woundwort, and Yellow Loosestrife—noted for their tall stature and yellow blooms. The plate is signed by Emanuel Sweert, a prominent Dutch nurseryman and artist of the early 17th century.</summary>

<keywords>Goldenrod, Saracen's Woundwort, Yellow Loosestrife, Virga aurea, Consolida Saracenica, Lysimachia lutea, Emanuel Sweert, medicinal herbs, 17th-century botany, florilegium</keywords>

--- Page 287 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following page 134’s depiction of Goldenrod and Yellow Loosestrife, this plate continues the study of the genus *Lysimachia*. While page 134 focused on the common yellow variety, page 135 introduces specialized forms, including the "hooded" variety and the reddish-purple variety documented by the Italian botanist Mattioli.</meta>

->III.<-
->*Hooded Loosestrife*<-
<note>original: "Lysimachia Galericulata." Known today as *Scutellaria galericulata* or Marsh Skullcap. In the 17th century, it was often classified with Loosestrifes. The name "Galericulata" refers to the *galericulum* (a small cap or hood), describing the unique shape of the flower's protective calyx.</note>

->I.<-
->*Greater Pannonian*<-
->*Yellow Loosestrife*<-
<note>original: "Lysimachia lutea Pannonica maior." This specimen refers to a large yellow variety sourced from Pannonia, a Roman province encompassing parts of modern-day Hungary and Austria. Regional variants were of great interest to Renaissance collectors seeking to catalog the "Flora" of different empires.</note>

->II.<-
->*Mattioli’s Second Loosestrife*<-
->*with Reddish Flowers*<-
<note>original: "Lysimachia altera Matthioli flore rubescente." This is likely Purple Loosestrife (*Lythrum salicaria*). The label cites Pietro Andrea Mattioli, a famous 16th-century physician and botanist. In early botany, "altera" (the other) was a common way to distinguish a second species within the same general family.</note>

<summary>This page features a botanical engraving of three distinct plants categorized under the broad "Loosestrife" family: the hooded Marsh Skullcap, the Greater Pannonian Yellow Loosestrife, and the reddish-flowered variety described by the botanist Mattioli.</summary>

<keywords>Loosestrife, Marsh Skullcap, Pannonian Yellow Loosestrife, Purple Loosestrife, Pietro Andrea Mattioli, botanical engraving, 17th-century botany, Lysimachia, medicinal plants</keywords>

--- Page 288 ---
<meta>Continuity: Moving from the diverse species of Loosestrife categorized on page 135, this plate shifts focus to members of the Mustard family (Brassicaceae). These plants were highly valued in the 17th century not only for their botanical interest but for their dual roles as culinary spices and potent medicinal remedies.</meta>

->II.<-
->White Mustard<-
<note>original: "Sinapi albam." Today known as *Sinapis alba*. The White Mustard is a Mediterranean native characterized by its hairy stems and bright yellow flowers. In early modern medicine, its seeds were crushed and applied as "mustard plasters" to treat respiratory ailments and joint pain.</note>

->I.<-
->One-Seeded Garden Gold-of-Pleasure<-
<note>original: "Myagrum Hortense Monospermon." Now identified as *Myagrum perfoliatum* or Bird’s-foot. The name "monospermon" (one-seeded) distinguishes this species from its relatives which typically carry multiple seeds in their pods. The genus name *Myagrum* is derived from the Greek word for "fly-catcher," based on a historical—though mistaken—belief that the sticky residue of some related plants trapped insects.</note>

<summary>This botanical plate illustrates two significant members of the mustard family: the common White Mustard, used for both food and medicine, and the rarer One-Seeded Gold-of-Pleasure.</summary>

<keywords>White Mustard, Sinapis alba, Myagrum perfoliatum, Gold-of-Pleasure, Brassicaceae, medicinal herbs, 17th-century botany, botanical illustration, cruciferous plants</keywords>

--- Page 289 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the study of the Mustard family (Brassicaceae) on the previous page, this plate transitions to members of the Rose family (Rosaceae). Specifically, it highlights species of the genus Potentilla, known in the early modern period for their distinctive hand-shaped leaves and their "astringent" or drying medicinal properties.</meta>

<gloss>Engraved by Servatius Raeven.</gloss>
<note>Servatius Raeven was a Dutch engraver active in the late 16th and early 17th centuries, often collaborating on significant botanical works to provide the scientific accuracy required by scholars and physicians.</note>

->II.<-
->Greater Cinquefoil<-
<note>original: "Quinquefolium maius." The name literally translates to "great five-leaf," describing the plant's palm-shaped leaves. In the 17th century, the cooling properties of Cinquefoil were often used in syrups and ointments to treat "burning" fevers and skin inflammations.</note>

->I.<-
->Marsh Cinquefoil<-
<note>original: "Quinquefolium palustre." Now classified as *Comarum palustre*, this plant is uniquely adapted to peat bogs and marshes. The illustration carefully highlights the thick, horizontal <term>rhizome</term> (an underground stem), which was historically used to tan leather and produce a red dye.</note>

->III.<-
->Tormentil<-
<note>original: "Tormentilla." Today known as *Potentilla erecta*. This plant is distinguished from other cinquefoils by its four-petaled flowers rather than five. Its name comes from the Latin *tormentum*, referring to the "torment" of abdominal pain or dysentery, which the plant's tannin-rich root was famously used to cure.</note>

<summary>This botanical plate illustrates three medicinal members of the Rose family: the Greater Cinquefoil, the Marsh Cinquefoil, and the Tormentil, each valued for its specific healing properties and distinct root structures.</summary>

<keywords>Greater Cinquefoil, Marsh Cinquefoil, Tormentil, Potentilla, Rosaceae, Servatius Raeven, botanical engraving, medicinal herbs, rhizome, 17th-century pharmacy</keywords>

--- Page 290 ---
<meta>Continuity: While the previous page examined the Rosaceae (Rose family), specifically the Cinquefoils, this plate moves into the Fabaceae (Legume or Pea family). These plants, characterized here by their trifoliate leaves—clusters of three leaflets—were often categorized together in early herbals due to their similar leaf shapes and medicinal uses in treating inflammatory conditions.</meta>

->II. **Foreign Hare’s-foot Trefoil.** &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; I. **Cultivated Blue Melilot.** &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; III. **Greater Hare’s-foot Trefoil.**<-

->II.<-
->**Foreign Hare’s-foot Trefoil**<-
<note>original: "Lagopus peregrina." The name *Lagopus* comes from the Greek for "hare's foot," a reference to the soft, fuzzy texture of the flower heads which early botanists thought resembled the paw of a rabbit. This "foreign" variety was noted for its more slender stems compared to local meadow species.</note>

->I.<-
->**Cultivated Blue Melilot**<-
<note>original: "Lotus Urbana." Historically known as Blue Fenugreek or Sweet Trefoil (*Trigonella caerulea*). The term "Urbana" (urban or cultivated) distinguishes it from wild clovers. In the Renaissance, this plant was highly prized for its intense, spicy aroma; it was dried and placed in chests to scent linens and was a key ingredient in traditional Swiss green cheeses.</note>

->III.<-
->**Greater Hare’s-foot Trefoil**<-
<note>original: "Lagopus Maior." A larger relative of the common Hare's-foot clover. These plants contain <term>tannins</term> (astringent compounds), and in early medicine, they were often brewed into teas to "bind the belly" or stop hemorrhaging, following the common botanical logic that fuzzy or "hairy" plants could help staunch the flow of blood.</note>

<summary>This illustration depicts three members of the clover family, highlighting the "Hare's-foot" varieties named for their soft, tufted flower heads and the aromatic Cultivated Blue Melilot.</summary>

<keywords>Hare's-foot Trefoil, Blue Melilot, Lagopus, Lotus Urbana, Fabaceae, medicinal herbs, aromatic plants</keywords>

--- Page 291 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous discussion of Hare’s-foot clovers and Blue Melilot, this plate focuses on the Pitch Trefoil (Bituminaria bituminosa). These plants, while still categorized as trefoils due to their three-part leaves, were distinguished by early botanists for their unique chemical scents, moving from the sweet fragrance of the Melilot to the heavier, resinous odors of the "bituminous" varieties.</meta>

->II.<-
->**Scentless Pitch Trefoil**<-
<note>original: "Trifolium Bituminosum inodorum." The name *Bituminosum* refers to bitumen or mineral pitch (tar). This specific variety was noted for sharing the physical form of the pitch trefoil but lacking its characteristic pungent, asphalt-like aroma.</note>

<column-break/>

->**Scented Pitch Trefoil**<-
<note>original: "Trifolium Bituminosum odoratum." Now commonly known as the Pitch Trefoil or Arabian Pea, this plant is famous in botanical history for the strong smell of tar or gasoline emitted by its leaves when crushed. In traditional medicine, it was often used as a stimulant or to treat chronic coughs, as the strong "pitchy" scent was thought to clear the respiratory passages.</note>

<summary>This botanical plate illustrates two varieties of the Pitch Trefoil, highlighting the distinction between the scented and scentless forms of a plant known for its unusual tar-like aroma.</summary>

<keywords>Pitch Trefoil, Bituminaria bituminosa, Scented Trefoil, Scentless Trefoil, Bitumen, Botany, 17th-century engraving</keywords>

--- Page 292 ---
<meta>Continuity: Having concluded the varieties of Pitch Trefoil on the previous page, this plate introduces the Goat’s-rue (Galega) and the common Yellow Melilot. These plants belong to the Fabaceae (pea) family and were highly regarded in early modern botany for their medicinal properties, particularly as "sudorifics"—remedies used to induce sweating to break a fever.</meta>

->II.<-
->**Goat’s-rue with ash-colored flowers**<-
<note>original: "Galega flore cinereo." Known scientifically today as *Galega officinalis*. The name *Galega* comes from the Greek word for milk (*gala*), as it was traditionally believed that feeding this plant to cows would increase their milk yield. The term "ash-colored" (cinereous) refers to the pale, grayish-blue or lavender tint of the blossoms.</note>

<column-break/>

->I.<-
->**German Melilot with yellow flowers**<-
<note>original: "Melilotus Germanica flore luteo." Now commonly called Yellow Sweet Clover (*Melilotus officinalis*). While often used as fodder for livestock, it was also valued by apothecaries for its sweet, hay-like scent—caused by a compound called coumarin—and was used in plasters to reduce swelling and inflammation.</note>

<column-break/>

->III.<-
->**Goat’s-rue with white flowers**<-
<note>original: "Galega flore albo." This is a white-flowering variant of the common Goat's-rue. In the 17th century, this plant was considered a powerful "alexipharmic," or an antidote to poison and the plague, often prescribed to strengthen the heart and combat infectious diseases.</note>

<summary>This botanical plate illustrates two color varieties of Goat’s-rue alongside the yellow-flowered German Melilot, plants prized in the 17th century for their agricultural and anti-fever applications.</summary>

<keywords>Goat's-rue, Galega officinalis, Melilot, Yellow Sweet Clover, Fabaceae, Medicinal Botany, Plague Remedies, 17th-century engraving</keywords>

--- Page 293 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page's focus on Goat's-rue and Melilot, this plate shifts to the Acanthus and the 'Scorpion-grasses.' This transition moves from plants primarily valued for fodder and fever-reduction to those of significant architectural and morphological interest, as documented by the great Renaissance botanists Mattioli and Dodoens.</meta>

->II.<-
->**Scorpion-grass of Mattioli**<-
<note>original: "Scorpioides Matthi: tertia Dod:" This refers to a plant identified by the Italian physician Pietro Andrea Mattioli. The name *Scorpioides* was given to various plants—such as the Scorpion-vetch or Forget-me-not—whose flower spikes or seed pods curl into a shape resembling a scorpion's tail. The text notes this is the "third" variety described by the Flemish botanist Rembert Dodoens.</note>

<column-break/>

->III.<-
->**Scorpion-grass of the same author**<-
<note>original: "Scorpioides Eius: tertia Dodonei." The word "Eius" (his/the same) indicates that this variety is also attributed to Mattioli in his commentaries. Renaissance botanists often cross-referenced each other's work to reconcile different regional names for the same species.</note>

<column-break/>

->I.<-
->**Prickly Bear’s Breech**<-
<note>original: "Acanthus Spinoſus." This plant is perhaps most famous for its role in classical architecture; its elegant, jagged leaves provided the aesthetic model for the carvings found on Corinthian column capitals. The "spinosus" designation refers to the sharp, thistle-like points on the leaf lobes. Beyond its beauty, it was used in traditional medicine as a soothing treatment for burns and gout.</note>

<summary>This page features a prominent illustration of the Prickly Bear’s Breech (Acanthus spinosus), a plant iconic in both Greco-Roman architecture and early medicine, alongside two varieties of Scorpion-grass as categorized by Mattioli and Dodoens.</summary>

<keywords>Prickly Bear's Breech, Acanthus spinosus, Scorpion-grass, Scorpioides, Pietro Andrea Mattioli, Rembert Dodoens, Botanical Illustration, Corinthian Architecture</keywords>

--- Page 294 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page's focus on the Prickly Bear's Breech and varieties of Scorpion-grass, this plate introduces the 'Smooth' variety of the Acanthus. It also transitions to the Jasmine family, moving from wilder architectural plants to those prized for their delicate flowers and sweet perfumes in early modern European gardens.</meta>

->II<-
->**Yellow-flowered Jasmine**<-
<note>original: "Iaſminum flor. lut." (Jasminum flore luteo). This refers to the Yellow Jasmine, likely *Jasminum humile*. While many jasmine varieties were imported to Europe from the East, the yellow variety was often specifically cultivated for its vibrant color, which stood out against the more common white varieties in late Renaissance gardens.</note>

<column-break/>

->I<-
->**White-flowered Jasmine**<-
<note>original: "Iaſminum flor. alb." (Jasminum flore albo). The Common White Jasmine (*Jasminum officinale*) was a favorite of the era, frequently grown near windows or over trellises so its evening fragrance could drift indoors. Beyond its beauty, early herbalists used jasmine oil in ointments to "warm" the skin and soothe nervous tension.</note>

---

->III<-
->**Smooth Bear’s Breech**<-
<note>original: "Acanthus Leuis" (Acanthus laevis, now usually referred to as *Acanthus mollis*). The descriptor "Leuis" means "smooth" or "soft," highlighting that unlike the *Acanthus spinosus* seen on the previous page, this plant lacks sharp prickles. This specific plant is the botanical source of the iconic "Acanthus leaf" motif found on the capitals of Corinthian columns. In the medicine of this period, its mucilaginous leaves were often used as a soothing poultice for burns or to treat gout.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates two primary varieties of Jasmine—yellow and white—alongside the Smooth Bear’s Breech (Acanthus mollis), a plant of immense significance to both classical architecture and herbal medicine.</summary>

<keywords>Yellow Jasmine, White Jasmine, Smooth Bear's Breech, Acanthus mollis, Jasminum officinale, botanical illustration, Corinthian column, Renaissance gardening</keywords>

--- Page 295 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page’s focus on Jasmines and the architectural Acanthus, this page shifts toward plants valued for their utility in both the apothecary’s shop and the dyer’s workshop. It introduces the Safflower, used for its vibrant pigments, and two varieties of Roman Chamomile, a staple of European herbal medicine.</meta>

->I.<-
# Cultivated Safflower
<note>original: "Cnicus Sativus." Known today as Safflower (*Carthamus tinctorius*). In the early modern period, this plant was highly prized not just for its oil-rich seeds, but for its flower heads. The dried petals provided a potent red or yellow dye for textiles and were sometimes used as a more affordable substitute for saffron, earning it the nickname "bastard saffron."</note>

---

# Roman Chamomile
## with double flowers.
<note>original: "Chamæmelum Romanum flore multiplici." This is the "double-flowered" variety of Roman Chamomile (*Chamaemelum nobile*). Gardeners of this era often preferred "multiplied" or "double" blossoms for their ornamental beauty, as the yellow central disks are replaced by dense, white, petal-like ray florets, giving the flower a pom-pom appearance.</note>

<column-break/>

->II.<-
# Roman Chamomile
## with single flowers.
<note>original: "Chamæmelum Romanum flore simplici." This is the "single-flowered" or "natural" form of Roman Chamomile. While less showy than the double variety, this version was often preferred by herbalists for medicinal preparations. Chamomile was famous as a "plant physician," believed to heal other plants grown near it, and was commonly used in teas and poultices to soothe digestion and "quiet the nerves."</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the Safflower, a vital source of dye, alongside two distinct forms of Roman Chamomile, highlighting the early modern interest in "double" vs "single" floral varieties.</summary>

<keywords>Safflower, Roman Chamomile, double flowers, single flowers, botanical dyes, herbal medicine, Cnicus Sativus, Chamaemelum nobile</keywords>

### III. I. II.
## Mattioli’s Thistle. Greater Yellow Knapweed. Dyer’s Saw-wort.

<note>original: "Cirsium Matthioli. Iacea maior flore luteo. Serratula Tinctoris."</note>

->I.<-
# Greater Yellow Knapweed
<note>original: "Iacea maior flore luteo." This plant, belonging to the genus now known as *Centaurea*, was distinguished by its large, golden-yellow flower heads. In early modern herbals, knapweeds were often called "Ironweeds" because of their incredibly tough, woody stems. They were frequently used in "wound drinks" and poultices to treat ruptures and bruises.</note>

<column-break/>

->II.<-
# Dyer’s Saw-wort
<note>original: "Serratula Tinctoris." Now known as *Serratula tinctoria*. The common name "Saw-wort" refers to the sharply toothed or "serrated" edges of its leaves. Its Latin name *Tinctoris* identifies it as a "painter's" or "dyer's" plant; it was highly sought after for producing a light-fast, vibrant yellow dye for wool and silk, often preferred over other yellow-producing weeds because it did not fade easily.</note>

<column-break/>

->III.<-
# Mattioli’s Thistle
<note>original: "Cirsium Matthioli." This species was named in honor of Pietro Andrea Mattioli, the influential 16th-century physician and commentator on Dioscorides. This variety of thistle was often referred to as the "Melancholy Thistle." According to the "Doctrine of Signatures," its soft spines suggested it was gentle enough to treat internal ailments, specifically believed to "drive away melancholy" and comfort the heart.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three distinct thistle-like plants: Mattioli's Thistle, the Greater Yellow Knapweed, and the Dyer's Saw-wort, highlighting their uses in traditional medicine and the textile dyeing industry.</summary>
<keywords>Mattioli’s Thistle, Greater Yellow Knapweed, Dyer’s Saw-wort, Cirsium, Serratula, botanical dyes, Renaissance botany, textile arts</keywords>

### III. I. II.
## Mattioli’s Thistle. Greater Yellow Knapweed. Dyer’s Saw-wort.

<note>original: "Cirsium Matthioli. Iacea maior flore luteo. Serratula Tinctoris."</note>

->II. Black knapweed with purple flower. I. Black knapweed with white flower. III. Foreign thistle with blue flower.<-
<note>original: "II. Jacea nigra flore purpureo. I. Iacea nigra flore albo. III. Cnicus peregrinus flore cæruleo."</note>

->I.<-
# Greater Yellow Knapweed
<note>original: "Iacea maior flore luteo." This plant, belonging to the genus now known as *Centaurea*, was distinguished by its large, golden-yellow flower heads. In early modern herbals, knapweeds were often called "Ironweeds" because of their incredibly tough, woody stems. They were frequently used in "wound drinks" and poultices to treat ruptures and bruises.</note>

<column-break/>

->II.<-
# Dyer’s Saw-wort
<note>original: "Serratula Tinctoris." Now known as *Serratula tinctoria*. The common name "Saw-wort" refers to the sharply toothed or "serrated" edges of its leaves. Its Latin name *Tinctoris* identifies it as a "painter's" or "dyer's" plant; it was highly sought after for producing a light-fast, vibrant yellow dye for wool and silk, often preferred over other yellow-producing weeds because it did not fade easily.</note>

<column-break/>

->III.<-
# Mattioli’s Thistle
<note>original: "Cirsium Matthioli." This species was named in honor of Pietro Andrea Mattioli, the influential 16th-century physician and commentator on Dioscorides. This variety of thistle was often referred to as the "Melancholy Thistle." According to the "Doctrine of Signatures"—the historical belief that a plant's physical characteristics indicated its medicinal use—its relatively soft spines suggested it was gentle enough to treat internal ailments, specifically those related to "black bile" or melancholy.</note>

<summary>This page features a detailed botanical illustration of an artichoke alongside several thistle-like plants including Knapweed and Saw-wort. It highlights the Renaissance interest in plants that served both as medicinal treatments for "melancholy" and as essential materials for the dyeing industry.</summary>
<keywords>artichoke, knapweed, saw-wort, Mattioli, botanical illustration, yellow dye, medicinal herbs, thistles</keywords>

->II.<-
->White-flowered Wild Thistle<-
<note>original: "Acantium Sylvestre flore albo." This plant is likely a variety of what we now call a Cotton Thistle or Scotch Thistle. The name "Acantium" derives from the Greek word for "thorn." Early botanists distinguished this "wild" (sylvestre) variety specifically by its white flowers, which stood out against the more common purple or pink hues of most thistles.</note>

<column-break/>

->I.<-
->Globe-headed<-
->Thistle<-
<note>original: "Carduus Sphærocephalus." Now known to modern science as *Echinops sphaerocephalus*. The name is a literal description of its appearance, combining the Greek words for "sphere" and "head." These plants were often admired by early modern gardeners for their perfectly round, steel-blue or grey flower heads, which look like miniature spiked maces or morning stars.</note>

<summary>This page features two impressive thistle species, detailing the spherical flower heads of the Globe-headed Thistle and the rare white coloration of a wild Acanthus variety. These detailed engravings showcase the early 17th-century fascination with the intricate, defensive structures of wild flora.</summary>

<keywords>Globe-headed thistle, Wild thistle, Echinops sphaerocephalus, Acantium sylvestre, botanical illustration, 17th-century botany, thorns</keywords>

--- Page 300 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page follows the botanical survey of prickly plants, moving from the knapweeds of page 147 to four specific varieties of thistles and spiny herbs valued for their medicinal and decorative qualities. This page focuses on species characterized by their dramatic textures and distinctive colors.</meta>

->II.<-
->Blessed Thistle<-
<note>original: "Carduus Benedictus." Now classified as *Centaurea benedicta*. In early modern medicine, this plant was considered a "cure-all." Its name, "Blessed," reflects the high esteem in which it was held; it was frequently used in tonics to treat the plague, fevers, and digestive issues.</note>

<column-break/>

->I.<-
->Common Black Chameleon<-
<note>original: "Chamæleon niger vulgaris." This is the Black Chameleon Thistle. It was named "Chameleon" by ancient botanists who believed its foliage changed color with the seasons or soil. The "black" designation usually refers to the dark color of the root, which was often used in traditional remedies despite being considered somewhat toxic in large doses.</note>

<column-break/>

->III.<-
->Tabernaemontanus’s Spiny-Thorn<-
<note>original: "Heracantha Tabernæm." This label attributes the plant to the descriptions of Jacobus Theodorus Tabernaemontanus, a foundational 16th-century botanist. "Heracantha" is a Greco-Latin hybrid term for a "sacred thorn" or "protective spine," likely referring to a species of *Acanthus* or a related thistle-like plant with architectural, spiny leaves.</note>

<column-break/>

->IIII.<-
->Bitter Blue Sea Holly<-
<note>original: "Eryngium totum cœruleum amara." Known today as Sea Holly (*Eryngium*). The description "entirely blue" refers to the plant's remarkable steel-blue color that saturates the flower heads and the surrounding bracts. While "amara" (bitter) refers to its taste, its roots were often candied in sugar to create a popular Elizabethan sweetmeat called "kissingcomfits," believed to be an aphrodisiac.</note>

<summary>This illustration catalogs four spiny plants, including the medically revered Blessed Thistle and the visually striking Sea Holly. The page demonstrates the 17th-century practice of naming plants after famous botanists like Tabernaemontanus and categorizing them by their color and bitter properties.</summary>

<keywords>Blessed Thistle, Black Chameleon Thistle, Sea Holly, Eryngium, Tabernaemontanus, botanical medicine, Renaissance botany, carduus benedictus</keywords>

->II.<-
->Yellow-flowered Rock Rose<-
<note>original: "Helianthemum flore luteo." The name *Helianthemum* comes from the Greek *helios* (sun) and *anthemon* (flower), so named because the delicate blossoms open only in the presence of bright sunlight. Today, these are often found in dry, rocky gardens and are valued for their resilience.</note>

<column-break/>

->I.<-
->Sea Holly<-
<note>original: "Eringium marinum." Now scientifically known as *Eryngium maritimum*. This is a classic maritime plant with stiff, leathery, and spiny leaves that protect it from salt and wind. In the era this manuscript was written, the roots were often dug up, boiled, and preserved in sugar to create "candied eryngoes," a popular sweetmeat believed to have aphrodisiac properties.</note>

<column-break/>

->III.<-
->White-flowered Rock Rose<-
<note>original: "Helianthemum flore albo." A white-petaled variation of the Rock Rose. Like the yellow variety, it is characterized by its woody base and opposite pairs of leaves, appearing here as a delicate counterpart to the much sturdier, prickly Sea Holly in the center of the plate.</note>

<summary>This page features a detailed botanical illustration of the Sea Holly (Eryngium), a coastal plant of great medicinal and culinary interest in the early modern period, flanked by two color varieties of the Rock Rose (Helianthemum).</summary>
<keywords>Sea Holly, Eryngium marinum, Rock Rose, Helianthemum, maritime botany, medicinal roots, sun-flowers, botanical engraving</keywords>

-> III. <-
-> **Red-flowered Hyssop** <-
<note>original: "Hyssopus flore rubro." Hyssop is a semi-woody shrub native to Southern Europe. While the deep blue variety is the most iconic, early modern gardeners prized these color variations—red, white, and blue—for their ornamental value in knot gardens.</note>

-> II. <-
-> **White-flowered Hyssop** <-
<note>original: "Hyssopus flore albo." This white cultivar was often used alongside the red and blue types to create heraldic or patterns in formal gardens. Medicinally, hyssop was used to treat respiratory ailments and was famously cited in the Bible for ritual purification.</note>

<column-break/>

-> I. <-

-> **Sea Holly** <-
<note>original: "Eryngium Pannonicum latifolium." This species is native to the Pannonian Basin of Central Europe (covering modern-day Hungary and its neighbors). It is distinguished from the maritime Sea Holly by its taller stature and broader leaves.</note>

-> II. <-
-> **Broad-leaved Shrubby Hawkweed** <-
-> **with Many Flowers** <-
<note>original: "Hieracium fruticescens latifolium polyanthos." Hawkweeds are members of the daisy family. The name "Hieracium" is derived from the Greek word for hawk (*hierax*); following the writings of Pliny the Elder, early modern herbalists believed hawks consumed the plant’s sap to sharpen their eyesight.</note>

---

-> I. <-
-> **The Squirting Cucumber** <-
<insert>DK</insert> <note>These initials likely indicate a specific collector's mark or a reference to a botanical authority recognized by the owner of this manuscript.</note>
<note>original: "Cucumis Asininus." Known today as *Ecballium elaterium*, this plant is famous for its explosive seed dispersal. When the bristly fruit reaches maturity or is disturbed, it detaches and squirts its seeds and bitter juice at high pressure. Historically, this potent liquid was harvested as a "physic" to treat dropsy, though its toxicity required great caution.</note>

---

-> III. <-
-> **Common Garden Rosemary** <-
<note>original: "Rosmarinus Coronarius." The Latin *coronarius* identifies this as a plant used for "coronas" or garlands. Rosemary was a cornerstone of the Renaissance garden, prized for its evergreen foliage and fragrance. It was traditionally woven into wreaths for weddings and funerals to symbolize fidelity and "remembrance," as famously noted by Shakespeare.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three plants: the many-flowered Broad-leaved Hawkweed, the explosive Squirting Cucumber (noted for its purgative properties), and the Common Garden Rosemary used in ceremonial garlands.</summary>

-> II. <-
-> **Wild Pellitory-of-the-wall** <-
<note>original: "Parietaria sylvestris." The genus name *Parietaria* is derived from the Latin *paries*, meaning "wall," because these plants commonly grow in the mortar of old ruins and stone fences. Often called "clear-weed" in English folklore, it was used historically to clean glassware due to its rough texture and as a remedy for urinary complaints.</note>

---

-> I. <-
-> **Common Caper Spurge** <-
<note>original: "Cataputia vulgaris." Now known as *Euphorbia lathyris*, this plant was historically called "Cataputia" because its seeds were used as a "catapotium" or pill. It was a well-known, albeit dangerous, purgative. Its architectural appearance, with leaves arranged in four perfect columns around the stem, makes it a striking feature in early modern botanical gardens.</note>

---

-> III. <-
-> **Moneywort** <-
<note>original: "Nummularia." Also known as Creeping Jenny or Herb Twopence (*Lysimachia nummularia*). The name comes from the Latin *nummulus*, meaning a small coin, referring to the pairs of round, flat leaves that resemble coins scattered across the earth. It was highly valued by herbalists as a "vulnerary" (a plant used to heal wounds).</note>

<summary>This page features a copperplate engraving of three medicinal plants: the Wild Pellitory-of-the-wall, the upright Caper Spurge, and the creeping Moneywort. These illustrations showcase the Renaissance interest in "signatures"—physical characteristics that hint at a plant's name or medicinal use.</summary>

<keywords>Pellitory-of-the-wall, Caper Spurge, Moneywort, Herb Twopence, medicinal plants, botany, Renaissance engraving, JSZ, signatures of nature</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: Page 152 focused on plants found in rugged environments like stone walls and ruins. Page 153 shifts the focus toward the "Sativa" and "Domestica" varieties—plants that were actively cultivated in Renaissance kitchen and physic gardens for their culinary and medicinal virtues.</meta>

<column-break/>

->III.<-
## **True Savory**
<note>original: "Thÿmbra legitima." This is the *Satureja thymbra*, often called Cretan Savory or Pink Savory. In early modern botany, "legitima" (meaning "lawful" or "true") was used to distinguish the plant described by ancient authorities like Dioscorides from similar-looking local species.</note>

->I.<-
## **Cultivated Purslane**
<note>original: "Portulaca Sativa." Known today as *Portulaca oleracea*, this is a succulent herb characterized by its thick, fleshy leaves and reddish stems. In the Renaissance, it was highly valued as a "cooling" herb to counteract fevers and "burning" of the stomach, as well as a popular salad green.</note>

->II.<-
## **Garden Savory**
<note>original: "Satureia Domestica." This is Summer Savory (*Satureja hortensis*). Unlike its woodier Mediterranean cousins, this "domestic" variety was a staple of European herb gardens. It was famously used to season legumes—earning it the name "bean herb"—and was believed to aid digestion and suppress flatulence.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three aromatic and succulent herbs essential to the Renaissance household: the exotic True Savory of the Mediterranean, the common Garden Savory, and the fleshy Cultivated Purslane. These plants highlight the intersection of culinary use and humoral medicine, where kitchen herbs doubled as remedies for digestive and inflammatory ailments.</summary>

<keywords>True Savory, Cretan Savory, Cultivated Purslane, Garden Savory, Summer Savory, culinary herbs, medicinal succulents, Renaissance gardening</keywords>

->III.<-

->**White Roman Wormwood**<-
<note>original: "Absinthium Ponticum album." Known today as *Artemisia pontica* or Roman Wormwood. This species is smaller and more delicate than common wormwood. Its silvery-white foliage and milder bitterness made it a favorite for flavoring fortified wines, a tradition that survives in the production of modern vermouth.</note>

<column-break/>

->**Common Wormwood**<-
<note>original: "Absinthium vulgare." This is the standard Common Wormwood (*Artemisia absinthium*), perhaps the most famous bitter herb in history. Its name "wormwood" derives from its ancient use as a vermifuge to expel intestinal parasites. In the Renaissance, it was also used to protect clothing from moths and as a remedy for various digestive complaints.</note>

<column-break/>

->**Broad-Leaved Sea Wormwood**<-
<note>original: "Absinthium latifolium marinum." Likely a variety of *Artemisia maritima* or Sea Wormwood. The "marinum" designation indicates its natural habitat in salt marshes and coastal areas. Early botanists were keen to distinguish these coastal varieties, which often had different aromatic qualities compared to those grown inland.</note>

<summary>This page features detailed engravings of three species of Wormwood, a plant genus central to early modern medicine for its antiparasitic and digestive properties. The plate distinguishes between the delicate Roman variety, the ubiquitous common variety, and the specialized sea-dwelling variety.</summary>

<keywords>Wormwood, Absinthium, Roman Wormwood, Common Wormwood, Sea Wormwood, medicinal plants, vermifuge, botanical illustration, Renaissance botany</keywords>

->III. **Common Sneezewort** &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; I. **Prickly Poppy** &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; II. **Foreign Goldilocks**<-
<note>original: "III. Ptarmica Vulgaris. I. Papaver spinosum. II. Chrysocome Peregrina."</note>

<column-break/>

->III.<-
->**Common Sneezewort**<-
<note>original: "Ptarmica Vulgaris." Now classified as *Achillea ptarmica*. Its name comes from the Greek word *ptarmos* (sneezing); the plant was traditionally used as a "sternutatory," meaning the dried leaves or flowers were inhaled as snuff to induce sneezing. This was believed to clear the brain and relieve headaches or sinus congestion.</note>

<column-break/>

->I.<-
->**Prickly Poppy**<-
<note>original: "Papaver spinosum." Most likely *Argemone mexicana*, also known as the Mexican Prickly Poppy. Introduced to Europe from the Americas, it was distinguished by early botanists for its thistle-like leaves and sharp, spiny seed pods. Although related to the opium poppy, it was used primarily for its purgative properties and as a treatment for eye ailments.</note>

<column-break/>

->II.<-
->**Foreign Goldilocks**<-
<note>original: "Chrysocome Peregrina." The name translates to "golden-haired," referring to the bright yellow, tassel-like flower heads. This likely refers to a species of *Chrysocoma* or *Linosyris*. The term "peregrina" (foreign/traveling) indicates it was a non-native species, often imported to botanical gardens from the Mediterranean or Africa for its ornamental beauty.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three botanically distinct plants: the Sneezewort (used to induce sneezing), the Prickly Poppy (a New World arrival), and the Foreign Goldilocks (valued for its golden flowers). The engravings highlight the varied morphology of Renaissance-era herbal collections, ranging from local medicinal weeds to exotic imports.</summary>

<keywords>Sneezewort, Prickly Poppy, Goldilocks, Ptarmica, Papaver spinosum, Chrysocome, medicinal herbs, Renaissance botany, botanical illustration, sternutatory</keywords>

->III. **Large Pale Heartsease** &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; I. **Yellow Horned Poppy** &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; II. **Large Violet Heartsease**<-
<note>original: "III. Flos Trinitatis pallidus maior. I. Papaver Corniculatum luteum. II. Flos maior Trinitatis violaceus."</note>

->III.<-
->**Large Pale Heartsease**<-
<note>original: "Flos Trinitatis pallidus maior." The name "Flower of the Trinity" was a common pre-Linnaean name for the Wild Pansy (*Viola tricolor*), so called because its three-colored petals were seen as a symbol of the Holy Trinity. This "pale" variety likely refers to a specimen with lighter cream or white-dominant petals. It was frequently used in herbal syrups to treat respiratory issues and skin inflammations.</note>

<column-break/>

->I.<-
->**Yellow Horned Poppy**<-
<note>original: "Papaver Corniculatum luteum." Currently classified as *Glaucium flavum*. This plant is a striking member of the poppy family found on coastal shores. It is named "horned" due to its remarkably long, curved seed pods (visible in the engraving), which can grow up to ten inches long. While its roots were sometimes used in folk medicine for cleansing wounds, the entire plant is considered toxic.</note>

<column-break/>

->II.<-
->**Large Violet Heartsease**<-
<note>original: "Flos maior Trinitatis violaceus." A deeper-colored variety of the Heartsease or Wild Pansy. The name "Heartsease" suggests its traditional use as a cardiac tonic and a remedy for "broken hearts" or grief. Early botanists distinguished between these varieties primarily by their color—in this case, "violaceus" or violet—and the size of the bloom.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the Yellow Horned Poppy, notable for its elongated seed pods, alongside two color varieties of Heartsease, a plant steeped in both religious symbolism and traditional herbal medicine.</summary>
<keywords>Yellow Horned Poppy, Heartsease, Wild Pansy, Flower of the Trinity, botanical illustration, Glaucium flavum, Viola tricolor, medicinal herbs</keywords>

### I
### **Red Double-Flowered**
### **Poppy**
<note>original: "Papaver flore pleno rubrum." This is a cultivated variety of the opium poppy (*Papaver somniferum*). The term "double-flowered" refers to a botanical mutation where the plant's reproductive parts (stamens) develop as extra petals, creating the dense, ruffled look seen in the engraving. In the 17th century, these were highly prized in "florilegia" (flower books) for their dramatic appearance in gardens. Like other poppies, it was known for the "somniferous" or sleep-inducing qualities of its sap.</note>

<column-break/>

### II.
### ***Red Wild Poppy***
<note>original: "Papaver Erraticum rubrum." This is the common field poppy (*Papaver rhoeas*), often called the Corn Poppy because it grew abundantly in grain fields. The Latin "Erraticum" means "wandering" or "wild," distinguishing it from the sturdier garden varieties. In traditional medicine, a syrup made from these bright red petals was frequently used as a mild sedative and a remedy for pleurisy and coughs, particularly for children and the elderly.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates two distinct forms of red poppies: the ornate, multi-petaled cultivated double poppy and the delicate, four-petaled wild field poppy. It highlights the early modern interest in both horticultural showpieces and common medicinal herbs.</summary>
<keywords>Poppy, Papaver, Red Poppy, Double Flower, Wild Poppy, Botany, Medicinal Plants, 17th-century Illustration</keywords>

->Double poppy of a silvery color<-
<note>original: "Papaver flore pleno argentei coloris." This variety is noted for its "silvery" hue, likely describing a pale, shimmering white or a very light greyish-lavender bloom that would have stood out for its metallic luster in a garden.</note>

<note>A small "SB" monogram is engraved on the stem of the central poppy plant. This mark belongs to one of the engravers—likely Wolfgang Kilian or another craftsman in the workshop—who translated the original botanical drawings onto copper plates for printing.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three distinct cultivars of double-flowered poppies, ranging from vibrant cinnabar-red to a delicate silver. The central specimen is a particularly dense "multiplex" variety featuring white petals with red-tipped edges.</summary>

<keywords>Double Poppy, Papaver somniferum, Cinnabar-red, Multiplex flowers, Silver poppy, Botanical engraving, 17th-century horticulture, Basilius Besler</keywords>

# Page 161

### Smaller wild daisy III with mixed-colored flowers. Smaller wild daisy II with red flowers.
<note>original: "Bellis Sylvestris minor III. fl: mixto. Bellis Sylvestris II. minor flore rubro."</note>

->I.<-
->**Indian Cress**<-
<note>original: "Nasturtium Indicum." Despite its name, this plant is not a true watercress. It was brought to Europe from Peru in the 16th century. It was called "Nasturtium" (literally "nose-twister") because its pungent, peppery taste reminded botanists of common watercress. Today, it is known scientifically as *Tropaeolum majus*.</note>

<note>This illustration is scientifically significant for its early depiction of ecology; two caterpillars are shown crawling on the large, shield-shaped (peltate) leaves. These are likely the larvae of the Large White butterfly, which are frequently found on these plants.</note>

->II.<-
->**Smaller wild daisy with red flowers**<-
<note>original: "Bellis Sylvestris II. minor flore rubro." This specimen represents a color variation of the common wild daisy, which usually features white petals. Here, the artist highlights a variety with distinct red or deep pink blooms.</note>

->III.<-
->**Smaller wild daisy with mixed-colored flowers**<-
<note>original: "Bellis Sylvestris minor III. fl: mixto." The "mixed" description refers to petals that are white at the base but transition to pink or red at the tips—a common sight in European meadows and a popular feature in early ornamental gardening.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the exotic Indian cress (Nasturtium) alongside two color varieties of the common wild daisy. The inclusion of caterpillars on the Nasturtium leaves highlights an early botanical interest in the relationship between plants and insects.</summary>

<keywords>Indian Cress, Nasturtium, Bellis sylvestris, Wild Daisy, Tropaeolum majus, peltate leaves, caterpillars, entomology, 17th-century botany, garden history</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: Following the varieties of field daisies and the exotic, shield-leaved Indian cress on page 161, this page moves toward plants with complex flower spikes and distinctive seed structures. # Page 162

->II.<-
->**Sainfoin with red flowers, as described by Dodoens**<-
<note>original: "Onobrychis Dodon flore rubro." The name *Onobrychis* is derived from the Greek words for "donkey" and "to bray," suggesting it was a fodder so desirable that it caused animals to bray with excitement. The name "Dodon" refers to Rembert Dodoens, a famous 16th-century Flemish physician and botanist whose works were a primary reference for this volume.</note>

->I.<-
->**Shield-shaped French Honeysuckle with a sweetly blushing flower**<-
<note>original: "Hedysarum Clypeatum flore suauiter rubente." A native of the Mediterranean, this plant is notable for its vibrant red-pink flowers. The term *Clypeatum* (shield-bearing) refers to the unique shape of its seed pods, which are divided into flat, rounded segments that resemble a series of small bucklers or shields.</note>

->III.<-
->**Jerusalem Oak**<-
<note>original: "Botrys." This plant, a member of the goosefoot family, is highly aromatic and resinous. The name *Botrys* is the Greek word for "a cluster of grapes," describing the way its tiny flowers and seeds bunch together along the stems. Historically, it was used as a treatment for coughs and to protect clothing from moths.</note>

<summary>This page presents three detailed botanical illustrations: Sainfoin, French Honeysuckle, and Jerusalem Oak. Each is selected for its distinct morphology, particularly the "shield-like" seeds of the Honeysuckle and the "grape-like" clusters of the Jerusalem Oak.</summary>

<keywords>Sainfoin, French Honeysuckle, Jerusalem Oak, Rembert Dodoens, Onobrychis, Hedysarum, Botrys, medicinal herbs, fodder plants</keywords>

--- Page 315 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page's focus on leguminous plants like Sainfoin and French Honeysuckle, this page continues exploring the pea family (Fabaceae) by illustrating three distinct varieties of Lupins. These plants were highly valued in the early modern period not only for their striking floral spikes but also as a "green manure" to enrich agricultural soil.</meta>

# Page 163

->III. **Fragrant Wild Lupin with yellow flowers**<-
<note>original: "Lupinus Sylvestris flor: luteo odoratus." This is likely the European Yellow Lupin (*Lupinus luteus*). Unlike many other lupins, this variety was specifically prized for its sweet scent, often compared to the smell of orange blossoms.</note>

->I. **Greater Cultivated Lupin**<-
<note>original: "Lupinus Sativus maior." Known today as the White Lupin (*Lupinus albus*), this has been a staple crop in the Mediterranean for millennia. The seeds are edible but contain bitter alkaloids that must be leached out by soaking in water before they can be safely consumed.</note>

->II. **Narrow-leaved Wild Lupin with blue flowers**<-
<note>original: "Lupinus Sylvestris angustifol. Flor: cæruleo." The term *angustifol.* is an abbreviation for *angustifolius*, meaning "narrow-leaved." This blue-flowered variety is distinguished by its more slender, delicate foliage compared to the broader leaves of the cultivated variety.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three members of the Lupin genus, highlighting the differences between cultivated and wild species through their flower color, fragrance, and leaf shape. The detailed engraving showcases their entire anatomy, including the distinctive "palm-like" leaves and the heavy seed pods characteristic of the legume family.</summary>

<keywords>Lupins, White Lupin, Yellow Lupin, Blue Lupin, legumes, botany, seed pods, soil enrichment, medicinal plants</keywords>

# Page 164

->**II. Common white-flowered Lupin**<-
<note>original: "Lupinus vulgaris flore albo." This refers to the *Lupinus albus*, a plant utilized since Egyptian times. It was a vital agricultural crop because it can thrive in poor, acidic soils where other legumes fail, simultaneously enriching the ground with nitrogen.</note>

->**I. Exotic white-flowered Lupin**<-
<note>original: "Lupinus exoticus flore albo." The term "exotic" in early modern botany usually designated a plant recently introduced from foreign lands—often the Americas or the Levant—or a variety that was notably larger or more ornate than the local species. The illustration shows a much more robust flower spike than the common variety.</note>

->**III. Camerarius’s Winged Pea**<-
<note>original: "Lotus Tetragonolobus Camerarij." Today known as *Tetragonolobus purpureus* or the Asparagus Pea. The name honors Joachim Camerarius the Younger (1534–1598), a famous German physician and botanist who maintained one of the finest botanical gardens in Nuremberg. The term *Tetragonolobus* refers to the "four-angled" or winged shape of the seed pods, which are clearly visible in the engraving.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates two varieties of white lupine—distinguishing between the common European type and a larger exotic version—alongside the distinctively shaped winged pea. These plants were studied for their agricultural utility as well as their unique seed pod morphology.</summary>

<keywords>White Lupin, Winged Pea, Asparagus Pea, Joachim Camerarius, exotic plants, botanical illustration, legumes, early modern botany</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: Page 165 continues the botanical survey from the previous page. While the previous page focused on legumes like Lupins and Winged Peas, this page serves as a transition. # Page 165

->**Foreign Pea**<-
<note>original: "Pisum peregrinum." A "peregrine" or foreign plant was one not native to the local region. This variety is distinguished by its large leaves and robust tendrils, likely brought to Europe from the Mediterranean or the Americas.</note>

->**Great Common Houseleek**<-
<note>original: "Sedum vulgare magnum." Known today as *Sempervivum tectorum*. Its Latin name means "always living," reflecting its incredible hardiness. In the 17th century, it was commonly planted on the thatched roofs of houses to protect against lightning and fire, a tradition rooted in folklore.</note>

->**Earth-nut and False Ground-nut**<-
<note>original: "Glans terræ et Pseudoapios." This refers to plants with edible tubers. *Glans terræ* literally means "acorn of the earth." These were often the Earthnut Pea (*Lathyrus tuberosus*), prized for its small, sweet-tasting root tubers.</note>

->**VI. Marsh Stonecrop with flesh-colored flowers**<-
<note>original: "Sedum palustre flore incarnato." The term *incarnato* (flesh-colored) describes the pale pink or reddish hue of the blossoms. These plants were typically found in damp, boggy environments, contrasting with the typical "stonecrops" that prefer dry rocks.</note>

->**Lesser Stonecrop, or Worm-grass**<-
<note>original: "Sedum minus sive vermicularis." The name *vermicularis* (worm-like) refers to the thick, succulent leaves that resemble small caterpillars or worms crawling along the stems.</note>

->**III. Lesser Caustic Stonecrop**<-
<note>original: "Sedum minus causticum." Commonly known today as Biting Stonecrop or Wall Pepper (*Sedum acre*). It earned the name "causticum" because its leaves have a sharp, acrid taste that can cause a burning sensation or even blisters on the skin if handled or eaten in large quantities.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates various hardy succulents and tuberous plants, specifically focusing on the Houseleek and different varieties of Stonecrop. It demonstrates the early modern interest in "ever-living" plants and those with edible or medicinal roots.</summary>

<keywords>Foreign Pea, Houseleek, Stonecrop, Earthnut, Sedum, Succulents, 17th-century botany, medicinal plants</keywords>

# Page 166

<column-break/>

->III.<-
->**Wild Everlasting Pea**<-
<note>original: "Lathyris Sylvestris." Now known scientifically as *Lathyrus sylvestris*. This is a perennial climbing plant native to Europe. It is easily identified by its "winged" stems and the way it uses tendrils to scramble over other vegetation. In early herbals, "sylvestris" (of the woods) usually denoted a wild variety rather than a cultivated garden crop.</note>

<column-break/>

->I.<-
->**Job's Tears**<-
<note>original: "Lachryma Iobi." A tall, grain-bearing tropical plant (*Coix lacryma-jobi*) related to maize. It was a great curiosity in 17th-century European gardens. Its name comes from the hard, shiny, tear-shaped structures that surround the flowers and seeds. These "tears" were so durable they were frequently strung together to make rosaries and necklaces.</note>

<column-break/>

->II.<-
->**Narbonne Vetch**<-
<note>original: "Lathyris Narbonensis." Now classified as *Lathyrus narbonensis*. Named after the Narbonne region in Southern France, this plant is a close relative of the broad bean. It was often studied by early botanists for its large, dark flowers and its use as a hardy fodder crop for livestock in Mediterranean climates.</note>

<summary>This page features a botanical engraving of two wild climbing legumes—the Wild Everlasting Pea and the Narbonne Vetch—alongside the exotic Job's Tears grass.</summary>
<keywords>Wild Everlasting Pea, Job's Tears, Narbonne Vetch, Lathyrus, botanical illustration, seeds, legumes, 17th-century botany</keywords>

<note>original: "Clematis peregrina incarnata simplici flore." The term "peregrina" (foreign/exotic) suggests this was a species introduced to the region from the Mediterranean. Like its "flesh-colored" neighbor, it is a climbing variety that uses its leaf stalks to wind around supports.</note>

->II<-
->**Montpellier Scammony**<-
<note>original: "Scammonia Monspelliens." Now known as *Cynanchum acutum* or acute-leaved cynanchum. Although it resembles a bindweed with its climbing habit and arrow-shaped leaves, it was historically categorized by its medicinal properties; its milky sap was often harvested to create a powerful purgative.</note>

->I<-
->**Greater White Bindweed**<-
<note>original: "Conuoluulus maior flore albo." This is the common Great Bindweed (*Calystegia sepium*). It is easily recognized by its large, heart-shaped leaves and brilliant white, funnel-shaped flowers that appear in midsummer. In the 17th century, it was admired for its vigorous growth, even as it was known for its ability to "bind" or wrap tightly around other garden plants.</note>

<column-break/>

->III<-
->**Sea Bindweed**<-
<note>original: "Soldanella Marina." Today classified as *Calystegia soldanella*, this plant is a specialist of the seashore. Unlike the climbing varieties, it prostrates itself along the sand. Its thick, kidney-shaped leaves are a natural adaptation to the salty, windy environment of coastal dunes.</note>

->II.<-
->**Common Bladder Cherry**<-
<note>original: "Halicacabum vulgatius." This is the *Physalis alkekengi*, commonly known today as the Chinese Lantern or Winter Cherry. The name *Halicacabum* is derived from the Greek words for "salt-cask," describing the way the papery calyx (the outer shell) swells to protect the fruit inside, much like a protective vessel.</note>

->I.<-
->**Heart Pea**<-
<note>original: "Pisum cordatum." Now scientifically known as *Cardiospermum halicacabum* or the Balloon Vine. The name "Heart Pea" refers to the small, dark seeds found inside the triangular, air-filled pods; each seed is marked with a perfect white heart shape. In the 17th century, it was often grouped with other "bladder" plants despite being a climbing vine from a different botanical family.</note>

->III.<-
->**Camerarius’s Indian Bladder Cherry or Nightshade**<-
<note>original: "Halicacabum seu Solanum Indicum Camerarij." This name identifies the plant as a variety described by the famous German botanist Joachim Camerarius the Younger. In this era, "Indian" often referred to plants brought from the Americas or the East Indies. It is a species of *Physalis*, likely a tropical relative of the common winter cherry, valued for its ornamental husks and medicinal properties.</note>

<note>original: "Caryophyllus flore miniano rubescens punctatus." The term *miniatus* or *miniano* refers to the color of "minium" (red lead), a bright orange-red pigment. *Punctatus* indicates the petals are covered in fine "points" or dots.</note>

<column-break/>

->**Carnation with a larger flower, half of which is**<-
->**flesh-colored, and the other half variegated with**<-
->**red and white stripes and spots; double-petaled.**<-
<note>original: "Caryophyllus flore maiore, dimidiata parte carneus dimidiata vero altera rubris & albis strijs & punctis variegatus plenus." This describes a chimera or "bicolor" carnation, a mutation highly sought after by Renaissance florists. "Full" (*plenus*) denotes a double flower with many layers of petals, as seen in the central engraving.</note>

<column-break/>

**III**

**Medium vermilion Carnation,**
**turning white.**
<note>original: "Caryophyllus miniatus mediales albescens." The term *albescens* suggest a flower that fades to white or has white highlights as it matures.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three distinct varieties of double-petaled Carnations, focusing on the intricate "broken" color patterns and speckled textures that were the height of botanical fashion in the 1600s.</summary>

**II**
**Double Carnation with a**

<note>original: "Caryophyllus multiplex flore carneo." The term *carneo* (flesh-colored) refers to a pale, pinkish-peach or soft rose hue, a popular color in early modern floral gardens. *Multiplex* signifies a "double" flower, where the plant has been bred to produce extra layers of petals instead of reproductive organs.</note>

**I**

<note>original: "Caryophyllus multiplex folio florum ex rubro & albo dimidiatim diuisis et punctatis." This central specimen describes a "chimera" or variegated variety. *Dimidiatim* suggests the color is split down the middle of the petal, a symmetry highly sought after by 17th-century collectors. *Punctatis* indicates the presence of fine dots or "stippling" across the surface.</note>

### II.
### White-flowered Lavender.
<note>original: "Lavendula flor: albo." A white variety of the common lavender (*Lavandula angustifolia*). In early modern herbals, white lavender was often considered more delicate in scent than the blue variety and was frequently used in perfumery and medicinal washes.</note>

<column-break/>

### I
### Great Double Carnation
### with a red flower.
<note>original: "Caryophyllus maximus plenus flore rubro." The word *plenus* (full) is the historical botanical term for what we now call a "double" flower. Through selective breeding, gardeners encouraged the plant to produce a high volume of petals, hiding the reproductive parts of the flower. The *maximus* (greatest) designation highlights its large size compared to wild field pinks.</note>

<column-break/>

### III
### Blue-flowered Lavender.
<note>original: "Lavendula flor: cæruleo." This refers to the standard violet-colored lavender. While the Latin *cæruleo* means blue, in botanical contexts of this period, it frequently encompassed the entire blue-to-purple spectrum.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the "Great Double Carnation," a highly cultivated garden variety, alongside white and blue Lavender, showcasing the Renaissance interest in both aesthetic floral size and aromatic garden plants.</summary>

### III
->**Female Southernwood** or **Lavender Cotton**<-
<note>original: "Abrotanum fœmina sive Chamæcyparissus." Today known as *Santolina chamaecyparissus*, this plant is not a true lavender. The name *Chamæcyparissus* translates to "ground cypress," a reference to its silvery, scale-like leaves that resemble the foliage of a cypress tree. In early modern medicine, it was often used as a vermifuge (to expel parasites).</note>

<column-break/>

### I
->**Full Carnation**, bright red<-
->like the **blossoms of the Peach tree**<-
<note>original: "Caryophyllus plenus læte rubescens instar florum Persici mali." A "full" (*plenus*) flower is what modern gardeners call a "double" flower, bred to have extra layers of petals for a more opulent appearance. The author notes that the specific shade of red—likely a vibrant, warm pinkish-red—closely mimics the color of a flowering peach tree (*Persicum malum*).</note>

<column-break/>

### II
->**Foreign Germander**<-
<note>original: "Marum peregrinum." This refers to *Teucrium marum*, commonly known as Cat Thyme. Despite its name, it is a type of germander, not thyme. It was labeled "foreign" (*peregrinum*) because it originated in the Western Mediterranean (Spain and the Balearic Islands) and was a prized exotic in Northern European botanical collections. It is famous for its pungent, mustard-like scent that attracts cats.</note>

### III
->**Sundew** or **Dew of the Sun**<-
<note>original: "Rorella seu ros Solis." Known today by the genus *Drosera*, this is a carnivorous plant. The name "Dew of the Sun" refers to the glistening, sticky droplets on its glandular hairs that look like morning dew but do not evaporate in the sun. In the Renaissance, it was used in "aqua rosae solis," a cordial or herbal liqueur believed to have life-extending properties.</note>

<column-break/>

### I
->**Full purple-red Carnation**,<-
->**speckled and with fringed leaves**<-
<note>original: "Caryophyllus plenus purpurascens punctatus et laciniatis folijs." This describes a "double" or "full" variety of carnation, highly prized for its dense layers of petals. The term "purpurascens" indicates a deep crimson or reddish-purple hue, while "punctatus" tells us the petals are flecked or dotted with a contrasting color. Though the Latin says "laciniatis folijs" (fringed leaves), in the context of carnations, this often refers to the jagged, serrated edges of the petals that give the flower a ruffled appearance.</note>

<column-break/>

### II
->**Water Plantain**<-
<note>original: "Alisma." While *Alisma* is the modern name for the Water Plantain, a marsh-dwelling plant, early botanical works sometimes used the term for terrestrial plants like *Doronicum* (Leopard's Bane) if they possessed similar rosette-shaped leaves. Given the description of daisy-like flowers, this specimen likely represents a type of Leopard's Bane or a related mountain herb rather than the aquatic variety.</note>

A "double" (<term>multiplex</term>) flower is one bred to have many more petals than the standard wild variety, a trait highly sought after by Renaissance gardeners. This "greatest" (<term>maximus</term>) variety indicates a large-headed flower, while "variegated" (<term>variegatus</term>) highlights the striking streaks or "breaks" of color on its petals.</note>

# OF THE GARDEN OF EICHSTÄTT

# *The Autumn Section*
<note>original: "Classis Autumnalis." In this famous florilegium by Basler, the plants are organized into "Classes" based on the season in which they bloom. This section identifies flowers reaching their peak in the late summer and autumn months.</note>

The descriptor <term>picts</term> (an abbreviation for the Latin *pictus*, or "painted") indicates that these white flowers feature decorative spots or streaks of color, a trait highly prized by early modern gardeners.</note>

<meta>This page serves as an index for the "Autumn Section" (Classis Autumnalis) of the florilegium. # THE AUTUMN
# SECTION.

### Folio 1.

1. **Love Apples with yellow fruit:** Love-apples or Gold-apples. <note>These are early varieties of tomatoes (*Solanum lycopersicum*), which were often grown as ornamental curiosities in the 16th century.</note>
2. **Variegated Sugar-melon:** Fragrant Sugar-melons.
3. **Apple-shaped False Colocynth:** False Colocynth-apples with greenish-black spots.

### Folio 2.

1. **Love Apples with red fruit:** Red Gold-apples / Love-apples.

### Folio 3.

**Eggplant with pale fruit:** Yellow Eggplant / Mad-apple. <note>The name "Mad-apple" reflects the Renaissance belief that eating eggplants could cause insanity.</note>

### Folio 4.

1. **Female Balsam-apple:** Female Balsam-herb.
2. **Male Balsam-apple with crimson fruit:** Male Balsam-herb / or <term>Momordica</term>-apple.
3. **Momordica with yellowish-red fruit:** Pale golden-yellow <term>Momordica</term>-apple. <note>Momordica refers to the Bitter Melon or Balsam Apple.</note>

### Folio 5.

1. **Fruit-bearing Nightshade:** Mad-apple from the land of the Moors / or Moor-apples. <note>Likely a variety of eggplant or ornamental pepper.</note>
2. **Common Marjoram:** Common Garden Marjoram.

### Folio 6.

1. **Large long Indian Pepper:** Large long Indian Pepper. <note>The "Indian" label here refers to the West Indies (the Americas); these are Chili peppers (*Capsicum*).</note>
2. **Smaller Indian Pepper with curved pods:** Long small Indian Pepper / with crooked little pods.

### Folio 7.

**Medium Indian Pepper:** Medium-long Indian or Calicut Pepper / Pod-pepper / Brazil-pepper.

### Folio 8.

1. **Smallest upright Indian Pepper:** Small red Pod-pepper / with upright little pods.
2. **Indian Pepper with upright forked pods:** Long Indian Pepper / with upright little pods / which appear split at the bottom.

### Folio 9.

1. **Indian Pepper with yellow pods:** Long Indian Pepper with yellow pods.
2. **Broad Golden Indian Pepper:** Large golden-yellow Pod-pepper.

### Folio 10.

1. **Largest round Indian Pepper:** Large Indian Pod-pepper / with red fruit hanging downwards.
2. **Smallest Pepper with round pods:** Small Indian Pepper with round fruit.

### Folio 11.

1. **Largest round upright Indian Pepper:** Large upright Indian Pepper with red pods.
2. **Medium-long upright Indian Pepper:** Medium-sized upright Indian Pepper / with red pods.

### Folio 12.

1. **Round prickly Indian Pepper:** 

<column-break/>

Round red Indian Pepper / with small points like little thorns.
2. **Medium round Indian Pepper:** Medium-sized round red Indian Pepper.

### Folio 13.

1. **Oblong heart-shaped Indian Pepper:** Long Indian Pepper / shaped like a heart.
2. **Heart-shaped Indian Pepper:** Round Indian Pepper / shaped like a heart.

### Folio 14.

**Red Indian Reed:** Red Indian Flowering Reed / with fire-red flowers. <note>Known today as the Canna Lily.</note>

### Folio 15.

**Yellow Indian Reed spotted with red marks:** Golden-yellow Indian Flowering Reed / with flowers sprinkled with black dots.

### Folio 16.

1. **Indian Jasmine with red and variegated flowers:** Speckled Indian Flowers / mixed with red and white spots. <note>Actually *Mirabilis jalapa*, the "Marvel of Peru," which was often called Indian Jasmine.</note>
2. **Autumnal Gentian with Lesser Centaury leaves and blue flowers:** Blue Heaven-stalk / or Small Gentian / with narrow leaves.
3. **Many-flowered Autumnal Gentian, from blue to purplish:** Heaven-stalk / or Small Gentian / with purple-blue flowers / blooming in Autumn.

### Folio 17.

**Indian Jasmine, or the Miraculous Flower of Peru:** Speckled Indian Flowers / which are mixed with small yellow and red spots.

### Folio 18.

1. **Elecampane:** Elecampane root.
2. **Wild Marjoram, called Onites:** Foreign Wild Marjoram / or Oregano.
3. **Common Wild Marjoram:** Oregano or common Wild Marjoram.

### Folio 19.

**Three-colored Amaranth:** Parrot-feathers.

### Folio 20.

**Greater Amaranth with red plumes:** Small Hawkweed / with broad leaves.

2. **Crested Amaranth:** Cockscomb.

### <gloss>1</gloss> **Love-apples with red fruit.** <note>original: "Poma amoris fructu rubro"; "Love-apple" was the standard early modern name for the tomato (*Solanum lycopersicum*). While we now associate tomatoes with Italian cuisine, 16th and 17th-century Europeans often grew them as ornamental curiosities, sometimes fearing they were poisonous due to their membership in the nightshade family.</note>

**Related Terms:**
<term>Love-apples</term> (tomato), <term>Solanum lycopersicum</term> (scientific name), botanical illustration, herbal, fruit.

<note>The illustration on this page depicts the tomato plant in high detail, showing the characteristic lobed leaves and the heavy, ribbed fruit that was common in early cultivars. The inclusion of the root system at the bottom was a standard practice in scientific herbals to provide a complete view of the specimen for identification.</note>
