<meta>This page follows the botanical plate for the red-fruited tomato. It continues the sequence of documenting members of the Solanaceae (nightshade) family, transitioning from the "Love-apple" to the eggplant. The layout mirrors the previous page's scientific presentation.</meta>

->**Eggplant with pale fruit.**<-

<note>original: "Melanzana fructu pallido"; the name "Melanzana" is an Italian derivative of the Medieval Latin *mela insana*, literally translating to "mad apple." This reflects an early European belief that plants in the nightshade family could cause madness or other maladies if consumed.</note>

### **Eggplant with pale fruit.**

**Related Terms:**
<term>Eggplant</term> (also known as aubergine), <term>Solanum melongena</term> (the scientific name), <term>Pale-fruited</term> (referring to white or light-yellow varieties), botanical illustration.

<note>The detailed engraving on this page depicts the eggplant plant in its entirety. While we often associate eggplants with a deep purple color today, many early varieties imported to Europe produced white, egg-shaped fruits—hence the English name "eggplant." The illustration meticulously captures the plant's "fibrous root system," its "ovate leaves" with wavy edges, and the "five-pointed, star-shaped flowers" that are characteristic of the genus *Solanum*. The heavy, smooth-skinned fruit is shown hanging from the branches, weighted by its own mass.</note>

<summary>This page presents a botanical illustration of a pale-fruited eggplant, providing its early scientific name and documenting its physical characteristics from root to fruit.</summary>

<keywords>Eggplant, Melanzana, Solanum melongena, Botanical illustration, Nightshade family, Horticulture, Pale fruit</keywords>

--- Page 344 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical catalog from the previous entries on the nightshade family (tomatoes and eggplants). While the previous page focused on the Solanum genus, this plate introduces "Balsams," which in the 16th and 17th centuries included both the garden balsam (Impatiens) and the climbing balsam apple (Momordica). The transition follows a logic of garden plants prized for their ornamental and medicinal fruits.</meta>

## I. Female Balsam.
<note>original: "Balſamina fœmina"; the "female" designation in early modern botany often referred to a plant with more delicate features or smaller stature compared to a "male" counterpart, rather than a modern biological sex classification. This is likely the Garden Balsam (*Impatiens balsamina*).</note>

### III. Bitter Gourd with yellowish-red fruit.
<note>original: "Momordica fructu luteo rubescente"; the genus name *Momordica* comes from the Latin *mordere* (to bite), describing the jagged, "bitten" appearance of the seeds or the fruit's husk. This species is commonly known today as the Balsam Apple.</note>

### II. Male Balsam with crimson fruit.
<note>original: "Balsamina Mas fructu puniceo"; *puniceo* refers to a deep reddish-purple or scarlet hue. This plant, labeled "male," is another variety of the climbing balsam vine, distinguished here by its vibrant fruit color.</note>

->**A Study of Balsams and Bitter Gourds**<-

<note>This engraving captures the intricate details of three distinct plants. In the center (I) is the upright **Female Balsam**, characterized by its thick, succulent stem and flowers that sprout close to the stalk. Flanking it (II and III) are two varieties of climbing vines. These are **Balsam Apples** (*Momordica balsamina*), which were exotic curiosities in European gardens of this era. The illustration highlights their "palmate" leaves—shaped like an open hand—and their distinctive, warty, egg-shaped fruits. When ripe, these fruits burst open to reveal seeds covered in a bright red pulp, a detail hinted at by the descriptions of "yellowish-red" and "crimson" fruits.</note>

<summary>This page features a botanical engraving of the garden balsam and two varieties of the climbing balsam apple, documenting their growth habits and the specific colors of their ornamental fruits.</summary>

<keywords>Balsam, Bitter Gourd, Momordica, Impatiens balsamina, Botanical illustration, Horticulture, Balsam Apple</keywords>

<meta>The previous page concluded the section on the "Balsams" (Impatiens and Momordica), which were prized for their decorative and exploding seed pods. This page transitions to the Nightshade family, specifically focusing on the "Apple-bearing" varieties that were then exotic curiosities in European gardens, alongside a well-known aromatic herb.</meta>

## II. Common Marjoram
<note>original: "Amaracus vulgaris"; known today as *Origanum majorana*. In early modern botany, "Amaracus" was the classical name for sweet marjoram, a fragrant herb used for its essential oils and culinary properties.</note>

# Apple-bearing Nightshade
<note>original: "Solanum Pomiferum"; this is a historical classification for the tomato (*Solanum lycopersicum*) or certain ribbed varieties of eggplant. The name literally means "apple-bearing nightshade," reflecting the European effort to categorize New World plants within familiar Latin naming systems.</note>

->**A Study of Nightshades and Aromatic Herbs**<-

<note>This engraving showcases the anatomical detail of two very different plants. The central figure is the **Apple-bearing Nightshade**. At the time of this printing, these plants—ancestors of the modern tomato—were often grown as ornamental curiosities. The artist has emphasized the "ribbed" or "lobed" structure of the fruit, which resembles a small pumpkin, a characteristic of many early heirloom varieties brought from the Americas. To the bottom left is the **Common Marjoram**, a staple of the European herb garden. By placing them together, the author contrasts the exotic, heavy-fruiting nightshade with the familiar, delicate textures of a common medicinal and culinary shrub.</note>

<summary>This page features a detailed engraving of an early tomato variety, then categorized as an "apple-bearing nightshade," alongside the common marjoram herb. It highlights the transition of New World plants into European botanical catalogs during the early modern period.</summary>

<keywords>botany, tomato history, marjoram, Solanum Pomiferum, Amaracus vulgaris, nightshade family, herbarium, engraving</keywords>

<meta>The previous page concluded the section on the Nightshade family, specifically the "Apple-bearing" varieties like the tomato and the aromatic marjoram. This page continues the study of the Solanaceae (Nightshade) family by introducing the "Indian Peppers"—known today as chili peppers—which were similarly exotic imports from the Americas during this period.</meta>

## Very Large Long 
## Indian Pepper
<note>original: "Piper Indicum maximum longum"; these are varieties of *Capsicum annuum*. Though called "peppers," they are unrelated to the black pepper corn (*Piper nigrum*) of Asia. They were named "Indian" because they originated in the "West Indies" of the Americas.</note>

<column-break/>

## II.
## Smaller Indian Pepper
## with curved pods.
<note>original: "Piper Indicum minus recurvis siliquis." The term <term>siliquis<gloss>pods or husks</gloss></term> refers to the fruit of the plant. The "recurved" description matches the hooked or curled appearance of the smaller chili varieties shown in the engraving.</note>

->**The Discovery of Pungent Fruits**<-

<note>This engraving serves as a botanical record of the diverse forms the chili pepper could take. Unlike the previous page's "Apple-bearing Nightshade" (the tomato), which was often viewed with suspicion or grown merely for ornament, these "Indian Peppers" were quickly recognized for their culinary and medicinal potential as a substitute for expensive imported black pepper. The illustration captures the plants' transition from delicate five-petaled flowers to the heavy, drooping fruit. By displaying the exposed <term>fibrous root systems<gloss>a network of thin, moderately branching roots</gloss></term>, the artist provides a complete anatomical guide for the early modern gardener attempting to cultivate these tropical plants in European soil.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates two varieties of chili peppers, categorized at the time as "Indian Peppers," documenting their various shapes and growth habits. It reflects the 17th-century fascination with New World species and their integration into European botanical science.</summary>

<keywords>chili peppers, Capsicum, Indian pepper, botanical illustration, New World plants, Nightshade family, pods</keywords>

### Red Indian Reed.
<note>original: "Canna Indica rubra"</note>

<term>Red Indian Reed<gloss>Currently known as the Canna Lily or "Indian Shot" (Canna indica). Despite the name "Indian," this plant is native to the tropical Americas and was introduced to Europe in the 16th century, where its striking flowers and large leaves made it a popular ornamental plant.</gloss></term>

<term>Rhizome<gloss>The thick, bulbous horizontal stem depicted at the base of the plant. This structure allows the plant to store nutrients and survive through different seasons, a feature of great interest to early modern botanists studying plant survival and propagation.</gloss></term>

<term>Seed Pods<gloss>The bristly, egg-shaped pods shown at the top of the stalk. These contain extremely hard, lead-like seeds. The common name "Indian Shot" refers to the historical claim that these seeds were once used as a substitute for lead shot in muskets when traveling through the Americas.</gloss></term>

<term>Parallel Venation<gloss>The distinct, side-by-side pattern of veins in the leaves. This botanical detail was carefully rendered in 17th-century engravings to help distinguish between different broad-leafed tropical species.</gloss></term>

<summary>This page features a detailed copperplate engraving of the Red Indian Reed, providing a full anatomical record of the plant including its rhizome, fibrous roots, and distinctive bristly seed pods. It reflects the 17th-century botanical practice of documenting New World species that were valued for both their exotic beauty and their unique physical properties.</summary>

<keywords>Red Indian Reed, Canna indica, Indian Shot, botanical illustration, rhizome, seed pods, New World flora, 17th-century botany</keywords>

### Yellow Indian Reed
<term>Yellow Indian Reed<gloss>A variety of *Canna indica*. The Latin term *lutea* (yellow) identifies this as a specific color form, often cultivated in European gardens for its striking contrast with the more common red varieties.</gloss></term>

<term>Spotted with red marks<gloss>Translating the Latin *rubris maculis punctata*. This refers to the decorative speckles on the petals, which served as important diagnostic features for 17th-century botanists trying to categorize the many variations appearing in "New World" plants.</gloss></term>

<term>Botanical engraving<gloss>A printmaking technique using a copper plate. The fine hatching seen in the large leaves and the fibrous roots of the rhizome allows for a high degree of scientific accuracy, which was essential for identification before the advent of photography.</gloss></term>

### The Marvel of the Indies and Autumnal Blooms

<term>Indian Jasmine<gloss>Despite the name, this plant is likely *Mirabilis jalapa*, known today as the "Four o'clock flower" or "Marvel of Peru." In the 17th century, exotic imports from the Americas were often grouped under the umbrella term "Indian" and named for their resemblance to familiar fragrant flowers like jasmine.</gloss></term>

<term>Variegated flower<gloss>Translating the Latin *variegato*. This refers to the striking stripes and splashes of color on the petals. Such "broken" colors were a major obsession for early modern gardeners, who viewed these natural mutations as a sign of a garden's prestige.</gloss></term>

<term>Lesser Centaury leaves<gloss>Translating *folijs Centaurij minoris*. Before a standardized naming system existed, botanists described the leaves of new plants by comparing them to well-known local herbs like the Lesser Centaury (*Centaurium erythraea*), which has small, oval, opposite leaves.</gloss></term>

<term>Gentianella<gloss>A diminutive of Gentian. These plants were highly valued in the 1600s for their intense blue and purple pigments—colors that were relatively rare in the garden and highly difficult for artists to capture accurately in print.</gloss></term>

<summary>This page presents a botanical study of three distinct plants: a variegated "Indian Jasmine" (Mirabilis jalapa) and two varieties of Autumn Gentians. The engraving emphasizes the contrast between the large, exotic trumpet flowers of the Jasmine and the delicate, prolific bell-flowers of the European gentians.</summary>

<keywords>Indian Jasmine, Mirabilis jalapa, Gentianella, Autumn Gentian, botanical engraving, variegated flowers, 17th-century horticulture, medicinal herbs</keywords>

### Bitter Roots and Fragrant Herbs

<term>Elecampane<gloss>Translating the Latin *Helenium*. This tall, sturdy plant was a staple of the monastic garden. The illustration focuses on its most valuable part: the thick, tuberous root system. In early modern medicine, these roots were candied or boiled to treat "shortness of breath" (asthma) and to strengthen the stomach. Folk etymology often linked the name to Helen of Troy, suggesting the plant grew where her tears fell.</gloss></term>

<term>Pot Marjoram<gloss>Translating *Origanum Onites dictum*. A Mediterranean native, this herb was distinguished from its wilder relatives by its softer, more delicate leaves and its habit of growing well in garden pots. It was valued for its sweet, spicy scent, which was believed to comfort the brain and nerves.</gloss></term>

<term>Wild Marjoram<gloss>Translating *Origanum vulgare*. This is the plant we recognize today as Oregano. While we treat it primarily as a seasoning, the 17th-century botanist saw it as a powerful "warming" herb capable of clearing the liver and treating various aches. The artist has carefully rendered the tiny, dense clusters of flowers at the tips of the stems to help the reader distinguish it from the more open-flowering Pot Marjoram.</gloss></term>

<summary>This page illustrates three significant medicinal and culinary herbs: Elecampane, known for its healing roots, alongside Pot Marjoram and Wild Marjoram. The plate emphasizes the botanical details—such as root structures and flower density—necessary for accurate identification in the garden and the apothecary.</summary>

<keywords>Elecampane, Helenium, Marjoram, Oregano, Origanum, medicinal herbs, botanical engraving, Renaissance botany</keywords>

### Of Crimson Plumes and Sharp-Sighted Herbs

<term>Great Red Amaranth<gloss>Translating the Latin *Amarantus major panniculis rubris*. This striking plant, known today as "Love-lies-bleeding," was a favorite in early modern pleasure gardens. The name *Amarantus* is derived from the Greek word for "unfading," as the vibrant crimson flower spikes (or panicles) retain their color even after the plant has dried. It was often used as a symbol of immortality.</gloss></term>

<term>Small Hawkweed<gloss>Translating *Hieracium minus*. The name "Hawkweed" stems from an ancient folk belief, recorded by the Roman naturalist Pliny, that hawks would drink the milky sap of these plants to sharpen their vision. This specific variety is noted for its "broad-leaved" appearance, resembling *Hyoseris* (Swine-succory), a common dandelion-like herb of the European countryside.</gloss></term>

<term>Small Salamanca Catchfly<gloss>Translating *Sesamoides salmanticum parvum*. This delicate plant is a member of the *Silene* family, often called "Catchflies" because of their sticky stems. The name *Sesamoides* refers to its seeds, which early botanists thought resembled sesame. The designation *salmanticum* identifies its origin in the region of Salamanca, Spain, showcasing the era's growing interest in documenting regional European biodiversity.</gloss></term>

<summary>This page illustrates three distinct plants: the dramatic Great Red Amaranth, the medicinal Small Hawkweed, and the delicate Salamanca Catchfly. It reflects the early 17th-century transition from purely functional herbalism toward a broader interest in ornamental beauty and regional plant classification.</summary>

<keywords>Great Amaranth, Hawkweed, Salamanca Catchfly, botanical engraving, 17th-century gardening, panicles, Spanish flora, folklore</keywords>

### The Unfading Flowers of the Garden

<term>Crested Amaranth<gloss>Translating the Latin *Amarantus Cristatus*. This plant is most commonly known today as the "Cockscomb." The name refers to the unusual, fasciated growth of the flower head, which expands into a thick, velvety, and undulating shape resembling the comb of a rooster. In the 1600s, such "monstrosities" of nature were highly sought after by collectors for their architectural beauty.</gloss></term>

<term>Spiked Amaranth<gloss>Translating the Latin *Amarantus Spicatus*. This variety represents the more natural, vertical growth habit of the genus. The term *spicatus* describes the flower's arrangement in a "spike," similar to an ear of grain. Renaissance gardeners often called these plants "Flower-Gentle" or "Velvet Flower" because of the soft texture of the crimson blossoms.</gloss></term>

<note>The Amaranth was a symbol of immortality in the early modern world because its flowers do not wither or lose their color quickly after being cut. This "unfading" quality (from the Greek *amarantos*) made it a favorite subject for botanical illustrators, who sought to capture its vibrant reds and purples in print.</note>

<summary>A botanical illustration plate depicting two varieties of Amaranth: the fan-shaped "Cockscomb" and the vertical "Spiked" variety. These plants were celebrated in the 17th century for their vibrant, long-lasting colors and exotic forms.</summary>

<keywords>Amaranth, Cockscomb, botanical illustration, Crested Amaranth, Spiked Amaranth, early modern gardening, ornamental plants</keywords>

--- Page 362 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, which concluded the section on ornamental Amaranths (the "unfading flowers"), this plate shifts focus to Figure I: the Tobacco plant. While Amaranths were prized for their symbolic and aesthetic value in European gardens, this page introduces a plant that was, at the time, a celebrated but controversial new arrival from the Americas, often grouped with medicinal herbs or exotic rarities.</meta>

->I.<-
->**Broad-leaved Tobacco**<-
<note>original: "Tabacum latifolium"</note>

### The Great Herb of the Indies

<term>Broad-leaved Tobacco<gloss>Translating the Latin *Tabacum latifolium*. The term *latifolium* (from *latus*, wide, and *folium*, leaf) highlights the most striking feature of this variety: its massive, expansive foliage. In the 17th century, tobacco was often called the "Holy Herb" or "Sovereign Herb" due to its purported ability to cure everything from headaches to the plague.</gloss></term>

<term>Nicotiana<gloss>The botanical genus name for tobacco. It honors Jean Nicot, the French ambassador to Portugal, who sent tobacco seeds and leaves to Queen Catherine de' Medici in 1560, promoting it as a miraculous medicine. This illustration captures the plant at the height of its growth, showing the sturdy central stalk and the delicate, trumpet-shaped flowers that crown the top.</gloss></term>

<note>The engraving meticulously details the plant's lifecycle. At the base, we see the heavy, "ovate" (egg-shaped) leaves with deep veins that were harvested and dried. At the summit, the artist has rendered the tubular blossoms and closed buds, which in life would range from pale green to a soft pink. For the 17th-century viewer, this image was more than a botanical study; it was a portrait of a "New World" wonder that was rapidly transforming European social and medicinal habits.</note>

<summary>A full-page botanical engraving of the broad-leaved tobacco plant (Tabacum latifolium), detailing its large leaves and flowering structure. The page serves to document an exotic species that was viewed by early modern Europeans as both a medicinal marvel and a botanical curiosity from the Americas.</summary>
<keywords>Tobacco, Tabacum latifolium, Nicotiana, medicinal herbs, New World plants, botanical illustration, Jean Nicot</keywords>

It also introduces a native wetland fruit, the cranberry, reflecting the era's interest in both exotic imports and local medicinal flora.</meta>

### New World Wonders and Wetland Fruits

<term>Lesser Tobacco<gloss>Translating the Latin *Nicotiana minor*. This plant is known today as *Nicotiana rustica*. It was often referred to as "Yellow Henbane" (*Hyoscyamus luteus*) by early botanists because its pale flowers and potent narcotic properties resembled the native European henbane. It was frequently used by indigenous peoples of the Americas for ceremonial purposes due to its high nicotine content.</gloss></term>

<term>Cranberries<gloss>Translating *Oxicoccon* (from the Greek *oxys*, meaning sharp or acid, and *kokkos*, meaning berry) and *Vaccinia palustria* (marsh whortleberries). This trailing, vine-like plant grows in acidic bogs. The illustration captures its delicate, creeping stems and the round, tart fruits that were prized for their ability to resist spoilage on long sea voyages.</gloss></term>

<term>Narrow-leaved Tobacco<gloss>Translating *Nicotiana maior angustifolia*. The descriptor *angustifolia* (from *angustus*, narrow, and *folium*, leaf) distinguishes this variety from the broad-leaved type shown on the previous page. This variety was noted for its height and its clusters of elegant, trumpet-shaped flowers, which are shown here in various stages of bloom at the top of the stalk.</gloss></term>

<note>This engraving serves as a comparative study. By placing the low-growing cranberry (Figure II) between two towering varieties of tobacco (Figures I and III), the artist highlights the incredible diversity of plant forms. The fine line-work in the leaves of the tobacco plants illustrates the prominent veins and "viscous" (sticky) texture that 17th-century herbalists often described when handling the fresh leaves.</note>

<summary>A detailed botanical plate illustrating two varieties of tobacco (narrow-leaved and minor) alongside the common cranberry, highlighting their physical characteristics and naming conventions in the 17th century.</summary>
<keywords>Tobacco, Nicotiana rustica, Cranberry, Botany, New World plants, medicinal herbs, Nicotiana maior, Oxicoccon</keywords>

The artist continues to use the same detailed engraving style to highlight the distinct reproductive organs of each plant—the spiny pods of the Thorn-apple, the spikes of the Sea Purslane, and the dense berry-cluster of the Dragon Arum.</meta>

### Toxic Blooms and Coastal Shrubs

<term>Thorn-apple<gloss>Translating the Latin *Stramonia*. This plant is known to modern science as *Datura stramonium*. The engraving clearly shows its characteristic "thorn-apples"—the spiny, egg-shaped seed capsules—and its large, funnel-shaped flowers. Early modern doctors and herbalists were wary of this plant; while it had uses in ointments for burns, it was primarily known as a dangerous narcotic and poison associated with the nightshade family.</gloss></term>

<term>Sea Purslane<gloss>Translating *Halimus*. This refers to *Atriplex halimus*, a resilient, silvery-green shrub that thrives in salty, coastal environments. The name comes from the Greek *halimos*, meaning "belonging to the sea." It was often studied for its ability to grow where other plants would wither from the salt, and its leaves were sometimes eaten as a salty salad herb or potherb.</gloss></term>

<term>Great Dragon Arum Berry Cluster<gloss>Translating *Botris Dracontiæ maior*. The word *botris* (from the Greek *botrys*) refers to a cluster or bunch of grapes, here describing the dense, club-like stalk of berries produced by the Dragon Arum (*Dracunculus vulgaris*). This plant was a favorite of early illustrators due to its exotic, almost alien appearance; the berries turn a brilliant orange-red when ripe, appearing after the plant's massive, carrion-scented flower has faded.</gloss></term>

<summary>This plate illustrates three botanically diverse species: the poisonous and medicinal Thorn-apple, the salt-hardy Sea Purslane, and the striking fruiting spike of the Dragon Arum. The page highlights the early modern fascination with plants that possessed extreme qualities, whether in their toxicity, habitat, or visual form.</summary>
<keywords>Thorn-apple, Jimsonweed, Sea Purslane, Dragon Arum, Datura stramonium, toxicology, coastal botany, botanical engraving</keywords>

### Exotic Seeds and Coastal Herbs

<term>Turkish Thorn-apple<gloss>Translating *Datura Turcarum*. This is a variety of the Datura plant, likely *Datura metel*. In the 16th and 17th centuries, many exotic plants were given the epithet "Turkish" if they arrived in Europe via the trade routes of the Ottoman Empire. Like the common Thorn-apple (*Stramonium*) seen on the previous page, this plant was famous for its large, trumpet-shaped flowers and its potent, often dangerous, narcotic properties.</gloss></term>

<term>Poley-mountain<gloss>Translating *Polium*. This refers to a species of *Teucrium*, a hardy herb found in the Mediterranean. The illustrator notes it has the "appearance of Lavender" (*facie Lavendulæ*), a common way for early botanists to describe plants before the modern taxonomic system was established. Poley was highly valued in ancient and medieval medicine for treating digestive issues and as an antidote to various poisons.</gloss></term>

<term>Toadflax<gloss>Translating *Linaria*. The name is derived from *Linum* (Flax), because the leaves of many species in this genus closely resemble those of the flax plant. This specific variety is described as "taller" (*elatior*) and "purple-violet" (*purpureo violacea*), likely referring to *Linaria purpurea*. These flowers were often grown in gardens for their height and striking color spikes.</gloss></term>

<summary>This page presents a detailed botanical engraving of three plants: the Turkish Thorn-apple, a variety of Poley-mountain, and a tall Purple-Violet Toadflax. It illustrates the early modern practice of identifying plants based on their resemblance to known species like lavender and flax.</summary>

<keywords>Turkish Thorn-apple, Datura, Poley-mountain, Teucrium, Toadflax, Linaria, botanical illustration, early modern medicine</keywords>

### The Great Millets

<term>Sorghum<gloss>Translating the Latin *Sorgum* and *Sorgo*. In the early modern period, these plants were often called "Great Millet" or "Indian Millet" to distinguish them from the smaller-seeded common millets found in Europe. Sorghum is a tall, robust grass that was highly valued for its grain, which could be ground into flour, and its stalks, which provided animal fodder. The engraving captures the plant's characteristic "panicle"—the dense, branching cluster of seeds at the crown of the stalk—and its broad, undulating leaves.</gloss></term>

<term>Red and White Varieties<gloss>The distinction between "red fruit" (*fructu rubro*) and "white fruit" (*fructu albo*) was a standard way for early botanists to classify different cultivars. While the illustrator treats them as two distinct types (I and II), they are variations of the species *Sorghum bicolor*. Red sorghum was often noted for its hardiness, while white sorghum was frequently preferred for human consumption due to its milder flavor.</gloss></term>

<summary>This page features a detailed botanical engraving of two sorghum varieties, categorized by the red or white color of their grain. It reflects the early modern interest in documenting diverse cereal crops introduced to Europe from Africa and Asia.</summary>

<keywords>sorghum, great millet, cereal grains, red sorghum, white sorghum, botanical illustration, panicle, crop varieties</keywords>

# I.
# The Peruvian Potato

## III.
## *Common Thyme*

### Tubers and Aromatic Herbs

<term>Peruvian Potato<gloss>Translating the Latin *Papas Peruanorum*. In the early 17th century, the potato was still an exotic rarity recently introduced from the Andes of South America. The name *Papas* is derived directly from the Quechua word for the tuber. At the time of this engraving (1613), the potato was grown more for its botanical interest and beautiful flowers than as a staple crop. Botanists of the era correctly identified its relationship to the nightshade family, noting the similarities in its blossoms to those of the tomato or bittersweet nightshade.</gloss></term>

<term>Wild and Common Thyme<gloss>The two plants at the bottom of the plate, *Serpillum citratum* (Lemon-scented Wild Thyme) and *Thymus vulgatior* (Common Thyme), represent the "physic garden" staples of Europe. *Serpillum* refers to "creeping" thyme, so named for its low-growing, vine-like stems. The "citrated" variety was prized for its distinct citrus fragrance. These herbs were staples of early modern medicine, used as antiseptics, cough remedies, and for digestive health.</gloss></term>

<summary>This page illustrates the "Peruvian Potato," documenting one of the earliest depictions of the plant in European botanical literature, alongside two varieties of medicinal thyme. It highlights the transition of the potato from an American curiosity to a documented botanical species.</summary>

<keywords>potato, Solanum tuberosum, Peru, New World plants, lemon thyme, common thyme, herbs, tubers, Basilius Besler</keywords>

# ->The Taro Plant<-

## III.
## *Orange-shaped Gourd*
<note>original: "Cucurbita Arantij forma"</note>

### Root Crops and Ornamental Gourds

<term>Taro <gloss>Translating the Latin *Colocasia*. This plant, often referred to in historical texts as the "Egyptian bean," was a vital food source in the Middle East and Mediterranean long before the potato arrived from the Americas. The engraving captures its most distinctive features: the large, heart-shaped (cordate) leaves and the thick, fibrous tuber or corm at the base. In early modern gardens, it was prized both for its edible roots and its impressive, "elephant ear" foliage.</gloss></term>

<term>Gourds and False Colocynth <gloss>The two fruits flanking the Taro belong to the gourd family (*Cucurbitaceae*). The "False Colocynth" (*Pseudocolocyntis*) is a relative of the true Colocynth, a plant known for its intense bitterness and use as a potent purgative. Botanists of this period frequently classified these diverse fruits by their resemblance to other plants, describing them as "pear-shaped" (*pyri forma*) or "orange-shaped" (*Arantij forma*). Such gourds were often grown as garden curiosities or for use as decorative vessels.</gloss></term>

<summary>This page illustrates the Taro plant, a major Old World staple, alongside two decorative gourd varieties. It demonstrates the early modern botanical practice of classifying fruits based on their physical resemblance to other familiar species like pears and oranges.</summary>

<keywords>Taro, Colocasia, Colocynth, Gourd, botanical illustration, root vegetables, Cucurbitaceae, 17th-century botany</keywords>

While the Taro on page Z8 was a food staple, these specimens were valued primarily for their medicinal properties and aesthetic beauty in the early modern garden.</meta>

Common Lavender with blue flowers. <note>original: "Spica vulgaris flore cæruleo"</note>

I.
->Roman Cyclamen,<-
->[variety]<- <note>original: "Cyclamen Romanum." The OCR "Roma, num." is likely a reading of "Romanum," the standard designation for this variety in early modern botany.</note>

II.
Common Lavender with white flowers. <note>original: "Spica vulgaris flore albo"</note>

### Fragrant Herbs and Coiled Tubers

<term>Lavender <gloss>Translating the Latin *Spica vulgaris*. In the 17th century, lavender was often referred to as "Spike" (Spica) because its flower heads resemble ears of grain. The engraving displays two distinct color variants: the familiar blue and the rarer white. Beyond their fragrance, these plants were staples of the "physic garden," used to treat "passions of the heart" (anxiety) and headaches, and to protect clothing from moths.</gloss></term>

<term>Cyclamen <gloss>Often called "Sowbread" because its large, earth-bound tubers were a common forage for wild pigs. The "Roman" variety depicted here was prized by collectors for its beautifully marbled, heart-shaped leaves. A fascinating detail in this illustration is the depiction of the flower stems coiling into spirals; the plant does this to pull its seed pods down toward the soil once the flowering is over—a phenomenon that early botanists found endlessly intriguing.</gloss></term>

<summary>This page features a botanical engraving of two color varieties of Common Lavender alongside the Roman Cyclamen. It highlights the aesthetic and medicinal diversity of Mediterranean flora, emphasizing the unique growth habits of the Cyclamen's spiraling stems.</summary>

<keywords>Lavender, Cyclamen, Roman Cyclamen, Sowbread, medicinal herbs, 17th-century botany, Spica vulgaris</keywords>

->Late-blooming Cyclamen with<-
->ivy-like leaves.<- <note>original: "Cyclamen serotinum folijs hederaceis." Today, this species is known as *Cyclamen hederifolium*, prized for its autumn flowers and silver-marbled foliage.</note>

### The Hidden Beauty of the Earth-Apple

<term>Sowbread <gloss>The names "Cyclamen" and "Panis porcinus" (Sowbread) point to the plant's dual nature. While the flowers are delicate and nodding, the plant grows from a heavy, starch-rich tuber. These tubers were often called "Earth-Apples" in old herbals. Though toxic to humans if eaten raw—acting as a violent purgative—they were a favored food for forest pigs, which could consume them without harm.</gloss></term>

<term>Late-blooming (Serotinum) <gloss>The term "serotinum" indicates that this variety flowers late in the season, typically in autumn. Early modern botanists were fascinated by the plant's "ivy-like" (*hederaceis*) leaves, which often appear just as the flowers begin to fade or even after they have bloomed, providing ground cover throughout the winter months.</gloss></term>

<term>Camerarius’s Variety <gloss>By labeling specimen II as "of Camerarius," the illustrator acknowledges the specific scientific network of the 16th century. Joachim Camerarius helped bridge the gap between ancient herbal lore and modern botany; his inclusion here suggests this specific long-rooted variety was a prized specimen from his private botanical collection.</gloss></term>

<summary>This page illustrates three varieties of Cyclamen, emphasizing the distinctive shapes of their tubers and leaves. It highlights the Renaissance connection between botanical art and the private gardens of famous scholars like Joachim Camerarius.</summary>
<keywords>Cyclamen, Sowbread, Joachim Camerarius, Ivy-leaved Cyclamen, botanical illustration, Renaissance botany, tubers, medicinal plants</keywords>

### The Autumnal Awakening

<term>Meadow Saffron <gloss>The *Colchicum* is often called "The Naked Lady" because its flowers emerge from the bare earth in autumn, long after its leaves have withered away. While beautiful, early botanists warned of its high toxicity; it contains colchicine, which was used in ancient times as a treatment for gout but is fatal in higher doses.</gloss></term>

<term>Carolus Clusius <gloss>Many of the plants on this page are labeled "Clusij" (Clusius’s). Clusius was the director of the Leyden Botanical Garden and was instrumental in introducing the tulip, the potato, and many Mediterranean bulbs to Northern Europe. His precise observations helped shift botany from a branch of medicine to a science of its own.</gloss></term>

<summary>This page illustrates six varieties of autumn-blooming bulbs, including double-petaled Narcissus and variegated Meadow Saffron. It highlights the botanical contributions of Carolus Clusius, a central figure in the 16th-century "bulb fever."</summary>
<keywords>Autumn Narcissus, Colchicum, Meadow Saffron, Autumn Hyacinth, Crocus biflorus, Carolus Clusius, botanical engraving, 17th-century gardening</keywords>

->I.<-

<meta>This page transitions from the seasonal European flora of the previous page to exotic botanical "curiosities" from the Middle East and the Americas. It highlights the dramatic physical transformations of the Rose of Jericho and the distinctive anatomy of the West Indian Melon Cactus.</meta>

### Open Rose of Jericho.
<note>original: "Rosa Hiericontea aperta." Despite the name, this is not a true rose but a desert plant (*Anastatica hierochuntica*) belonging to the mustard family. This illustration shows the plant in its hydrated state, with its branches spread wide.</note>

### III. Closed Rose of Jericho.
<note>original: "Rosa Hiericontea conclusa." This depicts the plant in its desiccated, dormant state. In the arid deserts of North Africa and the Middle East, the plant curls into a tight ball to protect its seeds. It is often called a "resurrection plant" because it appears to come back to life when placed in water.</note>

# I. Melon Cactus.
<note>original: "Melocactos." This is an early record of a cactus from the genus *Melocactus*, commonly known as the "Turk’s Cap Cactus." Native to the Caribbean and South America, these were prized in early modern European botanical gardens as "exotic" marvels. The drawing clearly shows the <term>cephalium</term>—the reddish, woolly "cap" at the top—which is a specialized structure that only appears once the cactus has reached maturity and from which the flowers grow.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates two botanical wonders: the "resurrection" ability of the Rose of Jericho and the unique, ribbed structure of the Melon Cactus. Both plants represent the era's fascination with exotic species adapted to extreme environments.</summary>

<keywords>Rose of Jericho, Melocactus, Turk's Cap Cactus, resurrection plant, xerophyte, cephalium, desert flora, exotic botany</keywords>

# Aloe
<note>original: "Aloe." In the 17th century, the term "Aloe" was used broadly to describe various succulent plants from Africa and the Americas (including what we now call Agaves). This illustration likely depicts a species such as *Aloe arborescens* or *Aloe vera*, highly valued for their medicinal sap.</note>

->AL<-
<note>This monogram "AL" likely identifies the engraver or artist responsible for the copperplate. In botanical compendiums of this era, such as those by Besler or Merian, individual plates were often signed by the craftsmen who translated the artist's drawings into print.</note>

The illustration showcases the **Aloe** in full bloom, emphasizing its architectural form. The plant's most striking feature is its thick, textured stem, which is covered in the scarred bases of previous years' leaves, giving it a scaled appearance.

From the central rosette, several long, fleshy leaves emerge. These leaves are curved and twisted, possessing small **serrations** <note>saw-like teeth along the leaf margin</note> that serve as a defense against herbivores. Rising from the heart of the plant are three **inflorescences** <note>the complete flower head of a plant including stems and stalks</note> known as racemes. These stalks bear numerous small, bell-shaped flowers, which would typically range in color from yellow to vibrant orange.

At the base, the artist has meticulously rendered the thick, fibrous root system, demonstrating the plant’s ability to anchor itself firmly in rocky or sandy soils.

<summary>This page provides a detailed botanical study of an Aloe plant, emphasizing its unique flowering stalks and succulent anatomy. It reflects the early modern interest in documenting the structural complexity of non-European "exotic" flora.</summary>

<keywords>Aloe, succulent, inflorescence, botanical illustration, medicinal plants, raceme, xerophyte, 17th-century botany</keywords>

# Aloe

# Artichoke

->I.<-

->The Fruit of the Artichoke<-
<note>original: "Fructus Artisochi." The Latinized spelling "Artisochi" reflects the plant's journey into European scholarship via the Italian *carciofo*. Although botanically a flower bud, early modern texts often classified any edible portion of a plant as "fructus" or fruit.</note>

The engraving depicts the **Artichoke** <term>*Cynara scolymus*: a perennial thistle of the Mediterranean region, cultivated for its edible flower buds</term> in its immature state. The artist has meticulously rendered the geometric arrangement of the **bracts** <note>the tough, leaf-like scales that protect the flower</note>, which overlap one another to form a dense, protective head. 

Beneath the head, the thick, fibrous stem is shown with deep vertical ridges, conveying the plant's robust nature. Two small, deeply lobed leaves sprout near the base, hinting at the much larger, silvery foliage the plant would possess in the garden.

->B<-
<note>This stylized monogram "B" engraved on the stem identifies the artist or the copperplate engraver. In large-scale botanical publications of this era, such as the *Hortus Eystettensis*, several different engravers were often employed, and they frequently marked their work with such initials.</note>

This illustration serves not only as a scientific record of the plant’s anatomy but also as a testament to the high value placed on the artichoke as a luxury vegetable in the Renaissance and Baroque periods, often grown in the kitchen gardens of the elite.

<summary>This page features a masterfully detailed engraving of an artichoke head, highlighting the plant's complex structure and its status as a significant botanical specimen in 17th-century Europe.</summary>
<keywords>artichoke, Cynara scolymus, botanical illustration, engraving, Renaissance gardening, monogram B, bracts</keywords>

# Artichokes

II. **Genoese Artichoke** <note>original: "Cinara Genuenſium." A variety named for Genoa, Italy, a region long associated with high-quality Mediterranean produce.</note>

->I<-
->**The Larger Bolognese Artichoke**<-
<note>original: "Cinara maior Bolonienſis." Bologna was a major center for agricultural science in the Renaissance; its "larger" artichoke was a specifically bred cultivar prized for its scale and flavor.</note>

->III<-
->**The Common Artichoke**<-
<note>original: "Cinara ſeu Artiſchochi vulgatis." The text provides both the formal Latin *Cinara* and the common name *Artiſchochi*, which evolved from the Arabic *al-harshuf* into the Italian *carciofo* and eventually the English *artichoke*.</note>

<margin>Heinrich Ullrich</margin> <note>The signature of the engraver, Heinrich Ullrich (c. 1572–1630), a German artist known for his technical precision in copperplate engraving.</note>

The illustration displays three distinct forms of the **Artichoke** <term>*Cynara*: a genus of perennial thistles cultivated for their edible flower buds</term>. 

The central figure (**I**) showcases the plant's robust anatomy, featuring a thick, vertically ribbed stem and deeply serrated leaves. The "fruit" shown is actually the immature flower head, composed of many overlapping **bracts** <note>the tough, protective scales</note>. The artist uses fine cross-hatching to distinguish between the tightly closed head of the Genoese variety (**II**) and the more typical, slightly more mature common variety (**III**). 

This page serves as a visual catalog for a wealthy garden owner, identifying which specific regional varieties were being cultivated or studied.

<summary>This page features a detailed copperplate engraving by Heinrich Ullrich depicting three regional varieties of artichokes: the Bolognese, the Genoese, and the common variety. # The Indian Fig

->I<-
->**The Indian Fig of Eichstätt,**<-
->**grown luxuriantly from**<-
->**a single leaf.**<-
<note>original: "Ficus Indica Eystettenſis ex uno folio enata luxurians." The term *Ficus Indica* (Indian Fig) was the standard pre-Linnaean name for the *Opuntia* or Prickly Pear. The description emphasizes that this massive specimen grew from a single "leaf" (actually a pad or cladode), a common method of propagation that fascinated early botanists.</note>

<margin>I L</margin> <note>The monogram of the engraver, likely Wolfgang Kilian (1581–1662), a master engraver from Augsburg who collaborated with Basilius Besler on this work.</note>

The engraving depicts a magnificent specimen of the **Indian Fig** <term>*Opuntia*: a genus of cacti native to the Americas, known for their flat, paddle-like stems and edible fruit</term>. 

Because these plants are native to much warmer climates, the illustration shows the cactus growing in a large wooden tub, allowing it to be moved indoors to a "Greenhouse" or *Heizhaus* during the Bavarian winter. The plant's heavy, water-filled pads are supported by an elaborate three-section wooden trellis, preventing the weight of the succulent growth from snapping the central trunk. 

The artist has carefully included a **linear scale** <note>the numbered segments at the bottom left</note> to provide the viewer with an accurate sense of the plant’s impressive dimensions, a hallmark of the scientific rigor found in the *Hortus Eystettensis*. Small fruits are visible at the tips of the uppermost pads, which were often compared to common figs in shape, leading to the plant's early Latin name.

<summary>This page illustrates a large Prickly Pear cactus (Opuntia), celebrated for its successful cultivation from a single pad into a massive, trellised specimen within the Bishop of Eichstätt's garden.</summary>
<keywords>Indian Fig, Prickly Pear, Opuntia, succulent, botanical illustration, Wolfgang Kilian, Hortus Eystettensis, New World plants</keywords>

1.
# The True **Christmas Rose**
<note>original: "Helleborus niger legitimus." Literally "The True Black Hellebore." The term "legitimus" was used by early botanists to indicate that this was the specific plant described by ancient medical authorities like Dioscorides. It is named "black" for the color of its roots, despite its white or pinkish winter blooms.</note>

---

II:
## Larger Byzantine **Three-Petaled Snowdrop**
<gloss>Byzantine</gloss>
<note>original: "Leucoium bulbosum triphyllon Maius Byzantinum." In the 16th and 17th centuries, the name "Leucoium" (white violet) was used for what we now identify as Snowdrops (*Galanthus*) and Snowflakes (*Leucojum*). This "Byzantine" variety likely refers to a species brought to Europe from the Ottoman Empire.</note>

---

III.
## Smaller **Three-Petaled Snowdrop**.
<note>original: "Leucoium bulbosum triphyllon Minus." The term "triphyllon" refers to the flower appearing to have three main petals (the large outer tepals of the snowdrop).</note>

---

IIII.
## Larger **Six-Petaled Snowdrop**
<note>original: "Leucoium bulbosum hexaphyllon." This likely refers to the Spring Snowflake (*Leucojum vernum*), which, unlike the common snowdrop, has six petals of equal length, giving it a more symmetrical, bell-like appearance.</note>

---

V.
## Smaller **Six-Petaled Snowdrop**.

->III.<-

->**Black Hellebore with a green flower**<-

This is likely what we now call the Green Hellebore (*Helleborus viridis*). Like its relatives, it was valued for its potent, though dangerous, medicinal properties.</note>

--- Page 385 ---
<meta>Following the previous page’s study of the Green Hellebore and yellow Crocuses, this page introduces the larger "stinking" variety of Hellebore alongside many-flowered Spring Crocuses in variegated and white forms. ->I<-
->**Great Stinking Hellebore**<-
<note>original: "Helleboraster maximus." Known today as *Helleborus foetidus*, this plant is easily identified by its dark, finger-like leaves and clusters of nodding, bell-shaped green flowers. In the 17th century, it was called "Helleboraster" to distinguish it from the "true" Black Hellebore (the Christmas Rose). While its crushed leaves have an unpleasant scent, it was valued for its dramatic winter foliage.</note>

->II<-
->**Variegated many-flowered Spring Crocus**<-
<note>original: "Crocus vernus polyanthos variegatus." The term <term>polyanthos</term> (from the Greek for "many-flowered") describes a variety that produces multiple stems and blossoms from a single bulb. This "variegated" type features petals with intricate purple or brownish stripes, a characteristic often sought after by early botanical collectors.</note>

->III<-
->**White many-flowered Spring Crocus**<-
<note>original: "Crocus vernus polyanthos albus." This is the white-flowered counterpart to the variegated crocus. Besler’s illustration captures the plant in its entirety, showing the delicate white petals and the sturdy underground corm (bulb) from which the cluster of blossoms emerges.</note>

<summary>This page features a large-scale rendering of the Stinking Hellebore alongside two prolific varieties of Spring Crocus, distinguished by their many-flowered growth habit and petal colors.</summary>

--- Page 386 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page's focus on Hellebores and early spring Crocuses, this page transitions into other early spring woodland and meadow plants, including a Hellebore look-alike, the moisture-loving Marsh Marigold, and the symbolic Wild Pansy.</meta>

->III.<-
### False Black Hellebore
<note>original: "Pseudo Helleborus niger." Despite the name, this is likely the Spring Pheasant’s Eye (*Adonis vernalis*). Early botanists called it "false hellebore" because its dark, feathery foliage resembles that of the true Hellebore, though it produces brilliant yellow flowers rather than the white or green blooms of the Hellebore family.</note>

---

->II.<-
### Marsh Marigold with a simple flower
<note>original: "Caltha palustris simplici flore." A familiar sight in wet meadows and alongside streams, this plant's name—<term>palustris</term>—literally means "of the marsh." The "simple flower" refers to the single layer of five petals, distinguishing it from "double-flowered" varieties often found in gardens.</note>

---

->V.<-
### Yellow-flowered Spring Primrose
<note>original: "Primula veris flor: luteo." This is the common Cowslip. The name *Primula veris* translates to "the little firstling of spring," identifying it as a quintessential early bloomer. It is shown here with its characteristic <term>rosette</term>—a circle of leaves growing close to the ground.</note>

---

->I.<-
## Smallest
## Borage
<note>original: "Borrago Minima." A miniature relative of the common Borage. The illustration highlights the plant's surprisingly large <term>taproot</term>, the central downward-growing root that helps the plant survive in various soil conditions.</note>

---

->IIII.<-
### Herb of the Trinity
<note>original: "Herba Trinitatis." Known today as the Wild Pansy or Heartsease (*Viola tricolor*). It earned the name "Herb of the Trinity" because its petals typically display three distinct colors—purple, yellow, and white—which Renaissance observers viewed as a floral representation of the Holy Trinity.</note>

<summary>This botanical plate presents five spring-flowering plants, including the Marsh Marigold and the Wild Pansy, meticulously documented with their root systems and early modern names.</summary>

<keywords>False Black Hellebore, Adonis vernalis, Marsh Marigold, Caltha palustris, Cowslip, Primula veris, Borage, Wild Pansy, Viola tricolor, Herb of the Trinity, botanical engraving, Basilius Besler</keywords>

--- Page 387 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page's discussion on early spring woodland plants like the "Herb of the Trinity" (Wild Pansy) and the Marsh Marigold, this page focuses on some of the earliest and most robust perennials to emerge from damp winter soils: the Winter Aconite and the Butterbur.</meta>

->II.<-
### Winter Aconite
<note>original: "Aconitum hyemale." Now known to modern botanists as *Eranthis hyemalis*, the "winter flower." Although the author calls it a type of Aconite, it is actually a member of the buttercup family. It is famous for being one of the first flowers to appear, often blooming while snow is still on the ground. The illustration captures its characteristic "ruff" of green leaves situated just below the yellow blossom.</note>

---

->III.<-
### White-flowered Butterbur
<note>original: "Petasites flore albo." This is the white variety of the Butterbur (*Petasites albus*). The name <term>Petasites</term> is derived from the Greek word *petasos*, meaning a "wide-brimmed hat," a reference to the plant's massive leaves which grow large enough to be used as umbrellas. In this stage of growth, however, the focus is on the flowering stalk which appears before the leaves fully expand.</note>

---

->I.<-
### Common Butterbur
<note>original: "Petasites." Likely the *Petasites hybridus*. Historically referred to as "Pestilence Wort," it was highly valued in folk medicine for its supposed ability to treat the plague. The engraving emphasizes the plant’s complex root system, featuring a thick, horizontal <term>rhizome</term> (an underground stem) and a dense <term>thyrse</term>—the club-shaped cluster of small flowers shown at the top of the stalk.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates three early-blooming perennials: the golden Winter Aconite and two varieties of Butterbur, highlighting their distinctive flowering stalks and substantial root systems.</summary>
<keywords>Winter Aconite, Butterbur, Petasites, Eranthis hyemalis, rhizome, medicinal plants, botanical illustration, early spring flowers</keywords>

--- Page 388 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page's focus on the early-blooming Winter Aconite and Butterbur, this page illustrates five additional woodland and meadow plants that emerge in the early spring, noted for their distinctive flowers and medicinal uses.</meta>

->III.<-
### White Pasqueflower
<note>original: "Pulsatilla flore albo." Currently classified as a member of the *Pulsatilla* genus (part of the buttercup family). The name <term>Pulsatilla</term> is derived from the Latin *pulsare*, meaning "to strike" or "to shake," referring to the way these delicate flowers sway or "pulse" in the wind. This specific variety is noted for its brilliant white petals and the fine, silky hairs that cover its stems and leaves to protect it from late frosts.</note>

---

->II.<-
### Blue Pasqueflower
<note>original: "Pulsatilla flore cœruleo." This is the blue or violet variety of the Pasqueflower, often known as the "Easter Flower" because it blooms around the time of the spring holiday. The engraving highlights its nodding, bell-shaped bloom. Historically, these plants were used in small, careful doses for their sedative properties, though they are toxic if consumed raw.</note>

---

->I.<-
### Coltsfoot
<note>original: "Tussilago." Known today as *Tussilago farfara*. The name is a combination of the Latin *tussis* (cough) and *agere* (to act upon or drive away), marking it as one of the most famous historical remedies for chest complaints. The illustration shows the plant in its early flowering stage; the scaly, leafless stalks appear in early spring, while the large, hoof-shaped leaves—from which it gets its common English name—do not emerge until after the flowers have withered.</note>

---

->V.<-
### Golden Saxifrage
<note>original: "Saxifraga aurea." Known to modern botanists as *Chrysosplenium*. The name <term>Saxifraga</term> translates to "stone-breaker." This was often a reference to the plant's habit of growing in the crevices of rocks, leading ancient herbalists to believe, by the "Doctrine of Signatures," that the plant could help dissolve "stones" within the human body.</note>

---

->IIII.<-
### White Wood Anemone
<note>original: "Ranunculus nemorosus albus." While the author classifies this as a "White Grove Buttercup," it is known today as *Anemone nemorosa*. It is a quintessential spring ephemeral of ancient woodlands. The engraving shows its deeply lobed leaves and delicate, star-like white flower. It spreads slowly through the soil via a brittle, horizontal <term>rhizome</term> (underground stem), often forming a dense white carpet on the forest floor before the tree canopy closes.</note>

<summary>This page catalogs five early spring perennials, focusing on different varieties of Pasqueflowers and common woodland plants like Coltsfoot and Wood Anemone, highlighting their physical characteristics and traditional medicinal roles.</summary>
<keywords>Pasqueflower, Pulsatilla, Coltsfoot, Tussilago, Golden Saxifrage, Wood Anemone, spring ephemerals, medicinal herbs, rhizome</keywords>

--- Page 389 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page's descriptions of early spring herbs like Pasqueflower and Golden Saxifrage, this page illustrates four additional specimens including a medicinal vine, two woody shrubs, and a well-known spring bulb.</meta>

->I.<-
### Common Birthwort
->with a purple flower<-
<note>original: "Aristolochia Vulgaris flore purpureo." The name <term>Aristolochia</term> is derived from the Greek words *aristos* (best) and *lochia* (childbirth), referring to its ancient use in aiding labor. This "Common" variety is shown with its distinct heart-shaped leaves and the curved, tube-like flowers that characterize the genus.</note>

---

->II.<-
### Spurge-Laurel
<note>original: "Laureola." Now known as *Daphne laureola*. Although the name suggests a laurel, it is actually a species of Daphne. This evergreen shrub is one of the earliest to bloom in the forest understory, producing small, fragrant, yellowish-green flowers. Historically, its bark and berries were used in medicine, though they are highly toxic.</note>

---

->III.<-
### Dogwood
<note>original: "Cornus." Most likely the Cornelian Cherry (*Cornus mas*), a species of dogwood native to southern Europe. It is famous for its tiny yellow flowers that erupt on bare branches in late winter or very early spring, long before the leaves emerge. The wood is exceptionally hard—as dense as horn—which is why it bears a name derived from the Latin *cornu* (horn).</note>

---

->IIII.<-
### Spring Crocus
->with a violet flower<-
<note>original: "Crocus Vernus flore Violaceo." A familiar sight in early spring gardens. The illustration highlights the plant's <term>corm</term> (the bulb-like underground stem) and its narrow, silver-striped leaves. While its relative, the Autumn Crocus, provides the spice saffron, this spring variety was primarily valued for its vibrant color following the winter months.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates four distinct spring-blooming plants, ranging from the medicinal Birthwort to the hardy Dogwood and the decorative Spring Crocus, emphasizing their unique reproductive structures and early growth habits.</summary>
<keywords>Birthwort, Spurge-Laurel, Dogwood, Crocus, medicinal herbs, spring flora, botanical illustration, Aristolochia, Daphne, Cornus</keywords>
